<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/01-measurements-units-and-uncertainties.md -->

## Measurements, units, uncertainties and estimation

Every number in physics has a unit and an uncertainty. This file covers SI base and derived units, prefixes, converting units, errors and uncertainties (including on graphs), and order-of-magnitude estimates. It is used in every other topic.

### Overview

AS & A for every board. Practical techniques, planning and the required or core practicals of each board are collected in file 32. Graph skills specific to one experiment are in that experiment's file.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.1.1 to 3.1.3. Edexcel 1, 3, 4. OCR A 2.1.1, 2.1.2, 2.2.1. OCR B Module 2 (Fundamental data analysis). Eduqas C1.1(a) to (c).
- AQA says dimensional analysis is not required. OCR A 2.1.2(d) and Eduqas C1.1(c) ask you to check equations for homogeneity using base units.
- OCR B 2(b)(ii) adds the terms sensitivity and response time, and 2(b)(iii) asks for dot-plots of repeated readings to estimate the mean and spread and spot outliers.
- Eduqas C1.1(a) lists six base units (kg, m, s, A, mol, K). AQA lists the same six quantities. Nobody needs the candela.
- OCR A 2.1.2(f) asks for the convention for labelling graph axes and table headings (quantity / unit).

### 3.1.1 Use of SI units and their prefixes

*Refs: AQA 3.1.1 · Edexcel 1 · OCR A 2.1.1(a), 2.1.2(a) to (f) · OCR B 2(b)(i) · Eduqas C1.1(a) to (c). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The six SI base quantities used at A-level and their base units: mass (kg), length (m), time (s), amount of substance (mol), temperature (K) and electric current (A).
- Derived units are built from base units by multiplying and dividing, for example N = kg m s⁻², J = kg m² s⁻², W = kg m² s⁻³, C = A s, V = kg m² s⁻³ A⁻¹.
- The prefixes T, G, M, k, c, m, μ, n, p and f, with their powers of ten, and how to write values in standard form.
- How to convert between units of the same quantity, such as joules and electronvolts, or joules and kilowatt-hours.
- (OCR A, Eduqas) How to check that an equation is homogeneous: both sides must reduce to the same base units.

#### Explained

**Base and derived.** A base quantity is one chosen to be independent of the others; its unit is defined directly. A derived quantity is defined by an equation linking base quantities, and its unit follows from that equation. Speed is distance ÷ time, so its unit is m s⁻¹.

| Base quantity | Unit | Symbol |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Mass | kilogram | kg |
| Length | metre | m |
| Time | second | s |
| Electric current | ampere | A |
| Temperature | kelvin | K |
| Amount of substance | mole | mol |

To find the base units of a derived unit, write down a defining equation and substitute:

- Force F = ma → kg × m s⁻² = kg m s⁻² (the newton).
- Energy W = Fs → kg m s⁻² × m = kg m² s⁻² (the joule).
- Power P = W/t → kg m² s⁻³ (the watt).
- Charge Q = It → A s (the coulomb).
- Potential difference V = W/Q → kg m² s⁻³ A⁻¹ (the volt).
- Resistance R = V/I → kg m² s⁻³ A⁻² (the ohm).
- Pressure p = F/A → kg m⁻¹ s⁻² (the pascal).

**Prefixes.**

| Prefix | Symbol | Multiplier |
| --- | --- | --- |
| tera | T | 10¹² |
| giga | G | 10⁹ |
| mega | M | 10⁶ |
| kilo | k | 10³ |
| centi | c | 10⁻² |
| milli | m | 10⁻³ |
| micro | μ | 10⁻⁶ |
| nano | n | 10⁻⁹ |
| pico | p | 10⁻¹² |
| femto | f | 10⁻¹⁵ |

Squared and cubed units need care: 1 cm² = (10⁻² m)² = 10⁻⁴ m², and 1 mm³ = 10⁻⁹ m³.

**Useful conversions.**

- 1 eV = 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ J (the energy gained by one electron accelerated through 1 V).
- 1 kW h = 1000 W × 3600 s = 3.6 × 10⁶ J.
- 1 tonne = 10³ kg; 1 litre = 10⁻³ m³; 1 hour = 3600 s.
- Speed: 1 km h⁻¹ = 1000 m ÷ 3600 s ≈ 0.278 m s⁻¹.

**Homogeneity** (OCR A, Eduqas). An equation that is physically correct must have the same base units on both sides, and every term that is added must have the same units. A homogeneous equation can still be wrong (a missing ½ or 2π has no units), but a non-homogeneous one is always wrong.

**Graph and table labels** (OCR A 2.1.2(f)). Write a column heading or axis label as quantity / unit, for example "time / s" or "length / m". The numbers in the column are then pure numbers.

#### Worked example

1. Show that the equation for the speed of a wave on a string, v = √(T/μ), is homogeneous, where T is tension (N) and μ is mass per unit length (kg m⁻¹).

   T/μ has units kg m s⁻² ÷ kg m⁻¹ = m² s⁻². Square root: m s⁻¹, which is the unit of speed. Homogeneous.

2. A kettle of power 2.4 kW runs for 3.5 minutes. Find the energy in J and kW h.

   E = Pt = 2400 W × 210 s = 5.04 × 10⁵ J. In kW h: 2.4 kW × (3.5/60) h = 0.14 kW h. Check: 0.14 × 3.6 × 10⁶ = 5.04 × 10⁵ J.

3. An X-ray photon has energy 85 keV. Convert to joules.

   85 × 10³ × 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ = 1.36 × 10⁻¹⁴ J.

4. A wire has cross-sectional area 0.20 mm². Write this in m².

   0.20 × (10⁻³)² = 2.0 × 10⁻⁷ m².

#### Common mistakes

- Writing kg as a derived unit, or treating the newton as a base unit.
- Converting mm² to m² by multiplying by 10⁻³ instead of 10⁻⁶.
- Using Kg, KG or Mg when you mean kg; mixing up m (milli) and M (mega).
- Forgetting to convert minutes or hours to seconds before using P = E/t.
- Labelling an axis "time (seconds)" in some places and "s" in others; use quantity / unit throughout.

### 3.1.2 Limitation of physical measurements

*Refs: AQA 3.1.2 · Edexcel 4 · OCR A 2.2.1(a) to (d) · OCR B 2(a)(i), (ii), 2(b)(ii) to (iv), 2(c)(i), (ii). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The difference between random and systematic errors, and how each can be reduced or removed.
- The meanings of precision, repeatability, reproducibility, resolution and accuracy (OCR B also: sensitivity, response time, zero error).
- Absolute, fractional and percentage uncertainties, and how to give the uncertainty in a final answer.
- How to combine uncertainties when quantities are added, subtracted, multiplied, divided or raised to a power.
- How the number of significant figures in a value links to its uncertainty.
- How to draw error bars and find the uncertainty in the gradient and intercept of a straight-line graph using a best line and a worst acceptable line.

#### Explained

**Errors.** An error is the difference between a measured value and the true value. There are two kinds.

- *Random errors* make repeated readings scatter unpredictably either side of the mean. Examples: judging when a pendulum passes the centre, fluctuations in a reading, parallax that varies from reading to reading. Reduce their effect by repeating and averaging, by measuring many cycles (time 20 oscillations, not 1), and by plotting a graph and drawing a line of best fit. They cannot be removed completely.
- *Systematic errors* shift every reading in the same direction by the same amount or proportion. Examples: a zero error on a micrometer, a meter that reads 2% high, reaction time when starting and stopping a stopwatch the same way, heat loss in every run of a heating experiment. Repeating does not help. Reduce them by calibrating instruments against a known standard, checking and correcting zero errors, changing the method, or using a graph: a zero error shows up as an intercept but does not change the gradient.

**Key terms.**

| Term | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Accuracy | How close a measurement is to the true value |
| Precision | How close repeated measurements are to each other (small spread); says nothing about the true value |
| Repeatability | The same person, method and equipment give the same results when repeated |
| Reproducibility | Different people, methods or equipment give the same results |
| Resolution | The smallest change in the quantity that the instrument can show (one scale division, or the last digit on a digital display) |
| Sensitivity (OCR B) | Change in output per unit change in input, for example mV per °C |
| Response time (OCR B) | How long an instrument takes to settle on a new reading |
| Zero error | The instrument reads a non-zero value when it should read zero |

A set of readings can be precise but inaccurate (tightly grouped, but all offset by a systematic error), or accurate on average but imprecise (large random scatter about the true value).

**Uncertainty.** The uncertainty is the interval within which the true value is expected to lie, written as value ± uncertainty. It comes from:

- *Resolution*: for a single reading on a scale, take ± half the smallest division (some boards accept ± one division; state your choice). For a length found from two readings (both ends of a ruler), both readings carry an uncertainty.
- *Spread of repeats*: uncertainty ≈ half the range = (largest − smallest) ÷ 2. Leave out anomalies before calculating the mean.

The *absolute* uncertainty has the same units as the quantity. The *fractional* uncertainty is absolute ÷ value. The *percentage* uncertainty is fractional × 100%.

**Combining uncertainties.**

| Operation | Rule |
| --- | --- |
| y = a + b or y = a − b | Add the absolute uncertainties: Δy = Δa + Δb |
| y = ab or y = a/b | Add the percentage (fractional) uncertainties |
| y = aⁿ | Multiply the percentage uncertainty by n |
| y = ka (k an exact constant) | Same percentage uncertainty as a |
| y = √a | Half the percentage uncertainty of a |

OCR B 2(c)(ii) also accepts the *extreme-value* method: recalculate y using the largest and smallest values the inputs could take; half the difference is the uncertainty in y.

**Significant figures.** Give a final answer to the same number of significant figures as the least precise input data (or one more). The uncertainty itself is usually given to one (or at most two) significant figures, and the value is rounded to the same decimal place: 9.81 ± 0.13 m s⁻² is better written 9.8 ± 0.1 m s⁻².

**Error bars and gradients.** Draw each point with a vertical (and if needed horizontal) bar showing ± its absolute uncertainty. Draw the line of best fit through the error bars. Then draw the *worst acceptable line*: the steepest (or shallowest) straight line that still passes through all the error bars. Then:

- Uncertainty in gradient = |best gradient − worst gradient|.
- Percentage uncertainty in gradient = (that difference ÷ best gradient) × 100%.
- The uncertainty in the intercept is found in the same way from the two lines.

Some points may have error bars too small to draw; then use the scatter of the points.

**Dot-plots** (OCR B 2(b)(iii)). Plot each repeated reading as a dot on a number line. The cluster shows the likely value, the spread shows the uncertainty, and any point far from the cluster is a possible outlier to investigate (a misread scale, a timing slip).

**Largest source of uncertainty** (OCR B 2(a)(ii)). Calculate the percentage uncertainty of each measurement. The biggest one dominates, so improving the experiment means attacking that one first.

#### Worked example

1. A student measures the diameter of a wire with a micrometer at five places: 0.36, 0.38, 0.37, 0.37, 0.38 mm. Find the mean, its absolute uncertainty and the percentage uncertainty in the cross-sectional area.

   Mean = 1.86 ÷ 5 = 0.372 mm, so 0.37 mm. Half range = (0.38 − 0.36)/2 = 0.01 mm. Percentage uncertainty in d = 0.01/0.37 × 100 = 2.7%. A = πd²/4, so percentage uncertainty in A = 2 × 2.7% = 5.4% (π and 4 are exact).

2. The time for 10 swings of a pendulum is 15.6 ± 0.2 s and its length is 0.600 ± 0.002 m. Find g = 4π²L/T² and its uncertainty.

   T = 1.56 s; percentage uncertainty in T = 0.2/15.6 × 100 = 1.3% (timing 10 swings divides the absolute uncertainty by 10 but keeps the same percentage). g = 4π² × 0.600 / 1.56² = 9.73 m s⁻².
   Percentage uncertainty: L: 0.002/0.600 = 0.33%; T²: 2 × 1.3% = 2.6%. Total ≈ 2.9%.
   Δg = 0.029 × 9.73 = 0.28 m s⁻². Answer: g = 9.7 ± 0.3 m s⁻².

3. Two lengths are 25.0 ± 0.1 cm and 13.4 ± 0.1 cm. Find their difference.

   11.6 ± 0.2 cm. Note: the percentage uncertainty has risen from 0.4% to 1.7%, which is why taking a small difference of two large readings is a poor method.

4. On a graph the best-fit gradient is 4.20 V A⁻¹ and the steepest acceptable line has gradient 4.47 V A⁻¹. Find the percentage uncertainty.

   (4.47 − 4.20)/4.20 × 100 = 6.4%.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying precision and accuracy mean the same thing.
- Saying repeating readings reduces systematic error. It does not.
- Adding percentage uncertainties for a sum or difference. For + and −, add absolute uncertainties.
- Forgetting to double the percentage uncertainty when a quantity is squared.
- Quoting an uncertainty to four significant figures, or a value to more decimal places than its uncertainty.
- Drawing the worst line through the first and last points instead of through the extremes of the error bars.
- Calling human error a type of error. Name the actual source (reaction time, parallax) and whether it is random or systematic.

### 3.1.3 Estimation of physical quantities

*Refs: AQA 3.1.3 · Edexcel 3 · OCR A 2.1.1(b) · OCR B 2(c)(iii). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- What an order of magnitude is, and how to give a value to the nearest power of ten.
- Sensible estimates of everyday quantities, and how to combine them with physics to make further estimates.

#### Explained

An order of magnitude is the power of ten nearest to a value. 3000 is about 10³; 7000 is closer to 10⁴ (the switch is at about 3.16, the square root of 10, in the multiplier).

Estimating means choosing sensible round values, saying what you assumed, and then calculating. Final answers should be given to one significant figure or as a power of ten.

| Quantity | Typical value |
| --- | --- |
| Mass of an adult | 70 kg |
| Height of an adult | 1.7 m |
| Walking speed | 1.5 m s⁻¹ |
| Running speed (sprinter) | 10 m s⁻¹ |
| Car on a motorway | 30 m s⁻¹ |
| Mass of a car | 1000 to 1500 kg |
| Height of one storey | 3 m |
| Diameter of an atom | 10⁻¹⁰ m |
| Diameter of a nucleus | 10⁻¹⁵ to 10⁻¹⁴ m |
| Wavelength of visible light | 4 to 7 × 10⁻⁷ m |
| Power of a kettle | 2 to 3 kW |
| Mains p.d. (UK) | 230 V |
| Radius of the Earth | 6.4 × 10⁶ m |
| Density of water | 1000 kg m⁻³ |
| Density of air | 1.2 kg m⁻³ |
| Atmospheric pressure | 1.0 × 10⁵ Pa |

#### Worked example

1. Estimate the kinetic energy of a family car on a motorway.

   m ≈ 1200 kg, v ≈ 30 m s⁻¹. Ek = ½ × 1200 × 30² = 5.4 × 10⁵ J, so about 10⁶ J.

2. Estimate the number of air molecules in a classroom.

   Room 8 m × 6 m × 3 m = 144 m³, so about 150 m³. Mass of air = 1.2 × 150 = 180 kg. Mean molar mass of air ≈ 0.029 kg mol⁻¹, so moles ≈ 180/0.029 ≈ 6000 mol. Molecules ≈ 6000 × 6 × 10²³ ≈ 4 × 10²⁷, about 10²⁸ to the nearest order of magnitude.

3. Estimate the power a student develops running up a flight of stairs.

   m = 60 kg, height of a flight ≈ 3 m, time ≈ 4 s. P = mgh/t = 60 × 9.8 × 3 / 4 ≈ 440 W, so about 10² to 10³ W (roughly 400 W).

#### Common mistakes

- Giving an estimate to four significant figures; this suggests a precision you do not have.
- Not stating the assumed values, so the examiner cannot follow the method.
- Using wildly unrealistic values (a car of 100 kg, a person 3 m tall).

### Check questions

1. Express the ohm in SI base units. (2 marks)
2. Show that the equation E = ½mv² is homogeneous. (2 marks)
3. A battery stores 0.012 kW h. Convert this to joules. (1 mark)
4. Explain the difference between precision and accuracy, using a set of readings to illustrate each. (3 marks)
5. A student times a trolley over a fixed distance using a hand-held stopwatch. Identify one random and one systematic error and say how each could be reduced. (4 marks)
6. The side of a cube is measured as 2.50 ± 0.02 cm and its mass is 122.0 ± 0.5 g. Calculate its density in kg m⁻³ with the absolute uncertainty. (4 marks)
7. Describe how to find the uncertainty in the gradient of a straight-line graph with error bars. (3 marks)
8. Estimate the energy needed to boil the water in a full kettle, starting from room temperature. The specific heat capacity of water is 4200 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. R = V/I and V = W/Q (1); kg m² s⁻³ A⁻² (1).
2. Left: J = kg m² s⁻² (1); right: kg × (m s⁻¹)² = kg m² s⁻², so the same; ½ has no units (1).
3. 0.012 × 3.6 × 10⁶ = 4.3 × 10⁴ J (1).
4. Precision: how closely repeated readings agree / small spread (1); accuracy: how close the result is to the true value (1); a valid illustration, e.g. readings 9.52, 9.53, 9.52 m s⁻² for g are precise but not accurate if the true value is 9.81 (1).
5. Random: variation in reaction time between runs (1); reduce by repeating and averaging / using light gates (1). Systematic: e.g. always starting the stopwatch late / a stopwatch that runs slow / distance measured from the wrong mark (1); reduce by using light gates / calibrating / measuring from the correct point (1).
6. V = 2.50³ = 15.6 cm³ = 1.56 × 10⁻⁵ m³; ρ = 0.1220/1.5625 × 10⁻⁵ = 7810 kg m⁻³ (1). % uncertainty: side 0.8%, so volume 2.4% (1); mass 0.4% (1); total 2.8%, so ρ = 7800 ± 200 kg m⁻³ (1).
7. Draw line of best fit through the error bars (1); draw steepest or shallowest line that still passes through all the error bars (1); uncertainty = difference between the gradients (as a percentage of the best gradient) (1).
8. Assume 1.5 kg (1.5 litres) of water heated from 20 °C to 100 °C (1); E = mcΔθ = 1.5 × 4200 × 80 (1); = 5 × 10⁵ J (about 10⁶ J to an order of magnitude) (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/06-vectors-moments-and-equilibrium.md -->

## Scalars, vectors, moments and equilibrium

Forces have size and direction, so they add as vectors. An object is in equilibrium when the forces on it cancel and the moments about any point cancel. This file covers adding and resolving vectors, free-body diagrams, inclined planes, moments, couples, centre of mass and the conditions for equilibrium.

### Overview

AS & A for every board. OCR B does not examine moments or couples. Moments in rotating systems (torque and angular acceleration) are in file 24.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.4.1.1, 3.4.1.2 · Edexcel 12 to 14, 16, 23, 24 · OCR A 2.3.1, 3.2.1(e), 3.2.3 · OCR B 4.2 (vectors only) · Eduqas C1.1(d) to (f), (h) to (k), C1.3(b).
- AQA and OCR B limit calculations of resultants to two vectors at right angles; scale drawings can use other angles. Edexcel 14 likewise. OCR A 2.3.1(c) asks for a vector triangle for any two vectors.
- OCR A 3.2.3(d) asks for an experiment to find the centre of gravity of an irregular object (also suggested by Edexcel); OCR A 3.2.3(f) the triangle of forces for three coplanar forces.
- Eduqas specified practical: finding unknown masses using the principle of moments.
- Couples: AQA, OCR A, Eduqas. Edexcel does not name couples.

### 3.4.1.1 Scalars and vectors

*Refs: AQA 3.4.1.1 · Edexcel 12, 13, 14, 16 · OCR A 2.3.1(a) to (d), 3.2.1(e), 3.2.3(e), (f) · OCR B 4.2(a)(i), (iii), (b)(i), (iii), (c)(i), (ii) · Eduqas C1.1(d), (e), (f), (k), C1.3(b). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- A scalar has size only; a vector has size and direction. Examples of each.
- Adding vectors by calculation (two at right angles) or by scale drawing (any angle).
- Resolving a vector into two perpendicular components, including along and perpendicular to an inclined plane.
- Conditions for equilibrium of two or three coplanar forces at a point; using resolved forces or a closed triangle.
- Equilibrium means at rest or moving with constant velocity.
- Free-body diagrams.

#### Explained

| Scalars | Vectors |
| --- | --- |
| distance, speed, mass, time, energy, power, temperature, density, pressure, charge | displacement, velocity, acceleration, force (including weight), momentum, electric and gravitational field strength |

Weight is a vector (a force, downwards); mass is a scalar. Pressure is a scalar even though it comes from a force.

**Adding vectors.** Draw them tip to tail; the resultant goes from the start of the first to the end of the last.

- Two vectors at right angles: magnitude R = √(A² + B²); direction tan θ = B/A.
- Any angle (scale drawing): choose a scale, draw the first vector, draw the second from its tip at the correct angle, measure the resultant and its angle. (OCR A may also expect the cosine rule.)
- Subtracting a vector means adding its negative: Δv = v − u.

**Resolving.** A vector F at angle θ to the horizontal has components F cos θ (horizontal, adjacent to θ) and F sin θ (vertical). Choose axes that make the problem easy.

**Inclined plane.** For an object of weight W on a slope at angle θ to the horizontal:

- Component of weight down the slope = W sin θ.
- Component of weight perpendicular to the slope = W cos θ, balanced by the normal contact force R if there is no acceleration perpendicular to the slope.
- If the object is at rest, friction up the slope = W sin θ.

**Free-body diagrams** (Edexcel 16, OCR A 3.2.1(e), Eduqas C1.3(b)). Draw only the object, with every force acting on it as an arrow from the point where it acts, labelled with its name or symbol. Do not include forces the object exerts on other things. Common forces: weight W (from the centre of mass), normal contact force R or N (perpendicular to the surface), friction (along the surface, opposing sliding), tension T (along a string, away from the object), drag, upthrust, thrust.

**Equilibrium.** An object is in equilibrium if the resultant force on it is zero (and the resultant moment is zero, see below). It is then at rest or moving at constant velocity.

- Two forces: equal in size, opposite in direction, along the same line.
- Three coplanar forces at a point: either resolve in two perpendicular directions and set each sum to zero, or draw them tip to tail: they form a closed triangle (triangle of forces).

#### Worked example

1. A boat is rowed at 2.5 m s⁻¹ due north across a river flowing east at 1.2 m s⁻¹. Find its resultant velocity.

   R = √(2.5² + 1.2²) = 2.77 m s⁻¹. tan θ = 1.2/2.5, θ = 25.6° east of north.

2. A 4.0 kg box rests on a slope at 30°. Find the normal contact force and the friction force.

   W = 39.2 N. R = W cos 30° = 34.0 N. Friction = W sin 30° = 19.6 N up the slope.

3. A 12 N picture hangs from two strings, each at 40° to the horizontal. Find the tension in each.

   Vertical: 2T sin 40° = 12, so T = 9.3 N. Horizontal components cancel.

4. A sledge is pulled with a force of 80 N at 25° above the horizontal. Find the horizontal and vertical components.

   Horizontal 80 cos 25° = 72.5 N; vertical 80 sin 25° = 33.8 N.

#### Common mistakes

- Swapping sin and cos on a slope: the component down the slope is W sin θ, where θ is the slope angle.
- Including forces exerted by the object on other bodies in a free-body diagram.
- Saying an object moving at constant speed in a straight line cannot be in equilibrium.
- Adding magnitudes of vectors at an angle as if they were scalars.

### 3.4.1.2 Moments

*Refs: AQA 3.4.1.2 · Edexcel 23, 24 · OCR A 3.2.3(a) to (e) · Eduqas C1.1(h), (i), (j), (k). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The moment of a force about a point = force × perpendicular distance from the point to the line of action of the force.
- A couple is a pair of equal, opposite, coplanar forces whose lines of action do not coincide; moment (torque) of a couple = one force × perpendicular distance between the lines of action.
- The principle of moments.
- Centre of mass (and centre of gravity): for a uniform regular solid it is at the geometric centre.
- Equilibrium of an extended body: no resultant force and no resultant moment.
- (OCR A, Edexcel) Finding the centre of gravity of an irregular object experimentally.

#### Explained

**Moment** = Fd, unit N m, where d is the perpendicular distance from the pivot to the line of action. If the force is at angle θ to the rod, use the component perpendicular to the rod: moment = F × l sin θ.

**Principle of moments.** For an object in equilibrium, the sum of the clockwise moments about any point equals the sum of the anticlockwise moments about that point.

**Conditions for equilibrium of an extended object.**

1. The resultant force is zero (in every direction).
2. The resultant moment about any point is zero.

Tip: take moments about the point where an unknown force acts, so that force has zero moment and drops out.

**Couples.** Two equal and opposite forces F, a perpendicular distance d apart. The resultant force is zero, so there is no linear acceleration, but there is a turning effect: torque = Fd. Examples: turning a steering wheel with both hands, twisting a tap, the forces on a coil in a motor.

**Centre of mass and centre of gravity.** The centre of mass is the point where the whole mass can be taken to act; the centre of gravity is the point where the weight appears to act. In a uniform field they are the same point. For a uniform, symmetrical object it is at the geometric centre (middle of a uniform rod, centre of a sphere). An object hung freely comes to rest with its centre of gravity directly below the pivot. An object topples when its centre of gravity moves outside its base, because the weight then has a moment that tips it further.

**Finding the centre of gravity of an irregular lamina** (OCR A 3.2.3(d)). Hang the shape from a pin through a hole near its edge so it swings freely. Hang a plumb line from the same pin and draw the vertical line on the shape. Repeat from a second (and third, as a check) hole. The centre of gravity is where the lines cross. For a rod: balance it on a knife edge and find the balance point.

**Stability.** A low centre of gravity and wide base make an object stable: it must be tilted further before its centre of gravity passes outside the base.

#### Worked example

1. A uniform plank of length 4.0 m and weight 200 N rests on supports at each end. A 600 N person stands 1.0 m from the left end. Find the forces from each support.

   Moments about left end: R_right × 4.0 = 600 × 1.0 + 200 × 2.0 = 1000, so R_right = 250 N. Vertical: R_left = 800 − 250 = 550 N.

2. A spanner 0.25 m long is pushed at its end with 60 N at 70° to the handle. Find the moment about the nut.

   Perpendicular component = 60 sin 70° = 56.4 N. Moment = 56.4 × 0.25 = 14.1 N m.

3. (Eduqas practical) A metre rule balances at its 50.0 cm mark. A known 100 g mass at 10.0 cm balances an unknown mass at 75.0 cm. Find the unknown mass.

   100 × 40.0 = m × 25.0, so m = 160 g.

4. A uniform 2.0 m beam of weight 150 N is hinged to a wall and held horizontal by a cable attached at its far end at 30° above the horizontal. Find the cable tension.

   Moments about the hinge: T sin 30° × 2.0 = 150 × 1.0, so T = 150 N.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the distance along the rod instead of the perpendicular distance.
- Forgetting the weight of the beam itself, acting at its centre.
- Saying a couple produces a resultant force.
- Taking moments about a point and still including the force through that point.

#### Practical

**Principle of moments to find an unknown mass** (Eduqas specified practical). Balance a metre rule on a knife edge and note the balance point (its centre of gravity). Hang the unknown mass at a fixed distance on one side and move a known mass on the other side until the rule balances. Record both perpendicular distances from the pivot. Repeat for several positions; plot known-mass distance against unknown-mass distance (gradient = m_unknown/m_known). Reduce error by using thin loops of thread rather than thick hangers, reading distances at eye level, and checking balance by small taps.

### Check questions

1. Explain why a person walking at constant velocity is in equilibrium. (2 marks)
2. Two forces of 5.0 N and 12 N act at right angles. Find the resultant and its angle to the 12 N force. (3 marks)
3. A 50 kg skier stands still on a slope of 20°. Calculate the friction force on the skier. (2 marks)
4. Three forces act on a point object in equilibrium: 8.0 N east, 6.0 N north and a third force F. Find F. (2 marks)
5. Define the moment of a couple. (2 marks)
6. A uniform 3.0 m ladder of weight 250 N leans against a smooth wall at 60° to the ground. Calculate the horizontal force from the wall. (4 marks)
7. Describe how to find the centre of gravity of an irregular sheet of card. (4 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Constant velocity means zero acceleration (1); so resultant force is zero (by Newton's first law) (1).
2. R = √(25 + 144) = 13 N (1); tan θ = 5/12 (1); θ = 22.6° (1).
3. W = 50 × 9.81 = 490 N (1); friction = 490 sin 20° = 168 N (1).
4. F = √(64 + 36) = 10 N (1); direction south-west, at 36.9° south of west (or 53.1° west of south) (1).
5. One of the forces × perpendicular distance between their lines of action (1); the forces are equal, opposite and parallel (1).
6. Wall force horizontal, ground has normal and friction (1); moments about the foot: 250 × 1.5 cos 60° = F × 3.0 sin 60° (1); 187.5 = 2.598F (1); F = 72 N (1).
7. Hang from a pin through a hole so it swings freely (1); hang a plumb line from the pin and mark the vertical line (1); repeat from another hole (1); centre of gravity is where the lines cross; check with a third hole (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/30-density-pressure-upthrust-and-viscosity.md -->

## Fluids: density, pressure, upthrust, drag, viscosity and Stokes' law

Fluids (liquids and gases) push on things inside them and resist things moving through them. This file covers density and how to measure it, pressure (including pressure in a fluid, p = hρg), upthrust and Archimedes' principle, drag, viscosity, laminar and turbulent flow, Stokes' law and the falling-ball method for viscosity (Edexcel core practical 4).

### Overview

AS & A for Edexcel (49 to 52, CP4), OCR A (3.2.4, with 3.2.2 drag) and Eduqas (C1.1(g) density). AQA has density in 3.4.2.1 (also in file 09) and uses Stokes' law in the Millikan experiment (file 26). Terminal velocity in air is in file 07. Bernoulli's equation and the drag equation for sport (Eduqas option C) are in file 24.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel 49 to 52, CP4 · OCR A 3.2.1(d), 3.2.2(a), (b), 3.2.4 · Eduqas C1.1(g) · AQA 3.4.2.1 (density), 3.12.1.4 (Stokes' law) · OCR B 4.2(d)(iii) (terminal velocity of a ball in a viscous liquid).
- Viscosity and Stokes' law F = 6πηrv: Edexcel 51, CP4. AQA uses F = 6πηrv only in the Millikan option. OCR A and OCR B describe drag and terminal velocity without Stokes' law.
- Pressure in a fluid p = hρg and Archimedes' principle: OCR A 3.2.4(c). Edexcel 50 uses upthrust = weight of fluid displaced.
- Eduqas specified practical: measuring the density of solids.

### Density

*Refs: Edexcel 49 · OCR A 3.2.4(a) · Eduqas C1.1(g) · AQA 3.4.2.1. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- ρ = m/V, unit kg m⁻³.
- How to measure the density of regular and irregular solids and of liquids.

#### Explained

Density is mass per unit volume: ρ = m/V. 1 g cm⁻³ = 1000 kg m⁻³. Water: 1000 kg m⁻³. Air: about 1.2 kg m⁻³. Aluminium: 2700 kg m⁻³. Iron: 7900 kg m⁻³.

#### Common mistakes

- Using g cm⁻³ in a calculation that needs kg m⁻³.
- Forgetting to subtract the starting reading when finding volume by displacement.

#### Practical

**Density of solids** (Eduqas specified practical). Regular solid: measure dimensions with vernier calipers (or a micrometer for small dimensions) at several places, calculate volume; measure mass on a top-pan balance. Irregular solid: lower it into a displacement (eureka) can full to the spout, and collect and measure the displaced water in a measuring cylinder; or use the difference in readings of a measuring cylinder before and after. Combine percentage uncertainties (file 01).

#### Worked example

1. A brass cylinder has diameter 2.00 cm, length 5.00 cm and mass 133 g. Find its density.

   V = π × 1.00² × 5.00 = 15.7 cm³. ρ = 133/15.7 = 8.47 g cm⁻³ = 8470 kg m⁻³.

### Pressure and upthrust

*Refs: Edexcel 50 · OCR A 3.2.1(d), 3.2.4(b), (c). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Pressure p = F/A for solids, liquids and gases. Unit Pa (N m⁻²).
- Pressure in a fluid at depth h: p = hρg.
- Upthrust on an object in a fluid; Archimedes' principle: upthrust = weight of fluid displaced.

#### Explained

**Pressure** is force per unit area, acting perpendicular to a surface. In a fluid at rest, pressure acts equally in all directions at a given depth.

**Pressure at depth.** Consider a column of fluid of height h and cross-sectional area A. Its weight is mg = ρVg = ρAhg, which acts on area A, so the pressure due to the fluid is p = hρg. This adds to any pressure at the surface (atmospheric pressure, about 1.0 × 10⁵ Pa): total pressure = p_atm + hρg. Pressure depends on depth and density only, not on the shape of the container.

**Upthrust.** An object in a fluid has greater pressure on its bottom (deeper) than on its top, so there is a net upward force: upthrust. For a block of height h and horizontal area A: upthrust = (p_bottom − p_top)A = hρ_fluid gA = ρ_fluid V g = weight of fluid displaced. This is **Archimedes' principle**: the upthrust on an object in a fluid equals the weight of fluid it displaces.

**Floating and sinking.** If the object's weight is greater than the maximum upthrust (when fully submerged), it sinks: this happens when its density is greater than the fluid's. A floating object sinks until the upthrust equals its weight: it displaces its own weight of fluid.

#### Worked example

1. Find the pressure 25 m below the surface of the sea (ρ = 1030 kg m⁻³).

   Fluid pressure = 25 × 1030 × 9.81 = 2.53 × 10⁵ Pa. Total = 3.5 × 10⁵ Pa (with atmospheric pressure).

2. A 0.40 kg steel ball (ρ = 7900 kg m⁻³) hangs from a newtonmeter, fully submerged in water. Find the reading.

   V = 0.40/7900 = 5.06 × 10⁻⁵ m³. Upthrust = 1000 × 5.06 × 10⁻⁵ × 9.81 = 0.50 N. Weight = 3.92 N. Reading = 3.43 N.

3. A wooden block (ρ = 600 kg m⁻³) floats in water. What fraction is under water?

   Weight of block = weight of water displaced: ρ_wood V g = ρ_water V_sub g; V_sub/V = 0.60.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the object's density in the upthrust formula.
- Forgetting atmospheric pressure when the total pressure is asked for.

### Drag, viscosity and Stokes' law

*Refs: Edexcel 51(a), 51(b), 52, CP4 · OCR A 3.2.2(a), (b) · OCR B 4.2(d)(iii) · AQA 3.12.1.4. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Drag is the frictional (resistive) force on an object moving through a fluid; factors affecting drag (OCR A).
- Viscous drag on a small sphere: F = 6πηrv (Stokes' law), where η is the viscosity.
- Stokes' law applies only to small spheres moving slowly with laminar flow (no turbulence); viscosity depends on temperature.
- Edexcel CP4: falling-ball method for viscosity.

#### Explained

**Viscosity** η is a measure of a fluid's resistance to flow (its "thickness"). Unit Pa s (N s m⁻²). Water: about 1 × 10⁻³ Pa s; glycerol: about 1.4 Pa s; air: 1.8 × 10⁻⁵ Pa s. The viscosity of liquids falls sharply as temperature rises (warm honey flows more easily); for gases it rises slightly.

**Laminar and turbulent flow.** In laminar (streamline) flow, layers of fluid slide smoothly past each other; streamlines are smooth and do not cross; velocity at any point is steady. In turbulent flow, the fluid swirls in eddies; streamlines mix; drag is much larger. Flow becomes turbulent at higher speeds, around larger objects and around non-streamlined shapes.

**Drag in air** (OCR A 3.2.2(b)). Drag increases with speed (roughly ∝ v² for fast objects), with cross-sectional area, with the density of the fluid, and depends on the shape (streamlining reduces it) and surface roughness.

**Stokes' law.** For a small sphere of radius r moving at speed v through a fluid of viscosity η, with laminar flow: **F = 6πηrv**.

**Terminal velocity of a sphere in a liquid.** Three forces: weight W = (4/3)πr³ρ_s g (down), upthrust U = (4/3)πr³ρ_f g (up), viscous drag F = 6πηrv (up). At terminal velocity: W = U + F, so

6πηrv_t = (4/3)πr³(ρ_s − ρ_f)g → **v_t = 2r²(ρ_s − ρ_f)g/(9η)**

Terminal velocity increases with r² (bigger balls fall faster) and is larger for denser balls and less viscous liquids.

#### Worked example

1. A steel ball (r = 1.0 mm, ρ = 7800 kg m⁻³) falls through glycerol (ρ = 1260 kg m⁻³) at a terminal speed of 1.0 × 10⁻² m s⁻¹. Find the viscosity.

   η = 2r²(ρ_s − ρ_f)g/(9v) = 2 × (1.0 × 10⁻³)² × 6540 × 9.81/(9 × 0.010) = 1.43 Pa s.

2. Find the drag on a 0.50 mm radius sphere moving at 2.0 cm s⁻¹ through water (η = 1.0 × 10⁻³ Pa s).

   F = 6π × 1.0 × 10⁻³ × 5.0 × 10⁻⁴ × 0.020 = 1.9 × 10⁻⁷ N.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the diameter as r.
- Applying Stokes' law to large or fast objects (turbulent flow).
- Ignoring upthrust when finding viscosity.

#### Practical

**Edexcel CP4** (also OCR B 4.2(d)(iii), OCR A PAG1): falling-ball method for the viscosity of a liquid.

1. Fill a tall measuring cylinder (or tube) with the liquid (e.g. glycerol or washing-up liquid). Put rubber bands round it at measured intervals, starting well below the surface so the ball has reached terminal velocity.
2. Measure the diameter of a small steel ball with a micrometer; find its mass on a balance (or use a known density). Measure the liquid's density (mass of a known volume).
3. Drop the ball gently at the centre of the surface. Time it between bands (using a stopwatch, or a video with a ruler in shot). Check terminal velocity has been reached: equal times for equal distances.
4. Repeat with several balls; use different radii and plot v_t against r² (straight line through the origin, gradient 2(ρ_s − ρ_f)g/(9η)).
5. Record the temperature, since η depends strongly on it.

Errors: parallax when judging when the ball passes a band (view at eye level); the walls of the tube increase drag (use a wide tube, drop at the centre); reaction time (use video analysis). Safety: clean spills (slippery), retrieve balls with a magnet.

### Check questions

1. Show that the pressure at depth h in a fluid of density ρ is hρg. (3 marks)
2. A submarine is at 150 m depth in sea water (ρ = 1030 kg m⁻³). Calculate the force on a window of area 0.050 m², ignoring atmospheric pressure. (2 marks)
3. State Archimedes' principle and explain why a steel ship floats. (3 marks)
4. A 1.2 kg block of volume 4.0 × 10⁻⁴ m³ is fully submerged in oil of density 850 kg m⁻³. Calculate the upthrust and the resultant force. (3 marks)
5. State two conditions under which Stokes' law applies. (2 marks)
6. Derive an expression for the terminal velocity of a sphere falling through a viscous liquid. (4 marks)
7. Describe the falling-ball method for measuring viscosity. (6 marks)
8. Explain why the terminal velocity of a ball bearing in honey increases when the honey is warmed. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Column of fluid of area A and height h has volume Ah and mass ρAh (1); weight = ρAhg (1); pressure = weight/A = hρg (1).
2. p = 150 × 1030 × 9.81 = 1.52 × 10⁶ Pa (1); F = 7.6 × 10⁴ N (1).
3. Upthrust equals the weight of fluid displaced (1); the hull encloses a large volume of air so the ship displaces a large volume of water (1); it sinks until weight of water displaced equals its weight / its average density is less than water (1).
4. Upthrust = 850 × 4.0 × 10⁻⁴ × 9.81 = 3.3 N (1); weight = 11.8 N (1); resultant = 8.4 N downwards (1).
5. Any two: small sphere; low speed; laminar flow / no turbulence.
6. Weight = (4/3)πr³ρ_s g; upthrust = (4/3)πr³ρ_f g (1); drag = 6πηrv (1); at terminal velocity weight = upthrust + drag (1); v_t = 2r²(ρ_s − ρ_f)g/(9η) (1).
7. Tall tube of liquid with marks well below surface (1); measure ball radius with micrometer and densities (1); drop ball at centre, time between marks (1); check equal times for equal distances (terminal velocity) (1); calculate v_t; η = 2r²(ρ_s − ρ_f)g/(9v_t) (1); repeat / different radii, plot v against r²; note temperature (1).
8. Viscosity of honey decreases when warmed (1); smaller drag for a given speed, so larger terminal velocity (v_t ∝ 1/η) (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/07-motion-projectiles-and-newtons-laws.md -->

## Motion in a straight line, projectiles, drag and Newton's laws

This file covers how to describe motion (displacement, velocity, acceleration and their graphs), the equations for uniform acceleration, free fall and AQA required practical 3, projectiles, friction, drag and terminal speed, and Newton's three laws.

### Overview

AS & A for every board. Momentum and impulse are in file 08. Viscous drag and Stokes' law (Edexcel, OCR A) are in file 30. Circular motion is in file 12.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.4.1.3 to 3.4.1.5, RP3 · Edexcel 9 to 11, 15, 17 to 20, CP1 · OCR A 3.1, 3.2.1, 3.2.2, 3.5.1(a) · OCR B 4.2 · Eduqas C1.2, C1.3(a), (c).
- Measuring g: AQA RP3, Edexcel CP1, OCR A 3.1.2(b)(ii) (PAG1), OCR B 4.2(d)(ii), Eduqas specified practical.
- OCR A 3.1.2(c) asks for stopping distance: reaction time, thinking distance and braking distance. No other board names it.
- OCR B 4.2(c)(xi) asks you to model motion in small time steps (iterative modelling, by spreadsheet or by hand).
- Eduqas specified practical: investigating Newton's second law.
- OCR A 3.2.2 asks for factors affecting drag and techniques to find terminal velocity (PAG1); OCR B 4.2(d)(iii) terminal velocity of a ball bearing in oil or paper cones.
- AQA asks for lift (qualitatively); others do not.

### 3.4.1.3 Motion along a straight line

*Refs: AQA 3.4.1.3, RP3 · Edexcel 9, 10, 11, 19, CP1 · OCR A 3.1.1(a) to (d), 3.1.2(a) to (c) · OCR B 4.2(a)(vii), (b)(ii), (c)(iii), (c)(xi), (d)(i), (ii) · Eduqas C1.2(a) to (e). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Displacement, speed, velocity and acceleration; instantaneous and average velocity.
- v = Δs/Δt and a = Δv/Δt.
- Gradients and areas of displacement–time, velocity–time and acceleration–time graphs, for uniform and non-uniform acceleration (e.g. a bouncing ball).
- The four equations of uniformly accelerated motion (suvat) and how to derive them.
- The acceleration due to gravity, g.
- AQA required practical 3 / Edexcel CP1: g by free fall.

#### Explained

| Quantity | Definition | Type |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Displacement s | Distance in a stated direction from the start point | Vector |
| Speed | Distance travelled ÷ time | Scalar |
| Velocity v | Rate of change of displacement, Δs/Δt | Vector |
| Acceleration a | Rate of change of velocity, Δv/Δt | Vector |

*Average* velocity = total displacement ÷ total time. *Instantaneous* velocity is the velocity at one moment = gradient of the tangent to the s–t graph at that time.

**Graphs.**

| Graph | Gradient | Area under line |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Displacement–time | Velocity | (no meaning) |
| Velocity–time | Acceleration | Displacement |
| Acceleration–time | (rate of change of acceleration, not needed) | Change in velocity |

For a curved s–t graph, draw a tangent and find its gradient for instantaneous velocity. For a curved v–t graph, estimate the area by counting squares or splitting into trapezia.

**Bouncing ball** (dropped, upward positive). v–t graph: straight lines of gradient −g while in the air (falling: v becomes more negative; after the bounce, v jumps to a positive value and decreases). At each bounce, the velocity changes direction almost instantly. Each bounce is lower because energy is lost, so the positive velocity after each bounce is smaller. The areas above and below the axis between bounces give the heights.

**Equations of uniform acceleration.** Only valid when a is constant.

- v = u + at (from a = (v − u)/t)
- s = ½(u + v)t (average velocity × time)
- s = ut + ½at² (substitute v)
- v² = u² + 2as (eliminate t)

Derivations from the v–t graph: a straight line from u to v over time t. Its gradient a gives v = u + at. The area is a trapezium, s = ½(u + v)t; split into a rectangle ut and a triangle ½(v − u)t = ½at², giving s = ut + ½at².

Choose a positive direction and stick to it: for a ball thrown upwards, if up is positive, a = −9.81 m s⁻².

**Free fall.** An object falling with only gravity acting has acceleration g = 9.81 m s⁻² near the Earth's surface, whatever its mass.

**Stopping distance** (OCR A 3.1.2(c)). Stopping distance = thinking distance + braking distance. Thinking distance = speed × reaction time (constant speed during reaction time). Braking distance = v²/2a (from v² = u² + 2as with final speed 0), so it rises with the square of speed. Tiredness, alcohol, drugs and distraction increase reaction time; worn tyres, wet or icy roads, heavy loads and worn brakes increase braking distance.

**Iterative modelling** (OCR B 4.2(c)(xi)). Split time into small steps Δt. In each step: new velocity = old velocity + aΔt; new displacement = old displacement + vΔt. Repeat. With a constant force this reproduces the suvat results as Δt gets smaller; the same method can handle forces that change (drag, springs, gravity in orbits).

#### Worked example

1. A car accelerates uniformly from 8.0 m s⁻¹ to 20 m s⁻¹ in 6.0 s. Find its acceleration and the distance travelled.

   a = (20 − 8)/6 = 2.0 m s⁻². s = ½(8 + 20) × 6 = 84 m.

2. A ball is thrown upwards at 15 m s⁻¹ from a cliff 25 m above the sea. How long before it hits the sea? (up positive, g = 9.81)

   s = −25 m, u = 15, a = −9.81. −25 = 15t − 4.905t². 4.905t² − 15t − 25 = 0. t = [15 + √(225 + 490.5)]/9.81 = (15 + 26.75)/9.81 = 4.26 s.

3. A driver with reaction time 0.70 s travels at 25 m s⁻¹. Braking deceleration is 6.5 m s⁻². Find the stopping distance (OCR A).

   Thinking = 25 × 0.70 = 17.5 m. Braking = 25²/(2 × 6.5) = 48.1 m. Total = 65.6 m.

4. (OCR B) Model a ball dropped from rest using Δt = 0.1 s for two steps.

   Step 1: v = 0 + 9.81 × 0.1 = 0.981 m s⁻¹; s = 0 + 0.981 × 0.1 = 0.098 m. Step 2: v = 1.962 m s⁻¹; s = 0.098 + 0.196 = 0.294 m. (Exact: ½ × 9.81 × 0.2² = 0.196 m. The model overestimates because it uses the end-of-step velocity; smaller Δt makes it more accurate.)

#### Common mistakes

- Using suvat when acceleration is not constant.
- Mixing signs: if up is positive, g is negative.
- Reading displacement from the gradient of a v–t graph.
- Using total distance where total displacement is asked (a ball thrown up and caught has zero displacement).

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 3** (also Edexcel CP1, OCR A 3.1.2(b)(ii) PAG1, OCR B 4.2(d)(ii), Eduqas specified practical): determine g by a free-fall method.

*Electromagnet and trapdoor.* A steel ball is held by an electromagnet above a trapdoor. Switching off the magnet starts an electronic timer; the ball hitting the trapdoor stops it. Measure the height h (bottom of the ball to the trapdoor) with a metre rule. Repeat each height three times and average t. Vary h from about 0.2 m to 1.0 m.

Since u = 0, h = ½gt². Plot h against t²: straight line through the origin, gradient = g/2, so g = 2 × gradient.

*Light gate alternative.* Drop a card or a picket fence (interrupt card) through one or two light gates; data logger records speeds; v² = 2gh, or a = (v₂ − v₁)/Δt.

Errors: the magnet may hold the ball briefly after the current is switched off (residual magnetism), adding a constant delay; use a small current or a paper spacer. A systematic error in h (measuring from the wrong point) gives an intercept but not a change in gradient. Air resistance is negligible for a small dense ball. Random error: repeat and average. Safety: catch the ball so it does not roll away; keep feet clear.

### 3.4.1.4 Projectile motion

*Refs: AQA 3.4.1.4 · Edexcel 15 · OCR A 3.1.3(a), (b), 3.2.2(a) to (d) · OCR B 4.2(a)(ii), (d)(iii) · Eduqas C1.2(f), (g). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Horizontal and vertical motions of a projectile in a uniform gravitational field are independent.
- Solve projectile problems with suvat.
- Friction qualitatively; lift and drag qualitatively (AQA).
- Air resistance increases with speed; terminal speed.
- The effect of air resistance on the path of a projectile and on the top speed of a vehicle.
- (OCR A) Factors affecting drag; motion of objects falling with and without air resistance.

#### Explained

**Independence.** Ignoring air resistance, the only force on a projectile is its weight, which acts vertically. So:

- Horizontally: no force, so constant velocity: x = u_x t, where u_x = u cos θ.
- Vertically: constant acceleration g downwards: y = u_y t − ½gt², where u_y = u sin θ.
- Time links the two.

A ball dropped and a ball fired horizontally from the same height land at the same time.

For a launch from level ground at angle θ with speed u:

- Time of flight: 2u sin θ / g.
- Maximum height: u² sin²θ / 2g.
- Range: u² sin 2θ / g, greatest at θ = 45°.

The path (with no air resistance) is a parabola.

**Friction** is a force that opposes relative motion (or the tendency to move) between surfaces in contact. It transfers kinetic energy to thermal energy.

**Drag** is the resistive force on an object moving through a fluid. It increases with speed (roughly ∝ v² for large, fast objects such as cars; ∝ v at low speed for small objects, see file 30). It depends on the object's cross-sectional area and shape, and on the fluid's density and viscosity.

**Lift** (AQA) is an upward force on an object moving through a fluid, at right angles to the flow, caused by the shape or angle of the object deflecting the fluid downwards (an aircraft wing, a spinning ball).

**Terminal speed.** When an object falls through a fluid:

1. At first, speed is low, drag is small, and the resultant force ≈ weight, so a ≈ g.
2. As speed rises, drag rises, the resultant force falls, so acceleration falls.
3. Eventually drag (plus any upthrust) equals weight: resultant force zero, acceleration zero, constant terminal speed.

A skydiver opening a parachute: area increases, drag jumps above weight, the diver decelerates until a new, lower terminal speed is reached.

v–t graph for a falling object with drag: starts with gradient g, curves over, levels off at terminal speed.

**Vehicles.** The maximum speed of a car is reached when the driving force equals the total resistive force (drag + friction). A streamlined shape (lower drag) or a more powerful engine (greater driving force) raises the top speed.

**Air resistance and projectiles.** Drag reduces horizontal speed, so the range is shorter. The path is no longer symmetrical: the descent is steeper than the ascent, and the maximum height is lower.

#### Worked example

1. A ball is kicked at 18 m s⁻¹ at 35° above horizontal from level ground. Find the time of flight, the range and the maximum height.

   u_y = 18 sin 35° = 10.32 m s⁻¹, u_x = 18 cos 35° = 14.74 m s⁻¹. Time to top = 10.32/9.81 = 1.052 s; flight = 2.10 s. Range = 14.74 × 2.10 = 31.0 m. Max height = 10.32²/(2 × 9.81) = 5.43 m.

2. A stone is thrown horizontally at 12 m s⁻¹ from a 20 m high cliff. How far from the base does it land, and with what speed?

   Vertical: 20 = 4.905t², t = 2.02 s. Horizontal: x = 12 × 2.02 = 24.2 m. v_y = 9.81 × 2.02 = 19.8 m s⁻¹. Speed = √(12² + 19.8²) = 23.2 m s⁻¹.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the total speed u in the vertical equation instead of u sin θ.
- Saying the horizontal velocity changes during flight (with no air resistance).
- Saying at terminal velocity there is no force acting (forces balance; resultant is zero).
- Saying a heavier object falls faster in a vacuum.

### 3.4.1.5 Newton's laws of motion

*Refs: AQA 3.4.1.5 · Edexcel 17, 18, 20 · OCR A 3.2.1(a) to (d), (f), 3.5.1(a) · OCR B 4.2(a)(viii), (c)(v) · Eduqas C1.3(a), (c). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Newton's three laws and how to apply them.
- F = ma for constant mass.
- (Edexcel 18, OCR A 3.2.1(c)) Weight W = mg; gravitational field strength g = F/m.
- (OCR A 3.2.1(d)) Tension, normal contact force, upthrust and friction.
- Newton's third law pairs: equal, opposite, same type, act on different bodies.

#### Explained

**First law.** An object stays at rest, or moves with constant velocity, unless a resultant force acts on it.

**Second law.** The resultant force on an object is proportional to its rate of change of momentum. For a constant mass this becomes **F = ma**, where F is the resultant force. One newton is the force that gives 1 kg an acceleration of 1 m s⁻².

**Third law.** If body A exerts a force on body B, then B exerts a force on A that is equal in size and opposite in direction. The two forces:

- act on different bodies;
- are the same type of force (both gravitational, both contact, both electrostatic...);
- have the same line of action.

Example: the Earth pulls a book down (its weight); the book pulls the Earth up with an equal gravitational force. The table's upward push on the book is *not* the third-law pair of the book's weight (it is a contact force, and both act on the book); its pair is the book's downward push on the table.

**Weight and mass.** W = mg, where g is the gravitational field strength (9.81 N kg⁻¹ on Earth). Mass is the amount of matter and is the same everywhere; weight depends on g.

**Applying F = ma.**

1. Draw a free-body diagram for each object.
2. Find the resultant force along the direction of acceleration.
3. Use F = ma (for connected objects, either for the whole system or for each part).

**Lifts.** In a lift accelerating upwards at a, a person's apparent weight (the reading on scales, which is the normal contact force R) is R = m(g + a). Accelerating downwards: R = m(g − a). In free fall, R = 0 (weightlessness).

**Connected bodies.** Two masses joined by a string over a pulley (Atwood machine): the whole system accelerates at a = (m₁ − m₂)g/(m₁ + m₂) if friction is negligible.

#### Worked example

1. A 1200 kg car has a driving force of 3000 N and total resistive force of 900 N. Find its acceleration.

   F = 3000 − 900 = 2100 N. a = 2100/1200 = 1.75 m s⁻².

2. A 60 kg person stands on scales in a lift accelerating downwards at 1.5 m s⁻². Find the scale reading.

   R = m(g − a) = 60 × (9.81 − 1.5) = 499 N.

3. A 2.0 kg trolley on a smooth table is pulled by a string over a pulley attached to a 0.50 kg hanging mass. Find the acceleration and the tension.

   Whole system: a = 0.50 × 9.81 / 2.5 = 1.96 m s⁻². Trolley: T = 2.0 × 1.96 = 3.9 N. (Check hanging mass: 4.905 − 3.9 = 0.50 × 1.96 ✓.)

#### Common mistakes

- Using the driving force in F = ma instead of the resultant force.
- Naming weight and normal contact force as a third-law pair.
- Saying a moving object needs a resultant force to keep moving at constant velocity.
- Forgetting that the tension in the string in the pulley problem is less than the hanging weight when it accelerates.

#### Practical

**Investigating Newton's second law** (Eduqas specified practical; suggested by AQA and OCR B). A trolley on a friction-compensated runway (tilted until the trolley rolls at constant speed when pushed) is pulled by a string over a pulley by hanging masses. Measure acceleration with two light gates (a = (v₂² − v₁²)/2s) or a light gate and a double card. To vary force but keep total mass constant, move masses from the trolley to the hanger. Plot a against F (= m_hanging × g): a straight line through the origin; gradient = 1/total mass. To vary mass at constant force, add masses to the trolley and plot a against 1/m.

**Terminal velocity** (OCR A 3.2.2(d)(ii) PAG1, OCR B 4.2(d)(iii)). Drop paper cones from a set height and film them, or time a ball bearing through marked intervals in a tall tube of oil; when equal intervals take equal times, terminal velocity has been reached.

### Check questions

1. Sketch the velocity–time graph for a ball dropped onto a hard floor that bounces twice. Take upwards as positive. (3 marks)
2. A train decelerates uniformly from 30 m s⁻¹ to rest over 450 m. Find the deceleration and the time taken. (3 marks)
3. Describe how to determine g using an electromagnet, trapdoor and timer, including how to analyse the results. (5 marks)
4. A dart is thrown horizontally at 9.0 m s⁻¹ and hits the board 2.4 m away. How far has it dropped? (3 marks)
5. Explain why a skydiver reaches a terminal velocity. (4 marks)
6. State Newton's third law and give two properties of a pair of forces in it. (3 marks)
7. A 750 kg lift is pulled up by a cable. The tension is 8500 N. Find the acceleration. (2 marks)
8. A golfer hits a ball at 40 m s⁻¹ at 20° to the horizontal. Ignoring air resistance, find its range on level ground. Describe two ways air resistance would change the path. (5 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Straight lines with the same negative gradient (−g) while in the air (1); velocity jumps from negative to positive at each bounce (1); peak positive velocity after each bounce smaller than the speed just before it (1).
2. a = (0 − 900)/(2 × 450) = −1.0 m s⁻² (deceleration 1.0 m s⁻²) (2); t = 30/1.0 = 30 s (1).
3. Ball held by electromagnet; switching off starts timer; ball hitting trapdoor stops it (1); measure height with metre rule from bottom of ball (1); repeat each height and average; several heights (1); plot h against t² (1); gradient = g/2 (1).
4. t = 2.4/9.0 = 0.267 s (1); drop = ½ × 9.81 × 0.267² (1) = 0.35 m (1).
5. Initially only weight acts, accelerates at g (1); as speed increases drag increases (1); resultant force and acceleration decrease (1); when drag equals weight, resultant zero, speed constant (1).
6. If A exerts a force on B, B exerts an equal and opposite force on A (1); any two: same type of force; act on different bodies; same line of action / equal magnitude and opposite direction (2).
7. F = 8500 − 750 × 9.81 = 1142 N (1); a = 1.5 m s⁻² (1).
8. u_x = 37.6, u_y = 13.7 m s⁻¹ (1); time = 2 × 13.7/9.81 = 2.79 s (1); range = 105 m (1). Range shorter / lower maximum height (1); path not symmetrical, steeper descent (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/08-momentum-work-energy-and-power.md -->

## Momentum, impulse, work, energy, power and efficiency

Momentum and energy are both conserved, and between them they solve most collision and motion problems. This file covers momentum and its conservation, force as rate of change of momentum, impulse and force–time graphs, elastic and inelastic collisions and explosions, work done, power (including P = Fv), efficiency, and conservation of energy with kinetic and gravitational potential energy.

### Overview

AS & A for every board except Edexcel Topic 6 (impulse, two-dimensional collisions, Ek = p²/2m), which is A-level only. Elastic strain energy is in file 09. Angular momentum is in file 24.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.4.1.6 to 3.4.1.8 · Edexcel 21, 22, 25 to 30, 97 to 102, CP9, CP10 · OCR A 3.3, 3.5.1(b) to (e), 3.5.2 · OCR B 4.2 · Eduqas C1.3(d) to (f), C1.4.
- Edexcel 99 and CP10 need momentum conservation in two dimensions (resolving), and OCR A 3.5.2(b) collisions in two dimensions. AQA, OCR B and Eduqas limit calculations to one dimension.
- Edexcel 102 asks you to derive Ek = p²/2m.
- Edexcel CP9: force and change of momentum. Edexcel CP10: ICT analysis of collisions of small spheres.
- Eduqas C1.4(d) states the work–energy relationship Fx = ½mv² − ½mu² explicitly; C1.4(f) asks for dissipative forces.
- AQA mentions ethical transport design (crumple zones, airbags) and estimating energy from food.

### 3.4.1.6 Momentum

*Refs: AQA 3.4.1.6 · Edexcel 21, 22, 97 to 102, CP9, CP10 · OCR A 3.5.1(b) to (e), 3.5.2(a) to (c) · OCR B 4.2(a)(ix), (b)(i), (c)(iv), (vi), (d)(i) · Eduqas C1.3(d), (e), (f). Level: AS & A (Edexcel 97 to 102: A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- Momentum p = mv (a vector).
- The principle of conservation of linear momentum, applied in one dimension (Edexcel, OCR A also two dimensions).
- Force = rate of change of momentum: F = Δ(mv)/Δt.
- Impulse = FΔt = Δ(mv); impulse = area under a force–time graph.
- Impact forces depend on contact time (kicking a ball, crumple zones, packaging).
- Elastic and inelastic collisions; explosions.
- (Edexcel 102) Ek = p²/2m.

#### Explained

**Momentum** p = mv, unit kg m s⁻¹ (or N s). It has the direction of the velocity.

**Conservation of momentum.** In any interaction, if no external resultant force acts, the total momentum of the system stays constant. In one dimension:

m₁u₁ + m₂u₂ = m₁v₁ + m₂v₂

Choose a positive direction; velocities in the other direction are negative.

**Link to Newton's laws.** In a collision, A pushes on B and B pushes on A with equal and opposite forces (third law) for the same time. So the impulses are equal and opposite, and the momentum gained by one equals the momentum lost by the other. OCR B 4.2(a)(ix) puts it the other way round: if momentum is conserved, the forces must be equal and opposite.

**Newton's second law in momentum form.** F = Δp/Δt. When mass is constant this becomes F = mΔv/Δt = ma. The momentum form also works when mass changes (a rocket, a conveyor belt being loaded, water from a hose hitting a wall: F = (mass per second) × change in velocity).

**Impulse.** FΔt = Δ(mv). For a varying force, impulse = area under the force–time graph. Unit N s (= kg m s⁻¹).

**Reducing impact forces.** For a given change in momentum, a longer contact time means a smaller average force. Crumple zones, airbags, seat belts that stretch, cycle helmets with crushable foam, padded packaging, bending your knees when landing, and soft sports mats all increase stopping time. Conversely, follow-through in a golf swing or kick increases contact time to increase the change in momentum.

**Collisions.**

| Type | Momentum | Kinetic energy |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Elastic | Conserved | Conserved |
| Inelastic | Conserved | Not conserved (some becomes thermal, sound, deformation) |
| Perfectly inelastic | Conserved | Maximum loss; the objects stick together |

Total energy is always conserved; only kinetic energy can change. Check whether a collision is elastic by calculating total Ek before and after (Edexcel 101).

**Explosions.** Objects start at rest (zero momentum) and fly apart: total momentum afterwards is still zero, so m₁v₁ = −m₂v₂. Kinetic energy increases (from chemical or elastic energy). Example: the recoil of a gun.

**Ek = p²/2m** (Edexcel 102). Since p = mv, v = p/m and Ek = ½m(p/m)² = p²/2m. Useful when two objects have equal and opposite momenta: the lighter one has more kinetic energy.

**Two dimensions** (Edexcel 99, OCR A 3.5.2(b)). Momentum is conserved separately in two perpendicular directions. Resolve each velocity into x and y components and write one conservation equation for each.

#### Worked example

1. A 1.2 kg trolley at 3.0 m s⁻¹ hits a stationary 0.80 kg trolley; they stick together. Find their velocity and the kinetic energy lost.

   1.2 × 3.0 = 2.0v, v = 1.8 m s⁻¹. Ek before = ½ × 1.2 × 9 = 5.4 J. After = ½ × 2.0 × 1.8² = 3.24 J. Loss = 2.16 J (inelastic).

2. A 0.045 kg golf ball leaves the club at 65 m s⁻¹. Contact lasts 0.50 ms. Find the average force.

   F = Δp/Δt = 0.045 × 65 / 0.50 × 10⁻³ = 5850 N.

3. A 4.0 kg rifle fires a 0.010 kg bullet at 400 m s⁻¹. Find the recoil speed.

   0 = 0.010 × 400 + 4.0v, v = −1.0 m s⁻¹ (1.0 m s⁻¹ backwards).

4. A force–time graph is a triangle rising from 0 to 300 N in 0.02 s and falling back to 0 at 0.05 s. A 0.40 kg ball starts at rest. Find its final speed.

   Area = ½ × 0.05 × 300 = 7.5 N s. v = 7.5/0.40 = 18.75 m s⁻¹.

5. (Edexcel/OCR A, 2D) A 2.0 kg puck moving at 3.0 m s⁻¹ east hits a stationary 1.0 kg puck. Afterwards the 2.0 kg puck moves at 2.0 m s⁻¹ at 30° north of east. Find the velocity of the 1.0 kg puck.

   x: 6.0 = 2.0 × 2.0 cos 30° + 1.0v_x, v_x = 6.0 − 3.46 = 2.54 m s⁻¹. y: 0 = 2.0 × 2.0 sin 30° + 1.0v_y, v_y = −2.0 m s⁻¹. Speed = √(2.54² + 2²) = 3.23 m s⁻¹ at 38° south of east.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting that velocity (and momentum) is negative in the opposite direction.
- Saying kinetic energy is conserved in all collisions.
- Saying energy is lost in an inelastic collision; kinetic energy is transferred to other stores.
- Writing impulse = Ft for a varying force instead of using the area.
- Saying a longer stopping time reduces the change in momentum (it reduces the force).

#### Practical

**Edexcel CP9:** investigate the relationship between force and change of momentum. A trolley is pulled by a constant force (hanging mass over a pulley); two light gates (or a light gate and data logger) give the velocity at two points and the time between. Calculate Δ(mv)/Δt and compare with the applied force; or use a force sensor for a collision and integrate the force–time graph.

**Edexcel CP10, OCR A PAG1, OCR B 4.2(d)(i):** analyse collisions with ICT. Film ball bearings or air-track gliders colliding with a scale in view (or use light gates on an air track). Use frame-by-frame video analysis to find velocities before and after; calculate total momentum and kinetic energy before and after. For two-dimensional collisions on a table top, resolve velocities along two axes.

### 3.4.1.7 Work, energy and power

*Refs: AQA 3.4.1.7 · Edexcel 25, 29, 30 · OCR A 3.3.1(a), (b), (e), 3.3.3(a) to (c) · OCR B 4.2(a)(iv), (vi), (c)(vii), (x) · Eduqas C1.4(a), (b), (e), (g). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Work done W = Fs cos θ, where θ is the angle between the force and the displacement.
- Power is the rate of doing work = rate of energy transfer: P = ΔW/Δt = Fv.
- Work done by a variable force = area under a force–displacement graph.
- Efficiency = useful output power ÷ input power (or useful energy out ÷ energy in), as a ratio or a percentage.

#### Explained

**Work.** Work is done when a force moves its point of application in the direction of the force. W = Fs cos θ. Unit: joule (1 J = 1 N m). Work done = energy transferred.

- θ = 0: W = Fs.
- θ = 90°: W = 0. A force at right angles to motion does no work (e.g. the centripetal force on an orbiting satellite; carrying a bag horizontally at constant height).
- θ = 180°: negative work (friction and drag take energy out).

**Variable force.** Work = area under the force–displacement graph. For a spring obeying Hooke's law, the graph is a straight line from the origin, so W = ½Fx (file 09).

**Power.** P = W/t (unit W = J s⁻¹). For a force moving an object at constant velocity v: P = Fs/t = Fv. A car at a steady top speed has driving force = resistive force, so engine power = resistive force × speed.

**Efficiency.**

efficiency = useful energy output / total energy input = useful power output / total power input

It is always less than 1 (100%) because some energy is dissipated, usually as thermal energy.

#### Worked example

1. A crate is dragged 15 m along the floor by a rope at 30° to the horizontal with tension 120 N. Find the work done by the rope.

   W = 120 × 15 × cos 30° = 1560 J.

2. A car's engine provides 45 kW at a steady 30 m s⁻¹. Find the total resistive force.

   F = P/v = 45 000/30 = 1500 N.

3. An electric motor lifts a 2.0 kg mass through 1.5 m in 4.0 s. It draws 0.80 A at 12 V. Find its efficiency.

   Useful power = mgh/t = 2.0 × 9.81 × 1.5 / 4.0 = 7.36 W. Input = 12 × 0.80 = 9.6 W. Efficiency = 0.77 (77%).

#### Common mistakes

- Using sin θ in place of cos θ when the angle is between force and displacement.
- Saying work is done when holding a heavy object still (no displacement, no work, even though you tire).
- Using P = Fv with a driving force that is not the force actually moving at v.
- Giving efficiency greater than 100%.

#### Practical

**Efficiency of an electric motor** (suggested by AQA). Use a small motor to wind up a string and lift a known mass through a measured height. Measure current and p.d. with an ammeter and voltmeter and time with a stopwatch (or light gate). Efficiency = mgh / (VIt). Repeat for different masses. Errors: the mass accelerating at the start (so not all work is mgh), timing errors, varying current; lift through a larger height to reduce percentage uncertainty in time.

### 3.4.1.8 Conservation of energy

*Refs: AQA 3.4.1.8 · Edexcel 26, 27, 28 · OCR A 3.3.1(c), (d), 3.3.2(a) to (c) · OCR B 4.2(a)(v), (c)(viii), (ix) · Eduqas C1.4(c), (d), (f). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The principle of conservation of energy.
- ΔEp = mgΔh and Ek = ½mv².
- Energy problems with gravitational potential energy, kinetic energy and work done against resistive forces.
- (Eduqas) The work–energy relationship Fx = ½mv² − ½mu²; dissipative forces such as friction and drag.

#### Explained

**Conservation of energy.** Energy cannot be created or destroyed, only transferred from one store to another. The total energy of a closed system is constant.

**Kinetic energy** Ek = ½mv². Derivation (OCR A): a constant force F accelerates mass m from rest over distance s: work done Fs = mas, and v² = 2as, so Fs = ½mv².

**Gravitational potential energy** near the Earth's surface: ΔEp = mgΔh (g roughly constant). For large heights see file 14.

**Energy equations.** Write: energy at start = energy at end + energy dissipated.

- Falling object, no air resistance: mgh = ½mv², so v = √(2gh), independent of mass.
- With resistance: mgh = ½mv² + F_resistive × distance.
- A pendulum swings: Ek at the bottom = mgh at the top (height above the lowest point).

**Work–energy relationship.** The work done by the resultant force equals the change in kinetic energy: Fx = ½mv² − ½mu² (Eduqas C1.4(d)). Braking distance comes straight from this: F_brake × d = ½mv².

**Dissipative forces** (Eduqas C1.4(f)) such as friction and drag do negative work and transfer energy to thermal stores, so mechanical energy (Ek + Ep) falls.

**Energy from food** (AQA). Food labels give energy in kJ (or kcal; 1 kcal ≈ 4.2 kJ). A daily intake of about 10 MJ means an average power of 10⁷/86 400 ≈ 120 W.

#### Worked example

1. A 0.20 kg ball is dropped from 5.0 m. Find its speed when it hits the ground (no air resistance).

   v = √(2 × 9.81 × 5.0) = 9.9 m s⁻¹.

2. A 65 kg cyclist freewheels from rest down a hill that drops 12 m vertically over a 150 m road, reaching 11 m s⁻¹. Find the average resistive force.

   Ep lost = 65 × 9.81 × 12 = 7652 J. Ek gained = ½ × 65 × 121 = 3933 J. Work against resistance = 3720 J. F = 3720/150 = 24.8 N.

3. A 1100 kg car travelling at 20 m s⁻¹ brakes to rest in 32 m. Find the average braking force.

   F = ½mv²/d = 0.5 × 1100 × 400 / 32 = 6875 N.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the distance along the slope instead of the vertical height in mgh.
- Forgetting to square v in ½mv².
- Saying a heavier object reaches a higher speed falling the same height (ignoring drag, the speed is the same).

### Check questions

1. State the principle of conservation of linear momentum. (2 marks)
2. A 0.15 kg ball hits a wall at 12 m s⁻¹ and rebounds at 9.0 m s⁻¹. Contact lasts 0.040 s. Find the change in momentum and the average force on the ball. (4 marks)
3. Explain how a car's crumple zone reduces injuries in a crash. (3 marks)
4. A 3.0 kg mass moving at 4.0 m s⁻¹ collides with a 1.0 kg mass at rest. Afterwards the 3.0 kg mass moves at 2.0 m s⁻¹ in the same direction. Find the speed of the 1.0 kg mass and show whether the collision is elastic. (4 marks)
5. Derive Ek = p²/2m. (2 marks)
6. A pump lifts 200 kg of water per minute through 12 m. Its input power is 600 W. Calculate its efficiency. (3 marks)
7. A 70 kg skier starts from rest and descends 40 m vertically. 30% of the energy is dissipated. Calculate the final speed. (3 marks)
8. A truck has a maximum power of 300 kW and a top speed of 30 m s⁻¹ on a level road. Calculate the resistive force at top speed and explain why the truck cannot go faster. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Total momentum of a system remains constant (1); provided no external (resultant) force acts (1).
2. Δp = 0.15 × (9.0 − (−12)) = 3.15 kg m s⁻¹ (2); F = 3.15/0.040 = 79 N (2).
3. Crumple zone deforms, increasing the time over which the car (and occupants) stop (1); same change in momentum (1); so smaller force, since F = Δp/Δt (1).
4. 12 = 6.0 + 1.0v, v = 6.0 m s⁻¹ (2); Ek before = 24 J, after = 6 + 18 = 24 J (1); elastic (1).
5. v = p/m (1); Ek = ½m(p/m)² = p²/2m (1).
6. Useful power = (200 × 9.81 × 12)/60 = 392 W (2); efficiency = 392/600 = 0.65 (65%) (1).
7. Ep = 70 × 9.81 × 40 = 27 470 J (1); Ek = 0.70 × 27 470 = 19 230 J (1); v = √(2 × 19 230/70) = 23 m s⁻¹ (1).
8. F = P/v = 300 000/30 = 10 000 N (1); at top speed all the power is used to do work against resistive forces (1); resistance increases with speed, so no spare power/force to accelerate (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/12-circular-motion-and-simple-harmonic-motion.md -->

## Circular motion, simple harmonic motion, damping and resonance

Objects moving in circles and objects oscillating back and forth are both periodic. This file covers angular speed and the radian, centripetal acceleration and force, the defining equation of simple harmonic motion and its solutions, the mass–spring system and the simple pendulum (AQA required practical 7), energy in SHM, damping, forced oscillations and resonance.

### Overview

A-level only for every board. Orbits (gravity providing the centripetal force) are in file 14. Magnetic fields providing centripetal force on charged particles are in file 17. Rotational dynamics (moment of inertia) is in file 24.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.6.1, RP7 · Edexcel 103 to 107, 181 to 191, CP16 · OCR A 5.2, 5.3 · OCR B 5.1.1 (SHM), 5.1.2 (circular motion) · Eduqas C1.5, C1.6.
- AQA says the derivation of centripetal acceleration will not be examined; Edexcel 105 asks you to derive it from vector diagrams.
- Solutions: AQA and Edexcel use x = A cos ωt; OCR A and OCR B accept x = A sin ωt or A cos ωt; Eduqas uses x = A cos(ωt + ε) with a phase constant ε.
- OCR B 5.1.1(c)(ix) asks you to solve d²x/dt² = −(k/m)x by iterative numerical or graphical methods.
- Edexcel CP16: find an unknown mass from the resonant frequencies of known masses on a spring. Edexcel 191: damping and plastic deformation of ductile materials reduce amplitude.
- Eduqas specified practicals: g with a pendulum; investigating the damping of a spring. Eduqas C1.6(n): the importance of critical damping (e.g. car suspension).
- OCR A 5.3.3(e) and Eduqas C1.6(q): examples where resonance is useful and where it is a problem.

### 3.6.1.1 Circular motion

*Refs: AQA 3.6.1.1 · Edexcel 103 to 107 · OCR A 5.2.1(a) to (c), 5.2.2(a) to (d) · OCR B 5.1.2(a)(iv), (v), (c)(iii) · Eduqas C1.5(a) to (g). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Motion in a circle at constant speed involves acceleration and needs a centripetal force.
- The radian; converting between degrees and radians.
- Angular speed ω = v/r = 2πf = 2π/T.
- Centripetal acceleration a = v²/r = ω²r, directed towards the centre.
- Centripetal force F = mv²/r = mω²r.
- (Edexcel 105) Deriving a = v²/r from a vector diagram.

#### Explained

**Radians.** An angle in radians = arc length ÷ radius: θ = s/r. A full circle is 2π rad = 360°, so 1 rad ≈ 57.3°. To convert degrees to radians, multiply by π/180.

**Angular speed.** ω = Δθ/Δt, unit rad s⁻¹. For one revolution: ω = 2π/T = 2πf. The linear speed of a point at radius r is v = ωr (every point on a rigid rotating object has the same ω, but points further out have larger v).

**Why there is an acceleration.** Velocity is a vector. In circular motion at constant speed the direction keeps changing, so the velocity changes, so there is an acceleration. It points towards the centre (centripetal).

**Derivation** (Edexcel 105). In a short time Δt the object moves through angle Δθ. Its velocity changes from v₁ to v₂, both of size v, at angle Δθ apart. The vector Δv = v₂ − v₁ points towards the centre, and for small Δθ its size is vΔθ. So a = Δv/Δt = vΔθ/Δt = vω = v²/r = ω²r.

**Centripetal force.** By Newton's second law, a resultant force is needed towards the centre: F = mv²/r = mω²r. It is not a new kind of force; it is whatever resultant force points inwards:

- Tension in a string (a ball whirled round).
- Friction between tyres and road (a car turning a corner).
- Gravity (a satellite or the Moon).
- Horizontal component of the normal force (a car on a banked track) or of lift (a banking aircraft).
- Magnetic force (a charged particle in a magnetic field).

The centripetal force does no work, because it is perpendicular to the velocity, so the speed (and kinetic energy) does not change.

**Vertical circles.** A ball on a string in a vertical circle: at the top, T + mg = mv²/r; at the bottom, T − mg = mv²/r. The tension is greatest at the bottom. The string goes slack at the top if v² < gr. On a roller coaster loop, the rider just stays in contact at the top if v = √(gr). On a hump-back bridge, the car leaves the road at the top if v > √(gr).

**Conical pendulum.** A mass on a string moving in a horizontal circle: vertical component of tension balances weight (T cos θ = mg); horizontal component provides centripetal force (T sin θ = mv²/r). So tan θ = v²/(rg).

#### Worked example

1. A car of mass 900 kg goes round a bend of radius 40 m at 14 m s⁻¹. Find the friction force needed.

   F = mv²/r = 900 × 196/40 = 4410 N.

2. A drum in a washing machine of radius 0.25 m spins at 1200 revolutions per minute. Find ω and the centripetal acceleration of clothes at the edge.

   f = 20 Hz, ω = 2π × 20 = 126 rad s⁻¹. a = ω²r = 126² × 0.25 = 3950 m s⁻² (about 400g).

3. A 0.20 kg ball on a 0.60 m string moves in a vertical circle. At the top its speed is 3.0 m s⁻¹. Find the tension there.

   T = mv²/r − mg = 0.20 × 9/0.60 − 1.96 = 3.0 − 1.96 = 1.04 N.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the centripetal force acts outwards (there is no outward "centrifugal force" acting on the object in an inertial frame).
- Drawing centripetal force as an extra force on a free-body diagram alongside tension or friction.
- Using degrees in ω = θ/t.
- Saying the speed changes in uniform circular motion.

### 3.6.1.2 Simple harmonic motion (SHM)

*Refs: AQA 3.6.1.2 · Edexcel 181, 182, 184, 185 · OCR A 5.3.1(a), (b), (d), (e), (g) · OCR B 5.1.1(a)(v), (vi), (b)(iii), (vii), (c)(ix), (x) · Eduqas C1.6(a) to (h). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The condition for SHM: a ∝ −x (acceleration proportional to displacement and directed towards the equilibrium position).
- Defining equation a = −ω²x.
- x = A cos ωt and v = ±ω√(A² − x²).
- Graphs of x, v and a against time, and how each comes from the gradient of the one before.
- Maximum speed = ωA; maximum acceleration = ω²A.
- (Edexcel 181) F = −kx as the condition, and v = −Aω sin ωt, a = −Aω² cos ωt.
- (OCR A, Eduqas) The period of SHM is independent of amplitude (isochronous).

#### Explained

**Definition.** An object moves with simple harmonic motion if its acceleration is directly proportional to its displacement from a fixed point (the equilibrium position) and always directed towards that point: a = −ω²x. The minus sign shows the acceleration is opposite to the displacement. Equivalent: the restoring force is proportional to displacement, F = −kx.

**Terms.**

- Displacement x: distance from equilibrium.
- Amplitude A: maximum displacement.
- Period T: time for one complete oscillation; frequency f = 1/T.
- Angular frequency ω = 2πf = 2π/T.
- Phase difference between two oscillators: 2πΔt/T.

**Solutions.** If timing starts at maximum displacement: x = A cos ωt. If it starts at equilibrium moving in the positive direction: x = A sin ωt. In general (Eduqas): x = A cos(ωt + ε), where ε is the phase constant.

Differentiating x = A cos ωt:

- v = −Aω sin ωt
- a = −Aω² cos ωt = −ω²x

**Velocity and displacement.** v = ±ω√(A² − x²). Maximum speed v_max = ωA at x = 0 (equilibrium). Speed is zero at x = ±A (extremes).

**Acceleration.** Maximum acceleration a_max = ω²A at x = ±A; zero at equilibrium.

**Graphs.** If x = A cos ωt:

- x–t: cosine curve.
- v–t: negative sine curve (gradient of x–t); a quarter cycle (π/2) ahead of x.
- a–t: negative cosine curve (gradient of v–t); in antiphase with x (π out of phase).
- a–x graph: a straight line through the origin with negative gradient −ω².
