**Isochronous.** The period does not depend on the amplitude: larger swings travel further but faster.

**Iterative solution** (OCR B 5.1.1(c)(ix)). Start with x and v. In each small step Δt: a = −(k/m)x; v_new = v + aΔt; x_new = x + v_newΔt. Repeating traces out a sine curve.

#### Worked example

1. An object in SHM has amplitude 0.050 m and period 0.40 s. Find ω, the maximum speed and the maximum acceleration.

   ω = 2π/0.40 = 15.7 rad s⁻¹. v_max = 15.7 × 0.050 = 0.785 m s⁻¹. a_max = 15.7² × 0.050 = 12.3 m s⁻².

2. For the same object, find the speed when x = 0.030 m.

   v = 15.7 × √(0.050² − 0.030²) = 15.7 × 0.040 = 0.63 m s⁻¹.

3. Starting from maximum displacement, find the displacement after 0.15 s.

   x = 0.050 cos(15.7 × 0.15) = 0.050 cos(2.36 rad) = 0.050 × (−0.707) = −0.035 m. (Calculator in radians!)

#### Common mistakes

- Using degrees mode on a calculator with ωt.
- Saying acceleration is greatest at the equilibrium position (it is zero there; speed is greatest).
- Forgetting the minus sign, which shows the restoring direction.
- Saying the period depends on amplitude.

### 3.6.1.3 Simple harmonic systems

*Refs: AQA 3.6.1.3, RP7 · Edexcel 183, 188 · OCR A 5.3.1(c), (f), 5.3.2(a), (b) · OCR B 5.1.1(a)(vii), (c)(vi), (vii), (viii), (xi), (d)(i) · Eduqas C1.6(i), (j), (k). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Mass–spring system: T = 2π√(m/k).
- Simple pendulum: T = 2π√(l/g), using the small-angle approximation.
- Other oscillators (e.g. liquid in a U-tube) may be given with full information.
- How Ek, Ep and total energy vary with displacement and with time.
- The effects of damping on oscillations.
- AQA required practical 7: SHM with a mass–spring system and a simple pendulum.

#### Explained

**Mass–spring.** A mass m on a spring of stiffness k. Displaced by x from equilibrium, the restoring force is −kx, so a = −(k/m)x. Comparing with a = −ω²x: ω² = k/m, so

**T = 2π√(m/k)** (independent of g, so the same on the Moon).

This is true for horizontal and vertical springs (for a vertical spring, x is measured from the new equilibrium position where the spring is already stretched by mg/k).

**Simple pendulum.** A small mass on a light string of length l, displaced by a small angle θ. The restoring force along the arc is mg sin θ. For small angles (less than about 10°), sin θ ≈ θ = x/l, so F ≈ −(mg/l)x and a = −(g/l)x. So ω² = g/l and

**T = 2π√(l/g)** (independent of mass and, for small angles, of amplitude).

**Energy in SHM.** With no damping, total energy is constant.

- Potential energy (mass–spring): Ep = ½kx².
- Kinetic energy: Ek = ½mv² = ½mω²(A² − x²).
- Total E = ½kA² = ½mω²A².

Graphs against displacement: Ep is a parabola (U-shaped) from zero at x = 0 to ½kA² at ±A; Ek is an inverted parabola, maximum at x = 0; total is a horizontal line. Against time, Ek and Ep each vary as sin² or cos² with twice the frequency of the oscillation (they peak twice per cycle), and always add up to the constant total.

**Damping.** Real oscillators lose energy to their surroundings (friction, air resistance), so the amplitude decreases. This is damping. Over time, total energy falls (Edexcel 188, Eduqas C1.6(l)).

#### Worked example

1. A 0.40 kg mass on a spring oscillates with period 0.80 s. Find k.

   k = 4π²m/T² = 4π² × 0.40/0.64 = 24.7 N m⁻¹.

2. What length of pendulum has a period of 2.0 s on Earth?

   l = gT²/4π² = 9.81 × 4/39.5 = 0.994 m.

3. A 0.25 kg mass on a spring (k = 40 N m⁻¹) oscillates with amplitude 0.060 m. Find the total energy and the kinetic energy when x = 0.020 m.

   E = ½ × 40 × 0.060² = 0.072 J. Ep at 0.020 m = ½ × 40 × 0.020² = 0.008 J. Ek = 0.064 J.

#### Common mistakes

- Using T = 2π√(l/g) for a pendulum swinging through large angles.
- Saying the period of a pendulum depends on the mass of the bob.
- Thinking Ek and Ep oscillate at the same frequency as x.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 7** (also OCR A 5.3.1(c)(ii) PAG10, OCR B 5.1.1(d)(i), Eduqas specified practical "g with a pendulum"): investigate SHM with a mass–spring system and a simple pendulum.

*Pendulum.* Clamp the string between two small wooden blocks (so the pivot is fixed). Measure l from the pivot to the centre of the bob. Displace by a small angle (under 10°) and release. Use a fiducial marker (a pin or line) at the centre of the swing, where the bob moves fastest, to start and stop timing. Time 10 or 20 oscillations, repeat and average, and divide. Vary l from about 0.2 m to 1.0 m. Plot T² against l: straight line through the origin with gradient 4π²/g, so g = 4π²/gradient.

*Mass–spring.* Hang a spring from a clamp; add a mass hanger with masses. Pull down a little and release. Time 10 oscillations using a marker at the equilibrium position. Vary m. Plot T² against m: gradient 4π²/k. Compare k with the value from a static force–extension measurement.

Errors: timing from the extreme position (bob slow there, so hard to judge the moment) gives more random error; large amplitude makes the pendulum period slightly longer; the mass of the spring adds a little to the effective mass (an intercept on the T² against m graph). Safety: secure the clamp stand (G-clamp it to the bench) so it does not topple.

**Edexcel CP16:** find an unknown mass using the resonant frequencies of known masses. Hang known masses on a spring attached to a vibration generator driven by a signal generator. For each mass, slowly increase the frequency until the amplitude is largest (resonance) and record f. Since f = (1/2π)√(k/m), plot 1/f² against m (straight line). Hang the unknown mass, find its resonant frequency, and read its mass from the graph.

### 3.6.1.4 Forced vibrations and resonance

*Refs: AQA 3.6.1.4 · Edexcel 186, 187, 189, 190, 191 · OCR A 5.3.3(a) to (e) · OCR B 5.1.1(a)(viii), (b)(viii), (d)(ii) · Eduqas C1.6(l) to (q). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Free and forced vibrations (qualitatively).
- Resonance; how damping affects the sharpness of resonance.
- Examples of these effects in mechanical systems and with stationary waves.
- (OCR A, Eduqas) Light, heavy and critical damping; amplitude–driving frequency graphs.
- (Edexcel 191) How damping and the plastic deformation of ductile materials reduce amplitude.

#### Explained

**Free oscillation.** After an initial displacement, an object oscillates at its own natural frequency f₀, with no external driving force (other than its restoring force).

**Forced oscillation.** A periodic external force (the driver) makes the object oscillate at the driving frequency.

**Resonance.** When the driving frequency equals (or is very close to) the natural frequency, the amplitude of the forced oscillation becomes very large. Energy is transferred from the driver to the oscillator most efficiently, because the driving force is in phase with the velocity. At resonance the driven system is a quarter of a cycle (π/2) behind the driver; well below f₀ they are in phase, and well above f₀ they are in antiphase.

**Damping.**

| Type | Behaviour after a displacement |
| --- | --- |
| Light (under) damping | Oscillates with slowly decreasing amplitude (exponential decay of amplitude); period nearly unchanged |
| Critical damping | Returns to equilibrium in the shortest time without oscillating |
| Heavy (over) damping | Returns slowly to equilibrium without oscillating |

Critical damping is used in car suspension (the car does not bounce, and the wheel returns quickly), and in moving-coil meters (the pointer settles quickly). Heavy damping is used in door closers.

**Effect of damping on resonance.** Greater damping:

- reduces the maximum amplitude at every driving frequency;
- makes the resonance peak broader (less sharp);
- shifts the peak slightly below f₀.

Amplitude against driving frequency: a curve rising to a peak near f₀ and falling again; lightly damped systems have a tall narrow peak; heavily damped ones a low broad hump.

**Plastic deformation** (Edexcel 191). If an oscillating structure deforms plastically (for example a ductile metal bending), energy is absorbed and not returned, reducing the amplitude. This is used in earthquake protection and car bumpers.

**Examples.**

*Useful:* tuning a radio (the circuit resonates at the station's frequency), musical instruments (air columns and strings resonate; see stationary waves, file 04), microwave ovens (water molecules absorb at a resonant frequency), MRI (nuclei resonate in a magnetic field), quartz clocks.

*Problems:* bridges oscillating in wind or with marching feet (the Millennium Bridge needed dampers), buildings in earthquakes, car panels rattling at certain engine speeds, washing machine vibrations during spin. Solutions: add damping, change mass or stiffness so the natural frequency is far from likely driving frequencies.

**Stationary waves as resonance.** A string or air column has several natural frequencies (its harmonics). Driving it at one of them gives a large-amplitude stationary wave.

#### Worked example

1. A car's suspension has springs with total k = 6.0 × 10⁴ N m⁻¹ and the car's mass is 1200 kg. At what frequency of road bumps would it resonate?

   f₀ = (1/2π)√(k/m) = (1/2π)√(50) = 1.13 Hz. If bumps are 10 m apart, this happens at 11 m s⁻¹. Shock absorbers (critical damping) prevent dangerous amplitudes.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying resonance occurs when driving frequency equals amplitude (it is about frequency).
- Saying damping changes the natural frequency greatly (it changes it slightly).
- Saying critical damping makes the system stop instantly.

#### Practical

**Damping of a spring** (Eduqas specified practical; OCR A 5.3.3(b), OCR B 5.1.1(d)(ii)). Hang a mass on a spring and attach a card to increase air resistance (or immerse the mass in water). Displace and release; record the amplitude at the end of every few oscillations, using a ruler behind the mass or a video with a scale. Plot amplitude against time (or number of oscillations): an exponential decay. A larger card gives faster decay. ln A against t is a straight line.

**Resonance with a vibration generator** (Barton's pendulums demonstration). Several pendulums of different lengths hang from a string with one heavy driver pendulum. Only the pendulum with the same length as the driver swings with a large amplitude, and it lags the driver by a quarter cycle.

### Check questions

1. Explain why an object moving in a circle at constant speed is accelerating. (2 marks)
2. A satellite moves in a circular orbit of radius 7.0 × 10⁶ m with period 98 minutes. Calculate its speed and centripetal acceleration. (3 marks)
3. State the conditions for simple harmonic motion. (2 marks)
4. A mass on a spring oscillates with amplitude 4.0 cm and frequency 2.5 Hz. Calculate the maximum speed and the speed when it is 2.0 cm from equilibrium. (4 marks)
5. Sketch displacement–time, velocity–time and acceleration–time graphs for one cycle of SHM starting from maximum displacement. (3 marks)
6. Derive T = 2π√(l/g) for a simple pendulum, stating the approximation used. (4 marks)
7. Describe how to measure g using a simple pendulum, explaining how to reduce uncertainty. (6 marks)
8. Sketch amplitude–driving frequency curves for light and heavy damping on the same axes and explain the differences. (4 marks)
9. Explain why critical damping is used in car suspension. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Velocity is a vector; its direction is constantly changing (1); so velocity changes, which is an acceleration (towards the centre) (1).
2. v = 2πr/T = 2π × 7.0 × 10⁶/5880 = 7.5 × 10³ m s⁻¹ (2); a = v²/r = 8.0 m s⁻² (1).
3. Acceleration proportional to displacement from equilibrium (1); directed towards equilibrium / opposite to displacement (1).
4. ω = 2π × 2.5 = 15.7 rad s⁻¹ (1); v_max = 15.7 × 0.040 = 0.63 m s⁻¹ (1); v = 15.7√(0.040² − 0.020²) (1) = 0.54 m s⁻¹ (1).
5. Displacement: cosine starting at +A (1); velocity: negative sine, zero at start (1); acceleration: negative cosine, starting at −ω²A (antiphase with x) (1).
6. Restoring force = mg sin θ (1); for small angles sin θ ≈ θ = x/l (1); a = −(g/l)x, which is SHM with ω² = g/l (1); T = 2π/ω = 2π√(l/g) (1).
7. Measure length to centre of bob (1); small angle (<10°) (1); time 10+ oscillations with fiducial marker at equilibrium (1); repeat and average (1); vary l, plot T² against l (1); g = 4π²/gradient (1).
8. Both peak near natural frequency (1); light damping: tall, narrow peak (1); heavy damping: lower amplitude at all frequencies, broader peak (1); peak slightly below f₀ for heavier damping (1).
9. Returns to equilibrium in the shortest time (1); without oscillating, so the car does not keep bouncing (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/13-thermal-physics-ideal-gases-and-kinetic-theory.md -->

## Thermal physics: internal energy, specific heat and latent heat, ideal gases and kinetic theory

This file links the large-scale behaviour of matter (temperature, pressure, volume, heating) to the motion of its particles. It covers internal energy, temperature scales and absolute zero, specific heat capacity and specific latent heat, the gas laws and ideal gas equation (AQA required practical 8), the kinetic theory derivation of pV = ⅓Nm(c_rms)², molecular kinetic energy and temperature, and (for OCR B) the Boltzmann factor.

### Overview

A-level only for every board. The first law of thermodynamics, p–V diagrams and engines (AQA option 3.11.2) are in file 25; Eduqas C1.8's first-law points are covered here because they are core for Eduqas. Black-body radiation (Edexcel 153 to 155) is in file 20. Thermal conduction and U-values (Eduqas option D) are in file 35.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.6.2, RP8 · Edexcel 144 to 152, CP12 to CP14 · OCR A 5.1 · OCR B 5.2 · Eduqas C1.7, C1.8.
- Edexcel CP12: calibrating a thermistor in a potential divider as a thermostat. CP13: specific latent heat. CP14: p against V at constant temperature (Boyle's law).
- OCR A 5.1.3(b), (d) and OCR B 5.2.1(d)(i), Eduqas specified practical: electrical methods for specific heat capacity (and OCR A for latent heat). AQA suggests an electrical method or the method of mixtures.
- Eduqas specified practical and OCR B 5.2.1(d)(ii): estimating absolute zero using the gas laws.
- OCR B only: random walk of gas molecules (distance ∝ √N steps) and the Boltzmann factor e^(−E/kT) with activation energy processes (5.2.2).
- Eduqas C1.8 includes the first law ΔU = Q − W, W = pΔV and U = (3/2)nRT for a monatomic gas, as core content.
- OCR A 5.1.2(c) and AQA: Brownian motion as evidence for atoms.

### 3.6.2.1 Thermal energy transfer

*Refs: AQA 3.6.2.1 · Edexcel 144, 146, 147, CP12, CP13 · OCR A 5.1.1(a) to (d), 5.1.2(a), (b), (d) to (g), 5.1.3(a) to (d) · OCR B 5.2.1(a)(i), (c)(i), (d)(i) · Eduqas C1.8(a), (b), (d), (e), (f), (i), (j), (k). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Internal energy is the sum of the randomly distributed kinetic and potential energies of the particles in a body.
- Internal energy increases when energy is transferred to the body by heating or when work is done on it (and decreases the other way); a qualitative first law.
- During a change of state, potential energies of the particles change but kinetic energies do not.
- Q = mcΔθ for a change of temperature (c = specific heat capacity), including continuous flow.
- Q = ml for a change of state (l = specific latent heat of fusion or vaporisation).
- (OCR A, Eduqas) Thermal equilibrium; temperature scales; absolute zero; T(K) = θ(°C) + 273.

#### Explained

**Temperature and thermal equilibrium.** Energy flows by heating from a hotter object to a colder one. When no net energy flows between two objects in contact, they are in thermal equilibrium and have the same temperature (Eduqas C1.8(e)). Heat and work are energy in transit, not stored in the object (Eduqas C1.8(d), (f)).

**Absolute temperature.** The kelvin scale starts at absolute zero (0 K = −273.15 °C), the lowest possible temperature, at which particles have minimum internal energy (Eduqas C1.8(b)); in the simple kinetic model, their kinetic energy is zero. T/K = θ/°C + 273. A temperature change of 1 K equals a change of 1 °C.

**Solids, liquids and gases** (OCR A 5.1.2(a), (b)).

| State | Spacing | Arrangement | Motion |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Solid | Close | Regular lattice | Vibrate about fixed positions |
| Liquid | Close (slightly more than solid) | Irregular, no fixed positions | Move around each other, randomly |
| Gas | Far apart (about 10 × diameter) | Random | Move fast and randomly; collide |

**Internal energy.** The sum of the kinetic energies (from random motion) and potential energies (from the forces between particles) of all the particles. It increases if the object is heated or if work is done on it (e.g. compressing a gas, rubbing). This is the first law in words: ΔU = Q + W_on (or ΔU = Q − W_by; see file 25).

**Change of state.** While a solid melts or a liquid boils at constant temperature, the energy supplied breaks or weakens bonds: potential energy rises, kinetic energy (and so temperature) stays the same. A heating curve (temperature against time with constant power) shows sloping parts (temperature rising, kinetic energy rising) and flat parts (changes of state).

**Specific heat capacity** c: the energy needed to raise the temperature of 1 kg of a substance by 1 K. Q = mcΔθ. Water: 4200 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹; copper 390; aluminium 900.

**Specific latent heat** l (or L): the energy needed to change the state of 1 kg without a change of temperature. Q = ml. Fusion (solid ↔ liquid): water 3.3 × 10⁵ J kg⁻¹. Vaporisation (liquid ↔ gas): water 2.26 × 10⁶ J kg⁻¹. Vaporisation needs much more energy because the particles must be separated completely and work is done pushing back the atmosphere.

**Continuous flow.** For a fluid flowing at mass rate m/t through a heater of power P: P = (m/t)cΔθ. Example: an electric shower.

**Method of mixtures.** Energy lost by the hot substance = energy gained by the cold: m₁c₁(θ₁ − θ) = m₂c₂(θ − θ₂), assuming no losses to the surroundings.

#### Worked example

1. How much energy is needed to turn 0.50 kg of ice at −10 °C into water at 20 °C? (c_ice = 2100, c_water = 4200 J kg⁻¹ K⁻¹, l_f = 3.3 × 10⁵ J kg⁻¹)

   Warm ice: 0.50 × 2100 × 10 = 10 500 J. Melt: 0.50 × 3.3 × 10⁵ = 165 000 J. Warm water: 0.50 × 4200 × 20 = 42 000 J. Total = 2.2 × 10⁵ J.

2. An electric shower heats water from 12 °C to 40 °C at a flow rate of 0.12 kg s⁻¹. Find the power.

   P = 0.12 × 4200 × 28 = 1.4 × 10⁴ W (14 kW).

3. 0.20 kg of copper at 100 °C is dropped into 0.30 kg of water at 20 °C. Find the final temperature.

   0.20 × 390 × (100 − θ) = 0.30 × 4200 × (θ − 20). 7800 − 78θ = 1260θ − 25 200. 33 000 = 1338θ, θ = 24.7 °C.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying temperature rises during melting.
- Saying internal energy is only kinetic energy (true only for an ideal gas).
- Saying heat is "contained" in an object.
- Forgetting to convert grams to kilograms.

#### Practical

**Specific heat capacity by an electrical method** (OCR A 5.1.3(b) PAG11, OCR B 5.2.1(d)(i), Eduqas specified practical; suggested by AQA). Put an immersion heater and a thermometer in holes in an insulated metal block of known mass (or in a known mass of liquid in an insulated beaker, stirred). Connect the heater to a supply with an ammeter and voltmeter. Record the starting temperature, switch on and start a stopwatch; record temperature every minute. Energy supplied = VIt. Plot θ against t: gradient = VI/(mc), so c = VI/(m × gradient). Using the gradient of the straight part reduces the effect of the slow start (the heater warming up). Errors: energy lost to the surroundings makes c too large; insulate, add a lid, start below room temperature and finish the same amount above it.

**Edexcel CP13** (also OCR A 5.1.3(d)): specific latent heat. For fusion: put crushed ice in a funnel with an immersion heater; collect the melted water in a beaker for a set time with the heater on, and in an identical control funnel with the heater off (to allow for melting from the room). l = VIt / (m_heated − m_control). For vaporisation: weigh a beaker of boiling water on a balance while heating; l = P Δt / Δm.

**Edexcel CP12:** calibrate a thermistor in a potential divider as a thermostat. Put the thermistor in a water bath with a thermometer, in series with a fixed (or variable) resistor across a supply; record Vout across the fixed resistor at each temperature as the water is heated. Plot Vout against temperature; use the graph to set the variable resistor so the output reaches a switching voltage at the required temperature.

**Phase change with a data logger** (AQA suggested). Heat stearic acid (or melt and let it cool) with a temperature probe and data logger; the flat part of the graph shows the change of state.

### 3.6.2.2 Ideal gases

*Refs: AQA 3.6.2.2, RP8 · Edexcel 148, 150, 151, CP14 · OCR A 5.1.4(a), (d), (g), (h) · OCR B 5.2.1(a)(ii), (b)(i), (ii), (c)(ii), (d)(ii), (iii) · Eduqas C1.7(a), (d), (e), C1.8(g), (h). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The gas laws as experimental relationships between p, V, T and mass.
- The concept of absolute zero.
- Ideal gas equation: pV = nRT for n moles; pV = NkT for N molecules.
- Work done by a gas at constant pressure = pΔV.
- The Avogadro constant N_A, the molar gas constant R and the Boltzmann constant k = R/N_A.
- Molar mass and molecular mass.
- AQA required practical 8: Boyle's law and Charles's law.

#### Explained

**Gas laws** (for a fixed mass of gas):

| Law | Constant | Relationship | Graph |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Boyle's | Temperature | pV = constant (p ∝ 1/V) | p against 1/V: straight line through origin |
| Charles's | Pressure | V/T = constant (V ∝ T) | V against T(K): straight line through origin; against θ(°C): line meeting the axis at −273 °C |
| Pressure law | Volume | p/T = constant (p ∝ T) | p against θ(°C): extrapolates to zero at −273 °C |

Combined: pV/T = constant, so p₁V₁/T₁ = p₂V₂/T₂. Temperatures must be in kelvin.

**Absolute zero from the gas laws.** Extrapolating V (or p) against θ in °C back to zero always meets the temperature axis at about −273 °C, whatever the gas. This is absolute zero, where an ideal gas would have zero volume or pressure (its particles would have no kinetic energy).

**Moles.** 1 mole contains N_A = 6.02 × 10²³ particles (the Avogadro constant). n = N/N_A. Molar mass M = mass of 1 mol (kg mol⁻¹). Molecular mass m = M/N_A. Relative molecular mass M_r: molar mass in grams per mole (Eduqas C1.7(e): M = M_r/1000 kg mol⁻¹).

**Ideal gas equation.** pV = nRT, R = 8.31 J mol⁻¹ K⁻¹. Equivalently pV = NkT, with k = R/N_A = 1.38 × 10⁻²³ J K⁻¹. An ideal gas obeys this exactly at all pressures and temperatures; real gases do so at low pressure and well above their boiling point.

**Work done by a gas.** At constant pressure, a gas expanding by ΔV pushes back its surroundings: W = pΔV. On a p–V graph, work done = area under the line (also when p changes, Eduqas C1.8(h)).

#### Worked example

1. A cylinder of volume 0.050 m³ holds oxygen at 2.0 × 10⁶ Pa and 17 °C. Find the number of moles and the mass (M = 0.032 kg mol⁻¹).

   n = pV/RT = 2.0 × 10⁶ × 0.050 / (8.31 × 290) = 41.5 mol. Mass = 41.5 × 0.032 = 1.33 kg.

2. A gas at 300 K and 1.0 × 10⁵ Pa is heated to 450 K and compressed to half its volume. Find its new pressure.

   p₂ = p₁ × (V₁/V₂) × (T₂/T₁) = 1.0 × 10⁵ × 2 × 1.5 = 3.0 × 10⁵ Pa.

3. A gas expands at a constant pressure of 1.2 × 10⁵ Pa from 2.0 × 10⁻³ m³ to 3.5 × 10⁻³ m³. Find the work done by the gas.

   W = pΔV = 1.2 × 10⁵ × 1.5 × 10⁻³ = 180 J.

#### Common mistakes

- Using °C in the gas equations.
- Mixing up n (moles) and N (number of molecules).
- Using grams per mole as kg per mole.
- Saying Charles's law gives V ∝ θ in °C.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 8** (also Edexcel CP14 for Boyle's law, OCR A 5.1.4(d) PAG8, OCR B 5.2.1(d)(ii), (iii)): investigate Boyle's law and Charles's law.

*Boyle's law.* A column of air is trapped in a glass tube above oil. A pump (or syringe) pushes oil into the tube to compress the air; a Bourdon gauge measures pressure. Volume = length of air column × cross-sectional area (the length is proportional to volume for a uniform tube). Increase the pressure in steps, waiting a few moments each time for the air to return to room temperature (compression warms it). Plot p against 1/V: a straight line through the origin confirms p ∝ 1/V. Safety: wear goggles; do not exceed the gauge's safe pressure.

*Charles's law.* A capillary tube with a bead of concentrated sulfuric acid (or oil) trapping air, sealed at the bottom, is fixed to a ruler and placed in a beaker of water. Heat the water in steps, stirring and waiting for the trapped air to reach the water temperature; record the length of the air column (proportional to volume) and the temperature. Plot length against θ (°C) and extrapolate to zero length: the intercept is absolute zero, about −273 °C. Safety: the acid is corrosive (goggles, gloves); hot water.

**Estimating absolute zero with the pressure law** (Eduqas specified practical, OCR B 5.2.1(d)(ii)). A flask of air connected to a pressure gauge is put in water baths at different temperatures (ice, room, hot, boiling). Plot p against θ and extrapolate to p = 0.

### 3.6.2.3 Molecular kinetic theory model

*Refs: AQA 3.6.2.3 · Edexcel 147, 148, 149, 152 · OCR A 5.1.2(c), 5.1.4(b), (c), (e), (f), (h), (i) · OCR B 5.2.1(a)(iii) to (vi), (b)(iii), (c)(iii) · Eduqas C1.7(b), (c), (f), C1.8(c). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Brownian motion as evidence for the existence of atoms (molecules).
- The gas laws explained with a simple molecular model.
- The gas laws are empirical (from experiment); kinetic theory comes from theory.
- The assumptions of kinetic theory and the derivation of pV = ⅓Nm(c_rms)² using conservation of momentum.
- For an ideal gas, internal energy is only the kinetic energy of the particles.
- Average molecular kinetic energy: ½m(c_rms)² = (3/2)kT = 3RT/(2N_A).
- How understanding of gases changed over time.
- (OCR B) Random walk: distance moved after N steps ∝ √N. Boltzmann factor.

#### Explained

**Brownian motion.** Smoke particles in air, viewed through a microscope in a smoke cell with light shining from the side, move in random, jerky paths. This is because they are hit unevenly by much smaller, fast-moving, invisible air molecules. It was strong evidence that atoms and molecules exist (explained by Einstein in 1905).

**Explaining the gas laws.**

- Pressure: molecules collide with the walls; each collision changes their momentum, so the wall exerts a force on them and they exert an equal and opposite force on it. Pressure = total force ÷ area.
- Boyle's law: at constant T, mean speed is fixed. Halving the volume doubles the number of molecules per unit volume, so collisions with each area of wall per second double, so p doubles.
- Charles's law / pressure law: raising T increases mean speed; molecules hit the walls harder and more often. At constant volume pressure rises; at constant pressure the gas expands so that the collision rate per area falls back.

**Assumptions of kinetic theory** (for an ideal gas).

1. The gas contains a very large number of identical molecules moving randomly.
2. The volume of the molecules is negligible compared with the volume of the container.
3. There are no forces between molecules except during collisions (so no potential energy).
4. Collisions (with each other and with walls) are perfectly elastic.
5. The time spent in collisions is negligible compared with the time between them.
6. Newton's laws apply; gravity is negligible.

**Derivation of pV = ⅓Nm(c_rms)².**

1. Consider one molecule of mass m in a cube of side L, moving with velocity component u in the x-direction.
2. It hits a wall and rebounds elastically: change in momentum = mu − (−mu) = 2mu.
3. It travels 2L before hitting the same wall again; time between collisions = 2L/u.
4. Average force on the wall from this molecule = rate of change of momentum = 2mu/(2L/u) = mu²/L.
5. For N molecules with different speeds: total force = (m/L)(u₁² + u₂² + …) = (Nm/L) × mean(u²).
6. Pressure = force/area = (Nm/L³) × mean(u²) = (Nm/V) × mean(u²).
7. Molecules move randomly in three dimensions: mean(c²) = mean(u²) + mean(v²) + mean(w²) = 3 mean(u²).
8. So p = Nm(c_rms)²/(3V), giving **pV = ⅓Nm(c_rms)²**. Also p = ⅓ρ(c_rms)², where ρ = Nm/V.

**Root mean square speed.** c_rms = √(mean of c²): square every speed, take the mean, then the square root. It is slightly larger than the mean speed.

**Temperature and molecular kinetic energy.** Combine pV = ⅓Nm(c_rms)² with pV = NkT:

⅓Nm(c_rms)² = NkT → **½m(c_rms)² = (3/2)kT = 3RT/(2N_A)**

So the mean kinetic energy of a gas molecule is proportional to absolute temperature, and is the same for all gases at the same temperature. Heavier molecules move more slowly.

**Internal energy of an ideal gas.** There are no intermolecular forces, so no potential energy: internal energy = total kinetic energy = N × (3/2)kT = (3/2)nRT (for a monatomic gas; Eduqas C1.8(c), OCR A 5.1.4(i)).

**History** (AQA). Boyle (1662) and Charles (1787) found the gas laws by experiment. Bernoulli, then Clausius, Maxwell and Boltzmann (1800s) explained them with moving particles. The atomic model was only fully accepted after Brownian motion was explained (Einstein 1905, tested by Perrin 1908).

**Random walk** (OCR B 5.2.1(a)(vi)). A molecule in air travels a short distance (the mean free path, about 10⁻⁷ m) before colliding and changing direction randomly. After N steps of length s in random directions, its typical displacement from the start is s√N, not sN. This is why smells spread slowly in still air even though molecules move at hundreds of m s⁻¹.

**Boltzmann factor** (OCR B 5.2.2). At temperature T, the ratio of the number of particles in a state with energy E above another state is e^(−E/kT). kT is a typical thermal energy (about 0.025 eV at room temperature).

- If E is much bigger than kT (say E/kT > 30), the Boltzmann factor is tiny and the process hardly happens.
- If E is only a few times kT, the process happens readily.

Processes with an activation energy E depend strongly on temperature because the fraction of particles with enough energy is about e^(−E/kT): evaporation and boiling, thermionic emission from a hot cathode, ionisation in a flame, the rise in conduction electrons in a semiconductor (so its resistance falls when heated), viscous flow (liquids flow more easily when warm), and chemical reaction rates. A rough rule: when E ≈ 15 to 30 kT, the process becomes noticeable.

#### Worked example

1. Find the r.m.s. speed of nitrogen molecules (m = 4.65 × 10⁻²⁶ kg) at 20 °C.

   ½m(c_rms)² = 1.5 × 1.38 × 10⁻²³ × 293 = 6.07 × 10⁻²¹ J. c_rms = √(2 × 6.07 × 10⁻²¹ / 4.65 × 10⁻²⁶) = 511 m s⁻¹.

2. Five molecules have speeds 300, 400, 500, 600 and 700 m s⁻¹. Find the mean and r.m.s. speeds.

   Mean = 500 m s⁻¹. Mean of squares = (9 + 16 + 25 + 36 + 49) × 10⁴/5 = 2.7 × 10⁵. c_rms = 520 m s⁻¹.

3. Find the internal energy of 2.0 mol of helium at 300 K.

   U = 1.5 × 2.0 × 8.31 × 300 = 7.5 × 10³ J.

4. (OCR B) An activation energy is 0.60 eV. Find the Boltzmann factor at 300 K and at 600 K.

   kT at 300 K = 4.14 × 10⁻²¹ J = 0.0259 eV; E/kT = 23.2; e^(−23.2) = 8.4 × 10⁻¹¹. At 600 K: E/kT = 11.6; e^(−11.6) = 9.1 × 10⁻⁶. Doubling T increases the factor about 10⁵ times.

5. (OCR B) A molecule takes 10⁸ random steps of 70 nm. Estimate its displacement.

   s√N = 70 × 10⁻⁹ × 10⁴ = 7 × 10⁻⁴ m (0.7 mm), although it has travelled 7 m.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting to take the square root when finding c_rms.
- Writing pV = ⅓Nmc² with c as the mean speed instead of c_rms.
- Saying molecules stop at 0 °C.
- Saying all molecules in a gas have the same speed.
- In the derivation, writing the change of momentum as mu instead of 2mu.

### Check questions

1. Define the internal energy of a substance. (2 marks)
2. Explain why the temperature of water stays constant while it boils, even though energy is still supplied. (3 marks)
3. A 2.0 kW kettle contains 1.2 kg of water at 15 °C. Estimate how long it takes to boil, and then how much longer to boil away 0.10 kg. (4 marks)
4. Describe how to verify Boyle's law and how to analyse the results. (5 marks)
5. A balloon holds 0.012 m³ of helium at 1.1 × 10⁵ Pa and 22 °C. Calculate the number of helium atoms. (2 marks)
6. State four assumptions of the kinetic theory of an ideal gas. (4 marks)
7. Derive pV = ⅓Nm(c_rms)². (6 marks)
8. Show that the mean kinetic energy of a gas molecule is (3/2)kT, and calculate it at 400 K. (3 marks)
9. Explain how Brownian motion provides evidence for the existence of molecules. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Sum of the (randomly distributed) kinetic and potential energies (1); of all the particles in the substance (1).
2. Energy breaks / overcomes bonds between molecules (1); increases potential energy of molecules (1); mean kinetic energy unchanged, so temperature constant (1).
3. Q = 1.2 × 4200 × 85 = 4.28 × 10⁵ J (1); t = 214 s (about 3.6 min) (1); Q = 0.10 × 2.26 × 10⁶ = 2.26 × 10⁵ J (1); t = 113 s (1).
4. Trapped air column in tube, compressed by oil using pump; pressure on gauge (1); volume from length × area (1); change pressure in steps, wait for temperature to return to room temperature (1); plot p against 1/V (1); straight line through origin shows p ∝ 1/V (1).
5. N = pV/kT = 1.1 × 10⁵ × 0.012 / (1.38 × 10⁻²³ × 295) (1) = 3.2 × 10²³ (1).
6. Any four: large number of molecules moving randomly; negligible molecular volume; no intermolecular forces except in collisions; collisions perfectly elastic; collision time negligible compared with time between collisions; Newton's laws apply.
7. Molecule rebounds from wall, momentum change 2mu (1); time between collisions with same wall 2L/u (1); force = mu²/L (1); total force for N molecules = Nm mean(u²)/L; pressure = force/L² (1); mean(u²) = ⅓ mean(c²) by random motion in 3D (1); pV = ⅓Nm(c_rms)² (1).
8. pV = NkT = ⅓Nm(c_rms)², so ½m(c_rms)² = (3/2)kT (2); = 1.5 × 1.38 × 10⁻²³ × 400 = 8.3 × 10⁻²¹ J (1).
9. Smoke particles seen moving randomly / jerkily (1); due to uneven bombardment by much smaller molecules (1); the molecules cannot be seen, so the particle motion is indirect evidence they exist and move randomly (1).

## Thermodynamics and engines: the first law, p–V diagrams, engine cycles, the second law, heat pumps and refrigerators

Engines turn heat into work, and refrigerators use work to move heat. This file covers the first law of thermodynamics, isothermal, adiabatic, constant-pressure and constant-volume changes, p–V diagrams and the work done in a cycle, petrol and diesel engine cycles and indicator diagrams, engine power and efficiencies, the second law and the maximum efficiency of a heat engine, and the coefficients of performance of heat pumps and refrigerators.

### Overview

AQA option 3.11.2 (A-level only). Eduqas C1.8(f) to (j) (first law, W = pΔV, area under a p–V graph) is core content for Eduqas. Internal energy, the ideal gas equation and kinetic theory are in file 13.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.11.2 · Eduqas C1.8(f) to (j).
- Sign convention: AQA writes Q = ΔU + W, where W is work done *by* the system. Eduqas writes ΔU = Q − W with the same meaning of W. Some textbooks use W for work done *on* the gas (ΔU = Q + W); always check which is meant.
- Eduqas C1.8(j): for a solid or liquid, W is usually negligible, so Q ≈ ΔU.
- Engine cycles, the second law and reversed heat engines: AQA only.

### 3.11.2.1 First law of thermodynamics

*Refs: AQA 3.11.2.1 · Eduqas C1.8(f), (i), (j). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Q = ΔU + W, where Q is energy transferred to the system by heating, ΔU the increase in internal energy and W the work done by the system.
- Applications of the first law.

#### Explained

The first law is conservation of energy for thermal systems. Energy supplied to a gas by heating (Q) either increases its internal energy (ΔU) or is used by the gas to do work on its surroundings (W), or both:

**Q = ΔU + W**

Signs (AQA convention):

| Quantity | Positive | Negative |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Q | Heat flows into the gas | Heat flows out |
| ΔU | Internal energy (temperature, for an ideal gas) rises | Falls |
| W | Gas expands, doing work on the surroundings | Gas is compressed; work is done on it |

For an ideal gas, U depends only on temperature (U = (3/2)nRT for a monatomic gas), so ΔU = 0 whenever the temperature returns to its starting value.

For a solid or liquid (Eduqas C1.8(j)), volume changes are tiny, so W ≈ 0 and Q ≈ ΔU = mcΔθ.

#### Worked example

1. A gas absorbs 500 J of heat and does 200 J of work. Find ΔU.

   ΔU = Q − W = 300 J.

2. A gas is compressed: 150 J of work is done on it and it loses 60 J of heat. Find ΔU.

   Q = −60 J, W = −150 J. ΔU = −60 − (−150) = +90 J (it warms up).

#### Common mistakes

- Getting the sign of W wrong when the gas is compressed.

### 3.11.2.2 Non-flow processes

*Refs: AQA 3.11.2.2 · Eduqas C1.8(g), (i). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Isothermal, adiabatic, constant-pressure and constant-volume changes.
- pV = nRT; adiabatic: pV^γ = constant; isothermal: pV = constant; at constant pressure W = pΔV.
- Apply the first law to each.

#### Explained

A non-flow process happens to a fixed mass of gas in a closed container (e.g. a cylinder with a piston).

| Process | Condition | First law | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Isothermal | Constant T | ΔU = 0, so Q = W | pV = constant. Must happen slowly, with good thermal contact, so heat can flow in or out to keep T fixed |
| Adiabatic | No heat transfer, Q = 0 | ΔU = −W | pV^γ = constant. Happens quickly or with insulation. Compression: work done on gas raises U and T. Expansion: gas does work, so U and T fall |
| Constant pressure (isobaric) | Constant p | Q = ΔU + pΔV | W = pΔV. V/T = constant |
| Constant volume (isochoric) | Constant V | W = 0, so Q = ΔU | All heat goes into internal energy. p/T = constant |

**γ** (gamma) is the ratio of the molar heat capacities at constant pressure and constant volume, a constant for each gas (5/3 = 1.67 for a monatomic gas such as helium; 1.4 for air). Because γ > 1, an adiabatic curve on a p–V graph is steeper than an isothermal curve through the same point.

Combined with pV = nRT, an adiabatic change also obeys TV^(γ−1) = constant.

#### Worked example

1. Air (γ = 1.4) at 1.0 × 10⁵ Pa and 300 K is compressed adiabatically from 500 cm³ to 50 cm³. Find the final pressure and temperature.

   p₂ = p₁(V₁/V₂)^γ = 1.0 × 10⁵ × 10^1.4 = 2.5 × 10⁶ Pa. T₂ = T₁ × (p₂V₂)/(p₁V₁) = 300 × 25.1 × 0.1 = 750 K. (This is why diesel engines need no spark: compression heats the air enough to ignite the fuel.)

2. A gas expands at constant pressure 2.0 × 10⁵ Pa from 1.0 × 10⁻³ m³ to 3.0 × 10⁻³ m³ and gains 1000 J of heat. Find ΔU.

   W = pΔV = 400 J. ΔU = 1000 − 400 = 600 J.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying temperature is constant in an adiabatic change.
- Saying no heat flows in an isothermal change.

### 3.11.2.3 The p–V diagram

*Refs: AQA 3.11.2.3 · Eduqas C1.8(g), (h). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Representing processes on a p–V diagram.
- Work done = area under the graph (estimated).
- Cyclic processes: work done per cycle = area of the loop.
- Expressions for work done are needed only for constant pressure: W = pΔV.

#### Explained

On a graph of p (vertical) against V (horizontal):

- Constant pressure: horizontal line.
- Constant volume: vertical line.
- Isothermal: a curve, pV = constant (a hyperbola); isotherms at higher T are further from the origin.
- Adiabatic: a steeper curve crossing isotherms.

**Work done** by the gas = area under the curve between the two volumes (positive for expansion, negative for compression). For curved lines, count squares or split into strips.

**Cycles.** In a cycle the gas returns to its starting state, so ΔU = 0 over the whole cycle and net Q = net W. The net work done by the gas per cycle = area enclosed by the loop. If the loop is traversed clockwise, the gas does net work on its surroundings (a heat engine). Anticlockwise: net work is done on the gas (a refrigerator or heat pump).

#### Worked example

1. A gas goes round a rectangular cycle on a p–V diagram between 1.0 × 10⁵ and 3.0 × 10⁵ Pa and between 2.0 × 10⁻³ and 5.0 × 10⁻³ m³, clockwise. Find the net work done per cycle.

   Area = 2.0 × 10⁵ × 3.0 × 10⁻³ = 600 J done by the gas.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the area under only one side of a loop.

### 3.11.2.4 Engine cycles

*Refs: AQA 3.11.2.4. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The four-stroke petrol engine cycle and the diesel engine cycle, and their indicator diagrams.
- Comparison of real indicator diagrams with theoretical cycles; using indicator diagrams to predict and measure power and efficiency.
- Input power = calorific value × fuel flow rate.
- Indicated power = area of p–V loop × number of cycles per second × number of cylinders.
- Output (brake) power P = Tω; friction power = indicated power − brake power.
- Overall efficiency = brake power ÷ input power; thermal efficiency = indicated power ÷ input power; mechanical efficiency = brake power ÷ indicated power.

#### Explained

**Four-stroke petrol engine (Otto cycle).**

1. *Induction*: piston moves down, inlet valve open; a fuel–air mixture is drawn in at about atmospheric pressure.
2. *Compression*: both valves shut; piston moves up, compressing the mixture (approximately adiabatic); pressure and temperature rise. Just before the top, a spark ignites it.
3. *Power (expansion)*: combustion raises pressure sharply at almost constant volume; the hot gas pushes the piston down (approximately adiabatic expansion), doing work.
4. *Exhaust*: piston moves up, exhaust valve open; burnt gases pushed out.

Two revolutions of the crankshaft per cycle. Theoretical (ideal Otto) diagram: adiabatic compression, constant-volume heating, adiabatic expansion, constant-volume cooling, forming a closed loop with sharp corners.

**Diesel engine.** Only air is drawn in. It is compressed much more (compression ratio about 20:1 compared with about 9:1 for petrol), so it gets hot enough to ignite fuel injected at the top of the stroke, with no spark. In the theoretical diesel cycle, fuel burns as the piston starts to move down, so heat is added at roughly constant pressure (a horizontal line at the top of the loop) rather than constant volume.

**Real indicator diagrams.** A real engine's p–V loop (measured with a pressure sensor and a sensor for piston position) has rounded corners, because valves take time to open and close, combustion is not instantaneous, heat is lost through the cylinder walls (so changes are not truly adiabatic), and the working substance changes (fuel–air mixture becomes exhaust). There is a small negative "pumping loop" at the bottom from induction and exhaust (work done to move gas in and out). The real loop's area is smaller than the theoretical one.

**Powers and efficiencies.**

- Input power = calorific value (J kg⁻¹ or J m⁻³) × fuel flow rate (kg s⁻¹ or m³ s⁻¹).
- Indicated power = (area of loop, J) × cycles per second × number of cylinders. For a four-stroke engine, cycles per second = revolutions per second ÷ 2. This is the power the gas delivers to the pistons.
- Brake (output) power = Tω at the crankshaft.
- Friction power = indicated − brake (lost to friction in the engine).
- Thermal efficiency = indicated/input (how well heat becomes work in the gas).
- Mechanical efficiency = brake/indicated (how well the mechanism passes on that work).
- Overall efficiency = brake/input = thermal × mechanical.

#### Worked example

1. A four-cylinder four-stroke engine runs at 3000 rpm. Each indicator loop has area 450 J. The output torque is 120 N m. Fuel (calorific value 45 MJ kg⁻¹) is used at 3.5 × 10⁻³ kg s⁻¹. Find the indicated power, brake power, and the three efficiencies.

   Cycles per second per cylinder = 50/2 = 25. Indicated power = 450 × 25 × 4 = 45 kW. Brake power = 120 × (2π × 50) = 37.7 kW. Input = 45 × 10⁶ × 3.5 × 10⁻³ = 158 kW. Thermal = 45/158 = 0.29; mechanical = 37.7/45 = 0.84; overall = 37.7/158 = 0.24. Friction power = 7.3 kW.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting that a four-stroke engine completes one cycle every two revolutions.
- Mixing up indicated and brake power.

### 3.11.2.5 Second law and engines

*Refs: AQA 3.11.2.5. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- An engine cannot work by the first law alone.
- The second law: a heat engine must operate between a hot source and a cold sink.
- Efficiency = W/Q_H = (Q_H − Q_C)/Q_H; maximum theoretical efficiency = (T_H − T_C)/T_H.
- Why practical engines are less efficient.
- Making the most of W and Q_H, e.g. combined heat and power schemes.

#### Explained

The first law allows any process that conserves energy, but in practice it is impossible to take heat from a single source and turn it completely into work. The **second law of thermodynamics** says a heat engine must take heat Q_H from a hot source, convert part of it to work W, and reject the rest Q_C to a cold sink:

Q_H = W + Q_C

Efficiency = W/Q_H = (Q_H − Q_C)/Q_H.

**Maximum (Carnot) efficiency.** Even an ideal engine (no friction, reversible processes) has efficiency limited by the temperatures (in kelvin) of the source and sink:

**efficiency_max = (T_H − T_C)/T_H = 1 − T_C/T_H**

To raise efficiency, make the source hotter or the sink colder. A power station with steam at 800 K and a sink at 300 K has a maximum efficiency of 62.5%.

**Why real engines are less efficient**: friction between moving parts; heat lost through cylinder walls; incomplete combustion; time needed for valves and heat transfer means processes are not reversible; energy needed to pump gases in and out; the working gas does not reach the full flame temperature.

**Using rejected heat.** Combined heat and power (CHP) schemes use the "waste" heat from a power station or engine to heat nearby buildings or for industrial processes, so the overall fraction of fuel energy used rises to 70 to 80%.

#### Worked example

1. An engine takes in 1500 J of heat per cycle and rejects 1050 J. Find its efficiency and work per cycle.

   W = 450 J. Efficiency = 0.30.

2. Find the maximum efficiency of an engine running between 1800 K and 600 K.

   (1800 − 600)/1800 = 0.67.

#### Common mistakes

- Using °C in the Carnot formula.
- Saying better engineering could reach 100% efficiency.

### 3.11.2.6 Reversed heat engines

*Refs: AQA 3.11.2.6. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The basic principles and uses of heat pumps and refrigerators (not the details of real devices).
- Coefficients of performance: COP_ref = Q_C/W = Q_C/(Q_H − Q_C) = T_C/(T_H − T_C); COP_hp = Q_H/W = Q_H/(Q_H − Q_C) = T_H/(T_H − T_C).

#### Explained

A reversed heat engine uses work W to transfer heat Q_C from a cold space to a hot space, delivering Q_H = Q_C + W there. Heat cannot flow from cold to hot on its own (another form of the second law).

- **Refrigerator**: the purpose is to remove heat from the cold inside. COP_ref = Q_C/W.
- **Heat pump**: the purpose is to deliver heat to a warm building (taking heat from the cold outside air, ground or water). COP_hp = Q_H/W.

COP_hp = COP_ref + 1, and both can be much greater than 1: a heat pump with COP 3 delivers 3 J of heat for every 1 J of electrical work, which is why heat pumps are an efficient way to heat homes. The maximum (ideal) COPs are given by the temperatures; the smaller the temperature difference, the larger the COP. Real COPs are lower.

#### Worked example

1. A heat pump keeps a house at 293 K when the outside is 278 K. Find the maximum COP. If its real COP is a third of this and it delivers 9.0 kW, find the electrical power.

   COP_max = 293/15 = 19.5. Real COP = 6.5. W = 9.0/6.5 = 1.4 kW.

2. A freezer at 255 K is in a kitchen at 295 K. Find the maximum COP and the minimum work to remove 50 kJ.

   COP = 255/40 = 6.4. W = 50/6.4 = 7.8 kJ.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a COP above 1 breaks conservation of energy (energy is moved, not created).
- Swapping T_C and T_H in the two COP formulas.

### Check questions

1. State the first law of thermodynamics, defining each term with its sign convention. (3 marks)
2. Explain why the temperature of a gas rises when it is compressed adiabatically. (3 marks)
3. Sketch an isothermal and an adiabatic expansion from the same starting point on a p–V diagram. Explain which is steeper. (3 marks)
4. Describe the four strokes of a petrol engine and sketch the theoretical indicator diagram. (6 marks)
5. An engine has indicated power 60 kW and brake power 48 kW, using fuel with calorific value 44 MJ kg⁻¹ at 4.5 × 10⁻³ kg s⁻¹. Find the friction power, mechanical efficiency and overall efficiency. (4 marks)
6. Explain why no heat engine can be 100% efficient. (2 marks)
7. A power station's turbine takes steam at 840 K and its condenser is at 310 K. Find the maximum efficiency and suggest how waste heat could be used. (3 marks)
8. A refrigerator removes 300 J per second from food at 268 K with 80 W of input power. Calculate its COP and the heat delivered to the kitchen per second. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Q = ΔU + W (1); Q: energy transferred to the system by heating (positive in) (1); ΔU: increase in internal energy; W: work done by the system (positive on expansion) (1).
2. Q = 0, so ΔU = −W (1); compression means work done on gas, W negative (1); so ΔU positive; internal energy (mean KE) and temperature rise (1).
3. Two downward curves from same point (1); adiabatic steeper (1); in adiabatic expansion the gas cools as well as expanding, so pressure falls faster (pV^γ with γ > 1) (1).
4. Induction: piston down, fuel–air drawn in (1); compression: adiabatic compression, spark (1); power: combustion, pressure rises, gas expands pushing piston (1); exhaust: gases pushed out (1); diagram: two adiabatics joined by two constant-volume lines (1), clockwise loop (1).
5. Friction power = 12 kW (1); mechanical efficiency = 48/60 = 0.80 (1); input = 198 kW (1); overall = 48/198 = 0.24 (1).
6. Second law: engine must reject some heat to a cold sink (1); so W < Q_H; maximum efficiency (T_H − T_C)/T_H < 1 (1).
7. (840 − 310)/840 = 0.63 (2); combined heat and power, e.g. district heating (1).
8. COP = 300/80 = 3.75 (1)(1); Q_H = 380 W (1).

## Charge, current, potential difference, resistance, I–V characteristics and resistivity

This file defines current, charge, potential difference and resistance; explains conduction in metals with drift velocity; covers I–V characteristics of resistors, lamps and diodes; and covers resistivity, how resistance depends on temperature and light, and superconductivity, with AQA required practical 5.

### Overview

AS & A for every board. Series and parallel circuits, power, Kirchhoff's laws, potential dividers and internal resistance are in file 11.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.5.1.1 to 3.5.1.3, RP5 · Edexcel 31 to 33, 38 to 41, 47, 48, CP2 · OCR A 4.1, 4.2.1 to 4.2.4 · OCR B 3.1.2 · Eduqas C2.1, C2.2.
- Drift velocity I = nAvq (nAve): Edexcel 41, OCR A 4.1.2, Eduqas C2.1(g); OCR B 3.1.2(a)(viii) qualitatively (number density of charge carriers). Not in AQA.
- Conductance G = I/V = 1/R and conductivity σ = 1/ρ: OCR B only.
- Edexcel 47, 48 and OCR A 4.2.4(b), (c) model changes of resistance with temperature and light in terms of lattice vibrations and number of charge carriers.
- Superconductivity: AQA 3.5.1.3 and Eduqas C2.2(k) to (n). Not Edexcel or OCR.
- OCR A 4.2.2(e): an electron accelerated through V gains eV = ½mv².
- Resistivity practical: AQA RP5, Edexcel CP2, OCR A 4.2.4(a)(ii) (PAG3), OCR B 3.1.2(d)(ii), Eduqas specified practical. I–V characteristics: OCR A PAG3, OCR B 3.1.2(d)(i), Eduqas specified practical. Resistance with temperature: Eduqas specified practical (and suggested by AQA).

### 3.5.1.1 Basics of electricity

*Refs: AQA 3.5.1.1 · Edexcel 31, 32, 33 · OCR A 4.1.1(a) to (f), 4.1.2(a), (b), 4.2.1, 4.2.2(a), (d), (e), 4.2.3(a) · OCR B 3.1.2(a)(i), (ii), (viii), (b)(i), (ii), (c)(i), (ii) · Eduqas C2.1(a) to (g), C2.2(a), (b), (d), (e). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Current is the rate of flow of charge: I = ΔQ/Δt.
- Potential difference is work done (energy transferred) per unit charge: V = W/Q.
- Resistance R = V/I.
- (OCR A) The coulomb; the elementary charge e = 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ C; charge is quantised; conventional current and electron flow; circuit symbols.
- (Edexcel, OCR A, Eduqas) I = nAvq for charge carriers with number density n and drift velocity v.

#### Explained

**Charge and current.** Current is the rate of flow of charge: I = ΔQ/Δt. 1 ampere = 1 coulomb per second. In metals the charge carriers are free (delocalised) electrons; in electrolytes and gases, ions. Charge is quantised: any charge is a whole-number multiple of e = 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ C.

*Conventional current* flows from + to − around the circuit; electrons actually flow from − to +.

**Potential difference.** V = W/Q. 1 volt = 1 joule per coulomb. It is the energy transferred from electrical to other forms per unit charge passing through a component. An electron accelerated through a p.d. V gains energy eV: eV = ½mv² (OCR A 4.2.2(e)).

**Resistance.** R = V/I, unit the ohm (Ω = V A⁻¹). OCR B also uses conductance G = I/V = 1/R, unit siemens (S).

**Drift velocity** (Edexcel 41, OCR A 4.1.2, Eduqas C2.1(f), (g)).

Consider a wire of cross-sectional area A with n free charge carriers per cubic metre, each of charge q, drifting at mean speed v. In time t, carriers move a distance vt, so the charge passing a point is the charge in a volume Avt: Q = nAvtq. So:

**I = nAvq**

Electrons in a metal move randomly at high speed (about 10⁵ m s⁻¹), colliding with the vibrating ions of the lattice. A p.d. adds a small drift along the wire, typically less than 1 mm s⁻¹. The signal (the electric field) travels at nearly the speed of light, which is why lamps light instantly.

**Metals, semiconductors and insulators** (OCR B 3.1.2(a)(viii), Edexcel 41). The number density n explains the huge range of resistivities:

| Material | n / m⁻³ | Behaviour |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Metal (copper) | about 10²⁹ | Good conductor; n fixed |
| Semiconductor (silicon) | about 10¹⁶ to 10²³ | n rises steeply with temperature or light |
| Insulator | almost 0 | Very high resistance |

For the same current, a semiconductor with fewer carriers must have a much higher drift velocity.

**Circuit symbols** (OCR A 4.2.1, OCR B 3.1.2(b)(ii)): cell, battery, switch, fixed and variable resistor, filament lamp, ammeter, voltmeter, diode, LED, thermistor, LDR, fuse, capacitor, potentiometer.

#### Worked example

1. A charge of 45 C passes through a lamp in 2.0 minutes. Find the current and the number of electrons per second.

   I = 45/120 = 0.375 A. Electrons per second = 0.375/1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ = 2.3 × 10¹⁸.

2. A copper wire (n = 8.5 × 10²⁸ m⁻³) of diameter 1.0 mm carries 2.0 A. Find the drift velocity.

   A = π(0.5 × 10⁻³)² = 7.85 × 10⁻⁷ m². v = I/(nAe) = 2.0/(8.5 × 10²⁸ × 7.85 × 10⁻⁷ × 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹) = 1.9 × 10⁻⁴ m s⁻¹.

3. The same current flows through a thin section of the wire of half the diameter. What happens to the drift velocity?

   A is quartered, so v is four times larger.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying current is "used up" in a component.
- Confusing p.d. (energy per charge) with current (charge per second).
- Saying electrons travel round the circuit at nearly the speed of light.

### 3.5.1.2 Current–voltage characteristics

*Refs: AQA 3.5.1.2 · Edexcel 33, 38 · OCR A 4.2.3(b), (c), (d) · OCR B 3.1.2(a)(vi), (b)(iii), (d)(i) · Eduqas C2.2(c), (d). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- I–V characteristics for an ohmic conductor, a semiconductor diode and a filament lamp (also thermistor: Edexcel 38; LDR: OCR A 4.2.3(d)).
- Ohm's law: I ∝ V under constant physical conditions (e.g. constant temperature). It is a special case.
- Ideal ammeters have zero resistance; ideal voltmeters have infinite resistance.
- Graphs may have I or V on the horizontal axis.

#### Explained

**Ohmic conductor** (resistor at constant temperature): a straight line through the origin; R constant. Ohm's law: for a conductor at constant temperature, the current is directly proportional to the p.d.

**Filament lamp**: an S-shaped curve through the origin (symmetrical for reversed p.d.). As current rises, the filament heats up; its metal ions vibrate more; electrons collide more often; resistance increases. So the graph of I against V gets less steep at higher V. (R = V/I at a point, not the gradient.)

**Semiconductor diode**: in reverse bias, almost no current (very high resistance) until breakdown. In forward bias, almost no current until a threshold of about 0.6 V (silicon), then current rises steeply (resistance falls). An LED behaves similarly with a higher threshold (1.5 to 3 V depending on colour).

**Thermistor (NTC)** (Edexcel, OCR A): as current increases, it warms up, its resistance falls, and the I–V graph curves upwards (gets steeper).

**Resistance from a graph.** R = V/I at that point. Do not use the gradient of the curve; the gradient is only 1/R (or R, depending on axes) for a straight line through the origin.

**Measuring.** Use an ammeter in series and a voltmeter in parallel with the component, and a variable power supply or a potential divider so the p.d. can be varied from zero. Reverse the supply to get negative values. Use a protective resistor in series with a diode.

#### Worked example

1. At 6.0 V a lamp carries 0.50 A; at 12 V it carries 0.75 A. Find its resistance at each p.d. and explain the difference.

   6.0/0.50 = 12 Ω; 12/0.75 = 16 Ω. At higher current the filament is hotter, ions vibrate more, so electrons collide more often, so resistance is higher.

#### Common mistakes

- Finding resistance from the gradient of a curved I–V graph.
- Saying a filament lamp obeys Ohm's law.
- Saying a diode conducts equally in both directions once past 0.6 V.

### 3.5.1.3 Resistivity

*Refs: AQA 3.5.1.3, RP5 · Edexcel 39, 40, 41, 47, 48, CP2 · OCR A 4.2.3(d), 4.2.4(a), (b), (c) · OCR B 3.1.2(b)(i), (b)(iii), (c)(iii), (d)(ii), (d)(iv) · Eduqas C2.2(g) to (n). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Resistivity ρ = RA/L, so R = ρL/A.
- The qualitative effect of temperature on the resistance of metals and NTC thermistors; thermistors as temperature sensors; resistance–temperature graphs.
- (Edexcel 47, 48, OCR A, Eduqas) Explaining changes of resistance with temperature and with light (LDR) in terms of lattice vibrations and the number of charge carriers.
- Superconductivity: zero resistivity at and below a critical temperature; uses in strong magnets and loss-free power transmission. (AQA, Eduqas)
- AQA required practical 5: resistivity of a wire with a micrometer, ammeter and voltmeter.
- (OCR B) Conductivity σ = 1/ρ, G = σA/L.

#### Explained

**Resistivity.** R = ρL/A. Resistance is proportional to length (a longer path, more collisions) and inversely proportional to cross-sectional area (more paths side by side). Resistivity ρ (Ω m) is a property of the material at a given temperature. Copper: 1.7 × 10⁻⁸ Ω m. Nichrome: 1.1 × 10⁻⁶ Ω m. Silicon: about 10³ Ω m. Glass: about 10¹² Ω m.

**Metals and temperature.** As temperature rises, the positive ions vibrate with larger amplitude. Free electrons collide with them more often, which reduces drift velocity for a given p.d. n stays the same. So resistance rises (a positive temperature coefficient).

**NTC thermistors.** Made of a semiconductor. As temperature rises, more electrons gain enough energy to become free charge carriers: n rises greatly. This outweighs the increase in lattice vibration, so resistance falls sharply (and non-linearly). Resistance–temperature graph: steep curve falling towards a low value. Uses: temperature sensors (in a potential divider), thermostats, fire alarms, car engine sensors.

**LDRs** (light-dependent resistors). Light photons give electrons in the semiconductor enough energy to become free: n rises, so resistance falls as light intensity rises. Uses: light sensors, automatic street lights.

**Superconductivity** (AQA, Eduqas). Some materials have exactly zero resistivity when cooled to or below their critical temperature Tc (also called transition temperature). Above Tc, the resistivity is normal. Mercury: Tc = 4.2 K. Some ceramic copper oxide compounds (high-temperature superconductors) have Tc above 77 K, so liquid nitrogen (cheaper than liquid helium) can cool them. With no resistance, a current flows with no energy dissipated (no heating).

Uses:

- Producing very strong magnetic fields (MRI scanners, particle accelerators, maglev trains), since large currents can flow without heating the coils.
- Power cables that transmit electricity without energy loss (currently limited by cooling cost).
- Fast electronic circuits.

(Eduqas C2.2(j)) In a normal metal, collisions of free electrons with the lattice convert energy to heat; in a superconductor, the electrons pass through the lattice without these energy-losing collisions.

#### Worked example

1. A nichrome wire has diameter 0.32 mm and length 1.50 m. Find its resistance (ρ = 1.10 × 10⁻⁶ Ω m).

   A = π(0.16 × 10⁻³)² = 8.04 × 10⁻⁸ m². R = 1.10 × 10⁻⁶ × 1.50/8.04 × 10⁻⁸ = 20.5 Ω.

2. A wire is stretched to twice its length (same volume). By what factor does its resistance change?

   L doubles; A halves (volume = LA fixed). R = ρL/A rises by 2 × 2 = 4.

3. (OCR B) Find the conductance of a 4.0 m copper wire with A = 1.5 × 10⁻⁶ m² (σ = 5.9 × 10⁷ S m⁻¹).

   G = σA/L = 5.9 × 10⁷ × 1.5 × 10⁻⁶ / 4.0 = 22 S (R = 0.045 Ω).

#### Common mistakes

- Using diameter for radius when calculating A.
- Saying resistivity depends on the length of the wire.
- Saying metals have more resistance when hot "because electrons move faster"; it is the lattice vibrations.
- Saying a superconductor has a very small resistance; it is exactly zero.
- Saying thermistor resistance falls because ions vibrate less.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 5** (also Edexcel CP2, OCR A PAG3, OCR B 3.1.2(d)(ii), Eduqas specified practical): determine the resistivity of a wire.

1. Measure the diameter of the wire with a micrometer at several places along its length and in different orientations; take the mean. Check for zero error. A = πd²/4.
2. Tape the wire along a metre rule. Connect it in series with an ammeter, a switch and a low-voltage supply; connect a voltmeter across a length L of the wire using a crocodile clip (flying lead) as one contact.
3. For a range of lengths (e.g. 0.10 m to 0.90 m), record V and I and calculate R = V/I.
4. Plot R against L: a straight line through (or near) the origin. Gradient = ρ/A, so ρ = gradient × A.

Tips: keep the current low and switch off between readings so the wire does not heat (heating would raise R). Any contact resistance appears as an intercept, not in the gradient. The diameter has the largest percentage uncertainty (it is squared). Safety: the wire can get hot; use low current.

**I–V characteristics** (OCR A PAG3, OCR B 3.1.2(d)(i), Eduqas). Use a potential divider supply to vary p.d. from zero; record I and V; reverse connections for negative values; plot I against V.

**Resistance against temperature** (Eduqas specified practical; OCR B 3.1.2(d)(iv) calibrating a sensor). Place the thermistor (or a coil of wire) in a beaker of water with a thermometer. Heat in steps, stirring, and wait for the reading to settle before measuring R with an ohmmeter (or V and I). Plot R against θ. For calibration, this graph lets the thermistor be used as a thermometer.

### Check questions

1. Define the volt. (1 mark)
2. A phone charger delivers 1.5 A for 2.0 hours. Calculate the charge delivered. (2 marks)
3. Derive I = nAve. (3 marks)
4. Explain why the drift velocity of charge carriers in a semiconductor is much greater than in a metal wire of the same size carrying the same current. (2 marks)
5. Sketch the I–V characteristic of a filament lamp and explain its shape. (4 marks)
6. Explain why the resistance of an NTC thermistor falls as temperature rises. (3 marks)
7. Describe how to determine the resistivity of a metal wire. (6 marks)
8. State what is meant by superconductivity and give one use. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. One joule per coulomb / p.d. when 1 J of energy is transferred per coulomb of charge (1).
2. Q = It = 1.5 × 7200 (1) = 1.08 × 10⁴ C (1).
3. In time t carriers move distance vt; volume passing a point = Avt (1); number of carriers = nAvt; charge = nAvte (1); I = Q/t = nAve (1).
4. Semiconductor has far fewer charge carriers per unit volume / smaller n (1); I = nAve so v must be larger for the same I and A (1).
5. S-shaped curve through origin, gradient decreasing at higher p.d. (both quadrants) (1); current heats the filament (1); ions vibrate more, electrons collide more often (1); resistance increases, so current rises less for each step in p.d. (1).
6. Temperature rise gives electrons enough energy to become free / more charge carriers are released (1); number of charge carriers increases greatly (1); this outweighs increased lattice vibrations so resistance falls (1).
7. Measure diameter with micrometer at several points, average (1); calculate A = πd²/4 (1); circuit with ammeter in series, voltmeter across a measured length of wire (1); vary length with flying lead, measure with metre rule (1); calculate R = V/I for each length (1); plot R against L, ρ = gradient × A; low current to avoid heating (1).
8. Resistivity becomes zero (1); at and below a critical temperature (1); any use: strong magnetic fields in MRI / particle accelerators / loss-free power transmission (1).

## DC circuits: series and parallel, power, Kirchhoff's laws, potential dividers, e.m.f. and internal resistance

This file covers how current and p.d. share out in circuits (from conservation of charge and energy), combining resistors, electrical power and energy, potential dividers with sensors, and e.m.f. and internal resistance, with AQA required practical 6.

### Overview

AS & A for every board. Definitions of current, p.d. and resistance are in file 10. Capacitors in circuits are in file 16.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.5.1.4 to 3.5.1.6, RP6 · Edexcel 34 to 37, 42 to 46, CP3 · OCR A 4.1.1(g), 4.2.2(b), (c), 4.2.5, 4.3 · OCR B 3.1.2 · Eduqas C2.2(f), C2.3.
- OCR A 4.3.1(f) asks for circuits with more than one source of e.m.f. using Kirchhoff's laws; OCR A 4.2.5(c) the kilowatt-hour.
- Edexcel 42 asks how the potential varies along a uniform current-carrying wire.
- AQA says the potentiometer as a measuring instrument is not required.
- OCR B uses conductance: in parallel, G = G₁ + G₂; in series, 1/G = 1/G₁ + 1/G₂; and Vout = Vin × R₂/(R₁ + R₂).
- Internal resistance practical: AQA RP6, Edexcel CP3, OCR A PAG4, OCR B 3.1.2(d)(v), Eduqas specified practical.
- Edexcel CP12 (calibrating a thermistor in a potential divider as a thermostat) is in file 13 with thermal physics, but its circuit is explained here.

### 3.5.1.4 Circuits

*Refs: AQA 3.5.1.4 · Edexcel 34, 35, 36, 37 · OCR A 4.1.1(g), 4.2.5(a) to (c), 4.3.1(a) to (f) · OCR B 3.1.2(a)(iii), (v), (ix), (c)(i), (ii) · Eduqas C2.2(f), C2.3(a) to (d). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Resistors in series: R_T = R₁ + R₂ + R₃ + …; in parallel: 1/R_T = 1/R₁ + 1/R₂ + 1/R₃ + ….
- Energy and power: E = IVt; P = IV = I²R = V²/R.
- How current, p.d. and resistance relate in series and parallel circuits, including cells in series and identical cells in parallel.
- Conservation of charge (Kirchhoff's first law) and of energy (Kirchhoff's second law) in d.c. circuits; deriving the resistor combination rules from them (Edexcel 36).

#### Explained

**Kirchhoff's first law** (conservation of charge). The total current into a junction equals the total current out. Charge cannot build up at a point.

**Kirchhoff's second law** (conservation of energy). Around any closed loop, the sum of the e.m.f.s equals the sum of the p.d.s (IR terms). Energy given to each coulomb by sources equals energy transferred by that coulomb in components.

**Series.** Same current I through each. The p.d.s add: V = V₁ + V₂ + V₃. Dividing by I: R_T = R₁ + R₂ + R₃. The total resistance is larger than any one resistor.

**Parallel.** Same p.d. V across each branch. The currents add: I = I₁ + I₂ + I₃. Dividing by V: 1/R_T = 1/R₁ + 1/R₂ + 1/R₃. The total resistance is smaller than the smallest branch. Two equal resistors in parallel: R/2. Two resistors: R_T = R₁R₂/(R₁ + R₂).

**Cells.**

- Cells in series: e.m.f.s add (taking direction into account), and internal resistances add.
- n identical cells in parallel: total e.m.f. = one cell's e.m.f.; internal resistance = r/n. They can supply a larger current for longer.

**Power and energy.** P = IV. Substituting V = IR: P = I²R = V²/R. Energy transferred: E = IVt = Pt.

- P = I²R is useful when the current is known (series), P = V²/R when the p.d. is known (parallel).
- Kilowatt-hour (OCR A 4.2.5(c)): the energy transferred by a 1 kW device in 1 hour = 3.6 MJ. Cost = kW h used × price per kW h.

**Solving circuits.** Reduce combinations step by step to find total resistance and supply current, then work back out to find each current and p.d. For circuits with two e.m.f.s (OCR A 4.3.1(f)), label unknown currents, apply the first law at junctions and the second law around enough loops, and solve the simultaneous equations. A negative answer means the current flows the other way.

#### Worked example

1. A 6.0 Ω resistor is in series with a parallel pair of 4.0 Ω and 12 Ω, across a 12 V supply with no internal resistance. Find the supply current and the current in the 12 Ω resistor.

   Parallel pair: 4.0 × 12/16 = 3.0 Ω. Total = 9.0 Ω. I = 12/9.0 = 1.33 A. P.d. across the pair = 1.33 × 3.0 = 4.0 V. Current in 12 Ω = 4.0/12 = 0.33 A.

2. A 2.0 kW heater runs for 3.0 hours a day. Electricity costs 28p per kW h. Find the weekly cost.

   Energy = 2.0 × 3.0 × 7 = 42 kW h. Cost = 42 × 28 = £11.76.

3. (OCR A) Two batteries, ε₁ = 6.0 V and ε₂ = 4.0 V, are connected positive to positive through resistors 2.0 Ω (with ε₁) and 3.0 Ω (with ε₂), and a 5.0 Ω resistor connects the junctions. Find the current in the 5.0 Ω resistor.

   Let I₁ flow from ε₁ through 2.0 Ω, I₂ from ε₂ through 3.0 Ω, both into the junction; I₁ + I₂ flows through 5.0 Ω. Loop 1: 6.0 = 2.0I₁ + 5.0(I₁ + I₂). Loop 2: 4.0 = 3.0I₂ + 5.0(I₁ + I₂). So 7I₁ + 5I₂ = 6 and 5I₁ + 8I₂ = 4. Solving: I₁ = (48 − 20)/(56 − 25) = 0.903 A; I₂ = (4 − 5 × 0.903)/8 = −0.065 A. Current in 5.0 Ω = 0.84 A. (The negative I₂ means ε₂ is being charged.)

#### Common mistakes

- Adding resistances in parallel.
- Forgetting to take the reciprocal at the end of 1/R_T.
- Saying current divides equally between unequal parallel branches (more current goes through the smaller resistance).
- Using P = V²/R with the supply p.d. for a component in series.

### 3.5.1.5 Potential divider

*Refs: AQA 3.5.1.5 · Edexcel 42, 43, 44 · OCR A 4.3.3(a) to (c) · OCR B 3.1.2(a)(vii), (c)(iv), (d)(iii) · Eduqas C2.3(e). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- A potential divider supplies a fixed or variable p.d. from a power supply.
- Using variable resistors, thermistors and LDRs in potential dividers; designing circuits to achieve a given output (e.g. a sensor that turns on a device when it gets dark or cold).
- (Edexcel 42) The p.d. along a uniform wire carrying a current varies linearly with distance.

#### Explained

Two resistors in series across an input p.d. Vin share it in the ratio of their resistances: V₁/V₂ = R₁/R₂. The output across R₂ is:

**Vout = Vin × R₂/(R₁ + R₂)**

**Variable output.** Replace the pair with a potentiometer (a resistor with a sliding contact), or make one resistor variable. A potentiometer can give any p.d. from 0 to Vin, which is why it is used to vary p.d. smoothly from zero when measuring I–V characteristics.

**Sensor circuits.**

- Thermistor at the bottom (output across thermistor): as temperature rises, R_thermistor falls, so Vout falls. Put the thermistor at the top to make Vout rise with temperature (useful for a fan or alarm that switches on when hot).
- LDR at the bottom: as light gets brighter, R_LDR falls, Vout falls. Rises in the dark: suitable for switching on a street light.
- A variable resistor in place of the fixed one sets the temperature or light level at which the output reaches the switching voltage.

**Loading.** If a device with resistance R_L is connected across the output, it is in parallel with R₂, reducing the effective resistance and so reducing Vout. To avoid this, R_L should be much larger than R₂.

**Potential along a uniform wire** (Edexcel 42). A uniform wire with a current has equal resistance per unit length, so potential falls linearly from one end to the other. A sliding contact on the wire gives an output p.d. proportional to the distance.

#### Worked example

1. A 9.0 V supply is connected across 2.2 kΩ and 6.8 kΩ in series. Find the p.d. across the 6.8 kΩ resistor.

   Vout = 9.0 × 6.8/9.0 = 6.8 V.

2. A thermistor (resistance 1.5 kΩ at 20 °C, 400 Ω at 60 °C) is in series with a 1.0 kΩ resistor across 5.0 V. Find the p.d. across the fixed resistor at each temperature.

   20 °C: 5.0 × 1.0/2.5 = 2.0 V. 60 °C: 5.0 × 1.0/1.4 = 3.6 V. The output across the fixed resistor rises as it gets hotter.

3. A voltmeter of resistance 10 kΩ is connected across the 6.8 kΩ resistor in example 1. Find its reading.

   Parallel: 6.8 × 10/16.8 = 4.05 kΩ. Vout = 9.0 × 4.05/(2.2 + 4.05) = 5.8 V (lower than 6.8 V because of loading).

#### Common mistakes

- Putting the output across the wrong resistor.
- Forgetting the thermistor's resistance falls (not rises) with temperature.
- Ignoring the effect of a load connected across the output.

### 3.5.1.6 Electromotive force and internal resistance

*Refs: AQA 3.5.1.6, RP6 · Edexcel 45, 46, CP3 · OCR A 4.2.2(b), (c), 4.3.2(a) to (c) · OCR B 3.1.2(a)(iv), (c)(i), (d)(v) · Eduqas C2.3(f) to (i). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- E.m.f. ε = E/Q: the energy transferred to each coulomb of charge by the source.
- The terminal p.d. is less than the e.m.f. when a current flows, because of internal resistance: ε = I(R + r) = V + Ir.
- The difference between e.m.f. and p.d. in terms of energy transfer (OCR A 4.2.2(c)).
- Calculations where internal resistance is not negligible.
- AQA RP6 / Edexcel CP3: measure the e.m.f. and internal resistance from the variation of terminal p.d. with current.

#### Explained

**E.m.f. and p.d.** Both are measured in volts (J C⁻¹), but:

- E.m.f. ε is the energy transferred *to* each coulomb by the source (from chemical, mechanical or light energy into electrical).
- P.d. V is the energy transferred *from* each coulomb in a component (electrical into other forms).

**Internal resistance.** Real sources have resistance inside them (the chemicals in a cell, the coils of a generator). Some energy is dissipated inside the source as charge passes through it. So:

ε = I(R + r) → ε = V + Ir

where V = IR is the terminal p.d. and Ir is the "lost volts". When no current flows (open circuit, or a very high-resistance voltmeter), V = ε.

**Effects.**

- The terminal p.d. falls as the current drawn increases.
- Large currents heat the source (power I²r wasted).
- Short-circuit current (R = 0) = ε/r. A car battery has very small r (to deliver hundreds of amps to the starter motor); a high-voltage supply for schools has large r for safety.

**Graph.** V = ε − rI is a straight line (y = c + mx) when V is plotted against I. Intercept on the V axis = ε. Gradient = −r.

**Power to the load** (extension). Maximum power is delivered to the external resistance when R = r.

#### Worked example

1. A cell of e.m.f. 1.5 V and internal resistance 0.50 Ω is connected to a 2.5 Ω resistor. Find the current, terminal p.d. and lost volts.

   I = 1.5/3.0 = 0.50 A. V = 0.50 × 2.5 = 1.25 V. Lost volts = 0.25 V.

2. A battery's terminal p.d. is 8.4 V when it supplies 2.0 A and 7.8 V when it supplies 3.5 A. Find ε and r.

   8.4 = ε − 2.0r; 7.8 = ε − 3.5r. Subtract: 0.6 = 1.5r, r = 0.40 Ω. ε = 8.4 + 0.8 = 9.2 V.

3. Four cells, each 1.5 V, 0.30 Ω, are in series with a 4.8 Ω lamp. Find the current.

   ε = 6.0 V, r = 1.2 Ω. I = 6.0/6.0 = 1.0 A.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying e.m.f. is a force.
- Forgetting that the voltmeter across the terminals reads V, not ε, when a current flows.
- Using the gradient of the V–I graph as +r.
- Adding internal resistances of parallel cells instead of dividing.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 6** (also Edexcel CP3, OCR A PAG4, OCR B 3.1.2(d)(v), Eduqas specified practical): investigate the e.m.f. and internal resistance of a cell.

1. Connect the cell in series with a variable resistor (rheostat), an ammeter and a switch. Connect a voltmeter across the cell terminals. A small fixed resistor in series protects against short-circuiting.
2. Close the switch; record V and I. Open the switch between readings to stop the cell warming (r changes with temperature) and running down.
3. Change the resistance to get at least 6 pairs of readings over a wide range of current.
4. Plot V (y-axis) against I (x-axis). Draw the best straight line. ε = y-intercept; r = −gradient.

Errors: the e.m.f. falls slowly as the cell is used; internal resistance rises as the cell warms. A high-resistance digital voltmeter is close to ideal. Safety: do not short-circuit the cell (it can get very hot).

**Potential divider with sensors** (OCR A 4.3.3(c)(ii) PAG4, OCR B 3.1.2(d)(iii)). Build a thermistor or LDR potential divider; measure Vout as temperature (water bath) or light level (lamp at different distances, or a light meter) changes. Plot Vout against the variable.

### Check questions

1. State Kirchhoff's two laws and the conservation principle behind each. (4 marks)
2. Derive the formula for the total resistance of two resistors in parallel. (3 marks)
3. Three 6.0 Ω resistors can be connected in different ways. Find the largest and smallest total resistances possible and one other value. (3 marks)
4. A 60 W, 230 V lamp is used. Find its resistance and the current. (2 marks)
5. Design a potential divider circuit using an LDR and a 10 kΩ variable resistor whose output rises when it gets dark. Explain how it works. (4 marks)
6. Define e.m.f. and explain why the terminal p.d. of a battery falls when it supplies a larger current. (3 marks)
7. A battery has e.m.f. 12.0 V and internal resistance 0.20 Ω. Find the terminal p.d. when it delivers 15 A, and the power wasted inside the battery. (3 marks)
8. Describe how to measure the e.m.f. and internal resistance of a cell, including the graph you would plot. (6 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. First: sum of currents into a junction = sum out (1); conservation of charge (1). Second: sum of e.m.f.s = sum of p.d.s around a closed loop (1); conservation of energy (1).
2. Same p.d. V across each; I = I₁ + I₂ (1); V/R_T = V/R₁ + V/R₂ (1); 1/R_T = 1/R₁ + 1/R₂ (1).
3. Series: 18 Ω (1); parallel: 2.0 Ω (1); either 9.0 Ω (two parallel + one series) or 4.0 Ω (two series in parallel with one) (1).
4. I = P/V = 0.26 A (1); R = V²/P = 880 Ω (1).
5. LDR in series with variable resistor across supply, output taken across LDR (1); in the dark LDR resistance rises (1); LDR takes a larger share of the p.d., so Vout rises (1); variable resistor sets the light level at which switching occurs (1).
