6. Energy transferred to each coulomb by the source / ε = E/Q (1); internal resistance (1); more current means more lost volts Ir, so V = ε − Ir falls (1).
7. V = 12.0 − 15 × 0.20 = 9.0 V (2); P = I²r = 225 × 0.20 = 45 W (1).
8. Cell with variable resistor, ammeter in series and voltmeter across cell (1); protective resistor / switch off between readings (1); vary resistance, record V and I, at least 6 readings (1); plot V against I (1); intercept on V axis = e.m.f. (1); gradient = −r (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/16-capacitance.md -->

## Capacitance: capacitors, dielectrics, energy stored, charging and discharging

A capacitor stores charge, and so energy, on two conducting plates separated by an insulator. This file covers capacitance, the parallel-plate capacitor and dielectrics, energy stored, capacitors in series and parallel, and exponential charging and discharging through a resistor, with AQA required practical 9.

### Overview

A-level only for every board. The uniform field between plates is in file 15. Exponential decay of radioactive nuclei, which uses the same mathematics, is in file 18.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.7.4, RP9 · Edexcel 116 to 120, CP11 · OCR A 6.1, 6.2.3(b), 6.2.4(c) · OCR B 5.1.1 · Eduqas C2.4.
- Capacitors in series and parallel: OCR A 6.1.1(c), (d) and Eduqas C2.4(h). Not required by AQA or Edexcel (but useful).
- Dielectrics and relative permittivity: AQA 3.7.4.2 (including how polar molecules rotate in a field), OCR A 6.2.3(b), Eduqas C2.4(e). Edexcel and OCR B do not require dielectrics.
- OCR A 6.2.4(c): capacitance of an isolated sphere, C = 4πε₀R.
- OCR A 6.1.3(d) and OCR B 5.1.1(c)(iv): model discharge in small time steps (iterative / spreadsheet), from ΔQ/Δt = −Q/RC. OCR A 6.1.3(e): constant-ratio property of exponential decay.
- Edexcel CP11: use an oscilloscope or data logger to display charging and discharging. Eduqas specified practicals: charging/discharging, and energy stored in a capacitor.

### 3.7.4.1 Capacitance

*Refs: AQA 3.7.4.1 · Edexcel 116 · OCR A 6.1.1(a) to (e) · OCR B 5.1.1(a)(i), (c)(v) · Eduqas C2.4(a), (b), (c), (h). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- C = Q/V: charge stored per unit p.d. Unit the farad, F = C V⁻¹.
- (OCR A, Eduqas) Capacitors in series and in parallel.
- (Eduqas) A capacitor stores energy by moving charge from one plate to the other: the plates have equal and opposite charges and the net charge is zero.

#### Explained

A capacitor is two conducting plates separated by an insulator (the dielectric, or just air/vacuum). When connected to a supply, electrons flow onto one plate and off the other: one plate becomes +Q, the other −Q. "Charge stored" means Q, the charge on one plate. Current flows in the wires while it charges, but no charge crosses the gap.

**Capacitance.** C = Q/V. 1 farad is large; typical capacitors are μF, nF or pF.

**Combinations** (OCR A, Eduqas).

- Parallel: same p.d.; charges add: C_total = C₁ + C₂ + ….
- Series: same charge on each; p.d.s add: 1/C_total = 1/C₁ + 1/C₂ + ….

(These are the opposite way round from resistors.)

#### Worked example

1. A 470 μF capacitor is charged to 9.0 V. Find the charge stored.

   Q = CV = 470 × 10⁻⁶ × 9.0 = 4.2 × 10⁻³ C.

2. (OCR A, Eduqas) 20 μF and 30 μF in series across 12 V. Find the total capacitance and the p.d. across each.

   1/C = 1/20 + 1/30, C = 12 μF. Q = 12 × 10⁻⁶ × 12 = 1.44 × 10⁻⁴ C on each. V₂₀ = 7.2 V, V₃₀ = 4.8 V.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the net charge on a capacitor is 2Q (it is zero).
- Using the resistor rules for capacitors.

### 3.7.4.2 Parallel plate capacitor

*Refs: AQA 3.7.4.2 · OCR A 6.2.3(b), 6.2.4(c) · Eduqas C2.4(d), (e), (f). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- C = Aε₀ε_r/d for a parallel-plate capacitor.
- Relative permittivity ε_r (dielectric constant) and dielectric action; how a polar molecule rotates in an electric field.
- (OCR A) Isolated sphere: C = 4πε₀R.
- (Eduqas) The field between the plates is uniform: E = V/d.

#### Explained

**C = Aε₀ε_r/d**, where A is the area of overlap of the plates, d the separation, ε₀ = 8.85 × 10⁻¹² F m⁻¹, and ε_r the relative permittivity of the material between the plates (1 for a vacuum, about 1 for air).

- Bigger plates: more room for charge, larger C.
- Closer plates: stronger attraction between opposite charges, so more charge for the same p.d.; larger C.

**Relative permittivity.** ε_r = ε/ε₀ = C with dielectric ÷ C without. Typical values: paper 3.7, glass 5 to 10, water about 80.

**Dielectric action.** Many dielectric molecules are polar: one end slightly positive, the other slightly negative. In the field between the plates, they rotate so that their negative ends face the positive plate and positive ends face the negative plate. These aligned dipoles produce their own electric field opposing the applied field, so the net field (and so p.d.) between the plates is reduced for the same charge. Since C = Q/V, the capacitance increases; or, at a fixed p.d. from the supply, more charge flows on to the plates. With an alternating field the molecules keep flipping, absorbing some energy.

**Isolated sphere** (OCR A). A sphere of radius R holding charge Q has potential V = Q/(4πε₀R), so C = Q/V = 4πε₀R.

#### Worked example

1. Two square plates of side 0.20 m are 1.0 mm apart, separated by a dielectric with ε_r = 4.0. Find C.

   C = 0.040 × 8.85 × 10⁻¹² × 4.0 / 1.0 × 10⁻³ = 1.4 × 10⁻⁹ F (1.4 nF).

2. (OCR A) Find the capacitance of the Earth treated as an isolated sphere (R = 6.4 × 10⁶ m).

   C = 4π × 8.85 × 10⁻¹² × 6.4 × 10⁶ = 7.1 × 10⁻⁴ F, only about 700 μF.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the diameter of circular plates as the radius.
- Saying the dielectric conducts.
- Saying polar molecules move to the plates.

#### Practical

**Relative permittivity** (suggested by AQA). Measure the capacitance of two plates with a capacitance meter, first with air between them (using spacers) and then with a sheet of dielectric of the same thickness. ε_r = C_dielectric/C_air. Varying d (with several thin sheets) and plotting C against 1/d gives a straight line of gradient ε₀ε_rA.

### 3.7.4.3 Energy stored by a capacitor

*Refs: AQA 3.7.4.3 · Edexcel 117 · OCR A 6.1.2(a) to (c) · OCR B 5.1.1(a)(ii), (b)(vi), (c)(v) · Eduqas C2.4(g). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The area under a graph of p.d. against charge is the energy stored.
- E = ½QV = ½CV² = ½Q²/C.
- (OCR A) Uses of capacitors as energy stores.

#### Explained

Charging a capacitor means pushing more charge onto a plate that already holds charge of the same sign, which takes more work as V rises. A graph of V against Q is a straight line through the origin (V = Q/C). The work done adding a small charge ΔQ at p.d. V is VΔQ, a thin strip under the line. The total energy is the area under the line: a triangle, so

**E = ½QV = ½CV² = ½Q²/C**

When a capacitor is charged by a battery, the battery supplies QV, but only ½QV is stored; the other half is dissipated as heat in the resistance of the circuit, whatever that resistance is.

**Uses** (OCR A 6.1.2(c)). Camera flash (stores energy slowly from a battery, releases it quickly); defibrillator; back-up power for memory and clocks; smoothing the output of a rectifier; storing energy from regenerative braking (supercapacitors); timing circuits.

#### Worked example

1. A defibrillator stores 150 J at 2.0 kV. Find C.

   C = 2E/V² = 300/(4.0 × 10⁶) = 7.5 × 10⁻⁵ F (75 μF).

2. A 2200 μF capacitor is charged to 12 V and discharged through a lamp in 0.50 s. Find the average power.

   E = ½ × 2200 × 10⁻⁶ × 144 = 0.158 J. P = 0.32 W.

#### Common mistakes

- Using E = QV (that is the energy from the supply, not the energy stored).
- Forgetting the square in ½CV².

### 3.7.4.4 Capacitor charge and discharge

*Refs: AQA 3.7.4.4, RP9 · Edexcel 118, 119, 120, CP11 · OCR A 6.1.3(a) to (e) · OCR B 5.1.1(a)(iii), (b)(i), (iv), (v), (c)(iii), (iv), (d)(iii) · Eduqas C2.4(i), (j). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Graphs of Q, V and I against time for charging and discharging through a resistor; meaning of gradients and areas.
- Time constant RC; finding it from data and graphs.
- Time to halve: T½ = 0.69RC.
- Discharge: Q = Q₀e^(−t/RC), and the matching equations for V and I.
- Charge: Q = Q₀(1 − e^(−t/RC)).
- AQA RP9: charge and discharge, with log-linear plotting to find RC.
- (Edexcel 120) ln Q = ln Q₀ − t/RC, and similar for I and V.

#### Explained

**Discharging.** A capacitor charged to V₀ discharges through a resistor R. The current is I = V/R = Q/(RC). As charge flows off, V falls, so I falls, so charge flows off more slowly. The rate of loss is proportional to the charge left:

ΔQ/Δt = −Q/(RC)

This gives exponential decay:

- Q = Q₀e^(−t/RC)
- V = V₀e^(−t/RC)
- I = I₀e^(−t/RC) (current flows in the opposite direction to charging)

**Time constant** τ = RC (seconds, since Ω × F = s). After one time constant, Q has fallen to e⁻¹ = 0.37 of its starting value. After 5RC the capacitor is effectively discharged (less than 1% left).

**Half-time.** Setting Q = Q₀/2: e^(−t/RC) = ½, t = RC ln 2 = 0.69RC.

**Constant-ratio property** (OCR A 6.1.3(e)). In equal time intervals, the charge falls by the same fraction. For example, every 0.69RC it halves.

**Charging.** A capacitor is charged through R from a supply of e.m.f. ε (V₀). At first there is no charge, so the whole p.d. is across R, and the current is largest: I₀ = ε/R. As charge builds up, V_C rises, the p.d. across R falls, and the current falls.

- Q = Q₀(1 − e^(−t/RC)), V_C = V₀(1 − e^(−t/RC)): rise towards a maximum.
- I = I₀e^(−t/RC): current decays exponentially, as in discharge.
- After one time constant, Q has reached 63% of its final value.

**Gradients and areas.**

- Gradient of a Q–t graph = current.
- Area under an I–t graph = charge.

**Log-linear plots.** Taking natural logs of V = V₀e^(−t/RC): ln V = ln V₀ − t/RC. A graph of ln V against t is a straight line with gradient −1/RC and intercept ln V₀. This tests that the decay is exponential and gives RC accurately.

**Iterative modelling** (OCR A 6.1.3(d), OCR B 5.1.1(c)(iv)). Start with Q₀. In each small time step Δt: ΔQ = −(Q/RC)Δt; new Q = Q + ΔQ. With Δt = 0.1RC each step multiplies Q by 0.9; after 10 steps (t = RC) Q = 0.9¹⁰Q₀ = 0.35Q₀, close to the exact 0.37Q₀. Smaller steps are more accurate.

#### Worked example

1. A 100 μF capacitor charged to 12 V discharges through 47 kΩ. Find the time constant, the p.d. after 10 s, and the time for the p.d. to fall to 3.0 V.

   RC = 100 × 10⁻⁶ × 47 000 = 4.7 s. V = 12e^(−10/4.7) = 12 × 0.119 = 1.43 V. 3.0 = 12e^(−t/4.7), t = 4.7 ln 4 = 6.5 s.

2. The same capacitor is charged from 0 V through the same resistor from a 12 V supply. Find the p.d. after 4.7 s and the initial current.

   V = 12(1 − e⁻¹) = 7.6 V. I₀ = 12/47 000 = 2.6 × 10⁻⁴ A.

3. A capacitor's p.d. falls from 10.0 V to 6.0 V in 2.0 s while discharging through 20 kΩ. Find C.

   6.0/10.0 = e^(−2.0/RC); ln 0.6 = −0.511 = −2.0/RC; RC = 3.92 s; C = 196 μF.

#### Common mistakes

- Using log base 10 instead of natural log.
- Saying the current is constant while a capacitor charges.
- Thinking a capacitor discharges completely after one time constant.
- Saying charge passes through the dielectric.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 9** (also Edexcel CP11, OCR A 6.1.3(a)(ii) PAG9, OCR B 5.1.1(d)(iii), Eduqas specified practical): investigate charging and discharging of a capacitor.

1. Set up a capacitor (e.g. 1000 μF) and a resistor (e.g. 47 kΩ) in series with a two-way switch, so the capacitor can be charged from a d.c. supply and then discharged through the resistor. Put a voltmeter across the capacitor (or a data logger, or an oscilloscope for short time constants), or a microammeter in series.
2. Charge fully; switch to discharge and start a stopwatch. Record V (or I) every 5 or 10 s. With a large RC (tens of seconds), readings can be taken by hand; a data logger is better for short RC.
3. Plot V against t (exponential decay curve). Plot ln V against t: straight line, gradient = −1/RC. Compare RC with the product of the labelled values.
4. Repeat for charging: plot V against t, or ln(V₀ − V) against t (gradient −1/RC).

Errors: electrolytic capacitors have tolerances of ±20%; the voltmeter's own resistance provides a parallel discharge path (use a high-resistance digital meter); reaction time when reading meters (film the meters, or use a data logger). Safety: electrolytic capacitors must be connected the right way round (they can explode if reversed); do not exceed the rated voltage.

**Energy stored** (Eduqas specified practical). Charge a capacitor to different p.d.s and discharge it through a joulemeter, or through a small motor lifting a mass, or measure the temperature rise of a resistor; compare with ½CV², or plot energy against V² (straight line, gradient C/2).

### Check questions

1. Define capacitance. (1 mark)
2. Explain how a dielectric increases the capacitance of a parallel-plate capacitor. (3 marks)
3. Show that the energy stored by a capacitor is ½QV. (3 marks)
4. Calculate the energy stored in a 4.7 mF capacitor charged to 25 V. (2 marks)
5. A capacitor discharges through a resistor. Sketch graphs of V against t and of ln V against t, and state how to find RC from each. (4 marks)
6. A 220 μF capacitor discharges through a 100 kΩ resistor from 9.0 V. Calculate the charge remaining after 30 s. (3 marks)
7. Explain why the charging current falls as a capacitor charges. (3 marks)
8. Describe how to determine the time constant of an RC circuit experimentally. (5 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Charge stored per unit p.d., C = Q/V (1).
2. Polar molecules in the dielectric align / rotate in the field (1); their field opposes the applied field (1); so less p.d. for the same charge (or more charge for the same p.d.), so C = Q/V increases (1).
3. V–Q graph is a straight line through origin (1); work to add charge ΔQ at p.d. V is VΔQ, so energy = area under graph (1); triangle area = ½QV (1).
4. E = ½CV² (1) = ½ × 4.7 × 10⁻³ × 625 = 1.5 J (1).
5. V–t: exponential decay curve; RC is time to fall to 0.37V₀ (or T½/0.69) (2); ln V–t: straight line with negative gradient (1); RC = −1/gradient (1).
6. RC = 22 s (1); Q₀ = 220 × 10⁻⁶ × 9.0 = 1.98 × 10⁻³ C (1); Q = 1.98 × 10⁻³ e^(−30/22) = 5.1 × 10⁻⁴ C (1).
7. As charge builds up the p.d. across the capacitor increases (1); p.d. across the resistor = ε − V_C falls (1); so current = (ε − V_C)/R falls (1).
8. Capacitor and resistor with two-way switch, charge from supply then discharge (1); voltmeter / data logger across capacitor (1); record V at regular time intervals (1); plot ln V against t (1); RC = −1/gradient (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/09-materials-stress-strain-and-young-modulus.md -->

## Materials: Hooke's law, stress, strain, Young modulus and material structure

How do solids stretch, and when do they break? This file covers Hooke's law and spring constants, elastic and plastic behaviour, force–extension and stress–strain graphs, elastic strain energy, the Young modulus and how to measure it (AQA required practical 4), and, for OCR B and Eduqas, how the structure of metals, ceramics, glasses and polymers explains their behaviour.

### Overview

AS & A for every board. Density is introduced here for AQA (3.4.2.1); density, pressure, upthrust and viscosity for Edexcel, OCR A and Eduqas are in file 30.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.4.2, RP4 · Edexcel 53 to 58, CP5 · OCR A 3.4.1, 3.4.2 · OCR B 3.2 · Eduqas C2.5.
- Young modulus of a wire: AQA RP4, Edexcel CP5, OCR A 3.4.2(d)(ii) (PAG2), OCR B 3.2(d)(ii), Eduqas specified practical.
- Force–extension of rubber bands, polythene strips and spring arrangements: OCR A 3.4.1(d)(ii), OCR B 3.2(d)(i), Eduqas specified practical.
- Material structure: OCR B 3.2(a)(iii) (metals, ceramics, polymers, dislocations, chain entanglement) and Eduqas C2.5(d) to (g) (crystalline, amorphous, polymeric; dislocations and their barriers; necking; crack propagation; toughened glass; rubber). AQA and Edexcel do not require structure.
- OCR B 3.2(a)(ii) asks for direct evidence of the size and spacing of particles (scanning tunnelling microscope, oil-drop experiment).
- Edexcel 55 uses compression as well as tension and names the yield point; OCR A 3.4.2(c) uses ultimate tensile strength.

### 3.4.2.1 Bulk properties of solids

*Refs: AQA 3.4.2.1 · Edexcel 53, 55, 56, 58 · OCR A 3.4.1(a) to (d), 3.4.2(a), (b), (c), (e), (f) · OCR B 3.2(a)(i) to (iii), (b)(i) to (iv), (c)(i), (d)(i) · Eduqas C2.5(a), (c) to (g). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Density ρ = m/V (AQA).
- Hooke's law F = kΔL; k is the stiffness or spring constant; the elastic limit.
- Tensile stress and tensile strain; breaking stress.
- Elastic strain energy = ½FΔL = area under a force–extension graph.
- Plastic behaviour, fracture and brittle behaviour on force–extension graphs.
- Energy conservation with elastic strain energy (spring energy → kinetic and gravitational potential energy).
- Simple stress–strain curves.
- Limit of proportionality, elastic limit, yield point, elastic and plastic deformation (Edexcel 55).
- (OCR B, Eduqas) How the microstructure of metals, glasses, ceramics and polymers explains their mechanical behaviour.

#### Explained

**Density** ρ = m/V, unit kg m⁻³. To find the density of a regular solid, measure dimensions with vernier calipers or a micrometer and mass with a balance; for an irregular solid, find its volume by displacement of water.

**Hooke's law.** The extension of a spring or wire is directly proportional to the applied force, up to the limit of proportionality: F = kΔL, where k (N m⁻¹) is the spring constant (stiffness). A stiffer spring has a larger k.

**Springs in combination** (OCR B 3.2(d)(i)).

- Series: 1/k_total = 1/k₁ + 1/k₂ (two identical springs in series: k/2; each stretches by the full force).
- Parallel: k_total = k₁ + k₂ (two identical: 2k; the force is shared).

**Key points on a force–extension (or stress–strain) graph for a ductile metal such as copper.**

| Point | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Limit of proportionality | End of the straight-line region; beyond it Hooke's law no longer applies |
| Elastic limit | The largest force after which the material still returns to its original length when the force is removed |
| Yield point | Where the material suddenly stretches a lot for little or no extra force; plastic flow begins |
| Ultimate tensile strength (UTS) | The maximum stress the material can take |
| Breaking point (fracture) | Where it breaks; breaking stress |

**Elastic and plastic deformation.**

- Elastic: the material returns to its original shape when the force is removed. Atoms are pulled slightly apart but bonds are not broken; energy stored is recovered.
- Plastic: permanent deformation. In metals, planes of atoms slip past one another. When unloaded, the material keeps a permanent extension; unloading follows a line parallel to the original elastic line.

**Material behaviour.**

| Term | Meaning | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Stiff | High Young modulus; small strain for a given stress | Steel, diamond |
| Strong | High breaking stress | Steel, Kevlar |
| Ductile | Can be drawn into wires; large plastic deformation before fracture | Copper |
| Brittle | Breaks with little or no plastic deformation | Glass, cast iron, ceramics |
| Tough | Absorbs a lot of energy before breaking (resists crack growth) | Rubber, many metals |
| Hard | Resists surface indentation or scratching | Diamond |
| Malleable | Can be hammered into shape | Gold |

**Graphs to recognise.**

- Ductile metal (copper): straight line, then curve, a long plastic region, necking (a narrow waist forms), then fracture.
- Brittle (glass, cast iron): straight line all the way to sudden fracture; no plastic region.
- Polymer (rubber): curved from the start (Hooke's law only roughly obeyed), large strain, low Young modulus. Loading and unloading curves differ (hysteresis): the area between them is energy transferred to thermal energy, which is why bouncing rubber warms up.
- Polythene: stretches plastically and a great deal; it "necks" and draws out.

**Elastic strain energy.** Work done stretching = area under the force–extension graph. Within Hooke's law: E = ½FΔL = ½k(ΔL)². For a non-linear graph, count squares. When a stretched catapult or spring is released, elastic strain energy becomes kinetic energy (and gravitational potential energy if it goes upwards): ½kx² = ½mv². Some energy is always dissipated.

Energy per unit volume = ½ × stress × strain (area under the stress–strain graph).

**Breaking stress** is the stress at which the material breaks. It is a property of the material, not of the sample's size.

**Transport design** (AQA). Crumple zones are designed to deform plastically, absorbing kinetic energy over a longer distance and time and reducing forces on passengers.

**Structure of materials** (OCR B 3.2(a)(iii), Eduqas C2.5(d) to (g)).

- *Crystalline solids* (metals, many ceramics): atoms in a regular repeating lattice.
- *Amorphous solids* (glass, many ceramics): atoms are disordered, with no long-range order.
- *Polymers*: long chain molecules, tangled together (rubber, polythene).

*Metals.* Metals are made of many small crystals (grains). The lattice contains dislocations: lines where an extra half-plane of atoms is squeezed in. Under stress, a dislocation can move through the lattice one row of bonds at a time, so planes slip past each other at much lower stress than if whole planes had to slide at once. This is why metals are ductile. Metals are strengthened by blocking dislocation movement: adding foreign atoms (alloying, e.g. carbon in steel), creating more dislocations that tangle (work-hardening), or making smaller grains (more grain boundaries). Ductile fracture: after yielding, the wire thins at one point (necking), stress there rises, and the wire tears apart.

*Glasses and ceramics.* No mobile dislocations, so they cannot deform plastically. They obey Hooke's law up to fracture. Tiny surface cracks concentrate stress at their tips; under tension the crack tip bonds break one after another and the crack runs through the material (brittle fracture by crack propagation). Breaking stress is raised by:

- reducing surface imperfections (thin glass fibres are very strong);
- putting the surface under compression, so cracks are squeezed shut (toughened glass, cooled so its surface is in compression; pre-stressed concrete, cast round stretched steel rods so the concrete is compressed). Under compression, brittle materials are strong.

*Rubber.* Long chains are coiled and tangled, held loosely by a few cross-links. Stretching straightens the chains against their thermal motion (which tends to coil them up again), so it is easy at first (low Young modulus, large strain). Once the chains are straight, the bonds within the chains must be stretched and it becomes much stiffer.

**Evidence for the size of particles** (OCR B 3.2(a)(ii)). A scanning tunnelling microscope (STM) images individual atoms on a surface, showing spacing of about 0.1 to 0.3 nm. In the oil-drop experiment, a drop of oil of known volume spreads on water into a layer one molecule thick: thickness = volume ÷ area, giving molecular size of order 10⁻⁹ m.

#### Worked example

1. A spring stretches 40 mm under a 6.0 N load. Find k and the energy stored.

   k = 6.0/0.040 = 150 N m⁻¹. E = ½ × 6.0 × 0.040 = 0.12 J.

2. A catapult with k = 400 N m⁻¹ is pulled back 0.15 m and fires a 0.020 kg stone. Find the maximum speed of the stone.

   ½kx² = ½ × 400 × 0.0225 = 4.5 J = ½ × 0.020 × v², v = 21 m s⁻¹.

3. Two springs of k = 200 N m⁻¹ are joined in series. A 4.0 N weight hangs from them. Find the total extension.

   k_total = 100 N m⁻¹. Extension = 0.040 m.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the elastic limit and the limit of proportionality are always the same point.
- Calling a material that breaks at a high stress "tough" (that is strong; tough means absorbs much energy).
- Using ½FΔL when the graph is not a straight line.
- Saying brittle materials are weak; glass is strong in compression.
- Saying dislocations make metals stronger (mobile dislocations make them weaker and ductile; tangled or pinned ones make them stronger).

### 3.4.2.2 The Young modulus

*Refs: AQA 3.4.2.2, RP4 · Edexcel 54, 56, 57, CP5 · OCR A 3.4.2(c), (d) · OCR B 3.2(a)(iv), (b)(ii), (c)(ii), (d)(ii) · Eduqas C2.5(b). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Tensile stress σ = F/A; tensile strain ε = ΔL/L; Young modulus E = stress/strain = FL/(AΔL).
- Use a stress–strain graph to find the Young modulus (gradient of the linear part).
- One simple method of measuring the Young modulus (AQA RP4).

#### Explained

- **Stress** = force ÷ cross-sectional area, σ = F/A. Unit Pa (N m⁻²).
- **Strain** = extension ÷ original length, ε = ΔL/L. No unit (a ratio).
- **Young modulus** E = σ/ε = FL/(AΔL). Unit Pa. It measures stiffness of the *material*, independent of the sample's dimensions (unlike k, which depends on the length and thickness of the sample). E only applies within the limit of proportionality.

| Material | E / GPa |
| --- | --- |
| Rubber | 0.01 to 0.1 |
| Polythene | 0.2 to 1 |
| Bone | about 15 |
| Copper | about 120 |
| Steel | about 200 |

Link with k: k = EA/L.

#### Worked example

1. A steel wire 2.0 m long, diameter 0.50 mm, stretches 1.2 mm under a 40 N load. Find E.

   A = π(0.25 × 10⁻³)² = 1.96 × 10⁻⁷ m². Stress = 40/1.96 × 10⁻⁷ = 2.04 × 10⁸ Pa. Strain = 1.2 × 10⁻³/2.0 = 6.0 × 10⁻⁴. E = 3.4 × 10¹¹ Pa. (Higher than typical steel; worth checking the readings.)

2. A copper wire (E = 1.2 × 10¹¹ Pa) 1.5 m long with A = 3.0 × 10⁻⁷ m² supports 25 N. Find the extension.

   ΔL = FL/(AE) = 25 × 1.5 / (3.0 × 10⁻⁷ × 1.2 × 10¹¹) = 1.04 × 10⁻³ m.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the diameter as the radius when finding A.
- Forgetting to convert mm to m (and mm² to m²).
- Giving strain a unit.
- Finding E from the curved part of the graph.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 4** (also Edexcel CP5, OCR A PAG2, OCR B 3.2(d)(ii), Eduqas specified practical): determine the Young modulus of a metal wire.

1. Clamp a long (2 to 3 m) thin wire at one end; run it over a pulley at the edge of the bench to a mass hanger. (Alternatively use Searle's apparatus with a reference wire and a vernier.)
2. Put a marker (a strip of tape or a pointer) on the wire near the pulley, above a fixed metre rule.
3. Measure the original length L from the clamp to the marker with a tape measure.
4. Measure the diameter with a micrometer at several points and orientations; average. Calculate A = πd²/4.
5. Add masses in equal steps (e.g. 100 g); record the marker position each time and find the extension. Also record readings while unloading, to check the elastic limit was not exceeded.
6. Plot stress against strain (or F against ΔL). E = gradient of the straight part (or gradient × L/A).

Why: a long wire gives a measurable extension; a thin wire gives a large stress for small loads. The biggest percentage uncertainty is usually in the diameter (squared) and the extension. Safety: wear goggles (the wire may snap and whip); put a box or cushion under the masses; keep feet clear.

**Force–extension of rubber, polythene and springs** (OCR A PAG2, OCR B 3.2(d)(i), Eduqas). Hang the sample from a clamp; add masses and measure length with a metre rule next to it, reading at eye level with a pointer. Measure during unloading too: rubber shows hysteresis, polythene shows large plastic deformation.

### Check questions

1. State Hooke's law. (2 marks)
2. Distinguish between elastic and plastic deformation. (2 marks)
3. Sketch stress–strain graphs for a ductile metal, a brittle material and rubber on the same axes. (3 marks)
4. A 0.80 m nylon fishing line of diameter 0.40 mm stretches 12 mm when a 30 N force acts. Calculate the Young modulus of nylon. (4 marks)
5. Describe how to measure the Young modulus of a copper wire. Include how you would minimise uncertainty. (6 marks)
6. A spring of k = 80 N m⁻¹ is compressed by 0.050 m and then releases a 0.010 kg ball vertically. Calculate the maximum height reached, ignoring losses. (3 marks)
7. (OCR B, Eduqas) Explain why metals are ductile but glass is brittle. (4 marks)
8. (Eduqas) Explain how pre-stressed concrete is made stronger in tension. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Extension is directly proportional to the force applied (1); up to the limit of proportionality (1).
2. Elastic: returns to original shape when force removed (1); plastic: permanent deformation / does not return (1).
3. Ductile: linear then long curved plastic region to fracture (1); brittle: straight line to fracture with no plastic region (1); rubber: curve, low gradient, large strain (1).
4. A = π(0.20 × 10⁻³)² = 1.26 × 10⁻⁷ m² (1); stress = 2.39 × 10⁸ Pa (1); strain = 0.015 (1); E = 1.6 × 10¹⁰ Pa (1).
5. Long wire clamped, over pulley, with masses (1); measure original length with tape measure (1); diameter with micrometer at several places, averaged (1); add masses, measure extension with marker and rule / vernier (1); plot stress against strain, E = gradient (1); long thin wire to give large extension; check unloading to ensure elastic limit not passed (1).
6. ½kx² = ½ × 80 × 0.0025 = 0.10 J (1); mgh = 0.10, h = 0.10/(0.010 × 9.81) (1) = 1.0 m (1).
7. Metals have dislocations (1) which move easily under stress, allowing planes to slip / plastic flow (1); glass is amorphous with no mobile dislocations (1); surface cracks propagate under tension so it fractures without plastic deformation (1).
8. Concrete cast around steel rods held in tension; when released the rods compress the concrete (1); a tensile load must first overcome this compression before cracks can open, so cracks do not propagate (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/14-gravitational-fields-and-orbits.md -->

## Fields, gravitational fields, gravitational potential and orbits

A field lets one object push or pull another without touching it. This file introduces force fields, then covers Newton's law of gravitation, gravitational field strength, gravitational potential and potential energy, equipotentials, graphs of g and V against distance, and the motion of planets and satellites (Kepler's laws, energy of orbits, escape velocity, geostationary orbits).

### Overview

A-level only for every board. Electric fields, and the side-by-side comparison of electric and gravitational fields, are in file 15. Uniform-field energy changes mgh are in file 08. Galaxy rotation, binary stars and the radial-velocity method (Eduqas C2.8(e) to (m)) are in file 21.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.7.1, 3.7.2 · Edexcel 174 to 180 · OCR A 5.4 · OCR B 5.1.2 · Eduqas C2.6, C2.8(a) to (d).
- Kepler's three laws by name: OCR A 5.4.3(a), Eduqas C2.8(a). AQA and Edexcel only need T² ∝ r³ (derived).
- OCR B 5.1.2(b)(ii), (iii) stresses areas and gradients: potential difference = area under g–r; change in potential energy = area under force–distance; force = −gradient of energy–distance.
- OCR A 5.4.4(c), (d) and Edexcel 178: gravitational potential energy E = −GMm/r. AQA uses ΔW = mΔV.
- Eduqas C2.6(e): net potential and resultant field from several point masses (or charges).
- Escape velocity: AQA, OCR A 5.4.4(e). Not named by Edexcel, OCR B or Eduqas (but follows from the energy ideas).

### 3.7.1 Fields

*Refs: AQA 3.7.1 · Edexcel 174 · OCR A 5.4.1(a), (e) · Eduqas C2.6(a), (c). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- A force field is a region in which a body experiences a non-contact force.
- A force field can be represented as a vector; its direction is found by inspection.
- Force fields arise from mass (gravitational), static charge (electric) and moving charges (magnetic).
- Similarities between gravitational and electrostatic forces: both are inverse-square laws, both use field lines, potential and equipotential surfaces. Difference: masses always attract; charges can attract or repel.

#### Explained

A field describes how a force varies through space. At any point we define field strength as force per unit of the property that feels it: per kilogram for gravity, per coulomb for electric fields. Field lines show the direction of the force on a test object; their closeness shows the field strength. A uniform field has parallel, equally spaced lines; a radial field has lines pointing to or from a centre.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying gravity can be repulsive.
- Drawing field lines that cross.

### 3.7.2.1 Newton's law

*Refs: AQA 3.7.2.1 · Edexcel 176 · OCR A 5.4.2(a) · OCR B 5.1.2(c)(iv) · Eduqas C2.8(b). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Gravity is a universal attractive force between all matter.
- F = Gm₁m₂/r² for point masses, where G = 6.67 × 10⁻¹¹ N m² kg⁻².
- (OCR A, Eduqas) Spherical bodies act as point masses at their centres (outside them).

#### Explained

Every pair of masses attracts with a force proportional to the product of the masses and inversely proportional to the square of the distance between their centres. Double the distance: force falls to a quarter. The force on each mass is the same size (Newton's third law). G is tiny, so the force between everyday objects is negligible; gravity dominates only when at least one mass is huge.

A uniform sphere behaves, for points outside it, as if all its mass were at its centre (OCR A 5.4.1(b), Eduqas C2.6(b)), so r is measured from the centre.

#### Worked example

1. Estimate the gravitational force between two 70 kg people 1.0 m apart.

   F = 6.67 × 10⁻¹¹ × 70 × 70 / 1.0² = 3.3 × 10⁻⁷ N, utterly negligible.

2. Find the force between the Earth (6.0 × 10²⁴ kg) and the Moon (7.3 × 10²² kg), 3.8 × 10⁸ m apart.

   F = 6.67 × 10⁻¹¹ × 6.0 × 10²⁴ × 7.3 × 10²² / (3.8 × 10⁸)² = 2.0 × 10²⁰ N.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the distance from the surface instead of from the centre.
- Forgetting to square r.

### 3.7.2.2 Gravitational field strength

*Refs: AQA 3.7.2.2 · Edexcel 175, 177 · OCR A 5.4.1(b), (c), (d), 5.4.2(b), (c) · OCR B 5.1.2(a)(ii), (iii), (c)(iv) · Eduqas C2.6(b), (c), (e). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Gravitational field lines.
- g = F/m (force per unit mass); g = GM/r² in a radial field.
- (OCR A) The field near the Earth's surface is approximately uniform.

#### Explained

**Definition.** Gravitational field strength at a point is the force per unit mass on a small test mass placed there: g = F/m, unit N kg⁻¹ (equivalent to m s⁻²). It is a vector, directed towards the mass.

**Radial field.** Dividing F = GMm/r² by m: g = GM/r². Field lines point radially inwards towards the centre of the mass.

**Uniform field.** Near the Earth's surface over small height changes, g is nearly constant (9.81 N kg⁻¹): parallel, equally spaced field lines pointing down.

**Graph of g against r** for a planet of radius R: outside, g = GM/r², falling as 1/r². (Inside a uniform planet g rises linearly from zero at the centre to g_surface at R; not required by most boards.)

**Combining fields** (Eduqas C2.6(e)). Field strength is a vector: add the contributions from each mass as vectors. Between the Earth and the Moon there is a point where their fields cancel: GM_E/x² = GM_M/(d − x)².

**Deriving the Earth's mass.** g = GM/R², so M = gR²/G = 9.81 × (6.37 × 10⁶)²/6.67 × 10⁻¹¹ = 5.97 × 10²⁴ kg.

#### Worked example

1. Find g at the height of the International Space Station, 400 km above the Earth's surface (R = 6.37 × 10⁶ m, M = 5.97 × 10²⁴ kg).

   r = 6.77 × 10⁶ m. g = 6.67 × 10⁻¹¹ × 5.97 × 10²⁴/(6.77 × 10⁶)² = 8.69 N kg⁻¹. (Astronauts float because they are in free fall, not because g is zero.)

2. At what distance from the Earth's centre is g one ninth of its surface value?

   g ∝ 1/r², so r = 3R = 1.9 × 10⁷ m.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying there is no gravity on the ISS.
- Treating g as a constant at large distances.

### 3.7.2.3 Gravitational potential

*Refs: AQA 3.7.2.3 · Edexcel 178 · OCR A 5.4.4(a) to (d) · OCR B 5.1.2(a)(i), (iii), (b)(i) to (iv), (c)(i), (ii), (v), (vi) · Eduqas C2.6(d), (e), (f). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Gravitational potential at a point is the work done per unit mass in bringing a small mass from infinity to that point; it is zero at infinity.
- Gravitational potential difference; work done ΔW = mΔV.
- Equipotential surfaces; no work is done moving along one.
- V = −GM/r in a radial field; the significance of the negative sign.
- Graphs of g and V against r; g = −ΔV/Δr; ΔV = area under the g–r graph.
- (Edexcel, OCR A, OCR B) Gravitational potential energy E_p = mV = −GMm/r; force–distance and energy–distance graphs.

#### Explained

**Gravitational potential** V at a point: the work done per unit mass to bring a small test mass from infinity to that point. Unit J kg⁻¹.

V = −GM/r

- Zero at infinity (by definition).
- Negative everywhere else, because gravity is attractive: work is done *by* the field as a mass falls in from infinity, so energy is released, not supplied. You would have to do positive work to take it back out to infinity.
- Increases (becomes less negative) with distance.

**Potential difference.** Moving mass m between two points with potential difference ΔV needs work ΔW = mΔV. Near the Earth's surface ΔV = gΔh, giving ΔW = mgΔh (Eduqas C2.6(f), OCR B 5.1.2(c)(i)).

**Gravitational potential energy** (Edexcel, OCR A, OCR B): E_p = mV = −GMm/r. Change in E_p between r₁ and r₂: ΔE_p = GMm(1/r₁ − 1/r₂).

**Equipotentials.** Surfaces of equal potential. In a radial field they are spheres around the mass; near the Earth's surface they are horizontal planes. Equipotentials are always perpendicular to field lines. Moving along an equipotential involves no work (no change in potential, and the force is perpendicular to the motion). Equipotentials for equal steps of V get further apart as r increases.

**Field and potential.** g = −ΔV/Δr: the field strength is minus the gradient of the potential–distance graph. Conversely, the area under a g–r graph between two radii is the potential difference.

Similarly (OCR B 5.1.2(b)(ii), (iii)): the area under a force–distance graph is the change in potential energy, and force = minus the gradient of the potential energy–distance graph.

**Graphs.**

- V against r: −1/r curve, negative, approaching zero from below as r → ∞, steepest near the surface.
- g against r: 1/r² curve, positive, falling faster than V.

#### Worked example

1. Find the gravitational potential at the Earth's surface and the energy needed to lift a 1000 kg satellite from the surface to a height of 2.0 × 10⁶ m.

   V_surface = −GM/R = −6.67 × 10⁻¹¹ × 5.97 × 10²⁴/6.37 × 10⁶ = −6.25 × 10⁷ J kg⁻¹. At r = 8.37 × 10⁶ m: V = −4.76 × 10⁷ J kg⁻¹. ΔV = 1.49 × 10⁷ J kg⁻¹. Energy = mΔV = 1.49 × 10¹⁰ J. (Using mgh would give 1.96 × 10¹⁰ J, too much, because g falls with height.)

2. At a point the potential changes from −5.0 × 10⁷ to −4.8 × 10⁷ J kg⁻¹ over 100 km. Estimate g there.

   g = −ΔV/Δr = −(2.0 × 10⁶)/(1.0 × 10⁵), magnitude 20 N kg⁻¹ (towards decreasing r).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying potential is zero at the surface of the Earth.
- Forgetting the minus sign in V = −GM/r, or thinking a more negative V is higher.
- Using mgh for large distances.
- Saying work is done moving a satellite around a circular orbit.

### 3.7.2.4 Orbits of planets and satellites

*Refs: AQA 3.7.2.4 · Edexcel 180 · OCR A 5.4.3(a) to (e), 5.4.4(e) · OCR B 5.1.2(a)(v) · Eduqas C2.8(a) to (d). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Orbital period and speed related to orbital radius; derivation of T² ∝ r³.
- Energy of an orbiting satellite: kinetic, potential and total.
- Escape velocity.
- Synchronous orbits; low orbits and geostationary orbits, including the plane and radius of a geostationary orbit.
- (OCR A, Eduqas) Kepler's three laws; using orbital data to find the mass of the central body.

#### Explained

**Kepler's laws** (OCR A, Eduqas).

1. Planets move in ellipses with the Sun at one focus.
2. A line from the Sun to a planet sweeps out equal areas in equal times (so planets move faster when closer to the Sun).
3. The square of the orbital period is proportional to the cube of the mean distance from the Sun: T² ∝ r³.

**Circular orbits.** Gravity provides the centripetal force:

GMm/r² = mv²/r → **v = √(GM/r)**

Closer orbits are faster. With v = 2πr/T:

GM/r² = 4π²r/T² → **T² = (4π²/GM)r³**

This is Kepler's third law, and the constant depends only on the central mass. So measuring T and r for a satellite (or moon) gives the central mass: M = 4π²r³/(GT²) (Eduqas C2.8(d)). Plotting log T against log r gives a straight line of gradient 1.5.

**Energy in orbit.**

- Kinetic: E_k = ½mv² = GMm/2r.
- Potential: E_p = −GMm/r.
- Total: E = E_k + E_p = −GMm/2r.

A satellite in a lower orbit has more kinetic energy but less (more negative) total energy. To move to a higher orbit, energy must be supplied (the total becomes less negative), but the satellite ends up slower. Atmospheric drag on a low satellite removes energy, so it spirals inwards and speeds up.

**Escape velocity.** The minimum speed an object needs at the surface to escape to infinity (with no further propulsion). At infinity E_p = 0 and E_k ≥ 0, so at launch ½mv² − GMm/R ≥ 0:

**v_esc = √(2GM/R)** (11.2 km s⁻¹ for the Earth; independent of the mass of the object).

**Synchronous and geostationary orbits.** A synchronous orbit has a period equal to the rotation period of the body it orbits. A geostationary satellite:

- orbits above the equator (equatorial plane);
- has a period of 24 hours (strictly one sidereal day, 23 h 56 min);
- moves west to east, the same direction as the Earth's rotation;
- so it stays above the same point on the Earth.

Radius: r³ = GMT²/4π² → r = 4.2 × 10⁷ m (about 36 000 km above the surface). Uses: communications and TV broadcasting (fixed dishes), some weather satellites. Disadvantages: signal delay (round trip about 0.24 s), poor coverage near the poles.

**Low (polar) orbits.** A few hundred km up, period about 90 minutes. A satellite passing over the poles sees the whole Earth's surface as the Earth turns underneath. Uses: imaging, mapping, weather forecasting, spying, GPS (medium orbits). They are closer, so give higher-resolution images, need less powerful transmitters, but each is only in view for a short time, so many are needed for continuous coverage.

#### Worked example

1. Find the orbital speed and period of a satellite 800 km above the Earth.

   r = 7.17 × 10⁶ m. v = √(GM/r) = √(6.67 × 10⁻¹¹ × 5.97 × 10²⁴/7.17 × 10⁶) = 7.45 × 10³ m s⁻¹. T = 2πr/v = 6050 s (101 min).

2. Show that the radius of a geostationary orbit is about 4.2 × 10⁷ m.

   r³ = GMT²/4π² = 6.67 × 10⁻¹¹ × 5.97 × 10²⁴ × (86 400)²/39.48 = 7.53 × 10²² m³. r = 4.22 × 10⁷ m.

3. Jupiter's moon Io orbits at 4.22 × 10⁸ m with a period of 1.77 days. Find the mass of Jupiter.

   T = 1.53 × 10⁵ s. M = 4π²r³/(GT²) = 39.48 × 7.52 × 10²⁵ / (6.67 × 10⁻¹¹ × 2.34 × 10¹⁰) = 1.90 × 10²⁷ kg.

4. Find the escape velocity from the Moon (M = 7.35 × 10²² kg, R = 1.74 × 10⁶ m).

   v = √(2 × 6.67 × 10⁻¹¹ × 7.35 × 10²²/1.74 × 10⁶) = 2.37 × 10³ m s⁻¹.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a satellite needs fuel to stay in a circular orbit.
- Saying a geostationary satellite can be above London.
- Using the height above the surface as r.
- Saying escape velocity depends on the mass of the rocket.

### Check questions

1. Define gravitational field strength. (1 mark)
2. Mars has radius 3.4 × 10⁶ m and surface gravity 3.7 N kg⁻¹. Calculate its mass. (2 marks)
3. Define gravitational potential at a point and explain why it is negative. (3 marks)
4. Sketch graphs of g and V against r for points outside a planet of radius R. (3 marks)
5. Derive the relationship between the period and radius of a circular orbit. (4 marks)
6. Calculate the total energy of a 500 kg satellite in a circular orbit of radius 7.0 × 10⁶ m around the Earth. (2 marks)
7. State three features of a geostationary orbit. (3 marks)
8. Explain why a satellite in a low orbit slowly loses height and speeds up. (3 marks)
9. Show that escape velocity is independent of the mass of the escaping object and calculate it for the Earth. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Force per unit mass on a small test mass placed at that point (1).
2. M = gR²/G (1) = 3.7 × (3.4 × 10⁶)²/6.67 × 10⁻¹¹ = 6.4 × 10²³ kg (1).
3. Work done per unit mass in bringing a small mass from infinity to the point (1); zero at infinity (1); gravity attracts, so work is done by the field / energy released as mass moves in, so potential is less than zero (1).
4. g: positive, falls as 1/r² from the surface (1); V: negative, rises towards zero as 1/r (1); both start at r = R, g curve falls more steeply (1).
5. GMm/r² = mv²/r (1); v = 2πr/T (1); GM/r² = 4π²r/T² (1); T² = (4π²/GM)r³ (1).
6. E = −GMm/2r (1) = −6.67 × 10⁻¹¹ × 5.97 × 10²⁴ × 500/(1.4 × 10⁷) = −1.4 × 10¹⁰ J (1).
7. Any three: period 24 h; above equator / equatorial plane; same direction as Earth's rotation (west to east); stays above the same point; radius 4.2 × 10⁷ m.
8. Atmospheric drag does work against satellite / removes energy (1); total energy becomes more negative, so orbit radius decreases (1); v = √(GM/r), so speed increases as r decreases (1).
9. ½mv² = GMm/R; m cancels (1); v = √(2GM/R) (1) = √(2 × 6.67 × 10⁻¹¹ × 5.97 × 10²⁴/6.37 × 10⁶) = 1.1 × 10⁴ m s⁻¹ (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/15-electric-fields-and-potential.md -->

## Electric fields: Coulomb's law, field strength, potential, and comparison with gravity

Charges exert forces on each other through electric fields. This file covers Coulomb's law, electric field strength in radial and uniform fields, the path of a charged particle fired into a uniform field, electric potential and potential energy, equipotentials, graphs of E and V against distance, and a side-by-side comparison of electric and gravitational fields.

### Overview

A-level only for every board. Capacitors, including the parallel-plate capacitor and permittivity, are in file 16. Magnetic forces on moving charges are in file 17. Millikan's oil-drop experiment in full (AQA option 3.12.1.4) is in file 26.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.7.3 · Edexcel 108 to 115, 179 · OCR A 6.2 (except capacitance points, which are in file 16) · OCR B 6.1.2 · Eduqas C2.6.
- OCR B writes Coulomb's law with k = 1/(4πε₀): F = kqQ/r². Same law.
- OCR B 6.1.2(a)(iv) asks for evidence that charge is discrete (Millikan).
- Field-mapping practical with conducting paper or an electrolytic tank: suggested by AQA.
- OCR A 6.2.3(c) and AQA: motion of a charged particle in a uniform electric field.

### 3.7.3.1 Coulomb's law

*Refs: AQA 3.7.3.1 · Edexcel 110 · OCR A 6.2.1(b), 6.2.2(a) · OCR B 6.1.2(a)(ii), (c)(i). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Force between point charges in a vacuum: F = Q₁Q₂/(4πε₀r²), where ε₀ is the permittivity of free space (8.85 × 10⁻¹² F m⁻¹).
- Air can be treated as a vacuum.
- A charged sphere behaves as if its charge were at its centre.
- Compare the sizes of the gravitational and electrostatic forces between subatomic particles.

#### Explained

The force between two point charges is proportional to the product of the charges and inversely proportional to the square of their separation. Like charges repel (F positive), unlike attract (F negative). 1/(4πε₀) = 8.99 × 10⁹ N m² C⁻² (often written k).

**Comparing forces.** For two protons any distance apart, the ratio of electrostatic to gravitational force is (e²/4πε₀)/(Gm_p²) = (8.99 × 10⁹ × 2.56 × 10⁻³⁸)/(6.67 × 10⁻¹¹ × 2.80 × 10⁻⁵⁴) ≈ 1.2 × 10³⁶. Gravity is negligible between particles; it only dominates on large scales because large objects are nearly electrically neutral.

#### Worked example

1. Find the force between the electron and proton in hydrogen, 5.3 × 10⁻¹¹ m apart.

   F = 8.99 × 10⁹ × (1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹)²/(5.3 × 10⁻¹¹)² = 8.2 × 10⁻⁸ N (attractive).

2. Two small spheres carry +40 nC and −25 nC, 0.10 m apart. Find the force.

   F = 8.99 × 10⁹ × 40 × 10⁻⁹ × 25 × 10⁻⁹ / 0.010 = 9.0 × 10⁻⁴ N attractive.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting to square r.
- Using nC as C.

### 3.7.3.2 Electric field strength

*Refs: AQA 3.7.3.2 · Edexcel 108, 109, 111, 113, 115 · OCR A 6.2.1(a) to (d), 6.2.2(b), 6.2.3(a), (c) · OCR B 6.1.2(a)(i), (ii), (b)(iv), (c)(i), (ii) · Eduqas C2.6(a), (c), (e). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Electric field lines.
- E = F/Q: force per unit (positive) charge.
- Uniform field: E = V/d, derived from the work done moving charge between plates: Fd = QΔV.
- The path of a charged particle entering a uniform field at right angles.
- Radial field: E = Q/(4πε₀r²).

#### Explained

**Definition.** Electric field strength at a point is the force per unit charge on a small positive test charge placed there: E = F/Q. Units N C⁻¹ or V m⁻¹ (the same). It is a vector, pointing in the direction a positive charge would be pushed.

**Field lines.** Start on positive charges, end on negative charges, never cross; closer lines mean a stronger field. Radial field round a point charge: lines point straight out (positive) or in (negative). Two opposite charges: lines curve from + to −. Two like charges: a neutral point midway with no field.

**Uniform field.** Between two parallel plates with p.d. V and separation d, the field is uniform (except near the edges): parallel, equally spaced lines from + to −.

Derivation: moving charge Q from one plate to the other needs work W = QV. The force is constant, so W = Fd. So Fd = QV and E = F/Q = V/d.

**Radial field.** Dividing Coulomb's law by the test charge: E = Q/(4πε₀r²).

**Combining fields.** E is a vector: add contributions from each charge as vectors (Eduqas C2.6(e)).

**Charged particle in a uniform field.** A particle of charge q and mass m enters midway between plates at speed u, at right angles to the field. This is just like a projectile:

- Along the plates: no force, constant velocity u. Time between plates t = L/u (L = plate length).
- Across the plates: constant force F = qE = qV/d, so constant acceleration a = qE/m.
- Deflection y = ½at² = ½(qE/m)(L/u)².
- The path is a parabola. After leaving the plates it travels in a straight line.

Electrons are deflected towards the positive plate; positive ions towards the negative. Gravity is negligible for electrons and ions.

**Accelerating a particle** (OCR A 4.2.2(e), Edexcel 132). A charge accelerated from rest through p.d. V gains kinetic energy qV = ½mv².

#### Worked example

1. Find E between plates 4.0 mm apart with a p.d. of 600 V, and the force on an electron there.

   E = 600/0.0040 = 1.5 × 10⁵ V m⁻¹. F = eE = 2.4 × 10⁻¹⁴ N.

2. The p.d. is reduced to 60 V. An electron enters midway between the plates at 3.0 × 10⁷ m s⁻¹, parallel to them. The plates are 2.0 cm long. Find its deflection as it leaves.

   E = 60/0.0040 = 1.5 × 10⁴ V m⁻¹. a = eE/m = 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ × 1.5 × 10⁴/9.11 × 10⁻³¹ = 2.63 × 10¹⁵ m s⁻². t = 0.020/3.0 × 10⁷ = 6.67 × 10⁻¹⁰ s. y = ½ × 2.63 × 10¹⁵ × (6.67 × 10⁻¹⁰)² = 5.9 × 10⁻⁴ m (0.59 mm towards the positive plate). This is less than the 2.0 mm half-gap, so the electron leaves the plates. (At 600 V it would hit the positive plate.)

3. Find the field strength 2.0 cm from a +5.0 nC point charge.

   E = 8.99 × 10⁹ × 5.0 × 10⁻⁹ / (0.020)² = 1.1 × 10⁵ N C⁻¹, pointing away from the charge.

#### Common mistakes

- Using E = V/d for a radial field.
- Saying the horizontal velocity of the particle changes inside the plates.
- Getting the direction of force on an electron wrong (it is opposite to E).

#### Practical

**Mapping fields** (suggested by AQA). Place electrodes on conducting (Teledeltos) paper connected to a low-voltage supply. Use a voltmeter probe to find points at the same potential and mark them; these join into equipotential lines. Field lines are drawn at right angles to them. Parallel bar electrodes give straight, equally spaced equipotentials (uniform field); a point and a ring give circles (radial field).

### 3.7.3.3 Electric potential

*Refs: AQA 3.7.3.3 · Edexcel 112, 114, 115, 179 · OCR A 6.2.2(c), (d), 6.2.4(a), (b), (d), (e) · OCR B 6.1.2(a)(iii), (vi), (b)(i) to (iv), (c)(ii), (iii) · Eduqas C2.6(a), (d), (e). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Absolute electric potential: the work done per unit positive charge in bringing a small charge from infinity to the point; zero at infinity. Electric potential difference.
- ΔW = QΔV.
- Equipotential surfaces; no work is done moving a charge along one.
- V = Q/(4πε₀r) in a radial field.
- Graphs of E and V against r; E = −ΔV/Δr; ΔV = area under the E–r graph.
- (OCR A, OCR B) Electric potential energy = Qq/(4πε₀r); force–distance and energy–distance graphs.
- Similarities and differences between electric and gravitational fields.

#### Explained

**Potential.** V at a point = work done per unit positive charge bringing a small test charge from infinity to that point. Unit J C⁻¹ = V.

V = Q/(4πε₀r)

- Around a positive charge, V is positive (work must be done against repulsion to bring a positive test charge in) and falls with distance.
- Around a negative charge, V is negative.
- Potential is a scalar: add the potentials from several charges algebraically (with signs).

**Potential energy** (OCR A 6.2.4(e), OCR B). E_p = qV = Qq/(4πε₀r). Positive for like charges (they have to be pushed together), negative for unlike charges.

**Work.** Moving charge Q through p.d. ΔV: ΔW = QΔV.

**Equipotentials.** Surfaces of constant V; perpendicular to field lines. Spheres round a point charge; planes parallel to the plates in a uniform field. No work is done moving along one.

**Field and potential gradient.** E = −ΔV/Δr. The field points from high to low potential, and its strength is the steepness of the potential–distance graph. In a uniform field this gives E = V/d. The area under an E–r graph between two radii gives the potential difference between them.

**Graphs** (positive point charge): E against r falls as 1/r²; V against r falls as 1/r (both positive). For a negative charge V is negative and rises towards zero.

**Comparison of fields** (AQA 3.7.1, Edexcel 179, OCR A 6.2.2(c), OCR B 6.1.2(a)(vi), Eduqas C2.6(a)).

| | Gravitational | Electric |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Acts on | Mass | Charge |
| Force law | F = Gm₁m₂/r² | F = Q₁Q₂/(4πε₀r²) |
| Nature | Always attractive | Attractive or repulsive |
| Field strength | g = F/m = GM/r² | E = F/Q = Q/(4πε₀r²) |
| Potential | V = −GM/r (always negative) | V = Q/(4πε₀r) (sign of Q) |
| Field and potential | g = −ΔV/Δr | E = −ΔV/Δr |
| Uniform field | Near a planet's surface | Between parallel plates |
| Strength | Very weak | Very strong (about 10³⁶ times between protons) |
| Shielding | Cannot be shielded | Can be shielded by conductors |

Both are inverse-square laws, both use field lines and equipotentials, and potential is zero at infinity in both.

**Discreteness of charge** (OCR B 6.1.2(a)(iv)). In Millikan's experiment, tiny charged oil drops between horizontal plates were held still by adjusting the p.d.: qV/d = mg. The charges found were always whole-number multiples of 1.6 × 10⁻¹⁹ C, showing charge comes in units of e (details in file 26).

#### Worked example

1. Find the potential 0.050 m from a +2.0 μC charge, and the work needed to bring a +3.0 nC charge from infinity to that point.

   V = 8.99 × 10⁹ × 2.0 × 10⁻⁶/0.050 = 3.6 × 10⁵ V. W = QV = 3.0 × 10⁻⁹ × 3.6 × 10⁵ = 1.1 × 10⁻³ J.

2. An alpha particle (charge +2e) is fired at a gold nucleus (+79e). Find its closest approach if its initial kinetic energy is 5.0 MeV.

   At closest approach, all kinetic energy becomes potential energy: 5.0 × 1.60 × 10⁻¹³ = 8.99 × 10⁹ × 2 × 79 × (1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹)²/r. r = 3.64 × 10⁻²⁶/8.0 × 10⁻¹³ = 4.5 × 10⁻¹⁴ m. (This puts an upper limit on the size of the nucleus; see file 18.)

3. Two charges, +4.0 nC at x = 0 and −4.0 nC at x = 0.20 m. Find the potential and the field at x = 0.10 m.

   V = k(4 − 4) × 10⁻⁹/0.10 = 0. E: each contributes 8.99 × 10⁹ × 4.0 × 10⁻⁹/0.01 = 3600 N C⁻¹, both pointing towards the negative charge, so E = 7200 N C⁻¹ in the +x direction. (Zero potential does not mean zero field.)

#### Common mistakes

- Adding potentials as vectors, or field strengths as scalars.
- Saying zero potential means zero field.
- Forgetting that V can be negative near a negative charge.
- Mixing up potential (J C⁻¹) and potential energy (J).

### Check questions

1. Define electric field strength. (1 mark)
2. Calculate the ratio of electrostatic to gravitational force between an electron and a proton. (3 marks)
3. Derive E = V/d for a uniform field. (2 marks)
4. An electron is accelerated through 2000 V and then enters the gap between two plates 2.0 cm apart, parallel to them. The plates are 8.0 cm long with a p.d. of 150 V. Calculate the vertical deflection as it leaves. (5 marks)
5. Define electric potential at a point. (2 marks)
6. Sketch the equipotentials and field lines around an isolated negative point charge. (2 marks)
7. A proton is held 1.0 × 10⁻¹⁴ m from another proton and released. Calculate its maximum speed. (3 marks)
8. Give two similarities and two differences between gravitational and electric fields. (4 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Force per unit charge on a small positive test charge at that point (1).
2. F_e = ke²/r², F_g = Gm_em_p/r² (1); ratio = ke²/(Gm_em_p) (1) = 8.99 × 10⁹ × 2.56 × 10⁻³⁸/(6.67 × 10⁻¹¹ × 9.11 × 10⁻³¹ × 1.67 × 10⁻²⁷) = 2.3 × 10³⁹ (1).
3. Work to move Q between plates = QV = Fd (1); E = F/Q = V/d (1).
4. ½mu² = eV gives u = √(2 × 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ × 2000/9.11 × 10⁻³¹) = 2.65 × 10⁷ m s⁻¹ (1); E = 150/0.020 = 7500 V m⁻¹; a = eE/m = 1.32 × 10¹⁵ m s⁻² (1); t = 0.080/2.65 × 10⁷ = 3.02 × 10⁻⁹ s (1); y = ½at² (1) = 6.0 × 10⁻³ m (6.0 mm, less than the 10 mm half-gap, so it leaves) (1).
5. Work done per unit positive charge (1); to bring a small test charge from infinity to the point (1).
6. Concentric circles for equipotentials, closer together near the charge (1); radial field lines pointing inwards, perpendicular to equipotentials (1).
7. E_p = ke²/r = 2.30 × 10⁻¹⁴ J (1); shared equally: each proton gets 1.15 × 10⁻¹⁴ J (1); v = √(2 × 1.15 × 10⁻¹⁴/1.67 × 10⁻²⁷) = 3.7 × 10⁶ m s⁻¹ (1). (Accept 5.2 × 10⁶ m s⁻¹ if other proton held fixed.)
8. Similarities (any two): inverse square laws; field lines/equipotentials; potential zero at infinity; E or g = −gradient of potential. Differences (any two): gravity only attracts but electric can repel; acts on mass vs charge; gravitational potential always negative; electric force much stronger / can be shielded.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/20-telescopes-and-stars.md -->

## Astrophysics: telescopes, magnitudes, black-body radiation, spectral classes, the HR diagram and stellar evolution

How do we learn about stars we can never visit? This file covers refracting and reflecting telescopes, radio and other telescopes, resolution and collecting power, CCDs, the magnitude scale and distances in parsecs, black-body radiation with Wien's and Stefan's laws, stellar spectral classes, the Hertzsprung–Russell diagram, how stars form and evolve, and supernovae, neutron stars and black holes.

### Overview

AQA option 3.9 (A-level only). Also core content for Edexcel (Topic 9 black body, Topic 10 Space), OCR A (5.5) and Eduqas (C2.7). Line spectra and energy levels are in file 03; the diffraction grating is in file 05. Distances (parallax, standard candles), Doppler shift, Hubble's law, the Big Bang and exoplanets are in file 21. Lenses in general (Edexcel) are in file 22.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.9.1, 3.9.2 · Edexcel 153 to 156, 159, 160 · OCR A 5.5.1, 5.5.2(i) to (k) · OCR B 5.1.3(b)(i) · Eduqas C2.7.
- Telescopes, magnitudes, spectral classes and Schwarzschild radius: AQA only.
- Stellar evolution in detail (low-mass star to white dwarf; electron degeneracy pressure; Chandrasekhar limit; massive star to supernova, neutron star or black hole): OCR A 5.5.1(b) to (f). Edexcel 160 links the HR diagram to the life cycle. AQA: path of a Sun-like star on the HR diagram.
- Edexcel 156 and Eduqas C2.7(d): intensity I = L/(4πd²). AQA uses the inverse-square law with assumptions.
- Eduqas C2.7(e): multiwavelength astronomy (why we observe in different parts of the spectrum).
- OCR B 5.1.3(b)(i): using logarithmic scales for distances, sizes, masses, energies and brightness.
- OCR A 5.5.1(a): definitions of planets, planetary satellites, comets, solar systems, galaxies and the universe.

### 3.9.1.1 Astronomical telescope consisting of two converging lenses

*Refs: AQA 3.9.1.1. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- A ray diagram showing image formation in normal adjustment.
- Angular magnification in normal adjustment M = (angle subtended by image at eye) ÷ (angle subtended by object at unaided eye) = f_o/f_e.
- The focal lengths of the lenses.

#### Explained

A refracting telescope has a long-focal-length objective lens (f_o) and a short-focal-length eyepiece lens (f_e).

- Light from a distant object arrives as parallel rays. The objective forms a real, inverted image at its principal focus.
- In **normal adjustment**, the eyepiece is placed so its principal focus is at the same point. Rays leave the eyepiece parallel, so the final image is at infinity (viewed with a relaxed eye). The lenses are f_o + f_e apart.
- Ray diagram: draw a ray through the centre of the objective, at angle α to the axis, to the intermediate image in the shared focal plane; from that image point, draw a construction line through the centre of the eyepiece; all rays leave the eyepiece parallel to it, at angle β.

**Angular magnification** M = β/α. With small angles, α ≈ h/f_o and β ≈ h/f_e (h = height of intermediate image), so **M = f_o/f_e**. For a large magnification, use a long objective and a short eyepiece.

#### Worked example

1. A telescope has f_o = 1.20 m and f_e = 0.040 m. Find M and the length of the telescope in normal adjustment. The Moon subtends 9.0 × 10⁻³ rad. What angle does its image subtend?

   M = 30. Length = 1.24 m. β = 30 × 9.0 × 10⁻³ = 0.27 rad.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing M = f_e/f_o.
- Saying the final image in normal adjustment is at the eyepiece focal point (it is at infinity).

### 3.9.1.2 Reflecting telescopes

*Refs: AQA 3.9.1.2. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The Cassegrain arrangement: a parabolic concave primary mirror and a convex secondary mirror.
- The ray diagram up to the eyepiece.
- Relative merits of reflectors and refractors, with spherical and chromatic aberration.

#### Explained

**Cassegrain.** Light enters the open tube and reflects from a large concave **parabolic** primary mirror towards its focus. Before reaching the focus, it hits a small convex secondary mirror, which reflects it back through a hole in the centre of the primary to an eyepiece (or camera) behind it. This folds the light path, so a long focal length fits in a short tube.

**Aberrations.**

- *Chromatic aberration*: a lens refracts different wavelengths by different amounts (blue more than red), so they focus at different points: coloured fringes, blurred image. Mirrors reflect all wavelengths the same way, so they have none.
- *Spherical aberration*: with a spherical lens or mirror, rays far from the axis focus at a different point from rays near the axis, so the image is blurred. A parabolic mirror brings all rays parallel to the axis to one focus, removing it.

**Reflectors versus refractors.**

| | Refractor | Reflector |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Chromatic aberration | Yes (reduced with achromatic doublets) | None |
| Spherical aberration | Yes | None with a parabolic mirror |
| Size | Large lenses are very heavy, sag under their own weight, are supported only at the edge, and must be flawless all through the glass | Mirrors can be supported from behind and only the surface must be perfect; can be segmented; much larger possible |
| Light absorption | Some absorbed in glass (especially UV) | Little |
| Obstruction | None | Secondary mirror blocks some light and causes diffraction spikes |
| Maintenance | Sealed tube, low | Open tube; mirror coating degrades |

So all large modern optical telescopes are reflectors.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the Cassegrain secondary is concave (it is convex).
- Saying mirrors have chromatic aberration.

### 3.9.1.3 Single dish radio telescopes, I-R, U-V and X-ray telescopes

*Refs: AQA 3.9.1.3 · Eduqas C2.7(e). Level: A-level only (Eduqas AS & A).*

#### What you need to know

- How radio telescopes compare with optical telescopes: structure, positioning and use, resolving and collecting power.
- Infrared, ultraviolet and X-ray telescopes.
- (Eduqas) Multiwavelength astronomy: different wavelengths reveal different objects and processes.

#### Explained

**Radio telescopes** (e.g. Jodrell Bank). A large parabolic dish (wire mesh or metal) reflects radio waves to an antenna at the focus, which is connected to a receiver.

- *Similarities to optical reflectors*: parabolic reflector, focusing to a point, steered to track sources.
- *Differences*: wavelengths are about 10⁶ times longer (cm to m), so the dish can be a wire mesh (the gaps must be smaller than about λ/20) and the surface accuracy needed is far lower; but the resolution λ/D is much poorer, so dishes must be huge, or several are linked (interferometry, effective D of thousands of km). They can operate in daylight and cloudy weather and can be on the ground (radio passes through the atmosphere). They detect one point at a time, so the dish scans to build an image. Interference from human sources (phones, radar) is a problem, so they are built in remote areas.

**Infrared, ultraviolet and X-ray telescopes.**

- The atmosphere absorbs most UV, X-rays and much infrared, so these telescopes are placed in space (or IR telescopes on high, dry mountains).
- IR telescopes use mirrors, but must be cooled (to near absolute zero) or their own heat swamps the signal. They see through dust clouds, cool stars, star-forming regions, and very redshifted galaxies.
- UV telescopes are similar to optical reflectors (Cassegrain) with special coatings. They study hot stars and quasars.
- X-rays pass straight into normal mirrors, so X-ray telescopes use grazing incidence: X-rays glance off nested curved mirrors at shallow angles to focus. They observe very hot gas (millions of K), black hole surroundings, neutron stars and supernova remnants.

**Multiwavelength astronomy** (Eduqas). Each band shows different physics: radio (cold hydrogen gas at 21 cm, pulsars, galaxy jets, the CMB in microwaves), IR (cool and dust-hidden objects), optical (stars), UV (hot young stars), X-ray and gamma (extremely hot or violent events). Combining them gives a complete picture of an object.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying radio telescopes have better resolution than optical ones because they are bigger (λ is so much larger that resolution is usually worse for a single dish).
- Saying X-ray telescopes use ordinary curved mirrors at normal incidence.

### 3.9.1.4 Advantages of large diameter telescopes

*Refs: AQA 3.9.1.4. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Minimum angular resolution: Rayleigh criterion θ ≈ λ/D (in radians).
- Collecting power ∝ D².
- Comparison of the eye and the CCD in terms of quantum efficiency, resolution and convenience.

#### Explained

**Resolution.** Light passing through a circular aperture diffracts into an Airy disc. Two point sources (such as two stars close together) can just be resolved when the centre of one diffraction pattern lies on the first minimum of the other: θ ≈ λ/D (strictly 1.22λ/D for a circular aperture). A smaller θ means finer detail can be seen, so a larger diameter D gives better resolution. In practice, atmospheric turbulence ("seeing") limits ground-based telescopes unless adaptive optics or space telescopes are used.

**Collecting power.** The energy collected per second is proportional to the area of the objective, so to D². Doubling D collects 4 times as much light, so fainter objects can be seen and exposures are shorter.

**Charge-coupled device (CCD).** A silicon chip divided into many pixels; photons release electrons in each pixel (photoelectric effect), and the charge collected is proportional to the light received.

| | Eye | CCD |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Quantum efficiency (fraction of photons detected) | About 1% | 70% to over 80% |
| Wavelength range | 400 to 700 nm | Wider (near IR to UV) |
| Resolution | About 100 μm between receptors on the retina; limited by pupil | Pixels about 10 μm; usually limited by the telescope |
| Exposure | About 0.1 s (no storing) | Long exposures, minutes to hours, add up faint light |
| Convenience | No equipment, but no permanent record | Digital record, can be processed; needs cooling, power, computer |

#### Worked example

1. Find the minimum angular resolution of a 2.4 m telescope at 550 nm, and of a 76 m radio dish at 21 cm.

   Optical: 550 × 10⁻⁹/2.4 = 2.3 × 10⁻⁷ rad. Radio: 0.21/76 = 2.8 × 10⁻³ rad (about 12 000 times worse).

2. How many times more light does an 8.0 m mirror collect than a 2.0 m mirror?

   (8.0/2.0)² = 16.

#### Common mistakes

- Using degrees in θ = λ/D.
- Saying a bigger telescope makes stars look bigger (it makes them brighter and better resolved).

### 3.9.2.1 Classification by luminosity

*Refs: AQA 3.9.2.1 · OCR B 5.1.3(b)(i). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Apparent magnitude m; the Hipparcos scale; the dimmest stars visible to the naked eye have m = 6.
- A difference of 1 in magnitude = an intensity (brightness) ratio of 2.51.
- Brightness is a subjective scale.

#### Explained

Hipparchus ranked stars from 1 (brightest) to 6 (faintest visible). The modern scale is defined so that a difference of 5 magnitudes is exactly a factor of 100 in intensity, so 1 magnitude = 100^(1/5) = 2.512.

- Lower (more negative) m means brighter. Sirius: −1.5. Full Moon: −12.7. Sun: −26.7.
- For two stars: I₁/I₂ = 2.51^(m₂ − m₁), or m₁ − m₂ = −2.5 log₁₀(I₁/I₂).

This is a logarithmic scale (OCR B also uses log scales for all wide-ranging astronomical quantities), because the eye responds roughly logarithmically to intensity. Brightness is how bright a star appears to an observer; it depends on luminosity and distance.

#### Worked example

1. Star A has m = 2.0, star B has m = 7.0. How many times brighter does A appear?

   2.51⁵ = 100 times.

#### Common mistakes

- Thinking a larger magnitude means brighter.

### 3.9.2.2 Absolute magnitude, M

*Refs: AQA 3.9.2.2. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The parsec and the light year.
- Definition of M; m − M = 5 log₁₀(d/10), with d in parsecs.

#### Explained

**Light year (ly)**: the distance light travels in one year = 9.46 × 10¹⁵ m.

**Parsec (pc)**: the distance at which 1 AU (the mean Earth–Sun distance, 1.50 × 10¹¹ m) subtends an angle of 1 arcsecond (1/3600°). 1 pc = 3.09 × 10¹⁶ m = 3.26 ly. (Parallax is covered in file 21.)

**Absolute magnitude M**: the apparent magnitude the star would have at a distance of 10 pc. It measures luminosity, independent of distance.

Since intensity ∝ 1/d²: m − M = 5 log₁₀(d/10). The difference m − M is the distance modulus.

#### Worked example

1. A star with M = 1.5 has m = 6.5. Find its distance.

   6.5 − 1.5 = 5 log(d/10), log(d/10) = 1, d = 100 pc.

2. The Sun has m = −26.7 at 1 AU = 4.85 × 10⁻⁶ pc. Find its absolute magnitude.

   M = m − 5 log(d/10) = −26.7 − 5 log(4.85 × 10⁻⁷) = −26.7 + 31.6 = 4.9.

#### Common mistakes

- Using d in light years or metres in m − M = 5 log(d/10).

### 3.9.2.3 Classification by temperature, black-body radiation

*Refs: AQA 3.9.2.3 · Edexcel 153, 154, 155, 156 · OCR A 5.5.2(i), (j), (k) · Eduqas C2.7(a) to (d). Level: A-level only (Eduqas AS & A).*

#### What you need to know

- A black body absorbs all radiation falling on it and is the best possible emitter. Stars are assumed to be black bodies.
- The general shape of black-body curves.
- Wien's displacement law: λ_max T = 2.9 × 10⁻³ m K; use it to estimate temperatures.
- Stefan's law: P = σAT⁴ (luminosity L = 4πr²σT⁴); use it to compare power, temperature and size of stars. σ = 5.67 × 10⁻⁸ W m⁻² K⁻⁴.
- The inverse-square law and its assumptions: I = L/(4πd²).
- (Eduqas) A stellar spectrum is a continuous (black-body) spectrum with absorption lines from cooler outer gas.

#### Explained

**Black body.** An idealised object that absorbs all electromagnetic radiation that falls on it. A black body at temperature T emits a continuous spectrum whose shape depends only on T.

**Black-body curves** (intensity against wavelength): a smooth hump, rising steeply from short wavelengths to a peak at λ_max, then falling more gently at long wavelengths. Hotter bodies have:

- a peak at a shorter wavelength (Wien's law), so they look bluer;
- a much higher curve at every wavelength, with total area (power) ∝ T⁴ (Stefan's law).

**Wien's law.** λ_max T = 2.9 × 10⁻³ m K. The Sun's spectrum peaks near 500 nm, so T ≈ 5800 K. A red star (peak ~ 900 nm) is about 3200 K; a blue star (peak ~ 100 nm) about 29 000 K.

**Stefan's law.** Power emitted P = σAT⁴. For a spherical star, L = 4πr²σT⁴. A star can be very luminous because it is very hot or very large.

**Inverse-square law.** At distance d, intensity (power per unit area) received I = L/(4πd²). Assumptions: the star emits equally in all directions; no radiation is absorbed or scattered on the way (by dust or gas).

**Stellar spectra** (Eduqas C2.7(a)). The hot dense surface (photosphere) emits a continuous spectrum. Cooler gas in the outer atmosphere absorbs particular wavelengths, giving dark absorption lines, which show which elements are present.

#### Worked example

1. A star's spectrum peaks at 380 nm. Find its surface temperature.

   T = 2.9 × 10⁻³/380 × 10⁻⁹ = 7600 K.

2. Star X has twice the surface temperature and half the radius of the Sun. Compare its luminosity with the Sun's.

   L ∝ r²T⁴: (½)² × 2⁴ = 4 times the Sun's.

3. (Edexcel, Eduqas) The Sun's luminosity is 3.85 × 10²⁶ W. Find the intensity at the Earth (1.50 × 10¹¹ m).

   I = 3.85 × 10²⁶/(4π × (1.50 × 10¹¹)²) = 1360 W m⁻².

4. A red giant has T = 3500 K and L = 2.0 × 10²⁹ W. Find its radius.

   r = √(L/(4πσT⁴)) = √(2.0 × 10²⁹/(4π × 5.67 × 10⁻⁸ × 1.50 × 10¹⁴)) = √(1.87 × 10²¹) = 4.3 × 10¹⁰ m (about 60 times the Sun's radius).

#### Common mistakes

- Using °C in Wien's or Stefan's law.
- Forgetting to raise T to the fourth power.
- Saying a black body looks black when hot.

### 3.9.2.4 Principles of the use of stellar spectral classes

*Refs: AQA 3.9.2.4. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The main spectral classes O, B, A, F, G, K, M with their colours, temperatures and prominent absorption lines.
- How temperature affects absorption spectra, limited to the hydrogen Balmer lines, which need atoms in the n = 2 state.

#### Explained

| Class | Colour | Temperature / K | Prominent absorption lines |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| O | Blue | 25 000 to 50 000 | He⁺, He, H |
| B | Blue | 11 000 to 25 000 | He, H |
| A | Blue-white | 7 500 to 11 000 | H (strongest), ionised metals |
| F | White | 6 000 to 7 500 | Ionised metals |
| G | Yellow-white | 5 000 to 6 000 | Ionised and neutral metals |
| K | Orange | 3 500 to 5 000 | Neutral metals |
| M | Red | below 3 500 | Neutral atoms, TiO molecules |

The Sun is a G star (about 5800 K).

**Balmer lines.** These visible hydrogen absorption lines come from transitions from n = 2 to higher levels. A star only shows strong Balmer lines if many of its hydrogen atoms have electrons in the n = 2 level.

- Cool stars (K, M): most hydrogen is in the ground state (n = 1), so few atoms can absorb Balmer wavelengths: weak lines.
- Very hot stars (O): most hydrogen is ionised, so again few n = 2 atoms: weak lines.
- A stars (about 10 000 K): collisions excite many atoms to n = 2 without ionising them: strongest Balmer lines.

So the pattern of lines in a spectrum gives a star's temperature.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying hotter stars always have stronger hydrogen lines.

### 3.9.2.5 The Hertzsprung–Russell diagram

*Refs: AQA 3.9.2.5 · Edexcel 159, 160 · OCR A 5.5.1(g). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The general shape: main sequence, dwarfs and giants.
- Axes: absolute magnitude (−10 to +15) or luminosity, against temperature (50 000 K to 2500 K, decreasing to the right) or spectral class OBAFGKM.
- The position of the Sun.
- The path of a Sun-like star on the HR diagram from formation to white dwarf.

#### Explained

**Axes.** Vertical: luminosity (log scale, L/L_Sun from 10⁻⁴ to 10⁶) or absolute magnitude (−10 at the top to +15 at the bottom). Horizontal: surface temperature, *decreasing* from left (hot, 50 000 K, O) to right (cool, 2500 K, M), on a log scale.

**Regions.**

- *Main sequence*: a diagonal band from top left (hot, luminous, massive O and B stars) to bottom right (cool, dim, small red dwarfs). Stars spend about 90% of their lives here, fusing hydrogen to helium in their cores. The Sun is on the main sequence: M ≈ +5, about 5800 K.
- *Red giants*: top right (cool but very luminous, so very large).
- *Red supergiants*: very top right.
- *White dwarfs*: bottom left (hot but dim, so very small).

**Evolution of a Sun-like star** (AQA, Edexcel 160, OCR A 5.5.1(b) to (d)).

1. A cloud of dust and gas (nebula) collapses under gravity; gravitational potential energy becomes thermal energy. A protostar forms and moves onto the HR diagram from the right.
2. When the core is hot and dense enough (about 10⁷ K), hydrogen fusion starts. Outward radiation pressure (and gas pressure) balances gravity: a stable main-sequence star.
3. When hydrogen in the core runs out, the core contracts and heats; hydrogen fusion continues in a shell around it; the outer layers expand and cool. The star moves up and to the right: a red giant. Helium fuses to carbon (and oxygen) in the core.
4. The outer layers drift off as a planetary nebula. The hot core remains as a white dwarf (bottom left), which slowly cools and fades.

**White dwarf** (OCR A 5.5.1(d)). Very dense (a Sun's mass in Earth's size). No fusion. It does not collapse further because of electron degeneracy pressure: two electrons cannot occupy the same quantum state (Pauli exclusion principle), so electrons resist being squeezed. This works only for a core below the Chandrasekhar limit, 1.44 solar masses.

**Massive stars** (OCR A 5.5.1(e), (f)). A star much more massive than the Sun leaves the main sequence to become a red supergiant, fusing successively heavier elements in shells (up to iron). Iron fusion does not release energy (file 19), so the core collapses suddenly when its mass exceeds 1.44 solar masses; the outer layers rebound in a supernova. The remnant core becomes a neutron star (if under about 3 solar masses) or a black hole.

#### Common mistakes

- Drawing temperature increasing to the right.
- Saying stars move along the main sequence as they age.
- Saying white dwarfs are cool.

### 3.9.2.6 Supernovae, neutron stars and black holes

*Refs: AQA 3.9.2.6 · OCR A 5.5.1(e), (f). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Supernova: a rapid increase in absolute magnitude. Neutron stars: composition and density. Black holes: escape velocity greater than c.
- Gamma-ray bursts from the collapse of supergiants to neutron stars or black holes; their energy output compared with the Sun's total lifetime output.
- Type Ia supernovae as standard candles; the controversy over the accelerating universe and dark energy; the typical light curve of a type Ia supernova.
