- Supermassive black holes at the centres of galaxies.
- The Schwarzschild radius R_s ≈ 2GM/c².

#### Explained

**Supernova.** A star suddenly increases in brightness by a huge amount (absolute magnitude rises by about 15 to 20, to around −19), briefly outshining a whole galaxy, then fades over weeks or months.

- *Core-collapse supernovae* (type II): a massive star's iron core collapses; outer layers bounce off and are blasted away.
- *Type Ia*: a white dwarf in a binary system takes matter from its companion until it exceeds the Chandrasekhar limit, then explodes completely. Because they all explode at about the same mass, they all reach the same peak absolute magnitude (about −19.3). This makes them **standard candles**: measure their apparent magnitude at peak, and m − M = 5 log(d/10) gives the distance. Light curve: rises to the peak in about 20 days, then a fairly fast decline and a slower tail over months.

**Accelerating universe.** In 1998, distant type Ia supernovae were found to be fainter (further away) than expected for their redshift. This suggested that the expansion of the universe is accelerating, driven by an unknown "dark energy". This is controversial: the result relies on the assumption that type Ia supernovae are identical at all distances and that nothing (such as dust) dims them.

**Neutron stars.** After core collapse, electrons and protons are forced together to form neutrons (p + e⁻ → n + νₑ). A neutron star is made almost entirely of neutrons, has a radius of about 10 km and a density similar to nuclear density (about 10¹⁷ kg m⁻³). Neutron degeneracy pressure holds it up. Rapidly rotating neutron stars that emit beams of radio waves are pulsars.

**Black holes.** If the collapsing core is more than about 3 solar masses, nothing stops the collapse. A black hole is a region where the escape velocity exceeds the speed of light, so nothing, not even light, can escape. The boundary is the event horizon, at the Schwarzschild radius: setting escape velocity √(2GM/R) = c gives **R_s = 2GM/c²**.

**Gamma-ray bursts.** Very short (milliseconds to minutes) bursts of gamma rays, thought to come from the collapse of supergiants into neutron stars or black holes (long bursts), or the merging of neutron stars (short bursts). They can release about 10⁴⁴ J in seconds, about the same as the Sun emits in its whole 10-billion-year lifetime (3.9 × 10²⁶ W × 3 × 10¹⁷ s ≈ 10⁴⁴ J).

**Supermassive black holes.** Most galaxies have one at their centre, of millions to billions of solar masses. Evidence: stars near the centre of our galaxy orbit an invisible object of about 4 million solar masses at very high speeds.

#### Worked example

1. Find the Schwarzschild radius of a black hole of 10 solar masses (M_Sun = 2.0 × 10³⁰ kg).

   R_s = 2 × 6.67 × 10⁻¹¹ × 2.0 × 10³¹/(3.0 × 10⁸)² = 3.0 × 10⁴ m (30 km).

2. A type Ia supernova (M = −19.3) has peak apparent magnitude m = 16.7. Find its distance.

   m − M = 36 = 5 log(d/10); d = 10 × 10^7.2 = 1.6 × 10⁸ pc.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a black hole "sucks in" everything (far from it, its gravity is like any other mass).
- Saying neutron stars are made of protons and neutrons in equal numbers.

### Check questions

1. Draw a ray diagram for a refracting telescope in normal adjustment and show that M = f_o/f_e. (5 marks)
2. Give three advantages of reflecting telescopes over refracting telescopes. (3 marks)
3. Compare a single-dish radio telescope with an optical reflecting telescope. (4 marks)
4. Two stars are 1.5 × 10⁻⁶ rad apart. What is the minimum diameter of telescope that can resolve them at 500 nm? (2 marks)
5. Star P has m = 3.0 and is 50 pc away. Calculate its absolute magnitude. (2 marks)
6. The star Betelgeuse has a peak wavelength of 850 nm and luminosity 4.5 × 10³¹ W. Calculate its temperature and radius. (4 marks)
7. Explain why the Balmer absorption lines are weak in the spectra of both very hot and cool stars. (4 marks)
8. Describe the evolution of a star like the Sun from the main sequence to a white dwarf, and the path on the HR diagram. (5 marks)
9. Explain why type Ia supernovae can be used as standard candles. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Correct diagram: parallel rays into objective, intermediate image at shared focus, parallel rays out of eyepiece (2); α = h/f_o (1); β = h/f_e (1); M = β/α = f_o/f_e (1).
2. Any three: no chromatic aberration; parabolic mirror removes spherical aberration; mirrors supported from behind so can be larger; only surface must be perfect; less absorption of light; lighter.
3. Both use parabolic reflector to focus to a point / steerable (1); radio dish can be wire mesh as λ is large (1); radio resolution much poorer for same size, so dishes are larger (1); radio works in daylight / cloud; one-pixel detector scans (1).
4. D = λ/θ (1) = 500 × 10⁻⁹/1.5 × 10⁻⁶ = 0.33 m (1).
5. M = m − 5 log(d/10) (1) = 3.0 − 5 log 5 = −0.49 (1).
6. T = 2.9 × 10⁻³/850 × 10⁻⁹ = 3400 K (1); r = √(L/4πσT⁴) (1) = √(4.5 × 10³¹/(4π × 5.67 × 10⁻⁸ × 1.34 × 10¹⁴)) (1) = 6.9 × 10¹¹ m (1).
7. Balmer lines need hydrogen atoms in n = 2 (1); in cool stars most atoms are in the ground state (1); in very hot stars most hydrogen is ionised (1); so few atoms in n = 2 in both cases; strongest in A stars (1).
8. Main sequence: hydrogen fusion in core, stable (1); core hydrogen runs out, core contracts, shell burning, outer layers expand: red giant (1); moves up and to the right on HR diagram (1); outer layers lost as planetary nebula (1); core becomes white dwarf, bottom left, cools (1).
9. Explode when a white dwarf passes the Chandrasekhar limit (1); so all have the same mass / same peak absolute magnitude (1); measure apparent magnitude and use m − M = 5 log(d/10) to find distance (1).

## Cosmology: astronomical distances, the Doppler effect, Hubble's law, the Big Bang, dark matter, quasars and exoplanets

This file covers how astronomers measure distance (radar, parallax, standard candles), the Doppler shift of light, binary stars and the radial-velocity method, Hubble's law and the expanding universe, evidence for the Big Bang, the age and fate of the universe, dark matter and dark energy, quasars and the detection of exoplanets.

### Overview

A-level only for every board (AQA option 3.9.3). Magnitudes, the parsec definition, black bodies, the HR diagram and type Ia supernovae are in file 20. Orbits and Kepler's laws are in file 14. Relativistic time dilation (OCR B 5.1.3(a)(ii)) is in file 27.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.9.3 · Edexcel 157, 158, 161 to 163 · OCR A 5.5.3 · OCR B 5.1.3 · Eduqas C2.8(e) to (m).
- Parallax: Edexcel 157, OCR A 5.5.3(b), (c). Standard candles: Edexcel 158 (AQA uses type Ia in file 20).
- Radar measurements of distance and relative velocity: OCR B 5.1.3(a)(i), (iii).
- Galaxy rotation curves and dark matter: Eduqas C2.8(e), Edexcel 163, OCR A 5.5.3(o). Eduqas C2.8(f): possible links between the Higgs boson and dark matter.
- Binary stars: AQA (Doppler from binaries in the plane of orbit), Eduqas C2.8(g) (centre of mass and mutual period), C2.8(i), (j) (radial velocity curves to find masses).
- Critical density ρc = 3H₀²/(8πG): Eduqas C2.8(m).
- Quasars and exoplanets: AQA only (exoplanet radial-velocity curves also in Eduqas C2.8(j)).
- OCR A 5.5.3(d): the cosmological principle. OCR A 5.5.3(l), (n): evolution of the universe after the Big Bang.

### 3.9.3.1 Doppler effect

*Refs: AQA 3.9.3.1 · Edexcel 161, 162 · OCR A 5.5.3(e), (f) · OCR B 5.1.3(a)(iii), (c)(ii) · Eduqas C2.8(g) to (j). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Δf/f = v/c and z = Δλ/λ = −Δf/f ≈ v/c for v ≪ c, for optical and radio frequencies.
- Calculations on binary stars viewed in the plane of their orbit.
- Doppler shifts of galaxies and quasars.
- (Eduqas) Centre of mass of two bodies and their mutual orbital period; radial velocity; using radial velocity curves to find masses.
- (OCR B) Radar measurement of distance and relative velocity.

#### Explained

**Doppler effect.** When a source of waves moves relative to an observer, the observed frequency and wavelength change. Moving away: wavelength longer, frequency lower (for light, shifted towards the red: **redshift**). Moving towards: shorter wavelength (**blueshift**). Only the component of velocity along the line of sight (the radial velocity) causes a shift.

For speeds much less than c:

z = Δλ/λ ≈ v/c and Δf/f ≈ −v/c (frequency falls for a receding source)

where λ is the emitted (rest) wavelength. z is the redshift (positive for recession). Astronomers measure Δλ by comparing the positions of absorption lines in a star's or galaxy's spectrum with the same lines measured in a lab.

**Binary stars.** Two stars orbit their common centre of mass. Viewed edge-on (in the plane of the orbit), each star alternately approaches and recedes, so its spectral lines shift back and forth periodically. A graph of radial velocity against time is a sine curve. From it:

- Period T = time for one cycle.
- Orbital speed v = amplitude of the velocity curve.
- Orbital radius r = vT/2π.

**Centre of mass** (Eduqas C2.8(g)). For two masses m₁ and m₂ a distance d apart, the centre of mass is at distances r₁ = m₂d/(m₁ + m₂) from m₁ and r₂ = m₁d/(m₁ + m₂) from m₂ (so m₁r₁ = m₂r₂). Both stars have the same period: T² = 4π²d³/(G(m₁ + m₂)). The heavier star moves in the smaller circle, more slowly.

**Radar** (OCR B 5.1.3(a)(i), (iii)). A radio pulse is sent to a planet or asteroid; the echo returns after time t. Distance = ct/2 (assuming the speed of light is the same for all observers, so distance can be defined in terms of light travel time). Relative velocity from two pulses: if the echo times are t₁ and t₂ for pulses sent Δt apart, the change in distance is c(t₂ − t₁)/2 in time Δt. Or from the Doppler shift of the echo: Δf/f = 2v/c (the shift happens twice, on the way and back).

#### Worked example

1. A hydrogen line with rest wavelength 656.3 nm is observed at 662.8 nm in a galaxy. Find its recession speed.

   z = 6.5/656.3 = 9.90 × 10⁻³. v = zc = 2.97 × 10⁶ m s⁻¹.

2. A spectroscopic binary seen edge-on shows a line from one star varying by ±0.042 nm about 500.0 nm, with a period of 4.0 days. Find the star's orbital speed and radius.

   v = (0.042/500.0) × 3.0 × 10⁸ = 2.5 × 10⁴ m s⁻¹. r = vT/2π = 2.5 × 10⁴ × 3.46 × 10⁵/(2π) = 1.4 × 10⁹ m.

3. (Eduqas) Two stars of 2.0 and 1.0 solar masses (M_Sun = 2.0 × 10³⁰ kg) are 3.0 × 10¹¹ m apart. Find the position of the centre of mass and the period.

   From the heavier star: r₁ = 1.0 × 3.0 × 10¹¹/3.0 = 1.0 × 10¹¹ m. T = 2π√(d³/(G(m₁ + m₂))) = 2π√(2.7 × 10³⁴/(6.67 × 10⁻¹¹ × 6.0 × 10³⁰)) = 2π√(6.75 × 10¹³) = 5.2 × 10⁷ s (1.6 years).

4. (OCR B) A radar pulse returns from Venus 276 s after it is sent. Find the distance.

   d = 3.0 × 10⁸ × 276/2 = 4.1 × 10¹⁰ m.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the observed wavelength instead of the rest wavelength as λ.
- Saying redshift means the object looks red.
- Forgetting that only motion along the line of sight causes a shift.

### Distances: parallax and standard candles

*Refs: Edexcel 157, 158 · OCR A 5.5.3(a), (b), (c) · OCR B 5.1.3(c)(i). Level: A-level only. (AQA 3.9.2.2 defines the parsec; see file 20.)*

#### What you need to know

- The astronomical unit, the light year and the parsec.
- Stellar parallax and p = 1/d (p in arcseconds, d in parsecs).
- (Edexcel) Standard candles: objects of known luminosity; d from I = L/(4πd²).

#### Explained

**Units.** 1 AU = mean Earth–Sun distance = 1.50 × 10¹¹ m. 1 ly = 9.46 × 10¹⁵ m. 1 pc = 3.09 × 10¹⁶ m = 3.26 ly = 206 265 AU.

**Parallax.** As the Earth orbits the Sun, a nearby star appears to move against the background of very distant stars. Measure its position six months apart; the baseline is 2 AU. The parallax angle p is half the total angular shift (the angle subtended by 1 AU at the star). For small angles, tan p ≈ p (radians) = 1 AU/d. Defining the parsec as the distance where p = 1 arcsecond: **d (pc) = 1/p (arcsec)**. Parallax works up to a few hundred parsecs from the ground (angles below about 0.01 arcsec are hard to measure because of atmospheric blurring), and to thousands of parsecs from space (Gaia).

**Standard candles** (Edexcel 158). An object whose luminosity L is known (from its type). Measure its intensity (flux) I at the Earth, then d = √(L/(4πI)). Examples: Cepheid variable stars (their period of pulsation is related to their luminosity), type Ia supernovae (file 20). They extend distance measurements to other galaxies.

#### Worked example

1. A star has a parallax of 0.25 arcsec. Find its distance in pc, ly and m.

   d = 4.0 pc = 13 ly = 1.24 × 10¹⁷ m.

2. A Cepheid with L = 3.8 × 10³⁰ W gives I = 4.5 × 10⁻¹⁵ W m⁻² at the Earth. Find its distance.

   d = √(3.8 × 10³⁰/(4π × 4.5 × 10⁻¹⁵)) = √(6.72 × 10⁴³) = 8.2 × 10²¹ m (about 270 kpc).

#### Common mistakes

- Using the full angular shift over six months as p.
- Using p in degrees or radians in d = 1/p.

### 3.9.3.2 Hubble's law

*Refs: AQA 3.9.3.2 · Edexcel 162, 163 · OCR A 5.5.3(d), (g) to (o) · OCR B 5.1.3(a)(iv) · Eduqas C2.8(e), (f), (k), (l), (m). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Redshift and Hubble's law v = Hd.
- Interpretation as expansion of the universe; estimating the age of the universe (assuming H is constant).
- Big Bang theory, with evidence from the cosmic microwave background radiation and the relative abundance of hydrogen and helium.
- (OCR A, Edexcel, Eduqas) The cosmological principle; H₀ in km s⁻¹ Mpc⁻¹ and in s⁻¹; dark matter (galaxy rotation curves) and dark energy; the uncertain value of H₀ and the fate of the universe; critical density.

#### Explained

**Hubble's law.** The recession velocity of a distant galaxy is proportional to its distance: v = H₀d. H₀ ≈ 70 km s⁻¹ Mpc⁻¹. In SI units: 70 × 10³ m s⁻¹ / 3.09 × 10²² m = 2.3 × 10⁻¹⁸ s⁻¹. (Measurements disagree between about 67 and 74 km s⁻¹ Mpc⁻¹; this is the "Hubble tension".)

**Interpretation.** Almost all galaxies are moving away from us, and further ones faster. This does not mean we are at the centre: the whole of space is expanding, so every galaxy sees every other receding (like dots on an inflating balloon). The redshift of distant galaxies is really caused by space stretching the light as it travels (cosmological redshift).

**Cosmological principle** (OCR A 5.5.3(d)). On large scales the universe is homogeneous (the same everywhere) and isotropic (the same in every direction), and the laws of physics are universal.

**Age of the universe.** If expansion has always been at the same rate, all galaxies were together at t = 0. Time for a galaxy at distance d to get there at speed v: t = d/v = 1/H₀. With H₀ = 2.3 × 10⁻¹⁸ s⁻¹: t = 4.4 × 10¹⁷ s ≈ 14 billion years. (The current best estimate is 13.8 billion years.)

**The Big Bang theory.** The universe began about 14 billion years ago from an extremely hot, dense state and has been expanding and cooling since. The Big Bang created space and time themselves, so it did not happen at a point within space (OCR A 5.5.3(l)). Evolution (OCR A 5.5.3(n)): within the first fraction of a second, quarks and leptons formed; quarks combined into protons and neutrons; within a few minutes, some hydrogen fused to helium; after about 380 000 years, the universe cooled enough for atoms to form and become transparent; after about 200 million years the first stars and galaxies formed.

**Evidence.**

1. *Redshift of galaxies* (Hubble's law): the universe is expanding.
2. *Cosmic microwave background radiation (CMB)*: microwaves arriving equally from all directions, with a black-body spectrum at 2.7 K. It is the radiation released when atoms first formed (when the universe was about 3000 K), stretched into microwaves by the expansion since. Its uniformity, with tiny variations, fits the Big Bang.
3. *Abundance of hydrogen and helium*: about 75% hydrogen and 25% helium by mass. The Big Bang model predicts that, in the few minutes when the universe was hot enough for fusion, about this much helium formed before expansion cooled it too much. Stars alone cannot have made this much helium.

**Dark matter** (Eduqas C2.8(e), Edexcel 163, OCR A 5.5.3(o)). In a spiral galaxy, if most mass were in the bright centre, orbital speeds of stars should fall with distance from the centre (like planets: v = √(GM/r)). Instead, measured speeds (from Doppler shifts) stay roughly constant far out. This means there is much more mass than we can see, spread out in a halo: dark matter, which does not emit or absorb light. It is thought to make up about 27% of the universe's energy content. (Eduqas C2.8(f): the Higgs boson gives particles mass; some theories link dark matter to particles that interact with the Higgs field; this is uncertain.)

**Dark energy and the fate of the universe** (AQA, Edexcel 163, OCR A 5.5.3(o)). Type Ia supernovae show the expansion is accelerating, explained by "dark energy" (about 68% of the universe; ordinary matter about 5%). The fate of the universe depends on the balance of its density with the expansion:

- *Critical density* (Eduqas C2.8(m)): ρc = 3H₀²/(8πG) ≈ 9 × 10⁻²⁷ kg m⁻³ (a few hydrogen atoms per cubic metre). Denser than this, gravity would eventually stop and reverse expansion (Big Crunch); less dense, it expands for ever; equal, it slows towards zero.
- Controversy (Edexcel 163): the age depends on H₀, which is hard to measure accurately (distances to far galaxies are uncertain). The fate depends on the amount of dark matter and dark energy, neither of which has been detected directly.

#### Worked example

1. A galaxy has redshift z = 0.020. Find its distance using H₀ = 70 km s⁻¹ Mpc⁻¹.

   v = 0.020 × 3.0 × 10⁵ km s⁻¹ = 6000 km s⁻¹. d = 6000/70 = 86 Mpc.

2. Estimate the age of the universe if H₀ = 67 km s⁻¹ Mpc⁻¹.

   H₀ = 67 × 10³/3.09 × 10²² = 2.17 × 10⁻¹⁸ s⁻¹. t = 4.6 × 10¹⁷ s = 14.6 billion years.

3. (Eduqas) Calculate the critical density for H₀ = 2.3 × 10⁻¹⁸ s⁻¹.

   ρc = 3 × (2.3 × 10⁻¹⁸)²/(8π × 6.67 × 10⁻¹¹) = 9.5 × 10⁻²⁷ kg m⁻³.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the Big Bang was an explosion at a point in space.
- Forgetting to convert Mpc to m for SI units of H₀.
- Saying the CMB comes from stars.
- Saying the age of the universe is exactly 1/H₀ (it assumes constant expansion).

### 3.9.3.3 Quasars

*Refs: AQA 3.9.3.3. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Quasars are the most distant measurable objects.
- They were discovered as bright radio sources.
- They have large optical redshifts; estimates of distance and power output.
- They form from active supermassive black holes.

#### Explained

Quasars (quasi-stellar radio sources) were found in the 1960s as strong radio sources that looked like faint stars in optical telescopes. Their spectra have very large redshifts (z often above 1, up to about 7), so by Hubble's law they are extremely far away, billions of light years. To appear as bright as they do from so far, they must have enormous power outputs, around 10³⁹ to 10⁴¹ W, more than a thousand galaxies. Their brightness changes over days, so they must be small (light must cross them in that time, so less than a few light days across).

Explanation: a quasar is the active core of a young galaxy, powered by a supermassive black hole. Matter spiralling into it forms a hot accretion disc that radiates intensely before falling in, and jets of particles stream out along the poles. We see them as they were long ago (light has taken billions of years to arrive), when galaxies were young and their black holes were feeding.

#### Worked example

1. A quasar has z = 0.16. Estimate its distance (H₀ = 70 km s⁻¹ Mpc⁻¹), and its power if its intensity at Earth is 2.0 × 10⁻¹² W m⁻².

   v = 0.16c = 4.8 × 10⁴ km s⁻¹; d = 690 Mpc = 2.1 × 10²⁵ m. P = 4πd²I = 4π × (2.1 × 10²⁵)² × 2.0 × 10⁻¹² = 1.1 × 10⁴⁰ W.

(For large z, v ≈ zc is only approximate; relativistic formulae are not needed.)

#### Common mistakes

- Saying quasars are stars.

### 3.9.3.4 Detection of exoplanets

*Refs: AQA 3.9.3.4 · Eduqas C2.8(j). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Why exoplanets are hard to detect directly.
- The radial velocity (Doppler shift) method and the transit method.
- A typical transit light curve.

#### Explained

**Difficulties.** Planets are very small and faint and are very close to their star (in angle) compared with the star's glare; the star is billions of times brighter, so the planet is lost in its light, and the angle between them is below the telescope's resolution.

**Radial velocity method.** A star and planet orbit their common centre of mass. The star wobbles in a small orbit, so its spectral lines are Doppler shifted back and forth periodically (for an orbit not face-on). The period gives the planet's orbital period; the size of the star's velocity variation, with the star's mass, gives a minimum mass for the planet. Best for massive planets close to their stars (large, fast wobbles). Very precise spectrometers detect speeds of about 1 m s⁻¹.

**Transit method.** If the orbit is nearly edge-on to us, the planet passes in front of the star once per orbit, blocking a small fraction of its light. The light curve (intensity against time) is flat, then drops slightly (by about (r_planet/r_star)²: 1% for a Jupiter-size planet in front of a Sun-like star), stays low with a flat bottom while the planet is fully in front, then rises again; this repeats every orbit. The dip depth gives the planet's size; the time between dips gives its period (and so orbital radius by Kepler's third law). Used by the Kepler and TESS space telescopes. Only works for the small fraction of systems aligned edge-on.

#### Worked example

1. A transit reduces a star's intensity by 0.80%. The star's radius is 7.0 × 10⁸ m. Estimate the planet's radius.

   (r/R)² = 0.0080; r = 7.0 × 10⁸ × 0.089 = 6.3 × 10⁷ m (similar to Jupiter).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the radial velocity method sees the planet's own spectrum.
- Saying the transit light curve drops to zero.

### Check questions

1. Explain how the Doppler shift of absorption lines shows that a distant galaxy is receding. (3 marks)
2. A star's parallax is 0.040 arcsec. Calculate its distance in metres. (2 marks)
3. A galaxy's hydrogen line (rest 121.6 nm) is observed at 133.8 nm. Calculate its recession speed and distance (H₀ = 70 km s⁻¹ Mpc⁻¹). (4 marks)
4. Describe three pieces of evidence for the Big Bang theory. (6 marks)
5. Show that 1/H₀ gives an estimate of the age of the universe and calculate it for H₀ = 72 km s⁻¹ Mpc⁻¹. (4 marks)
6. Explain how the rotation curves of spiral galaxies suggest the existence of dark matter. (3 marks)
7. Explain why quasars are thought to be extremely powerful. (3 marks)
8. Describe the transit method of detecting exoplanets and sketch a typical light curve. (4 marks)
9. Two stars in a binary have masses in the ratio 3 : 1. Explain which star has the larger orbit and the larger Doppler shift. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Compare absorption lines with the same lines from a lab source (1); lines in galaxy spectrum are at longer wavelengths (redshifted) (1); so the source is moving away; z = Δλ/λ = v/c (1).
2. d = 1/0.040 = 25 pc (1) = 7.7 × 10¹⁷ m (1).
3. z = 12.2/121.6 = 0.100 (1); v = 3.0 × 10⁷ m s⁻¹ (1); = 3.0 × 10⁴ km s⁻¹; d = v/H₀ (1) = 430 Mpc (1).
4. Galaxies redshifted, further ones faster (Hubble's law) (1): universe expanding from a single point / origin (1); CMB: microwave radiation from all directions with 2.7 K black-body spectrum (1): radiation from the hot early universe, stretched by expansion (1); about 25% helium by mass (1): produced by fusion in the first few minutes, as predicted (1).
5. t = d/v and v = H₀d, so t = 1/H₀ (assuming constant expansion) (2); H₀ = 72 × 10³/3.09 × 10²² = 2.33 × 10⁻¹⁸ s⁻¹ (1); t = 4.3 × 10¹⁷ s ≈ 13.6 billion years (1).
6. If mass were concentrated in visible centre, orbital speed would decrease with distance (v ∝ 1/√r) (1); measured speeds stay nearly constant far from the centre (1); so there must be a lot of unseen mass spread through the galaxy / halo (1).
7. Very large redshifts, so very distant (1); yet bright, so must emit enormous power (by inverse square law) (1); power comes from matter falling into supermassive black hole (1).
8. Planet passes in front of star, blocking some light (1); detected as periodic dip in intensity (1); sketch: flat, drops with sloping edges to a flat-bottomed dip, rises back (1); depth gives planet size; interval gives period (1).
9. Both orbit the common centre of mass, which is closer to the heavier star (1); lighter star has orbit 3 times larger (1); same period, so lighter star moves 3 times faster and has larger Doppler shift (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/04-progressive-and-stationary-waves.md -->

## Progressive waves, polarisation and stationary waves

A progressive wave carries energy from place to place; a stationary wave stores it in a fixed pattern. This file covers the language of waves (amplitude, phase, wavelength), transverse and longitudinal waves, the electromagnetic spectrum, polarisation, intensity, and stationary waves on strings, in air columns and with microwaves, including AQA required practical 1.

### Overview

AS & A for every board. Superposition, interference, diffraction and refraction are in file 05. Oscilloscope use for frequency and timing is also in file 32.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.1, RP1 · Edexcel 59 to 64, 67 to 70, 82, CP6, CP7 · OCR A 4.4.1, 4.4.2(a) to (c), 4.4.4 · OCR B 3.1.1 (polarisation, v = fλ), 4.1 (standing waves) · Eduqas C3.1, C3.2(n), (o).
- Edexcel CP6 measures the speed of sound with a two-beam oscilloscope, signal generator, loudspeaker and microphone. Edexcel CP7 and AQA RP1 are the same string investigation.
- OCR A 4.4.4(e), OCR B 4.1(d)(v) and Eduqas (specified practical) find the speed of sound from stationary waves in a resonance tube (air column).
- Edexcel 70 and OCR A 4.4.1(g) ask for intensity I = P/A; OCR A also asks that intensity ∝ amplitude².
- AQA says Malus's law is not expected. Eduqas's specified practical measures how intensity varies as a polariser is rotated (in effect, Malus's law: I = I₀cos²θ).
- AQA says the words fundamental and overtone will not be used; OCR A 4.4.4(g) uses "fundamental mode (1st harmonic)".
- Eduqas C3.1(f) only needs displacement–time and displacement–position graphs for transverse waves.

### 3.3.1.1 Progressive waves

*Refs: AQA 3.3.1.1 · Edexcel 59, 60, 63, 70 · OCR A 4.4.1(a) to (e), (g), 4.4.2(a), (b) · OCR B 3.1.1(c)(v) · Eduqas C3.1(a), (d) to (h). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- A progressive wave transfers energy (not matter) by oscillations of the particles of a medium, or of electric and magnetic fields.
- Amplitude, frequency, wavelength, speed, period, phase and phase difference.
- c = fλ and f = 1/T.
- Phase difference in degrees, radians or as a fraction of a cycle.
- (Edexcel, OCR A) Intensity I = P/A; intensity is proportional to amplitude².
- (OCR A) The electromagnetic spectrum and orders of magnitude of each region's wavelength.
- (Eduqas) Wavefronts and rays: all points on a wavefront are in phase, and rays are perpendicular to wavefronts.

#### Explained

**Definitions.**

| Term | Meaning | Unit |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Displacement x | Distance and direction of a point from its equilibrium position | m |
| Amplitude A | Maximum displacement | m |
| Wavelength λ | Distance between two adjacent points oscillating in phase (e.g. crest to crest) | m |
| Period T | Time for one complete oscillation of a point | s |
| Frequency f | Number of complete oscillations per second; f = 1/T | Hz |
| Wave speed c (or v) | Distance travelled by the wave per second | m s⁻¹ |
| Phase | Where a point is in its cycle | rad or ° |
| Phase difference | How far one point's cycle is ahead of or behind another's | rad or ° |

**The wave equation.** In one period the wave moves one wavelength, so c = λ/T = fλ.

**Phase difference.** Two points a distance d apart along a wave have phase difference Δφ = 2πd/λ radians (or 360° × d/λ). In phase: Δφ = 0, 2π, 4π... (d = λ, 2λ...). In antiphase: Δφ = π, 3π... (d = λ/2, 3λ/2...).

**Graphs.**

- Displacement–distance (a snapshot at one instant): shows wavelength and amplitude.
- Displacement–time (one point over time): shows period and amplitude.

Make sure you know which you are reading: the horizontal distance between peaks is λ on one and T on the other.

**Wavefronts and rays.** A wavefront is a line joining points that are in phase (for example, all crests). Rays show the direction of travel and are at right angles to wavefronts.

**Intensity** (Edexcel 70, OCR A 4.4.1(g)). Intensity is the power per unit area at right angles to the direction of travel: I = P/A, in W m⁻². For a point source radiating equally in all directions, I = P/(4πr²), so intensity falls as 1/r². Intensity ∝ amplitude²: doubling the amplitude gives four times the intensity.

**Electromagnetic spectrum** (OCR A 4.4.2(a), (b); Eduqas C3.4(f) also). All EM waves are transverse, travel at c = 3.00 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹ in a vacuum, and consist of oscillating electric and magnetic fields at right angles to each other and to the direction of travel.

| Region | Typical wavelength |
| --- | --- |
| Radio | > 10⁻¹ m (up to km) |
| Microwave | 10⁻³ to 10⁻¹ m |
| Infrared | 7 × 10⁻⁷ to 10⁻³ m |
| Visible | 4 × 10⁻⁷ (violet) to 7 × 10⁻⁷ m (red) |
| Ultraviolet | 10⁻⁸ to 4 × 10⁻⁷ m |
| X-ray | 10⁻¹³ to 10⁻⁸ m |
| Gamma | < 10⁻¹⁰ m |

X-rays and gamma overlap: the difference is how they are made (X-rays from decelerating electrons, gamma from nuclei).

**Measuring frequency with an oscilloscope** (OCR A 4.4.1(b)(ii), OCR B 4.1(d)(i)). Measure the horizontal length of several complete cycles in divisions, multiply by the time-base setting (s per division) and divide by the number of cycles to get T; then f = 1/T.

#### Worked example

1. A sound wave has frequency 440 Hz and speed 340 m s⁻¹. Find the wavelength and the phase difference between two points 0.20 m apart along the wave.

   λ = 340/440 = 0.773 m. Δφ = 2π × 0.20/0.773 = 1.63 rad (93°).

2. On an oscilloscope with time-base 0.5 ms div⁻¹, three complete waves span 7.5 divisions. Find f.

   3T = 7.5 × 0.5 ms = 3.75 ms, so T = 1.25 ms and f = 800 Hz.

3. A lamp emits 60 W of light uniformly. Find the intensity 2.5 m away.

   I = 60/(4π × 2.5²) = 0.76 W m⁻².

#### Common mistakes

- Reading a period off a displacement–distance graph.
- Saying waves transfer matter.
- Giving phase difference in degrees when radians are asked for (or the reverse).
- Forgetting to square amplitude when comparing intensities.

### 3.3.1.2 Longitudinal and transverse waves

*Refs: AQA 3.3.1.2 · Edexcel 61, 62, 82 · OCR A 4.4.1(a), (e), (f), 4.4.2(c) · OCR B 3.1.1(a)(iv), 3.1.1(d)(ii) · Eduqas C3.1(b), (c). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- In a transverse wave the oscillations are at right angles to the direction of energy transfer; in a longitudinal wave they are parallel to it.
- Examples: sound (longitudinal), electromagnetic waves and waves on a string (transverse).
- All electromagnetic waves travel at the same speed in a vacuum.
- (Edexcel 61) Longitudinal waves described in terms of pressure variation and the displacement of molecules.
- Polarisation is evidence that a wave is transverse.
- Uses of polarisers: Polaroid material (sunglasses, camera filters) and aligning aerials for transmitting and receiving.

#### Explained

**Transverse.** Particles (or fields) oscillate perpendicular to the direction the wave travels. Examples: waves on a string, ripples on water (approximately), all EM waves, S-waves in earthquakes.

**Longitudinal.** Particles oscillate parallel to the direction of travel, forming compressions (high pressure, particles close) and rarefactions (low pressure, particles spread). Examples: sound, P-waves, a slinky pushed back and forth. Longitudinal waves need a medium; EM waves do not.

On a graph of displacement against position for a longitudinal wave, the compressions are where the displacement graph crosses zero going from positive to negative (particles on both sides moving towards that point), assuming displacement to the right is positive. Pressure is a maximum at compressions, which are a quarter of a wavelength from points of maximum displacement.

**Polarisation.** A transverse wave is plane-polarised if its oscillations are in one plane only. Unpolarised light (from a lamp or the Sun) has oscillations in all planes perpendicular to its direction.

- A polarising filter only lets through the component of oscillation along its transmission axis.
- Two filters: rotate the second (the analyser). Transmitted light falls to a minimum (zero) when the two axes are at 90° (crossed), and is maximum when they are parallel. Each full rotation gives two maxima and two minima.
- Longitudinal waves cannot be polarised, since their oscillation is always along the direction of travel. So polarisation shows that light is transverse.

**Malus's law** (Eduqas practical only; not AQA). Transmitted intensity through an analyser at angle θ to the polarised light: I = I₀cos²θ. Measure with a light sensor while rotating the analyser in 10° steps; a graph of I against cos²θ is a straight line through the origin.

**Microwaves** (OCR A 4.4.1(f)(iii), OCR B 3.1.1(d)(ii)). Microwave transmitters emit plane-polarised waves. A metal grille placed between transmitter and receiver blocks the waves when the rods are parallel to the electric field (the field drives currents in the rods, which absorb and reflect the energy), and lets them through when the rods are perpendicular. The receiver signal varies as the receiver itself is rotated.

**Applications.**

- Polaroid sunglasses: light reflected from water or roads is partly horizontally polarised; the vertical transmission axis cuts out the glare.
- TV and radio aerials: broadcast waves are polarised (vertically or horizontally). The receiving aerial rods must be aligned with the transmitter's plane of polarisation for the strongest signal. Neighbouring transmitters use different polarisations to reduce interference.
- Stress analysis: some plastics rotate the plane of polarisation by amounts that depend on stress; between crossed polarisers, coloured fringes show stress concentrations.

#### Worked example

1. Unpolarised light of intensity 40 W m⁻² passes through a polariser and then an analyser at 60° to it. Find the final intensity (Eduqas).

   After the first polariser: half the intensity, 20 W m⁻². After the analyser: 20 × cos²60° = 20 × 0.25 = 5.0 W m⁻².

#### Common mistakes

- Saying sound can be polarised.
- Saying a polariser "blocks horizontal waves". It transmits the component along its axis.
- Saying compressions are where displacement is greatest.

### 3.3.1.3 Principle of superposition of waves and formation of stationary waves

*Refs: AQA 3.3.1.3, RP1 · Edexcel 63, 64, 67, 68, 69, CP6, CP7 · OCR A 4.4.4(a) to (g) · OCR B 4.1(a)(i), 4.1(c)(i), 4.1(d)(iii), (v) · Eduqas C3.2(n), (o). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- A stationary wave forms when two waves of the same frequency (and similar amplitude) travel in opposite directions and superpose.
- Nodes (zero amplitude) and antinodes (maximum amplitude); adjacent nodes are λ/2 apart.
- Graphical explanation of how a stationary wave forms.
- Stationary waves on strings, with microwaves and with sound (air columns).
- Harmonics on a string; the first harmonic frequency f = (1/2l)√(T/μ).
- (Edexcel 68) Wave speed on a string v = √(T/μ).
- The differences between stationary and progressive waves.
- AQA required practical 1 and Edexcel CP7: how frequency depends on length, tension and mass per unit length. Edexcel CP6: speed of sound with an oscilloscope.

#### Explained

**Superposition.** When two waves meet, the resultant displacement at any point is the vector sum of the individual displacements.

**How a stationary wave forms.** A wave travels along a string, reflects at the fixed end and travels back. The incident and reflected waves have the same frequency, speed and (nearly) amplitude. Draw both at several instants (t = 0, T/8, T/4...) and add them: at some points they always cancel (nodes); midway between, they reinforce to give oscillation with twice the amplitude (antinodes). The pattern does not travel.

**Stationary vs progressive.**

| Property | Progressive | Stationary |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Energy | Transferred along the wave | Not transferred; stored |
| Amplitude | Same for all points (if no losses) | Varies from zero (nodes) to maximum (antinodes) |
| Phase | Changes continuously along a wavelength | All points between two adjacent nodes are in phase; points either side of a node are in antiphase |
| Wavelength | Distance between adjacent in-phase points | Twice the distance between adjacent nodes |
| Frequency | All points have the same frequency | All points (except nodes) have the same frequency |

**Strings fixed at both ends.** Both ends must be nodes, so a whole number of half-wavelengths fits on the string: l = nλ/2, λ = 2l/n.

- First harmonic (n = 1): one loop, λ = 2l, f₁ = v/2l.
- Second harmonic: two loops, λ = l, f₂ = 2f₁.
- nth harmonic: fₙ = nf₁.

The speed of a wave on a string is v = √(T/μ), where T is tension (N) and μ is mass per unit length (kg m⁻¹). So:

**f₁ = (1/2l)√(T/μ).**

So frequency ∝ 1/l, f ∝ √T and f ∝ 1/√μ. Thicker, heavier strings give lower notes; tightening a guitar string raises its pitch.

**Air columns** (OCR A, OCR B, Eduqas). Sound in a tube forms stationary waves. A closed end is a displacement node (air cannot move); an open end is a displacement antinode.

- Tube closed at one end: first harmonic has l = λ/4. Only odd harmonics: f, 3f, 5f... (l = λ/4, 3λ/4, 5λ/4...)
- Tube open at both ends: antinodes at both ends; l = λ/2 for the first harmonic; all harmonics: f, 2f, 3f...

(End corrections are not required by OCR B; in practice the antinode is slightly beyond the open end.)

**Microwaves.** A microwave transmitter faces a metal plate. The reflected wave superposes with the incident wave. Moving a probe receiver along the line between them shows maxima (antinodes) and minima (nodes) λ/2 apart. Measuring the distance across several nodes gives λ (about 3 cm).

**Resonance tube** (OCR A 4.4.4(e)(ii), OCR B 4.1(d)(v), Eduqas SP). A tuning fork (or speaker at known f) is held over a tube whose effective length can be changed (a tube in a water reservoir, or a sliding tube). Raise the tube until the sound is loudest: first resonance at l₁ ≈ λ/4. Continue to the second resonance at l₂ ≈ 3λ/4. Then λ = 2(l₂ − l₁), which removes the end correction. Speed v = fλ. Repeat with several frequencies and plot l against 1/f; gradient = v/4.

#### Worked example

1. A guitar string is 0.65 m long, has μ = 4.0 × 10⁻⁴ kg m⁻¹ and tension 70 N. Find the first harmonic frequency.

   v = √(70/4.0 × 10⁻⁴) = √(1.75 × 10⁵) = 418 m s⁻¹. f₁ = v/2l = 418/1.30 = 322 Hz.

2. The tension is doubled. Find the new first harmonic.

   f ∝ √T, so f = 322 × √2 = 455 Hz.

3. A tube closed at one end resonates first at 0.180 m and next at 0.545 m with a 470 Hz fork. Find the speed of sound.

   λ/2 = 0.545 − 0.180 = 0.365 m, so λ = 0.730 m. v = 470 × 0.730 = 343 m s⁻¹.

4. Microwave nodes: 8 nodes span 10.5 cm (7 gaps). Find f.

   λ/2 = 10.5/7 = 1.50 cm, so λ = 3.00 cm. f = 3.00 × 10⁸ / 0.0300 = 1.0 × 10¹⁰ Hz.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying adjacent nodes are one wavelength apart.
- Saying all points on a stationary wave have the same amplitude.
- Saying a stationary wave transfers energy along the string.
- Forgetting to convert mass per unit length from g m⁻¹ to kg m⁻¹.
- Using l = λ/2 for a tube closed at one end.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 1** (also Edexcel CP7, OCR B 4.1(d)(iii)): investigate how the frequency of stationary waves on a string depends on length, tension and mass per unit length.

1. Fix one end of a string to a vibration generator driven by a signal generator. Run the string over a pulley to a hanging mass, so tension T = mg. Use a movable bridge near the pulley to set the vibrating length l (vibration generator to bridge).
2. Start at low frequency and increase until the first harmonic (one loop with the largest amplitude) appears. Record f from the signal generator. Check by finding the 2nd harmonic at 2f.
3. Length: vary l with T and μ fixed; plot f against 1/l (straight line through the origin, gradient = v/2).
4. Tension: vary the hanging mass; plot f against √T (or f² against T), straight line, gradient (1/2l)√(1/μ).
5. Mass per unit length: use strings of different μ (measure by weighing a known length, or calculate from diameter and density); plot f against 1/√μ.

Improving accuracy: measure l with a metre rule to the nearest mm; judge the largest-amplitude point by sweeping the frequency up and down; use a strobe to see the nodes clearly. Safety: the hanging masses can fall, so keep feet clear and use a cushion; goggles if the string might snap.

**Edexcel CP6:** speed of sound with a two-beam oscilloscope. A signal generator drives a loudspeaker and also feeds one channel of the oscilloscope. A microphone in front of the speaker feeds the other channel. Move the microphone away from the speaker until the two traces are in phase again; the distance moved is one wavelength. Measure over several wavelengths (move until the traces return into phase n times) for a smaller percentage uncertainty. v = fλ. Alternatively, plot distance against number of wavelengths; gradient = λ.

### Check questions

1. Define wavelength and explain what is meant by two points being in antiphase. (2 marks)
2. Two points on a wave of wavelength 0.60 m are 0.45 m apart. Find the phase difference in radians. (2 marks)
3. Explain why the polarisation of light is evidence that light is a transverse wave. (2 marks)
4. Explain why a TV receiving aerial must be aligned with the transmitter aerial. (2 marks)
5. Explain, with reference to superposition, how a stationary wave forms on a string fixed at both ends. (4 marks)
6. Give two differences between a stationary wave and a progressive wave. (2 marks)
7. A string 0.80 m long under a tension of 60 N has a first harmonic of 150 Hz. Calculate its mass per unit length. (3 marks)
8. A tube is closed at one end. Its first resonance with a 512 Hz source is at 0.165 m. Estimate the speed of sound and the length for the next resonance. (3 marks)
9. Describe how to show that the frequency of the first harmonic on a string is inversely proportional to its length. (4 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Wavelength: distance between adjacent points oscillating in phase (1); antiphase: phase difference of π rad / 180° / half a cycle (1).
2. Δφ = 2π × 0.45/0.60 (1) = 4.7 rad (3π/2) (1).
3. Only transverse waves can be polarised (1); because longitudinal oscillations are always along the direction of travel so cannot be restricted to one plane (1).
4. Transmitted waves are plane polarised (1); the aerial rods must be in the same plane as the oscillating field to receive maximum signal (1).
5. Wave reflects at fixed end (1); incident and reflected waves have same frequency/speed/amplitude and travel in opposite directions (1); superpose (1); points where they always cancel are nodes; points where they reinforce are antinodes (1).
6. Any two: stationary stores energy / does not transfer it; amplitude varies along stationary wave; points between adjacent nodes in phase in stationary wave but phase varies continuously in progressive.
7. λ = 2l = 1.60 m, v = fλ = 240 m s⁻¹ (1); μ = T/v² (1) = 60/240² = 1.04 × 10⁻³ kg m⁻¹ (1).
8. λ = 4 × 0.165 = 0.660 m; v = 512 × 0.660 = 338 m s⁻¹ (1)(1); next resonance at 3λ/4 = 0.495 m (1).
9. Vibration generator and signal generator, string over pulley with fixed mass (constant tension), same string (1); vary length with bridge, find frequency of largest single loop each time (1); measure length with metre rule (1); plot f against 1/l: straight line through origin shows inverse proportion (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/05-interference-diffraction-and-refraction.md -->

## Interference, diffraction and refraction

When waves overlap they interfere; when they pass through gaps they spread out. This file covers path difference and coherence, Young's double slits, single-slit diffraction, the diffraction grating, refractive index, Snell's law, total internal reflection and optical fibres, with AQA required practical 2.

### Overview

AS & A for every board (OCR A 5.5.2(g), (h), the grating in the context of stellar spectra, is A-level only but is the same physics). Stationary waves are in file 04. Lenses are in file 22. The quantum (phasor) picture of two-slit interference for OCR B is in file 03.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.3.2, RP2 · Edexcel 65, 66, 71 to 74, 83 to 85, CP8 · OCR A 4.4.2(d), (e), 4.4.3, 4.4.1(f) · OCR B 4.1(a)(ii) to (v), (c)(ii), (iii), (d)(ii), (iv) · Eduqas C3.2(a) to (m), C3.3.
- Fringe spacing is written w = λD/s (AQA) and λ = ax/D (OCR A, Eduqas: a = slit separation, x = fringe spacing). Same equation.
- AQA requires fibre optics with material and modal dispersion, pulse broadening and absorption; Eduqas C3.3(g), (h) asks for multimode dispersion and monomode fibre. Edexcel and OCR A only need total internal reflection.
- Edexcel 83 asks for Huygens' construction to explain diffraction.
- Measuring the refractive index of a solid: Edexcel 74, OCR A 4.4.2(d)(ii) (PAG6), OCR B 4.1(d)(ii), Eduqas specified practical.
- Eduqas C3.3(b) uses n₁v₁ = n₂v₂ as well as Snell's law, and (c) relates Snell's law to the wave model.

### 3.3.2.1 Interference

*Refs: AQA 3.3.2.1, RP2 · Edexcel 65, 66 · OCR A 4.4.3(a) to (g) · OCR B 4.1(a)(ii), 4.1(b)(i), 4.1(c)(iii), 4.1(d)(iv) · Eduqas C3.2(d) to (h), (k) to (m). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Path difference, phase difference and coherence.
- Constructive interference when path difference = nλ; destructive when path difference = (n + ½)λ, for sources in phase.
- Interference and diffraction using a laser as a source of monochromatic, coherent light; safety with lasers.
- Young's double-slit experiment with two coherent sources or one source and double slits; fringe spacing w = λD/s.
- The pattern produced with white light.
- Interference with sound and microwaves.
- How ideas about the nature of light changed over time (Young's experiment supported the wave model).

#### Explained

**Coherence.** Two sources are coherent if they have the same frequency and a constant phase difference. For a visible, stable interference pattern the waves should also have similar amplitudes and (Eduqas C3.2(m)) oscillate in the same direction (same polarisation). Monochromatic means a single wavelength. A laser is monochromatic and coherent across its beam. Two separate lamps are not coherent: light is emitted in random short bursts, so the phase difference changes constantly. Old method: light from one lamp passes through a single slit first, then the double slit, so both slits are lit by the same wavefronts.

**Path difference.** The difference in the distances from the two sources to a point. Phase difference = 2π × path difference / λ.

- Constructive (bright, loud, maximum): path difference = 0, λ, 2λ... = nλ. Waves arrive in phase.
- Destructive (dark, quiet, minimum): path difference = λ/2, 3λ/2... = (n + ½)λ. Waves arrive in antiphase.

(If the sources themselves are in antiphase, these conditions swap.)

**Young's double slit.** Laser light falls on two narrow slits a distance s apart. Each slit diffracts the light so the beams overlap. On a screen a distance D away, bright and dark fringes alternate, equally spaced:

**w = λD/s** (valid when D ≫ s and w; small angles).

To make fringes wider: longer wavelength, larger D, or slits closer together.

**White light.** The central fringe is white (zero path difference for all wavelengths). The other fringes are coloured spectra, violet nearest the centre (shorter λ gives smaller w) and red outermost. Fringes overlap and blur further out.

**Sound and microwaves.** Two loudspeakers driven by the same signal generator give loud and quiet regions as you walk across the room. A microwave transmitter with a double-slit metal barrier gives maxima and minima that a probe detects; with λ ≈ 3 cm, slits a few cm apart give fringes several cm apart.

**Laser safety.** Never look into the beam or point it at anyone; avoid reflections from shiny surfaces (remove watches, rings); use a low-power (class 2) laser; put a warning sign on the door; keep the beam below eye level.

**History.** Newton's particle (corpuscular) model was dominant in the 1700s. Young's experiment (1801) showed interference, which only waves can do, and supported Huygens' wave model. Later, the photoelectric effect showed the wave model is incomplete (file 03).

#### Worked example

1. Laser light of wavelength 633 nm passes through slits 0.40 mm apart. Find the fringe spacing on a screen 2.5 m away.

   w = λD/s = 633 × 10⁻⁹ × 2.5 / 0.40 × 10⁻³ = 3.96 × 10⁻³ m (4.0 mm).

2. Ten fringe spacings measure 22 mm on a screen 1.80 m from slits 0.50 mm apart. Find λ.

   w = 2.2 mm. λ = ws/D = 2.2 × 10⁻³ × 0.50 × 10⁻³ / 1.80 = 6.1 × 10⁻⁷ m.

3. Two loudspeakers in phase emit 680 Hz sound (v = 340 m s⁻¹). A point is 3.00 m from one and 3.75 m from the other. Is it loud or quiet?

   λ = 0.50 m. Path difference = 0.75 m = 1.5λ, so destructive: quiet.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying coherent means "same frequency" only. Constant phase difference is essential.
- Measuring one fringe spacing instead of across many (larger uncertainty).
- Saying fringes get wider when slits get further apart.
- Saying the central fringe is coloured in white light.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 2** (also Edexcel CP8, OCR A 4.4.3(g)(ii) PAG5, OCR B 4.1(d)(iv), Eduqas specified practicals): investigate interference using Young's slits and a diffraction grating.

*Double slit.* Place a laser, double slit and a screen (or wall) in line, with D of 1 to 3 m. Measure D with a tape measure. Measure across as many fringes as possible (e.g. 10 spacings) with a metre rule or vernier calipers and divide. Find s from the slide label or by measuring with a travelling microscope. Calculate λ = ws/D, or vary D and plot w against D (gradient λ/s).

*Grating.* Shine the laser through a grating of known lines per mm, normal to the beam. Measure the distance from the central maximum to the nth-order maximum on each side (x) and the distance to the screen (D). tan θ = x/D. Then nλ = d sin θ with d = 1/(lines per metre). Using both sides and higher orders reduces uncertainty.

The grating gives a more precise value: maxima are sharp and bright and widely spaced, angles are large and easy to measure. Double slit fringes are dim and close together.

### 3.3.2.2 Diffraction

*Refs: AQA 3.3.2.2 · Edexcel 83, 84, 85, CP8 · OCR A 4.4.1(f), 4.4.3(g), 5.5.2(g), (h) · OCR B 4.1(a)(iv), (v), 4.1(c)(iii) · Eduqas C3.2(a), (b), (c), (i), (j). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The appearance of the single-slit diffraction pattern with monochromatic light and with white light.
- How the width of the central maximum depends on wavelength and slit width (qualitatively).
- The plane transmission diffraction grating at normal incidence; derivation of d sin θ = nλ.
- Applications of diffraction gratings.
- (Edexcel 83) Huygens' construction.
- (Eduqas) Little diffraction when λ is much smaller than the gap; spreading through about 180° when λ is similar to or bigger than the gap; why grating maxima are further apart and much sharper than double-slit fringes.

#### Explained

**Diffraction** is the spreading of waves as they pass through a gap or round an obstacle. It is greatest when the gap is about the same size as the wavelength. When the gap is much bigger than λ, the wave passes through with little spreading. A ripple tank shows this: wavefronts through a narrow gap become nearly semicircular.

**Huygens' construction** (Edexcel 83). Every point on a wavefront acts as a source of secondary wavelets that spread out in all directions. The new wavefront is the line tangent to all the wavelets (their envelope). At a gap, only the wavelets from the unblocked part continue, and those at the edges spread into the shadow region, so the wave curves round.

**Single slit.**

- Monochromatic light: a wide, bright central maximum, with narrower, much dimmer maxima either side. The central maximum is twice as wide as each side maximum.
- Narrower slit: more diffraction, so a wider central maximum (but dimmer, as less light passes).
- Longer wavelength: wider central maximum (red spreads more than blue).
- White light: a white central maximum with coloured edges; side maxima are spectra with violet on the inside and red on the outside.

(The first minimum is at sin θ ≈ λ/a for slit width a; useful for qualitative reasoning but not needed for AQA.)

**Diffraction grating.** Thousands of equally spaced parallel slits per mm. Light from every slit interferes. Bright maxima occur only where light from adjacent slits is in phase: path difference between adjacent slits = d sin θ = nλ.

*Derivation.* Adjacent slits are d apart. For light leaving at angle θ to the normal, the extra distance travelled by light from one slit compared with the next is d sin θ (from the right-angled triangle with hypotenuse d). Constructive interference needs this to be a whole number of wavelengths: d sin θ = nλ.

- d = 1/N, where N is the number of lines per metre.
- Maximum order: sin θ ≤ 1, so n_max is the whole-number part of d/λ.
- Because d is tiny, the maxima are far apart; because there are so many slits, each maximum is very sharp and bright (light from thousands of slits cancels almost everywhere else).

**Applications.** Spectroscopy (separating light from a star or a discharge tube into its wavelengths to identify elements, measure redshift); measuring wavelengths of lasers; X-ray crystallography (a crystal acts as a 3D grating for X-rays, revealing atomic spacing, e.g. the structure of DNA); CD and DVD surfaces act as reflection gratings.

#### Worked example

1. A grating has 300 lines per mm. Light of wavelength 589 nm falls on it normally. Find the angle of the second-order maximum and the highest order seen.

   d = 1/300 000 = 3.33 × 10⁻⁶ m. sin θ = 2 × 589 × 10⁻⁹ / 3.33 × 10⁻⁶ = 0.353, θ = 20.7°. d/λ = 5.66, so the highest order is 5.

2. A laser beam through a grating makes first-order spots 0.315 m either side of the centre on a screen 1.20 m away. The grating has 500 lines per mm. Find λ.

   tan θ = 0.315/1.20, θ = 14.7°. d = 2.0 × 10⁻⁶ m. λ = d sin θ = 2.0 × 10⁻⁶ × 0.254 = 5.08 × 10⁻⁷ m.

#### Common mistakes

- Using lines per mm as d.
- Using the distance on the screen as sin θ (it gives tan θ, or use tan⁻¹ first).
- Saying a narrower slit gives a narrower central maximum.
- Rounding n_max up instead of down.

### 3.3.2.3 Refraction at a plane surface

*Refs: AQA 3.3.2.3 · Edexcel 71, 72, 73, 74 · OCR A 4.4.2(d), (e) · OCR B 4.1(a)(iii), 4.1(c)(ii), 4.1(d)(ii) · Eduqas C3.3(a) to (h). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Refractive index n = c/cₛ, where cₛ is the speed of light in the substance; n of air ≈ 1.
- Snell's law: n₁ sin θ₁ = n₂ sin θ₂.
- Total internal reflection and the critical angle: sin θc = n₂/n₁.
- Step-index optical fibres: the core and the cladding, and why cladding is needed.
- Material and modal dispersion; pulse broadening and absorption, and their consequences (AQA; Eduqas multimode dispersion and monomode fibre).
- (OCR A, Edexcel, Eduqas, OCR B) How to measure refractive index.

#### Explained

**Refractive index.** Light slows down in a denser material. n = c/v, so n ≥ 1. Water 1.33, glass about 1.5, diamond 2.42. Since v = fλ and f does not change at a boundary, λ also falls in a denser medium. Equivalent forms: n₁v₁ = n₂v₂ (Eduqas C3.3(b)).

**Snell's law.** n₁ sin θ₁ = n₂ sin θ₂, angles measured from the normal. Going into a higher n, light bends towards the normal; into a lower n, away from it. OCR B writes n = sin i / sin r = c₁/c₂.

**Wave explanation** (OCR B 4.1(a)(iii), Eduqas C3.3(c)). When a wavefront meets a boundary at an angle, the part that enters the slower medium first travels more slowly, while the rest is still in the fast medium. The wavefront swivels and so the direction changes. In the time t that one end travels v₁t to reach the boundary, the other end travels v₂t in the new medium. Geometry gives sin θ₁/sin θ₂ = v₁/v₂, which is Snell's law.

**Total internal reflection (TIR).** Going from a higher n to a lower n, the refracted ray bends away from the normal. At the critical angle θc the refracted ray travels along the boundary (θ₂ = 90°): n₁ sin θc = n₂, so **sin θc = n₂/n₁** (or 1/n into air). If the angle of incidence exceeds θc, all the light is reflected. Conditions: light travelling into a lower refractive index, and angle of incidence > θc.

**Step-index optical fibres.** A thin glass core (high n) is surrounded by cladding of slightly lower n. Light travels along the core by repeated total internal reflection at the core–cladding boundary. The cladding:

- protects the core from scratches, which would let light escape;
- stops signal crossover (crosstalk) between touching fibres;
- keeps n₂ fixed, so the critical angle is predictable.

Because n_core and n_cladding are close, θc is large (often over 80°), so only rays travelling nearly along the axis are guided.

**Dispersion and pulse broadening.** Data is sent as pulses of light. If a pulse spreads out in time as it travels (pulse broadening), neighbouring pulses can overlap, so the receiver cannot tell them apart. This limits the bit rate and the distance before the signal must be regenerated.

- *Modal (multipath) dispersion*: rays entering at different angles take different paths; rays bouncing at steep angles travel further than rays along the axis, so they arrive later. Reduce by making the core very narrow (monomode fibre, so only the axial path is possible) or by using a smaller difference between core and cladding n.
- *Material (chromatic) dispersion*: n depends on wavelength, so different wavelengths travel at different speeds. Violet light is slower than red in glass. Reduce by using monochromatic light (a laser).

**Absorption.** Some energy is absorbed by the glass, so the pulse amplitude falls along the fibre. Pulses get smaller and wider; repeaters (regenerators) are needed at intervals. Use very pure glass and wavelengths (infrared around 1.3 to 1.55 μm) where absorption is low.

**Monomode fibres** (Eduqas C3.3(h)). The core is only a few micrometres across, so light can only travel straight along it; modal dispersion is eliminated, allowing very high data rates over long distances.

#### Worked example

1. Light enters glass (n = 1.52) from air at 40°. Find the angle of refraction and the speed in glass.

   sin θ₂ = sin 40°/1.52 = 0.423, θ₂ = 25.0°. v = c/n = 1.97 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹.

2. A fibre has a core of n = 1.48 and cladding of n = 1.46. Find the critical angle.

   sin θc = 1.46/1.48 = 0.9865, θc = 80.6°.

3. (Eduqas) A fibre is 2.0 km long with core n = 1.50. Compare the time for an axial ray with that of a ray hitting the boundary at exactly the critical angle when the cladding has n = 1.45.

   Axial: t = L/v = 2000 × 1.50 / 3.00 × 10⁸ = 1.000 × 10⁻⁵ s. Critical ray: path length = L/sin θc, where sin θc = 1.45/1.50 = 0.967; path = 2069 m, t = 1.034 × 10⁻⁵ s. Difference ≈ 0.34 μs: pulses must be at least this far apart.

#### Common mistakes

- Measuring angles from the surface instead of the normal.
- Saying TIR happens when light goes into a denser medium.
- Writing sin θc = n₁/n₂ (upside down, giving sin > 1).
- Saying the cladding "reflects" the light; TIR happens at the boundary because cladding has lower n.
- Mixing up modal (different paths) and material (different wavelengths) dispersion.

#### Practical

**Measuring refractive index of a glass block** (Edexcel 74, OCR A PAG6, OCR B 4.1(d)(ii), Eduqas specified practical). Place a rectangular block on paper and draw round it. Shine a narrow ray from a ray box into one side at a measured angle of incidence i. Mark the ray where it leaves, remove the block and join the points to draw the refracted ray inside. Measure r with a protractor. Repeat for several angles from 10° to 70°. Plot sin i against sin r: straight line through the origin, gradient n. A semicircular block can be used to find the critical angle directly (aim the ray at the centre of the flat face, from inside the curved side, and increase the angle until the refracted ray just grazes the surface): n = 1/sin C.

### Check questions

1. State what is meant by coherent sources, and explain why two separate light bulbs cannot produce an observable interference pattern. (3 marks)
2. In a double-slit experiment, λ = 520 nm, s = 0.25 mm and D = 1.60 m. Calculate the fringe spacing. Describe the change if the slit separation is halved. (3 marks)
3. Describe the pattern formed when white light passes through a double slit. (3 marks)
4. Derive the equation d sin θ = nλ for a diffraction grating. (3 marks)
5. A grating with 600 lines per mm is used with light of wavelength 450 nm. Calculate the number of maxima seen on the screen. (3 marks)
6. Explain why a grating gives a more accurate value of wavelength than a double slit. (2 marks)
7. Light in glass (n = 1.50) meets a boundary with water (n = 1.33). Calculate the critical angle. (2 marks)
8. Explain how modal dispersion arises in an optical fibre and state one way to reduce it. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Same frequency (and wavelength) and constant phase difference (1); light from bulbs is emitted in random bursts (1); so phase difference changes randomly / fringes move too fast to see (1).
2. w = 520 × 10⁻⁹ × 1.60 / 0.25 × 10⁻³ = 3.3 mm (2); fringe spacing doubles (1).
3. Central white fringe (1); other fringes are coloured / spectra (1); violet / blue on the inside nearest centre, red outside; fringes overlap further out (1).
4. Adjacent slits separated by d; path difference between them = d sin θ (1, with diagram); for a bright maximum light from adjacent slits must arrive in phase (1); so path difference = nλ (1).
5. d = 1.67 × 10⁻⁶ m (1); d/λ = 3.7, so n_max = 3 (1); 3 each side + central = 7 maxima (1).
6. Grating maxima are sharper / brighter (1); further apart / larger angles, so smaller percentage uncertainty in measurement (1).
7. sin C = 1.33/1.50 = 0.887 (1); C = 62.5° (1).
8. Rays travel at different angles / take different paths (1); longer paths take longer, so pulse spreads / broadens (1); use monomode (narrow core) fibre / reduce difference in n of core and cladding (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/26-turning-points-electrons-and-the-nature-of-light.md -->

## Turning points: the discovery of the electron, and wave–particle duality

Two great shifts in physics: the discovery that atoms contain electrons, and the discovery that light and matter behave as both waves and particles. This file covers cathode rays, thermionic emission, measuring the specific charge of the electron, Millikan's oil-drop experiment, Newton's and Huygens' theories of light, Young's experiment, Maxwell's electromagnetic waves, Hertz and Fizeau, the ultraviolet catastrophe and Planck's quanta, Einstein's photon explanation, de Broglie waves, and electron microscopes (TEM and STM).

### Overview

AQA option 3.12.1 and 3.12.2 (A-level only). The core physics of the photoelectric effect and electron diffraction is in file 03; interference in file 05; electric and magnetic forces on charges in files 15 and 17; Stokes' law in file 30; black-body radiation in file 20.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.12.1, 3.12.2 · Edexcel 90, 132 · OCR B 6.1.2(a)(iv), 5.2.2(a)(ii) · Eduqas C3.2(e).
- Edexcel 132: thermionic emission and accelerating electrons with electric and magnetic fields. Edexcel 90: how the wave and photon models of light developed over time.
- OCR B 6.1.2(a)(iv): evidence for the discreteness of electron charge (Millikan). OCR B 5.2.2(a)(ii): thermionic emission as an activation-energy process (Boltzmann factor, file 13).
- Eduqas C3.2(e): the historical importance of Young's experiment.

### 3.12.1.1 Cathode rays

*Refs: AQA 3.12.1.1. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- How cathode rays are produced in a discharge tube.

#### Explained

A discharge tube is a glass tube of gas at low pressure with a metal electrode at each end, connected to a high p.d. (several kV). The few ions present in the gas are accelerated by the field; positive ions hit the cathode and knock out electrons; electrons collide with gas atoms, ionising more (an avalanche) and exciting them so the gas glows. Some electrons stream from the cathode towards the anode: these were called cathode rays. Experiments showed cathode rays travel in straight lines (casting sharp shadows), carry negative charge, can turn a paddle wheel (they have momentum) and are deflected by electric and magnetic fields. Thomson (1897) showed they are particles with a fixed charge-to-mass ratio: electrons.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying cathode rays are electromagnetic waves.

### 3.12.1.2 Thermionic emission of electrons

*Refs: AQA 3.12.1.2 · Edexcel 132 · OCR B 5.2.2(a)(ii). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The principle of thermionic emission.
- The work done on an electron accelerated through a p.d. V: ½mv² = eV.

#### Explained

When a metal filament is heated (by passing a current through it), some free electrons gain enough kinetic energy to escape from the surface (they need more energy than the work function). This is thermionic emission. The number escaping rises very steeply with temperature (OCR B: a Boltzmann-factor process). In a vacuum tube, an anode at a high positive potential relative to the filament (cathode) accelerates the emitted electrons. Each gains kinetic energy eV (the electrons start with negligible speed):

½mv² = eV, so v = √(2eV/m)

A hole in the anode lets a narrow beam of electrons through (an electron gun), used in old CRT televisions, electron diffraction tubes, X-ray tubes and electron microscopes. The tube must be evacuated so electrons do not collide with gas molecules.

#### Worked example

1. Find the speed of an electron accelerated through 2500 V.

   v = √(2 × 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ × 2500/9.11 × 10⁻³¹) = 2.96 × 10⁷ m s⁻¹.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying electrons are emitted because the filament is negative (they are emitted because it is hot; the anode p.d. only accelerates them).

### 3.12.1.3 Specific charge of the electron

*Refs: AQA 3.12.1.3 · Edexcel 132. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- One method of determining e/m.
- The significance of Thomson's measurement.
- Comparison with the specific charge of the hydrogen ion.

#### Explained

**Method: fine-beam tube.** An electron gun in a glass bulb with low-pressure gas (so the beam path glows) is placed between a pair of Helmholtz coils that give a uniform magnetic field B perpendicular to the beam. The electrons move in a circle of radius r (measured with a scale).

- Magnetic force provides centripetal force: Bev = mv²/r, so v = Ber/m.
- Energy from the gun: ½mv² = eV.
- Combining: **e/m = 2V/(B²r²)**.

B is found from the coil current (calibrated, or with a Hall probe). Measure r for several V or B.

**Alternative: crossed fields (Thomson's method).** In a deflection tube, adjust an electric field E (between plates) and a magnetic field B so the beam goes straight: eE = Bev, giving v = E/B. Then switch off E and measure the radius in B alone (or use ½mv² = eV): e/m = v/(Br) = E/(B²r).

**Significance.** Thomson found e/m = 1.76 × 10¹¹ C kg⁻¹, the same whatever cathode metal or gas was used. So electrons are a component of all atoms: atoms are not indivisible. The value was about 1800 times larger than the specific charge of the hydrogen ion (9.6 × 10⁷ C kg⁻¹, the largest then known). Assuming the electron's charge is similar in size to the hydrogen ion's, the electron must be about 1800 times lighter: a subatomic particle.

#### Worked example

1. In a fine-beam tube, electrons accelerated through 250 V move in a circle of radius 0.050 m in a field of 1.07 mT. Find e/m.

   e/m = 2 × 250/((1.07 × 10⁻³)² × 0.050²) = 500/(1.145 × 10⁻⁶ × 2.5 × 10⁻³) = 1.75 × 10¹¹ C kg⁻¹.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying Thomson measured e and m separately.

### 3.12.1.4 Principle of Millikan's determination of the electronic charge, e

*Refs: AQA 3.12.1.4 · OCR B 6.1.2(a)(iv). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The condition for holding a charged oil drop stationary between oppositely charged plates: QV/d = mg.
- The motion of a falling drop with and without an electric field; using terminal speed to find the drop's mass and charge.
- Stokes' law, F = 6πηrv, to find the drop's radius.
- The significance of Millikan's results: quantisation of charge.

#### Explained

Fine oil drops are sprayed between two horizontal metal plates a distance d apart; some become charged by friction (or by X-rays ionising the air). The drops are viewed through a microscope.

**Step 1: field off.** A drop falls and quickly reaches terminal speed v₁, where its weight equals the viscous drag (ignoring upthrust): mg = 6πηrv₁. With m = (4/3)πr³ρ:

(4/3)πr³ρg = 6πηrv₁ → r = √(9ηv₁/(2ρg))

Measuring v₁ (time to fall between two marks on the eyepiece scale) gives r and so m.

**Step 2: field on.** A p.d. V is applied so the electric force QV/d acts upwards. Adjust V until the drop is held stationary: QV/d = mg, so Q = mgd/V. (Alternatively measure the new terminal speed with the field on.)

**Results.** Every charge measured was a whole-number multiple of 1.6 × 10⁻¹⁹ C. So charge is quantised: it comes in units of e, the charge of the electron. Combined with Thomson's e/m, this gave the mass of the electron.

#### Worked example

1. An oil drop (ρ = 880 kg m⁻³) falls at terminal speed 1.2 × 10⁻⁴ m s⁻¹ in air (η = 1.8 × 10⁻⁵ Pa s). Find its radius and mass. It is held still between plates 6.0 mm apart by 405 V. Find its charge.

   r = √(9 × 1.8 × 10⁻⁵ × 1.2 × 10⁻⁴/(2 × 880 × 9.81)) = √(1.13 × 10⁻¹²) = 1.06 × 10⁻⁶ m. m = (4/3)π(1.06 × 10⁻⁶)³ × 880 = 4.39 × 10⁻¹⁵ kg. Q = mgd/V = 4.39 × 10⁻¹⁵ × 9.81 × 6.0 × 10⁻³/405 = 6.4 × 10⁻¹⁹ C = 4e.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting that the drop must be at terminal speed for Stokes' law to give its radius.

### 3.12.2.1 Newton's corpuscular theory of light

*Refs: AQA 3.12.2.1 · Edexcel 90. Level: A-level only (Edexcel AS & A).*

#### What you need to know

- Newton's corpuscular theory compared with Huygens' wave theory in general terms.
- Why Newton's theory was preferred.

#### Explained

**Newton (corpuscles).** Light is a stream of tiny particles. Reflection: particles bounce elastically off a surface (angle of incidence = angle of reflection). Refraction: particles are attracted towards a denser medium as they reach the surface, so their velocity component perpendicular to the surface increases; they bend towards the normal and travel *faster* in the denser medium.

**Huygens (waves).** Light is a wave; each point on a wavefront is a source of secondary wavelets. Refraction happens because light travels *slower* in the denser medium, so wavefronts bend. Huygens's theory also predicted diffraction and interference.

**Why Newton's was preferred** (for about 100 years): Newton's great reputation (from his work on gravity and mechanics); light seemed to travel in straight lines with sharp shadows (diffraction of light is very small and hard to observe because λ is tiny); the speed of light in water could not yet be measured, so the two predictions could not be tested; the wave theory needed a medium (the "aether") to carry the waves through space; and Huygens's theory did not explain polarisation well at the time.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying Newton's theory predicted light slows down in glass (it predicted light speeds up).

### 3.12.2.2 Significance of Young's double slits experiment

*Refs: AQA 3.12.2.2 · Eduqas C3.2(e). Level: A-level only (Eduqas AS & A).*

#### What you need to know

- Explanation of the fringes in general terms (no calculations).
- The delayed acceptance of Huygens' wave theory.

#### Explained

Young (1801) passed light from a single source through two narrow slits. Where light from the two slits overlapped, there were bright and dark fringes. Bright fringes: waves arrive in phase (constructive interference). Dark fringes: waves arrive in antiphase and cancel (destructive). Particles from two slits would simply add, giving two bright patches, never darkness where light from both arrives. Interference is a property of waves, so the experiment strongly supported the wave theory.

Acceptance was slow: Newton's authority, criticism of Young's work, and the lack of a measurement of the speed of light in water. It was only after Fresnel's mathematical wave theory explained diffraction (1810s) and Foucault and Fizeau showed (1850) that light is slower in water than in air, as the wave theory predicted, that the wave theory was fully accepted.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying Young's experiment was immediately accepted as proof of the wave theory.

### 3.12.2.3 Electromagnetic waves

*Refs: AQA 3.12.2.3. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The nature of electromagnetic waves.
- Maxwell's formula c = 1/√(μ₀ε₀); μ₀ relates to the magnetic flux density from a current, ε₀ to the electric field of a charge.
- Hertz's discovery of radio waves, including measuring their speed.
- Fizeau's determination of the speed of light and its implications.

#### Explained

**Maxwell (1860s).** A changing electric field produces a magnetic field and a changing magnetic field produces an electric field. These can sustain each other and travel through space as an electromagnetic wave: oscillating electric and magnetic fields, perpendicular to each other and to the direction of travel (transverse). Maxwell calculated their speed:

c = 1/√(μ₀ε₀) = 1/√(4π × 10⁻⁷ × 8.85 × 10⁻¹²) = 3.00 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹

This matched the measured speed of light, so he concluded that light is an electromagnetic wave. ε₀ (permittivity of free space) sets the strength of electric fields from charges (Coulomb's law); μ₀ (permeability of free space) sets the strength of magnetic fields from currents.

**Hertz (1887).** Produced radio waves with a spark gap connected to an induction coil (high-voltage sparks oscillating at high frequency) and detected them with a loop of wire with a small gap, in which sparks appeared. He showed they reflect, refract, can be polarised (the detector only worked at certain orientations) and form stationary waves when reflected from a metal sheet. Measuring the node spacing gave λ; with the known frequency, v = fλ came out equal to c, supporting Maxwell.

**Fizeau (1849).** Light passed through a gap between the teeth of a rapidly rotating toothed wheel, travelled about 8.6 km to a mirror and back. At a certain speed of rotation, the returning light was blocked by the next tooth. The travel time 2d/c equals the time for the wheel to turn from a gap to a tooth: 1/(2Nf) for N teeth rotating at f revolutions per second. So c = 4dNf. He found c ≈ 3.1 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹, close to Maxwell's prediction, which confirmed that light is an electromagnetic wave.

#### Worked example

1. Fizeau's wheel had 720 teeth and the mirror was 8.63 km away. Light was first blocked at 12.6 revolutions per second. Find c.

   c = 4dNf = 4 × 8630 × 720 × 12.6 = 3.13 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying Hertz measured the speed of light directly (he measured radio waves).
- Mixing up ε₀ (electric fields) and μ₀ (magnetic fields).

### 3.12.2.4 The discovery of photoelectricity

*Refs: AQA 3.12.2.4 · Edexcel 90. Level: A-level only (Edexcel AS & A).*

#### What you need to know

- The ultraviolet catastrophe and black-body radiation.
- Planck's interpretation in terms of quanta.
- The failure of classical wave theory to explain photoelectricity.
- Einstein's explanation and its significance for the nature of electromagnetic radiation.

#### Explained

**Ultraviolet catastrophe.** Classical wave theory predicted that a black body should emit more and more intensity at shorter wavelengths, without limit: an infinite total energy, concentrated in the ultraviolet. Experiments showed the intensity rises to a peak and then falls to zero at short wavelengths (file 20).

**Planck (1900).** He assumed that the energy of the oscillators emitting the radiation could only take values in steps (quanta) of E = hf. At high frequency, each quantum is large, so few oscillators have enough energy to emit, and the intensity drops. His formula matched experiment exactly. Planck saw this as a mathematical device, not a statement about light itself.

**Photoelectric effect** (file 03). Wave theory predicted that any frequency of light would eventually release electrons, and that brighter light would give faster electrons. Experiments showed a threshold frequency, instant emission, and kinetic energy depending only on frequency.

**Einstein (1905).** Light itself consists of quanta (photons), each of energy hf, and each photon is absorbed by one electron: hf = φ + Ek(max). This explained all the observations. Significance: electromagnetic radiation has a particle nature as well as a wave nature. Millikan's careful experiments (1916) confirmed Einstein's equation, and Einstein received the Nobel Prize for it.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying Planck proposed photons (Einstein did; Planck quantised the oscillators' energy).

### 3.12.2.5 Wave–particle duality

*Refs: AQA 3.12.2.5. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- De Broglie's hypothesis p = h/λ; λ = h/√(2meV) for an electron accelerated through V.
- Low-energy electron diffraction; qualitative effect of electron speed on the pattern.

#### Explained

De Broglie (1924) proposed that if light waves have particle properties, particles should have wave properties, with λ = h/p. For an electron accelerated from rest through V: p = √(2meV), so λ = h/√(2meV).

Davisson and Germer (1927) fired low-energy electrons (tens of eV) at a nickel crystal and found strong reflection only at certain angles: a diffraction pattern, with the crystal lattice acting as a grating. Thomson (G.P.) passed electrons through thin metal foil and saw rings. At higher speeds (higher V), λ is shorter, so the diffraction angles are smaller and the pattern shrinks.

#### Worked example

1. Find the de Broglie wavelength of electrons accelerated through 54 V (as in Davisson and Germer).

   λ = 6.63 × 10⁻³⁴/√(2 × 9.11 × 10⁻³¹ × 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ × 54) = 6.63 × 10⁻³⁴/3.97 × 10⁻²⁴ = 1.67 × 10⁻¹⁰ m, similar to atomic spacing.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying faster electrons give larger diffraction angles.

### 3.12.2.6 Electron microscopes

*Refs: AQA 3.12.2.6. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Estimating the anode voltage needed for wavelengths of the order of atomic size.
- The principle of the transmission electron microscope (TEM).
- The principle of the scanning tunnelling microscope (STM).

#### Explained

The resolution of any microscope is limited by diffraction to about the wavelength used. Light (λ ≈ 500 nm) cannot resolve details smaller than about 200 nm. Electrons with a wavelength of about 0.1 nm could resolve atoms. From λ = h/√(2meV), λ = 0.1 nm needs V ≈ 150 V; in practice TEMs use 50 to 200 kV (much shorter λ, to penetrate the sample and reduce other limits).
