**TEM.** An electron gun produces a beam in a vacuum. Magnetic lenses (coils whose fields bend electron paths) focus the beam:

1. A condenser lens forms a parallel beam on the thin sample.
2. Electrons pass through the sample; denser parts scatter more electrons, so fewer pass through.
3. An objective lens forms a magnified image, and a projector lens magnifies it further onto a fluorescent screen or detector.

Image detail is limited by: the sample thickness (electrons slow down in it, increasing λ), aberrations of the magnetic lenses, and variations in electron speed (so they are not all focused at the same point).

**STM.** A very sharp conducting tip (ending in one atom) is held about 1 nm above a conducting surface, with a small p.d. between them. Electrons cannot classically cross the gap, but because of their wave nature they can "tunnel" through it. The tunnelling current is extremely sensitive to the gap width (it falls exponentially as the gap grows). The tip is scanned across the surface by piezoelectric controls.

- *Constant height mode*: the tip stays at a fixed height; changes in current map the surface.
- *Constant current mode*: a feedback system moves the tip up and down to keep the current constant; the tip's height traces the surface.

It can image individual atoms (and move them).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a TEM uses glass lenses.
- Saying STM tunnelling depends on electrons hitting the surface.

### Check questions

1. Explain how thermionic emission produces a beam of electrons, and calculate the speed of electrons accelerated through 400 V. (4 marks)
2. Describe how e/m for the electron can be found using a fine-beam tube and derive the expression used. (6 marks)
3. Explain the significance of Thomson's value of e/m. (3 marks)
4. In Millikan's experiment, explain how the mass of a drop is found. (4 marks)
5. Give two reasons why Newton's corpuscular theory was preferred to Huygens' wave theory. (2 marks)
6. Explain how Maxwell's prediction of c and Hertz's experiments supported the idea that light is an electromagnetic wave. (4 marks)
7. Explain what is meant by the ultraviolet catastrophe and how Planck resolved it. (3 marks)
8. Estimate the accelerating voltage needed to give electrons a wavelength of 0.050 nm. (3 marks)
9. Describe the operation of a scanning tunnelling microscope in constant current mode. (4 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Filament heated by current; electrons gain enough energy to leave the metal (1); accelerated by p.d. to anode in vacuum; beam through hole in anode (1); v = √(2eV/m) (1) = 1.19 × 10⁷ m s⁻¹ (1).
2. Electron gun with accelerating p.d. V; Helmholtz coils give uniform B perpendicular to beam (1); measure radius of circular path (1); Bev = mv²/r (1); ½mv² = eV (1); e/m = 2V/(B²r²) (1); measure B from coil current / Hall probe; repeat for different values (1).
3. Same value for all cathode materials / gases, so electrons are in all atoms (1); about 1800 times specific charge of H⁺ (1); so electron much lighter than an atom: subatomic particle (1).
4. With no field, drop falls at terminal speed (1); weight = viscous drag: mg = 6πηrv (1); m = (4/3)πr³ρ gives r = √(9ηv/2ρg) (1); then m from r and density (1).
5. Any two: Newton's reputation; diffraction of light not observed / sharp shadows; speed of light in water could not be measured; wave theory needed an aether.
6. Maxwell calculated speed of EM waves as 1/√(μ₀ε₀) (1); equal to the measured speed of light (1); Hertz produced and detected radio waves showing wave behaviour (reflection, polarisation, stationary waves) (1); measured their speed as c (1).
7. Classical theory predicted intensity increasing without limit at short wavelength / UV (1); experimental curve falls to zero (1); Planck: energy emitted in quanta E = hf, so high-frequency emission is suppressed (1).
8. λ = h/√(2meV), so V = h²/(2meλ²) (1) = (6.63 × 10⁻³⁴)²/(2 × 9.11 × 10⁻³¹ × 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ × (5.0 × 10⁻¹¹)²) (1) = 600 V (1).
9. Sharp tip close to surface with small p.d. (1); electrons tunnel across the gap, giving a current (1); current depends very strongly on gap width (1); feedback moves tip up and down to keep current constant as it scans; tip height maps the surface (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/03-photons-photoelectric-effect-and-energy-levels.md -->

## Photons, the photoelectric effect, energy levels and wave–particle duality

Light sometimes behaves as a wave and sometimes as a stream of particles (photons); electrons sometimes behave as particles and sometimes as waves. This file covers the evidence for each: the photoelectric effect, excitation and ionisation, line spectra and energy levels, and electron diffraction with the de Broglie wavelength.

### Overview

AS & A for every board, except OCR A 5.5.2 (energy levels and spectra in the context of stars), which is A-level only. Interference and diffraction of light are in file 05. Black-body radiation, Wien's law and Stefan's law are in file 20. The electron microscope and the history of the wave and particle models (AQA option 3.12) are in file 26.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.2.2 · Edexcel 86, 87, 90 to 96 · OCR A 4.5.1 to 4.5.3, 5.5.2(a) to (f) · OCR B 4.1(a)(vi) to (viii), 6.2.1(a)(iii), (iv) · Eduqas C3.4.
- AQA asks specifically about the fluorescent tube (excitation then fluorescence). Other boards do not name it.
- AQA does not require the experimental measurement of stopping potential; Eduqas C3.4(c) expects you to know how a vacuum photocell measures Ek max, and C3.4(d) the graph of Ek max against f.
- Determining the Planck constant using LEDs: OCR A 4.5.1(e) (PAG6), OCR B 4.1(d)(vi) (PAG6), Eduqas specified practical. Not an AQA required practical.
- OCR B 4.1(a)(vii) uses the "sum over paths" or phasor picture of quantum behaviour (Feynman's approach); no other board does. OCR B 6.2.1(a)(iv) and (b)(iii) model an atom as electron standing waves in a box.
- Eduqas C3.4(m) and (n): photon momentum p = h/λ and radiation pressure on a surface.
- Edexcel 89 (pulse-echo) is in file 23.

### 3.2.2.1 The photoelectric effect

*Refs: AQA 3.2.2.1 · Edexcel 90 to 95 · OCR A 4.5.1(a) to (e), 4.5.2(a) to (f) · OCR B 4.1(a)(vi), 4.1(b)(i), 4.1(c)(iv), 4.1(d)(vi) · Eduqas C3.4(a) to (g). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- When light of high enough frequency shines on a metal surface, electrons (photoelectrons) are emitted.
- There is a threshold frequency below which no electrons are emitted, however bright the light. Only the photon model explains this.
- The work function φ is the minimum energy needed to free an electron from the surface.
- Einstein's photoelectric equation: hf = φ + Ek(max).
- Stopping potential: the p.d. that just stops the most energetic photoelectrons, so eVs = Ek(max).
- (Edexcel, OCR A, Eduqas) The rate of emission is proportional to intensity; Ek(max) does not depend on intensity. A graph of Ek(max) against f is a straight line, gradient h, x-intercept f₀, y-intercept −φ.
- (OCR A, OCR B, Eduqas) Planck constant from LEDs.

#### Explained

**Photons.** The energy of a photon is E = hf = hc/λ. A photon is a quantum (a discrete packet) of electromagnetic energy. Each photon interacts with one electron, all at once (one-to-one).

**Observations that the wave model cannot explain.**

1. Below a threshold frequency f₀ no electrons are emitted, whatever the intensity. Wave model: any frequency should work if you wait long enough for the energy to build up.
2. Above f₀, emission starts at once, even in very dim light. Wave model: there should be a delay.
3. The maximum kinetic energy of the electrons depends on frequency, not on intensity. Wave model: brighter light should give faster electrons.
4. The number of electrons emitted per second is proportional to intensity.

**Photon explanation.** Each electron absorbs one whole photon. If hf < φ, no single photon has enough energy to free an electron, and electrons cannot save up energy from several photons, so nothing is emitted. If hf ≥ φ, the electron escapes immediately, and its energy left over is kinetic. Electrons from deeper in the metal lose some energy on the way out, so hf − φ is the maximum kinetic energy, not every electron's kinetic energy. A brighter beam has more photons per second, so more electrons per second, but each photon still has the same energy.

**Equations.**

- hf = φ + Ek(max)
- At threshold, Ek(max) = 0, so φ = hf₀ and f₀ = φ/h.
- Ek(max) = eVs, where Vs is the stopping potential.

**The Ek(max)–f graph.** Ek(max) = hf − φ has the form y = mx + c. Gradient = h for every metal. Intercept on the f-axis = f₀. Intercept on the Ek axis (extrapolated) = −φ. Different metals give parallel lines.

**Measuring Ek(max)** (Eduqas C3.4(c)). In a vacuum photocell, light falls on a curved metal cathode; a central wire anode collects electrons. Make the anode negative relative to the cathode and increase the reverse p.d. until the current (on a sensitive meter) falls to zero. That p.d. is Vs and Ek(max) = eVs (in eV, numerically equal to Vs in volts).

**Gold-leaf electroscope demonstration.** A negatively charged zinc plate on an electroscope: UV light makes the leaf fall (electrons emitted); bright visible light does nothing, because visible photons have less energy than the work function of zinc.

**Planck constant using LEDs** (OCR A PAG6, OCR B, Eduqas). An LED only lights when the p.d. across it reaches a threshold V₀ at which each electron crossing the junction has enough energy to make one photon: eV₀ ≈ hc/λ. Measure V₀ for several LEDs of known wavelength (increase p.d. slowly until the LED just glows, viewed through a tube to block room light, or find where the I–V graph starts to rise). Plot V₀ against 1/λ: gradient = hc/e, so h = gradient × e / c.

#### Worked example

1. Light of wavelength 250 nm falls on a metal with work function 3.2 eV. Find Ek(max) and the stopping potential.

   E = hc/λ = (6.63 × 10⁻³⁴ × 3.00 × 10⁸)/(250 × 10⁻⁹) = 7.96 × 10⁻¹⁹ J = 4.97 eV. Ek(max) = 4.97 − 3.2 = 1.77 eV = 2.8 × 10⁻¹⁹ J. Stopping potential = 1.8 V.

2. Find the threshold wavelength for that metal.

   φ = 3.2 × 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ = 5.12 × 10⁻¹⁹ J. λ₀ = hc/φ = 1.989 × 10⁻²⁵ / 5.12 × 10⁻¹⁹ = 3.9 × 10⁻⁷ m (388 nm, violet).

3. An LED of wavelength 630 nm just lights at 1.95 V. Estimate h.

   h = eV₀λ/c = 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ × 1.95 × 630 × 10⁻⁹ / 3.00 × 10⁸ = 6.6 × 10⁻³⁴ J s.

4. A 2.0 mW laser emits light of wavelength 532 nm. How many photons does it emit per second?

   E per photon = 1.989 × 10⁻²⁵ / 532 × 10⁻⁹ = 3.74 × 10⁻¹⁹ J. N = P/E = 2.0 × 10⁻³ / 3.74 × 10⁻¹⁹ = 5.3 × 10¹⁵ s⁻¹.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying brighter light gives electrons more energy.
- Saying every photoelectron has kinetic energy hf − φ; that is the maximum.
- Saying electrons absorb several photons to build up energy.
- Forgetting to convert eV to J (or the reverse) before combining with hf.
- Calling the threshold frequency "the frequency of the electrons".

### 3.2.2.2 Collisions of electrons with atoms

*Refs: AQA 3.2.2.2 · Edexcel 94 · OCR A 4.5.1(d) · Eduqas C3.4(k). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Ionisation: an electron is removed from an atom.
- Excitation: an electron moves to a higher energy level in the atom.
- How a fluorescent tube works in terms of excitation and ionisation.
- The electronvolt and how to convert between eV and J.

#### Explained

**The electronvolt.** 1 eV is the energy transferred when one electron moves through a p.d. of 1 V: 1 eV = 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ J. An electron accelerated through V volts gains eV joules, or V electronvolts, of kinetic energy.

**Excitation.** A free electron (or a photon) can give energy to an orbital electron. If the energy transferred exactly matches the gap between two levels, the orbital electron moves up: the atom is excited. A colliding free electron can give only part of its energy and carry on with the rest; a photon must be absorbed whole and must have exactly the right energy.

**Ionisation.** If an orbital electron receives at least the ionisation energy, it leaves the atom completely, and the atom becomes a positive ion. The ionisation energy of hydrogen in its ground state is 13.6 eV.

**De-excitation.** Excited atoms are unstable. The electron drops back to a lower level, emitting a photon of energy equal to the difference between the levels. It may drop in one step or several.

**Fluorescent tube.**

1. A high p.d. across the tube accelerates free electrons through low-pressure mercury vapour.
2. The fast electrons collide with mercury atoms, exciting (and ionising) them. Ionisation releases more free electrons, keeping the current going.
3. The excited mercury atoms de-excite, emitting mostly ultraviolet photons.
4. The UV photons are absorbed by the phosphor coating on the inside of the glass, exciting its atoms to high levels.
5. The phosphor atoms de-excite in several smaller steps, emitting lower-energy visible photons. This is fluorescence.

#### Worked example

1. An electron is accelerated through 12 V and hits a hydrogen atom in its ground state (−13.6 eV). Levels are −13.6, −3.4, −1.5 and −0.85 eV. Which excitations are possible?

   The electron has 12 eV. −13.6 → −3.4 needs 10.2 eV: possible. −13.6 → −1.5 needs 12.1 eV: not possible. So only n = 1 to n = 2. The electron leaves with 12 − 10.2 = 1.8 eV.

2. Convert 5.0 × 10⁻¹⁸ J to eV.

   5.0 × 10⁻¹⁸ / 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ = 31 eV.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying a photon can give part of its energy to an atom (in excitation it must be absorbed whole).
- Saying mercury atoms emit visible light directly in a fluorescent tube.
- Treating the electronvolt as a unit of p.d.

### 3.2.2.3 Energy levels and photon emission

*Refs: AQA 3.2.2.3 · Edexcel 96 · OCR A 5.5.2(a) to (f) · OCR B 4.1(a)(vi), 6.2.1(a)(iii) · Eduqas C3.4(h), (i), (j), (k). Level: AS & A (OCR A 5.5.2: A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- Line spectra (such as that of atomic hydrogen) are evidence that electrons in atoms occupy discrete energy levels.
- The photon emitted or absorbed in a transition has hf = E₁ − E₂.
- Energy levels may be given in J or eV, and are negative (zero is the energy of a free electron at rest).
- (OCR A, Eduqas) Emission and absorption line spectra and continuous spectra, and how each is produced; seeing them with a diffraction grating; each element has its own pattern of lines.
- (Eduqas) Finding ionisation energy from an energy level diagram.

#### Explained

**Energy level diagrams.** Electrons in an atom can only have certain energies. The lowest is the ground state (n = 1). Levels are negative because energy must be supplied to free the electron; the ionisation level is 0. Higher levels crowd together towards 0.

**Emission.** An electron dropping from E₁ to a lower E₂ emits one photon: hf = E₁ − E₂. Since only certain gaps exist, only certain frequencies appear: an emission line spectrum of bright lines on a dark background. Made by a hot, low-pressure gas, or a gas discharge tube.

**Absorption.** White light (a continuous spectrum) passes through a cool gas. Photons with exactly the right energy to lift electrons between levels are absorbed. The atoms re-emit in all directions, so those wavelengths are missing in the original direction: dark lines on a continuous spectrum. The dark lines are at the same wavelengths as the bright emission lines of the same element. Dark lines in the Sun's spectrum show which elements are in its outer layers (see file 20).

**Continuous spectrum.** A hot dense object (a filament, a star's surface) emits all wavelengths.

**Seeing spectra.** A diffraction grating spreads light into its wavelengths: each line in the source appears as a separate bright line in each order (dsin θ = nλ, file 05).

**Each element is different.** The set of levels is unique to each element, so its line spectrum is like a fingerprint.

**Hydrogen.** Transitions down to n = 2 give visible lines (the Balmer series); down to n = 1 give ultraviolet (Lyman); down to n = 3 give infrared.

**Number of lines.** With N levels available, the number of possible downward transitions is N(N − 1)/2.

**Electron standing waves** (OCR B 6.2.1(a)(iv), (b)(iii)). In a simple model, an electron confined in an atom behaves like a wave trapped in a box. Only whole numbers of half-wavelengths fit: L = nλ/2. Since λ = h/p and Ek = p²/2m, Ek = n²h²/(8mL²). Only certain energies are allowed, which explains discrete levels. A smaller box gives larger energies.

#### Worked example

1. In hydrogen, n = 3 is at −1.51 eV and n = 2 at −3.40 eV. Find the wavelength of the photon emitted when an electron falls from n = 3 to n = 2.

   ΔE = 1.89 eV = 3.02 × 10⁻¹⁹ J. λ = hc/ΔE = 1.989 × 10⁻²⁵ / 3.02 × 10⁻¹⁹ = 6.58 × 10⁻⁷ m (656 nm, red).

2. An atom has four levels. How many emission lines can it produce?

   4 × 3 / 2 = 6.

3. (Eduqas) The ground state of an atom is −10.4 eV. What is its ionisation energy in J?

   10.4 × 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ = 1.66 × 10⁻¹⁸ J.

4. (OCR B) Estimate the lowest energy of an electron confined in a box of length 0.10 nm.

   E = h²/(8mL²) = (6.63 × 10⁻³⁴)² / (8 × 9.11 × 10⁻³¹ × (1.0 × 10⁻¹⁰)²) = 6.0 × 10⁻¹⁸ J ≈ 38 eV.

#### Common mistakes

- Using E₁ + E₂ instead of the difference.
- Forgetting the negative signs and getting a negative photon energy; use the magnitude of the difference.
- Saying absorption lines are dark because no light reaches the detector at all; some re-emitted light does, but much less than the background.
- Saying the photon energy depends on the level the electron started in only.

### 3.2.2.4 Wave–particle duality

*Refs: AQA 3.2.2.4 · Edexcel 86, 87, 90, 95 · OCR A 4.5.3(a) to (c) · OCR B 4.1(a)(vii), (viii), 4.1(c)(v) · Eduqas C3.4(l), (m), (n). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Electron diffraction shows that particles have wave properties; the photoelectric effect shows that electromagnetic waves have particle properties.
- The de Broglie wavelength λ = h/p = h/mv.
- How and why the amount of diffraction changes when the momentum of the particles changes.
- Our understanding of the nature of matter has changed over time, and changes are checked by peer review and accepted by the scientific community.
- (OCR B) Quantum behaviour: the probability of a photon or electron arriving somewhere is found by adding phasors for every possible path.
- (Eduqas) p = h/λ applies to photons as well; radiation pressure.

#### Explained

**Particles as waves.** De Broglie suggested that any particle with momentum p has a wavelength λ = h/p. For a non-relativistic particle, p = mv and Ek = p²/2m, so λ = h/√(2mEk). For an electron accelerated through V: Ek = eV, so λ = h/√(2meV).

**Electron diffraction.** In an electron diffraction tube, electrons from a hot filament are accelerated through a few kV and pass through a thin film of graphite (polycrystalline carbon). A pattern of concentric rings appears on the fluorescent screen. Rings are a diffraction pattern: the atoms in the crystal act like a grating, and the spacing between atoms (about 10⁻¹⁰ m) is similar to the electron wavelength. This shows electrons behave as waves.

- Increase the accelerating p.d.: electrons move faster, p rises, λ falls, so the rings get smaller (less diffraction).
- Diffraction is only noticeable when λ is similar to the gap or object size. A ball has a de Broglie wavelength of around 10⁻³⁴ m, far too small for any effect.

**Light as particles.** The photoelectric effect (one photon, one electron, threshold frequency) needs the photon model. Interference and diffraction need the wave model. Neither model alone explains everything; each is used where it works.

**How ideas change.** Newton's particle model of light was replaced by Young's and Maxwell's wave model, which was then shown to be incomplete by the photoelectric effect. De Broglie's idea of matter waves (1924) was confirmed by electron diffraction experiments (1927). New ideas are published, checked by other scientists, and accepted only when experiments by independent groups agree.

**Photon momentum** (Eduqas C3.4(m), (n)). A photon has no rest mass but has momentum p = h/λ = E/c. When light is absorbed by a surface, its momentum is transferred, so it exerts a force: for a beam of power P absorbed completely, F = P/c; if reflected, F = 2P/c. Radiation pressure = F/A = I/c (absorbed) or 2I/c (reflected), where I is intensity. Solar sails use this.

**Quantum behaviour and phasors** (OCR B 4.1(a)(vii)). A quantum object (photon, electron) does not travel along one path. To predict where it arrives:

1. Consider every possible path from source to detector.
2. For each path, a phasor (a rotating arrow) turns at the frequency f of the quantum; for a photon the phasor makes one full turn per wavelength travelled, so the number of turns along a path = path length ÷ λ.
3. Add the final phasors for all paths tip to tail.
4. The probability of arrival is proportional to the square of the resultant amplitude.

Paths near the straight-line (or least-time) path have phasors that point the same way and add up; paths far from it have phasors pointing in all directions that cancel. This explains why light appears to travel in straight lines, reflects at equal angles and refracts, and it explains two-slit interference: the two groups of paths through the slits add (bright) when the path difference is a whole number of wavelengths and cancel (dark) when it is a half-number. For electrons, the phasor frequency is set by the electron's energy and the wavelength by λ = h/p.

#### Worked example

1. Find the de Broglie wavelength of an electron accelerated through 3.0 kV.

   Ek = 3000 × 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ = 4.80 × 10⁻¹⁶ J. p = √(2mEk) = √(2 × 9.11 × 10⁻³¹ × 4.80 × 10⁻¹⁶) = 2.96 × 10⁻²³ kg m s⁻¹. λ = 6.63 × 10⁻³⁴ / 2.96 × 10⁻²³ = 2.24 × 10⁻¹¹ m.

2. A proton and an electron have the same de Broglie wavelength. Compare their speeds.

   λ = h/mv is the same, so mv is the same. v_e/v_p = m_p/m_e = 1836. The electron is 1836 times faster.

3. Find the speed of a neutron with wavelength 0.15 nm (useful for diffracting from crystals).

   v = h/(mλ) = 6.63 × 10⁻³⁴ / (1.675 × 10⁻²⁷ × 1.5 × 10⁻¹⁰) = 2.6 × 10³ m s⁻¹.

4. (Eduqas) A 1.0 kW beam falls on a perfectly reflecting surface. Find the force on it.

   F = 2P/c = 2000 / 3.00 × 10⁸ = 6.7 × 10⁻⁶ N.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the rest mass in place of momentum for a photon; for a photon use p = h/λ.
- Saying faster electrons give bigger rings (they give smaller rings).
- Saying electron diffraction shows electrons are only waves.
- Forgetting to square-root when finding p from Ek.

### Check questions

1. Explain why no photoelectrons are emitted from a metal when the frequency of the light is below the threshold frequency, however intense the light. (3 marks)
2. Light of frequency 1.2 × 10¹⁵ Hz falls on sodium (work function 2.3 eV). Calculate the stopping potential. (3 marks)
3. Sketch a graph of Ek(max) against frequency for two metals with different work functions and state what the gradient and intercepts show. (4 marks)
4. Describe how a fluorescent tube produces visible light. (5 marks)
5. An atom has levels at −8.0, −4.5, −2.2 and −0.9 eV. Calculate the shortest wavelength it can emit when de-exciting between these levels. (3 marks)
6. Explain how a line absorption spectrum is formed. (3 marks)
7. Calculate the accelerating p.d. needed to give electrons a de Broglie wavelength of 0.050 nm. (3 marks)
8. In an electron diffraction tube, explain what happens to the rings when the accelerating voltage is increased. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Each electron absorbs one photon (1); photon energy hf is below the work function (1); greater intensity only means more photons per second, not more energy per photon; energy cannot be built up from several photons (1).
2. hf = 6.63 × 10⁻³⁴ × 1.2 × 10¹⁵ = 7.96 × 10⁻¹⁹ J = 4.97 eV (1); Ek(max) = 4.97 − 2.3 = 2.67 eV (1); Vs = 2.7 V (1).
3. Two parallel straight lines (1); gradient = h (same for both) (1); intercept on f-axis = threshold frequency (1); extrapolated intercept on Ek-axis = −φ; larger φ further right (1).
4. Electrons accelerated by high p.d. (1); collide with mercury atoms, exciting them (and ionising some, producing more electrons) (1); mercury atoms de-excite emitting UV photons (1); UV absorbed by phosphor coating, exciting its atoms (1); phosphor de-excites in smaller steps emitting visible photons (1).
5. Largest gap = −0.9 − (−8.0) = 7.1 eV (1); = 1.14 × 10⁻¹⁸ J (1); λ = hc/E = 1.75 × 10⁻⁷ m (1).
6. White light / continuous spectrum passes through cool gas (1); photons with energies equal to gaps between levels are absorbed, exciting electrons (1); re-emitted in all directions, so those wavelengths appear as dark lines in the transmitted spectrum (1).
7. p = h/λ = 6.63 × 10⁻³⁴ / 5.0 × 10⁻¹¹ = 1.33 × 10⁻²³ kg m s⁻¹ (1); Ek = p²/2m = 9.7 × 10⁻¹⁷ J (1); V = Ek/e = 600 V (1).
8. Electrons gain more kinetic energy / momentum (1); de Broglie wavelength decreases (1); less diffraction, so rings become smaller / closer to the centre (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/33-lasers.md -->

## Lasers: stimulated emission, population inversion, pumping and laser structure

LASER stands for Light Amplification by Stimulated Emission of Radiation. This file explains spontaneous and stimulated emission, why stimulated emission gives coherent light, why a population inversion is needed and why it cannot normally be achieved with two energy levels, three- and four-level systems, pumping, the structure of a laser, and semiconductor lasers.

### Overview

Eduqas C3.5 only (AS & A). Energy levels and photon emission are in file 03; coherence and interference in file 05; laser safety in file 05.

#### Board differences

- Eduqas C3.5(a) to (g) only. AQA 3.3.2.1 says students will not be asked how a laser works. Edexcel, OCR A and OCR B do not examine laser operation.

### C3.5 Lasers

*Refs: Eduqas C3.5(a) to (g). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- (a) Stimulated emission and how it gives coherent light.
- (b) A population inversion (N₂ > N₁) is needed for laser action.
- (c) A population inversion is not (usually) possible with a two-level system.
- (d) How a population inversion is reached in three- and four-level systems.
- (e) Pumping and its purpose.
- (f) The structure of a typical laser: an amplifying medium between two mirrors, one partially transmitting.
- (g) Advantages and uses of semiconductor lasers: small, cheap, far more efficient; used in CDs, DVDs, telecommunications.

#### Explained

**Three processes between two levels** (lower E₁, upper E₂, with photon energy hf = E₂ − E₁):

1. **Absorption**: a photon of energy hf is absorbed by an atom in E₁, which moves to E₂.
2. **Spontaneous emission**: an atom in E₂ drops to E₁ on its own, at a random time, emitting a photon in a random direction with random phase. Ordinary light sources work this way, so their light is incoherent.
3. **Stimulated emission**: a photon of energy exactly hf passes an atom already in E₂ and triggers it to drop to E₁. The atom emits a second photon that is identical to the first: same frequency, same phase, same direction and same polarisation. The original photon continues. One photon has become two: amplification.

Because the stimulated photons are copies of the stimulating photon, a chain of stimulated emissions produces light that is **coherent** (constant phase relationship), monochromatic (one frequency) and travels in one direction (highly collimated).

**Population inversion.** A photon passing through a collection of atoms can either be absorbed (by an atom in E₁) or cause stimulated emission (from an atom in E₂). The chances are equal per atom. So more photons are produced than absorbed only if more atoms are in the upper level than the lower: N₂ > N₁. This is a population inversion. Normally (in thermal equilibrium) N₁ > N₂, because the Boltzmann factor makes higher levels less populated, so light is absorbed overall.

**Why not two levels.** If you pump atoms from E₁ to E₂ with light of energy hf, that same light causes stimulated emission from E₂ back to E₁ as well. As N₂ approaches N₁, absorption and stimulated emission become equally likely; the best you can reach is N₂ = N₁, never N₂ > N₁.

**Three-level system.** Atoms are pumped from the ground state E₁ to a high, short-lived level E₃. They drop quickly (non-radiatively) to E₂, a **metastable** level with a long lifetime (around 1 ms rather than about 10 ns). Atoms pile up in E₂. Laser action occurs from E₂ to the ground state E₁. Problem: more than half of all the atoms must be removed from the ground state before N₂ > N₁, so intense pumping is needed (e.g. ruby laser).

**Four-level system.** Pump from E₀ (ground) to E₃; fast decay to metastable E₂; laser transition from E₂ to E₁; fast decay from E₁ to E₀. Because E₁ is above the ground state and empties quickly, it is almost always nearly empty, so a population inversion between E₂ and E₁ is easy to reach even with few atoms in E₂. Far more efficient (e.g. helium–neon, Nd:YAG lasers).

**Pumping.** The process of supplying energy to put atoms into the upper levels, so a population inversion is maintained. Methods: intense light from a flash lamp or another laser (optical pumping), an electric discharge through a gas (electrons collide with atoms), or an electric current (semiconductor lasers).

**Structure of a laser.**

- An **amplifying (gain) medium** in which the population inversion is created.
- A **pump** supplying energy.
- Two parallel **mirrors**, one at each end, forming an optical cavity. One is fully reflecting (100%); the other is partially transmitting (e.g. 99% reflecting).

Photons travelling along the axis bounce back and forth between the mirrors, passing through the medium many times and causing more and more stimulated emission, so the light is amplified. Photons travelling off-axis escape through the sides. A small fraction of the light passes through the partially transmitting mirror as the output beam. The cavity length is a whole number of half wavelengths, so a stationary wave forms, which helps select a single frequency.

**Semiconductor lasers** (laser diodes). A p–n junction of a semiconductor such as gallium arsenide. A forward current injects electrons and holes into the junction region, giving a population inversion; electrons recombining with holes emit photons, stimulating more recombinations. The polished ends of the crystal act as mirrors.

- Advantages: very small and cheap to mass-produce; far more efficient (converting a large fraction of electrical energy to light) than gas or crystal lasers; powered by low voltages; can be switched on and off very quickly (modulated) for data.
- Uses: reading and writing CDs, DVDs and Blu-ray discs; optical fibre telecommunications; barcode scanners; laser pointers; laser printers.

#### Worked example

1. A laser transition is between levels at −3.20 eV and −5.10 eV. Find the wavelength.

   ΔE = 1.90 eV = 3.04 × 10⁻¹⁹ J. λ = hc/ΔE = 1.989 × 10⁻²⁵/3.04 × 10⁻¹⁹ = 6.54 × 10⁻⁷ m (red).

2. A 5.0 mW laser emits at 650 nm. How many photons per second?

   E = 1.989 × 10⁻²⁵/650 × 10⁻⁹ = 3.06 × 10⁻¹⁹ J. N = 5.0 × 10⁻³/3.06 × 10⁻¹⁹ = 1.6 × 10¹⁶ s⁻¹.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying stimulated emission absorbs the incoming photon.
- Saying a population inversion means all atoms are in the upper level.
- Saying the pump light is the laser output.
- Saying both mirrors are partially transmitting.

### Check questions

1. Explain the difference between spontaneous and stimulated emission. (3 marks)
2. Explain why laser light is coherent. (2 marks)
3. What is a population inversion, and why is it needed for a laser? (3 marks)
4. Explain why a population inversion cannot normally be achieved in a two-level system. (2 marks)
5. Explain why a four-level laser is more efficient than a three-level laser. (3 marks)
6. Describe the structure of a laser and the role of each mirror. (3 marks)
7. Give two advantages of semiconductor lasers and two uses. (4 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Spontaneous: excited atom drops to a lower level at a random time, emitting a photon in a random direction/phase (1); stimulated: incoming photon of the right energy triggers the excited atom to drop (1); emitted photon has same frequency, phase and direction as the incoming photon (1).
2. Each stimulated photon is in phase with / identical to the photon that triggered it (1); so all photons in the beam have a constant phase relationship (1).
3. More atoms in the upper (excited) level than the lower level (1); photons are more likely to cause stimulated emission than to be absorbed (1); so the light is amplified (1).
4. Pumping photons also cause stimulated emission back down (1); so the best possible is equal numbers in each level, N₂ = N₁ (1).
5. In a four-level system the lower laser level is above the ground state (1); it empties rapidly so it is almost always nearly empty (1); so a population inversion needs only a few atoms in the upper level / less pumping (1).
6. Amplifying medium between two parallel mirrors (1); one fully reflecting, sending light back through the medium for further amplification (1); the other partially transmitting, letting out the beam (1).
7. Any two advantages: small, cheap, efficient, low voltage, fast switching (2); any two uses: CD/DVD players, optical fibre communication, barcode scanners, laser printers (2).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/18-radioactivity-decay-and-nuclear-radius.md -->

## Radioactivity: Rutherford scattering, alpha, beta and gamma radiation, decay, nuclear instability and nuclear radius

This file covers how alpha scattering revealed the nucleus, the properties of alpha, beta and gamma radiation, the inverse-square law for gamma (AQA required practical 12), background radiation, the random exponential decay of nuclei, half-life and activity, decay modes and the N–Z plot, and how the size of the nucleus is measured.

### Overview

A-level only for AQA, Edexcel, OCR A and OCR B. Eduqas has Rutherford scattering (C3.7(a), (b)) at AS and nuclear decay (C3.6) at A-level. Basic alpha and beta decay equations and the strong force are in file 02. Binding energy, fission, fusion and reactors are in file 19. Medical uses of radionuclides are in file 23.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.8.1.1 to 3.8.1.5, RP12 · Edexcel 131, 168 to 173, CP15 · OCR A 6.4.1, 6.4.3 · OCR B 5.1.1 (decay as exponential), 6.2.1, 6.2.2 · Eduqas C3.6, C3.7(a), (b).
- Inverse-square law for gamma: AQA RP12 and Eduqas specified practical. Edexcel CP15 instead investigates absorption of gamma by lead (I = I₀e^(−μx)).
- Half-life of protactinium: OCR B 5.1.1(d)(iv), OCR A 6.4.3(e)(ii) (PAG; or a simulation). Dice simulation: OCR A 6.4.3(f), Eduqas specified practical, suggested by AQA.
- OCR B 6.2.2(c)(ii), (iii): absorbed dose (gray) and effective dose (sievert = gray × quality factor). Also in Eduqas option B (file 23).
- Nuclear radius R = R₀A^(1/3), electron diffraction by nuclei and nuclear density: AQA 3.8.1.5, OCR A 6.4.1(f), (g). Eduqas C3.7(b) asks for the closest-approach estimate only.
- OCR A 6.4.3(h): radioactive dating (carbon-14).
- AQA 3.8.1.4: decay modes on the N–Z graph, nuclear excited states and technetium-99m. OCR B 6.2.2(a)(ii): "nuclear valley" of stability.

### 3.8.1.1 Rutherford scattering

*Refs: AQA 3.8.1.1 · Edexcel 131 · OCR A 6.4.1(a), (c) · OCR B 6.2.1(a)(ii), (b)(ii), (c)(ii) · Eduqas C3.7(a), (b). Level: A-level only (Eduqas AS & A).*

#### What you need to know

- Rutherford scattering, qualitatively.
- How understanding of the structure of the atom changed over time.
- (Eduqas C3.7(b)) Estimating the maximum Coulomb force on an alpha particle in the plum-pudding and nuclear models.

#### Explained

**The experiment** (Geiger and Marsden, 1909). A narrow beam of alpha particles from a radium source was aimed at a very thin gold foil in a vacuum. A zinc sulfide screen viewed through a microscope detected the alpha particles at different angles (each one made a tiny flash).

**Observations and conclusions.**

| Observation | Conclusion |
| --- | --- |
| Most alpha particles went straight through | Most of the atom is empty space |
| Some were deflected through small angles | There is a concentrated positive charge that repels them |
| A very few (about 1 in 8000) bounced back through more than 90° | The positive charge and most of the mass are concentrated in a tiny nucleus |

**History.** Thomson's plum-pudding model (1904) had positive charge spread through the atom with electrons dotted in it. In that model the electric field inside the atom is weak (the charge is spread out), so alphas could only be deflected slightly. Rutherford's nuclear model (1911) explained large-angle scattering. Later: Bohr added electron energy levels (1913); Chadwick discovered the neutron (1932).

**Eduqas estimate.** Maximum Coulomb force on an alpha (charge 2e) from a gold atom's charge (79e):

- Plum pudding: charge spread over the atom (r ≈ 10⁻¹⁰ m); maximum force at the surface: F = k(2e)(79e)/r² ≈ 3.6 × 10⁻⁶ N. Too small to reverse a fast alpha.
- Nuclear: all the charge within about 10⁻¹⁴ m: F ≈ 3.6 × 10² N, 10⁸ times larger, enough to turn it back.

**Path of an alpha** (OCR B 6.2.1(b)(ii)). Alphas follow hyperbolic paths around the nucleus. The closer the original line of travel is to the nucleus, the bigger the deflection; a head-on alpha comes straight back.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the alpha particles hit electrons and bounced back.
- Saying "most went through, so the nucleus is small" (this shows the atom is mostly empty; the large-angle back-scattering shows the nucleus is small, dense and positive).

### 3.8.1.2 α, β and γ radiation

*Refs: AQA 3.8.1.2, RP12 · Edexcel 168, 169, 170, 171, CP15 · OCR A 6.4.3(a), (b), (c) · OCR B 6.2.2(a)(i), (b)(i), (c)(ii), (c)(iii), (d)(i) · Eduqas C3.6(a), (b), (c). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Properties of alpha, beta and gamma radiation and how to identify them with simple absorption experiments.
- Applications: relative hazards to humans, thickness monitoring (aluminium foil, paper, steel), safe handling.
- The inverse-square law for gamma radiation: I = k/x². Verifying it experimentally.
- Background radiation: sources and correcting for it.
- Balancing risks and benefits of radiation in medicine.
- (OCR B) Absorbed dose (Gy) and effective dose (Sv).

#### Explained

| | Alpha α | Beta-minus β⁻ | Gamma γ |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Nature | Helium nucleus ⁴₂He | Fast electron | EM radiation (photon) |
| Charge | +2e | −e | 0 |
| Speed | About 0.05c | Up to about 0.99c | c |
| Ionising | Strongly (about 10⁴ ion pairs per cm in air) | Moderately (about 10² per cm) | Weakly (about 1 per cm) |
| Range in air | A few cm (fixed range for each source) | About 1 m (spread of ranges) | Very large; intensity falls as 1/x² |
| Stopped by | Paper or skin | A few mm of aluminium | Reduced by several cm of lead or metres of concrete (never fully stopped) |
| Deflected in fields | Yes, slightly (heavy) | Yes, strongly, opposite way to α | No |

(β⁺ particles are positrons; they annihilate almost at once with an electron, producing two gamma photons.)

**Why they differ.** Alpha particles are big, slow and doubly charged, so they interact strongly with every atom they pass, ionising heavily and losing energy quickly. Gamma photons are uncharged and interact only occasionally.

**Identifying radiation.** Use a Geiger–Müller tube and counter close to the source. Measure the count rate with nothing in between, then with paper, then with 3 mm of aluminium, then with lead. Correct each count for background. A drop with paper shows α; a further drop with aluminium shows β; what remains through aluminium (and is reduced by lead) is γ.

**Hazards.** Outside the body, alpha is the least dangerous (it cannot penetrate skin), gamma the most. Inside the body (swallowed or breathed in), alpha is the most dangerous because all its energy is deposited in a small volume of tissue, damaging cells and DNA.

**Safe handling.** Use long-handled tongs; never point a source at anyone; keep the time of exposure short; keep as far away as possible (inverse-square law for gamma); use shielding (lead-lined boxes); store sources in lead-lined containers; wash hands after.

**Thickness monitoring.** A source on one side of a sheet and a detector on the other. If the count rate changes, the thickness has changed, and rollers are adjusted. Use β for paper and thin aluminium foil (α would be stopped completely; γ would pass with almost no change). Use γ for steel sheet.

**Inverse-square law for gamma.** A point source emits gamma photons equally in all directions; the energy spreads over a sphere of area 4πx², so intensity (and count rate) I = k/x². (Alpha and beta are absorbed by air, so they do not follow this law.)

**Background radiation.** Always present. Sources: radon gas from rocks (the largest part in the UK), cosmic rays, rocks and buildings, food and drink (e.g. potassium-40), medical uses, nuclear industry and past weapons tests. Measure the background count rate (with no source, over a long time, e.g. 10 minutes) and subtract it from every reading.

**Dose** (OCR B 6.2.2(c)(ii), (iii)). Absorbed dose = energy absorbed per kilogram of tissue, in gray (1 Gy = 1 J kg⁻¹). Effective dose = absorbed dose × quality (weighting) factor, in sieverts (Sv). The quality factor is 1 for β and γ, 20 for α, because α does much more damage per joule. Risk of cancer rises roughly in proportion to effective dose (about 5% per Sv).

**Risks and benefits in medicine.** X-rays, CT scans and radioactive tracers give a dose that carries a small risk of cancer, so they are used only when the diagnostic or treatment benefit outweighs it, with the lowest dose possible.

#### Worked example

1. A gamma source gives a corrected count rate of 480 counts per minute at 0.20 m. Predict the count rate at 0.50 m.

   I ∝ 1/x²: 480 × (0.20/0.50)² = 77 counts per minute.

2. A 70 kg worker absorbs 2.1 mJ from alpha radiation. Find the absorbed and effective dose (quality factor 20).

   Absorbed dose = 2.1 × 10⁻³/70 = 3.0 × 10⁻⁵ Gy. Effective = 6.0 × 10⁻⁴ Sv (0.6 mSv).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying gamma radiation is completely stopped by lead.
- Saying alpha is the most dangerous in all cases.
- Forgetting to subtract background before testing the inverse-square law.
- Saying beta particles come from the electron shells (they come from the nucleus).

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 12** (also Eduqas specified practical): investigate the inverse-square law for gamma radiation.

1. Measure the background count with the GM tube for at least 5 minutes, with all sources away. Find the background count rate.
2. Place a sealed gamma source (cobalt-60) using tongs in a holder facing the GM tube along a metre rule.
3. Record the count over a fixed time (e.g. 2 minutes) at distances from about 5 cm to 1 m. Repeat and average.
4. Subtract the background rate to get the corrected count rate C.
5. The source is inside its holder and the GM tube detects some distance inside its window, so the true distance is x + d, where d is an unknown offset. Plot 1/√C against x: a straight line whose x-intercept gives −d. (Or plot C against 1/x² if d is negligible.)

Errors: radioactive decay is random, so count for longer at larger distances to reduce percentage uncertainty (uncertainty in a count N is about √N). Dead time of the GM tube at close range lowers counts. Safety: handle the source with tongs, keep it at arm's length, return it to its lead box promptly, do not point it at anyone, and keep exposure time short.

**Edexcel CP15** (also OCR B 6.2.2(d)(i)): absorption of gamma by lead. Place lead sheets of increasing thickness x between a gamma source and a GM tube at a fixed distance. Measure count rates, correct for background. The corrected count rate falls exponentially: C = C₀e^(−μx), where μ is the linear attenuation (absorption) coefficient. Plot ln C against x: straight line, gradient −μ. Half-value thickness = ln 2/μ.

### 3.8.1.3 Radioactive decay

*Refs: AQA 3.8.1.3 · Edexcel 168, 172, 173 · OCR A 6.4.3(a), (d) to (h) · OCR B 5.1.1(a)(iv), (b)(ii), (iv), (v), (c)(i), (ii), (d)(iv), 6.2.2(c)(i), (d)(ii) · Eduqas C3.6(a), (d) to (h). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Decay is random and spontaneous; each nucleus has a constant probability of decaying per unit time (the decay constant λ).
- ΔN/Δt = −λN; N = N₀e^(−λt); activity A = λN; A = A₀e^(−λt).
- Half-life T½ = ln 2/λ.
- Finding half-life from decay curves and log graphs.
- Questions may use molar mass and the Avogadro constant.
- Modelling with constant decay probability (dice, spreadsheets, iterative methods).
- Applications: storing radioactive waste, radioactive dating.

#### Explained

**Random and spontaneous.** It is impossible to predict when a particular nucleus will decay (random), and decay is not affected by external conditions such as temperature, pressure or chemical state (spontaneous). But for a large number of nuclei, the fraction that decays per second is constant: the decay constant λ (s⁻¹) is the probability that a given nucleus decays in one second.

**Activity** A is the number of decays per second, in becquerels (1 Bq = 1 decay per second). A = λN.

**Exponential decay.** The rate of decay is proportional to the number of undecayed nuclei: dN/dt = −λN. Solution:

- N = N₀e^(−λt)
- A = A₀e^(−λt)
- (also count rate C = C₀e^(−λt), and mass of the parent remaining)

**Half-life** T½: the average time for the number of undecayed nuclei (or the activity) of an isotope to halve. Setting N = N₀/2 gives T½ = ln 2/λ ≈ 0.693/λ. After n half-lives, N = N₀/2ⁿ.

**Graphs.**

- N (or A) against t: exponential curve; read off T½ as the time to fall to half, then half again (check it is constant).
- ln N (or ln A) against t: straight line, ln N = ln N₀ − λt. Gradient = −λ.

**Using moles.** N = (mass/molar mass) × N_A. For example 1.0 μg of radium-226: N = (1.0 × 10⁻⁹ kg / 0.226 kg mol⁻¹) × 6.02 × 10²³ = 2.66 × 10¹⁵ nuclei.

**Modelling** (OCR A 6.4.3(f), (g), OCR B 5.1.1(c)(ii), Eduqas practical, AQA suggested).

- Dice: roll 100 dice; remove all that show a six (each has probability 1/6 of "decaying" per throw); count the remainder; repeat. A graph of number remaining against throw number is an exponential decay with a "half-life" of about 3.8 throws (ln 2/ln(6/5)).
- Spreadsheet: ΔN = −λNΔt each step; N_new = N − λNΔt.

**Applications.**

- *Radioactive dating* (OCR A 6.4.3(h)). Living things take in carbon, including a constant small fraction of carbon-14 (T½ = 5730 years). When they die, intake stops and ¹⁴C decays. Measuring the activity per gram of carbon and comparing with living material gives the age. Rocks are dated using uranium/lead or potassium/argon ratios.
- *Medical tracers*: short half-lives (hours) so the patient is not exposed for long, but long enough for the test.
- *Radioactive waste*: some isotopes have half-lives of thousands of years, so waste must be stored securely for very long times (file 19).

#### Worked example

1. Iodine-131 has a half-life of 8.0 days. Find λ, and the activity after 20 days of a sample with initial activity 4.0 × 10⁶ Bq.

   λ = 0.693/(8.0 × 86 400) = 1.0 × 10⁻⁶ s⁻¹. A = 4.0 × 10⁶ e^(−0.693 × 20/8.0) = 4.0 × 10⁶ × 0.177 = 7.1 × 10⁵ Bq.

2. Find the activity of 1.0 μg of radium-226 (T½ = 1600 years).

   λ = 0.693/(1600 × 3.16 × 10⁷) = 1.37 × 10⁻¹¹ s⁻¹. N = 2.66 × 10¹⁵. A = 3.6 × 10⁴ Bq.

3. Wood from an ancient boat has 0.60 of the ¹⁴C activity per gram of living wood. Find its age.

   0.60 = e^(−λt); t = −ln 0.60/λ = 0.511 × 5730/0.693 = 4200 years.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the half-life is half the time it takes for all the nuclei to decay.
- Using days for T½ but seconds for t (or the reverse).
- Saying that after two half-lives the sample has completely decayed.
- Forgetting to subtract background from count rates before finding half-life.

#### Practical

**Half-life of protactinium-234** (OCR B 5.1.1(d)(iv), OCR A). A sealed bottle contains a uranium salt in water and an organic solvent. Shaking separates protactinium-234 into the organic layer (on top). Place a GM tube next to the top layer and record counts every 10 s for about 5 minutes. Subtract background. Plot ln(count rate) against t: gradient = −λ; T½ ≈ 70 s. (Done by teachers or with simulations in many schools; the bottle must not be opened.)

### 3.8.1.4 Nuclear instability

*Refs: AQA 3.8.1.4 · Edexcel 170 · OCR A 6.4.3(c) · OCR B 6.2.2(a)(ii) · Eduqas C3.6(a). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The graph of N (neutron number) against Z (proton number) for stable nuclei.
- Decay modes of unstable nuclei: α, β⁺, β⁻ and electron capture.
- Changes in N and Z caused by each decay, in decay equations.
- Nuclear energy level diagrams; nuclear excited states and γ emission; technetium-99m in medical diagnosis.

#### Explained

**The N–Z plot.** Stable nuclei lie along a band. For light nuclei (Z < 20) the band follows N = Z. For heavier nuclei, more neutrons than protons are needed (N > Z) because the extra neutrons add strong-force attraction without adding electrostatic repulsion. Above Z = 83 no nucleus is fully stable.

**Decay modes and where they occur.**

| Mode | Where on the plot | Change | Example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| α | Very heavy nuclei (Z > 82) | Z − 2, N − 2, A − 4 | ²²⁶₈₈Ra → ²²²₈₆Rn + ⁴₂α |
| β⁻ | Above the band (too many neutrons) | Z + 1, N − 1, A same | ¹⁴₆C → ¹⁴₇N + ⁰₋₁e + ν̄ₑ |
| β⁺ | Below the band (too many protons) | Z − 1, N + 1, A same | ¹¹₆C → ¹¹₅B + ⁰₊₁e + νₑ |
| Electron capture | Below the band (proton-rich; competes with β⁺) | Z − 1, N + 1 | ⁷₄Be + ⁰₋₁e → ⁷₃Li + νₑ |
| γ | Excited nucleus | No change in Z or N | ⁹⁹ᵐ₄₃Tc → ⁹⁹₄₃Tc + γ |

On an N–Z graph, α moves a nucleus 2 down and 2 left; β⁻ moves it 1 down and 1 right; β⁺ and electron capture move it 1 up and 1 left.

**Nuclear excited states and gamma.** After an α or β decay, the daughter nucleus is often left in an excited state (with extra energy). It drops to its ground state by emitting a gamma photon, whose energy equals the gap between nuclear energy levels (typically keV to MeV, much bigger than atomic levels). A nuclear energy level diagram shows parent and daughter levels with arrows for each decay.

**Technetium-99m** (the m means metastable). Made from molybdenum-99 (T½ = 66 h), which decays by β⁻ to Tc-99m. Tc-99m then emits a 140 keV gamma photon, with T½ = 6 hours. Ideal as a medical tracer: pure gamma (penetrates the body, little ionisation inside), easy to detect with a gamma camera, short half-life (low dose), long enough to do the scan, can be attached to many chemicals to target organs, and can be made on site in a hospital "generator".

**Nuclear valley** (OCR B). If binding energy is plotted against N and Z as a surface, stable nuclei sit along the floor of a valley. Nuclei on the valley sides decay "downhill" towards the floor by β⁻ or β⁺; very heavy nuclei at the far end decay by α.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying β⁻ decay reduces Z.
- Saying gamma emission changes the element.
- Drawing the stability band as N = Z for heavy nuclei.

### 3.8.1.5 Nuclear radius

*Refs: AQA 3.8.1.5 · OCR A 6.4.1(c), (f), (g) · Eduqas C3.7(b). Level: A-level only (Eduqas AS & A).*

#### What you need to know

- Estimating nuclear radius from the closest approach of alpha particles (using the Coulomb equation); finding it from electron diffraction.
- Typical values of nuclear radius (about 10⁻¹⁵ m).
- R = R₀A^(1/3), found from experiment; evidence that nuclear density is constant.
- Calculating nuclear density.
- The graph of intensity against angle for electron diffraction by nuclei.

#### Explained

**Closest approach.** An alpha particle fired head-on at a nucleus slows as it approaches and stops momentarily at distance r, where all its kinetic energy has become electric potential energy:

Ek = (2e)(Ze)/(4πε₀r) → r = 2Ze²/(4πε₀Ek)

This gives an upper limit for the nuclear radius (the alpha does not touch the nucleus). Limitations: the alpha's charge is spread out; the recoil of the nucleus is ignored; the strong force may act if it gets close enough.

**Electron diffraction.** High-energy electrons (hundreds of MeV) have de Broglie wavelengths about 10⁻¹⁵ m, similar to a nucleus. Fired at a thin film, they diffract around the nuclei. The intensity against angle falls from a central maximum to a first minimum (not zero) at θ, where sin θ ≈ 0.61λ/R. So R = 0.61λ/sin θ. Electrons are better than alphas because they are leptons (not affected by the strong force) and they probe the whole nucleus. At these energies, λ ≈ hc/E (relativistic).

**R = R₀A^(1/3).** Experiments show R₀ ≈ 1.2 fm (1.2 × 10⁻¹⁵ m; data booklet value 1.05 to 1.4 fm depending on method). Plotting R against A^(1/3) gives a straight line through the origin, gradient R₀. Or plot ln R against ln A: gradient 1/3.

**Constant density.** Volume = (4/3)πR³ = (4/3)πR₀³A, so volume ∝ A. Mass ≈ A × u. So density = Au/((4/3)πR₀³A) = 3u/(4πR₀³), the same for all nuclei: about 2.3 × 10¹⁷ kg m⁻³. This shows nucleons are packed together at a fixed spacing, as in a liquid drop, and that the nucleus is far denser than the atom (most of the atom's volume is empty).

#### Worked example

1. Find the closest approach of a 7.7 MeV alpha particle to a gold nucleus (Z = 79).

   r = 2 × 79 × (1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹)² × 8.99 × 10⁹/(7.7 × 1.60 × 10⁻¹³) = 3.64 × 10⁻²⁶/1.23 × 10⁻¹² = 3.0 × 10⁻¹⁴ m.

2. Find the radius of a ¹⁹⁷Au nucleus with R₀ = 1.2 fm, and compare with example 1.

   R = 1.2 × 10⁻¹⁵ × 197^(1/3) = 1.2 × 10⁻¹⁵ × 5.82 = 7.0 × 10⁻¹⁵ m. The closest approach is about 4 times larger, so it is only an upper limit.

3. Find the density of nuclear matter.

   ρ = 3 × 1.66 × 10⁻²⁷/(4π × (1.2 × 10⁻¹⁵)³) = 2.3 × 10¹⁷ kg m⁻³.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the closest approach equals the nuclear radius.
- Forgetting the cube root.
- Saying the first minimum of electron diffraction by nuclei goes to zero.

### Check questions

1. Describe the alpha scattering experiment and explain what each observation showed about the atom. (6 marks)
2. Describe how to show that a source emits only beta and gamma radiation. (4 marks)
3. Explain why alpha radiation is the most dangerous when a source is inside the body. (2 marks)
4. A corrected gamma count rate is 900 min⁻¹ at 0.10 m. At what distance would it be 100 min⁻¹? (2 marks)
5. Define half-life and decay constant, and show that T½ = ln 2/λ. (4 marks)
6. A sample of cobalt-60 (T½ = 5.27 years) has activity 2.0 × 10⁵ Bq. Find the number of cobalt nuclei and the mass of cobalt. (4 marks)
7. Strontium-90 decays by β⁻ emission. On an N–Z diagram, describe how the nucleus moves, and explain why it is unstable. (3 marks)
8. Calculate the radius of a ⁵⁶Fe nucleus (R₀ = 1.2 fm) and the ratio of its density to that of a ²⁰⁸Pb nucleus. (3 marks)
9. Explain why technetium-99m is suitable as a medical tracer. (4 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Alpha beam at thin gold foil in vacuum; detector at various angles (1); most pass straight through (1): atom mostly empty space (1); a few deflected through large angles / back (1): small, dense, positively charged nucleus (1); containing most of the mass (1).
2. Measure background; GM tube close to source (1); paper causes no drop in count, so no alpha (1); a few mm of aluminium causes a large drop: beta (1); remaining count above background only reduced by thick lead: gamma (1).
3. Alpha is the most strongly ionising (1); all its energy is deposited in a small volume of tissue near the source, damaging cells/DNA (1).
4. Count rate ∝ 1/x², 900/100 = 9, so distance × 3 (1) = 0.30 m (1).
5. Half-life: average time for the number of undecayed nuclei (activity) to halve (1); decay constant: probability per unit time that a nucleus decays (1); N₀/2 = N₀e^(−λT½) (1); ln 2 = λT½ (1).
6. λ = 0.693/(5.27 × 3.16 × 10⁷) = 4.16 × 10⁻⁹ s⁻¹ (1); N = A/λ = 4.8 × 10¹³ (1); moles = N/N_A = 8.0 × 10⁻¹¹ (1); mass = 8.0 × 10⁻¹¹ × 0.060 = 4.8 × 10⁻¹² kg (1).
7. Moves one down (N − 1) and one right (Z + 1) (1); it is above the stability band / has too many neutrons for its protons (1); β⁻ moves it towards the band (1).
8. R = 1.2 × 10⁻¹⁵ × 56^(1/3) = 4.6 × 10⁻¹⁵ m (2); ratio = 1, since density is the same for all nuclei (1).
9. Emits only gamma, which passes out of the body to be detected (1); low ionisation inside the body (1); half-life 6 hours: long enough for the scan, short enough to keep dose low (1); can be combined with different compounds to target organs / made on site (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/02-particles-antiparticles-and-quarks.md -->

## Particles: the atom, antiparticles, interactions and quarks

This file builds the particle picture of matter: what atoms and nuclei are made of, why some nuclei decay, antimatter and photons, the four fundamental interactions and their exchange particles, and the classification of particles into hadrons and leptons built from quarks. It ends with the conservation laws used to check whether a reaction can happen.

### Overview

AS & A for AQA, OCR B and Eduqas; A-level only for Edexcel (Topic 8) and OCR A (Module 6). Alpha scattering and nuclear radius are in file 18; binding energy, mass–energy and fission are in file 19; particle accelerators and detectors are in file 31.

#### Board differences

- AQA covers the most: exchange particles (virtual photon, W⁺, W⁻, pion), Feynman-style diagrams, strangeness, kaons and muons. No other board asks for strangeness conservation in such detail.
- Edexcel 140 adds that the symmetry of the quark–lepton model predicted the top quark; Edexcel 137 and 138 use ΔE = c²Δm and MeV/c² for creation and annihilation (AQA says E = mc² is not needed in calculations at this point).
- OCR A 6.4.2 needs u, d, s quarks and their charges, β⁻ and β⁺ decay in quark terms, and balancing quark equations. It does not need exchange particles.
- Eduqas C3.7 uses a three-generation picture but only tests the first generation (e, νₑ, u, d), and asks for the table of the four interactions with ranges. It accepts "quark number" in place of baryon number. Eduqas C3.7(k): neutrinos and quark flavour change only happen in weak interactions.
- OCR B 6.2.1 only needs a simple quark picture of protons and neutrons and the term list (gluon, hadron, lepton, lepton number).

### 3.2.1.1 Constituents of the atom

*Refs: AQA 3.2.1.1 · Edexcel 130 · OCR A 6.4.1(b), (d) · OCR B 6.2.1(b)(i). Level: AS & A (AQA, OCR B); A-level only (Edexcel, OCR A).*

#### What you need to know

- The simple nuclear atom: protons and neutrons in a tiny nucleus, electrons around it.
- The charge and mass of the proton, neutron and electron in SI and relative units.
- Specific charge (charge ÷ mass) of the proton, electron, nuclei and ions.
- Proton number Z, nucleon number A, and nuclide notation ᴬ_Z X.
- What isotopes are and how isotopic data are used.

#### Explained

| Particle | Charge / C | Relative charge | Mass / kg | Relative mass |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Proton | +1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ | +1 | 1.673 × 10⁻²⁷ | 1 |
| Neutron | 0 | 0 | 1.675 × 10⁻²⁷ | 1 |
| Electron | −1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ | −1 | 9.11 × 10⁻³¹ | 1/1836 (about 0.0005) |

- **Proton number Z** (atomic number): number of protons. It fixes the element.
- **Nucleon number A** (mass number): number of protons + neutrons.
- **Neutron number** N = A − Z.
- **Nuclide notation**: A written top left, Z bottom left of the symbol. Carbon-14 is ¹⁴₆C: 6 protons, 8 neutrons. A neutral atom also has Z electrons.
- **Isotopes**: atoms of the same element (same Z) with different numbers of neutrons (different A). They have the same chemistry but different nuclear stability. Uses of isotopic data: carbon-14 dating (the fraction of ¹⁴C in once-living material falls as it decays), tracers in medicine, finding average relative atomic mass.

**Specific charge** = charge ÷ mass, in C kg⁻¹.

- Electron: 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ ÷ 9.11 × 10⁻³¹ = 1.76 × 10¹¹ C kg⁻¹ (the largest of any common particle).
- Proton: 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ ÷ 1.673 × 10⁻²⁷ = 9.58 × 10⁷ C kg⁻¹.
- Neutron: 0.
- For a nucleus: charge = Ze, mass ≈ A × 1.67 × 10⁻²⁷ kg (protons and neutrons only).
- For an ion: count the protons and electrons to get the net charge; include every nucleon (and, strictly, electrons) in the mass.

#### Worked example

1. Find the specific charge of the ⁷₃Li nucleus.

   Charge = 3 × 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ = 4.80 × 10⁻¹⁹ C. Mass ≈ (3 × 1.673 + 4 × 1.675) × 10⁻²⁷ = 1.172 × 10⁻²⁶ kg. Specific charge = 4.10 × 10⁷ C kg⁻¹.

2. An ion of ²⁴₁₂Mg has lost two electrons. Find its specific charge.

   Charge = +2e = 3.20 × 10⁻¹⁹ C. Mass ≈ 24 × 1.67 × 10⁻²⁷ = 4.01 × 10⁻²⁶ kg (the 10 electrons add only 9.1 × 10⁻³⁰ kg, under 0.03%). Specific charge = 7.98 × 10⁶ C kg⁻¹.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the nucleon number for the charge of a nucleus.
- For a negative ion, forgetting the extra electrons give a negative charge.
- Saying isotopes have different numbers of protons.

### 3.2.1.2 Stable and unstable nuclei

*Refs: AQA 3.2.1.2 · OCR A 6.4.1(e) · OCR B 6.2.1(a)(vi). Level: AS & A (AQA); A-level only (OCR A).*

#### What you need to know

- The strong nuclear force holds the nucleus together: attractive up to about 3 fm, repulsive at separations below about 0.5 fm.
- Unstable nuclei decay by alpha or beta emission.
- Equations for alpha decay and β⁻ decay, including the antineutrino.
- The neutrino was proposed to explain how energy is conserved in beta decay.

#### Explained

Protons repel each other electrostatically. Gravity between nucleons is far too weak to stop this. The **strong nuclear force** acts between all nucleons (proton–proton, proton–neutron, neutron–neutron) and is the same for each pair.

- From about 0.5 fm to 3 fm it is strongly attractive; it is much stronger than the electrostatic repulsion at these distances.
- Beyond about 3 fm it falls to almost zero, so a nucleon only feels its nearest neighbours.
- Below about 0.5 fm it is repulsive, which stops the nucleus collapsing.

A graph of strong force against separation (label which direction is repulsion and which is attraction) starts large and repulsive at very small separation, crosses zero at about 0.5 fm, reaches a maximum attraction near 1 fm, then decays to zero by about 3 fm. The equilibrium separation of nucleons is where the force is zero.

**Why nuclei are unstable.** Large nuclei have many protons, and electrostatic repulsion acts across the whole nucleus while the strong force only acts between neighbours. Nuclei with too many protons, too many neutrons, or simply too many nucleons are unstable.

**Alpha decay.** Very large nuclei (Z above about 82) emit an alpha particle, a helium nucleus ⁴₂He:

ᴬ_Z X → ᴬ⁻⁴_{Z−2} Y + ⁴₂α. Example: ²³⁸₉₂U → ²³⁴₉₀Th + ⁴₂α.

**Beta-minus decay.** Neutron-rich nuclei: a neutron changes into a proton, emitting an electron and an electron antineutrino:

ᴬ_Z X → ᴬ_{Z+1} Y + ⁰₋₁β + ν̄ₑ. Example: ¹⁴₆C → ¹⁴₇N + ⁰₋₁β + ν̄ₑ.

**Why the neutrino was needed.** In alpha decay, every alpha particle from a given nuclide has the same kinetic energy. In beta decay, the electrons have a continuous range of energies up to a maximum. If only two particles were produced, conservation of energy and momentum would force a single energy. Pauli suggested a third, neutral, almost massless particle (the antineutrino) carries away the missing energy and momentum. It interacts so weakly that it was only detected about 25 years later.

#### Worked example

1. Write the equation for the β⁻ decay of ⁹⁰₃₈Sr.

   ⁹⁰₃₈Sr → ⁹⁰₃₉Y + ⁰₋₁β + ν̄ₑ. Check: A: 90 = 90 + 0; Z: 38 = 39 − 1.

2. Polonium-210 emits an alpha particle. Write the equation.

   ²¹⁰₈₄Po → ²⁰⁶₈₂Pb + ⁴₂α.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing a neutrino (not an antineutrino) in β⁻ decay.
- Saying the strong force acts between protons only, or that it acts on electrons.
- Saying the strong force is always attractive.

### 3.2.1.3 Particles, antiparticles and photons

*Refs: AQA 3.2.1.3 · Edexcel 137, 138, 141 · OCR A 6.4.2(a), (b), 6.4.4(c) · Eduqas C3.7(d), (e). Level: AS & A (AQA, Eduqas); A-level only (Edexcel, OCR A).*

#### What you need to know

- Every particle has an antiparticle with the same mass and rest energy but opposite charge (and opposite other quantum numbers).
- The positron, antiproton, antineutron and antineutrino are the antiparticles of the electron, proton, neutron and neutrino.
- The photon model of electromagnetic radiation: E = hf = hc/λ.
- Annihilation and pair production, and the energies involved (rest energies in MeV).
- (Edexcel) Use ΔE = c²Δm, and MeV, GeV, MeV/c² and GeV/c² with conversion to SI.

#### Explained

| Particle | Antiparticle | Symbol | Rest energy / MeV |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| electron e⁻ | positron | e⁺ | 0.511 |
| proton p | antiproton | p̄ | 938.3 |
| neutron n | antineutron | n̄ | 939.6 |
| neutrino νₑ | antineutrino | ν̄ₑ | about 0 |

The antiparticle has the same rest mass, opposite charge, and opposite baryon number, lepton number and strangeness. The antineutron is neutral, like the neutron, but has baryon number −1 and is made of antiquarks.

**Rest energy.** Mass is a store of energy: E = mc². The rest energy of an electron is 9.11 × 10⁻³¹ × (3.00 × 10⁸)² = 8.20 × 10⁻¹⁴ J = 0.511 MeV. Masses are often quoted as MeV/c² (electron 0.511 MeV/c²; proton 938 MeV/c²).

**Photons.** Electromagnetic radiation is emitted and absorbed in packets of energy called photons, each with energy E = hf = hc/λ, where h = 6.63 × 10⁻³⁴ J s is the Planck constant. A photon has no charge and no rest mass, and travels at c.

**Annihilation.** When a particle meets its antiparticle, both are destroyed and their total energy (rest energy plus kinetic energy) becomes two photons. Two are needed so that momentum is conserved: if the pair was nearly at rest, the photons travel in opposite directions with equal energy. For a slow electron and positron, each photon has energy 0.511 MeV. Use: positron emission tomography (PET), where a tracer emits positrons that annihilate with electrons and the two gamma photons are detected on opposite sides of the patient.

**Pair production.** A photon with enough energy can turn into a particle–antiparticle pair. Minimum photon energy = 2 × rest energy of the particle. For an electron–positron pair, E_min = 1.022 MeV. Any extra energy becomes kinetic energy of the pair. It happens near a nucleus, which recoils to conserve momentum. In a cloud chamber in a magnetic field, the two particles leave spiral tracks curving in opposite directions from the same point.

#### Worked example

1. Find the minimum frequency of a photon that can produce a proton–antiproton pair.

   E_min = 2 × 938.3 MeV = 1876.6 MeV = 1876.6 × 10⁶ × 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ = 3.00 × 10⁻¹⁰ J. f = E/h = 3.00 × 10⁻¹⁰ / 6.63 × 10⁻³⁴ = 4.53 × 10²³ Hz.

2. A slow electron and slow positron annihilate. Find the wavelength of each photon.

   E = 0.511 MeV = 8.18 × 10⁻¹⁴ J. λ = hc/E = (6.63 × 10⁻³⁴ × 3.00 × 10⁸) / 8.18 × 10⁻¹⁴ = 2.43 × 10⁻¹² m.

3. (Edexcel) A particle has mass 1.88 × 10⁻²⁸ kg. Express its mass in MeV/c².

   E = mc² = 1.88 × 10⁻²⁸ × 9.00 × 10¹⁶ = 1.69 × 10⁻¹¹ J = 1.69 × 10⁻¹¹ / 1.60 × 10⁻¹³ = 106 MeV. Mass = 106 MeV/c² (a muon).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying an antiparticle has opposite mass. Mass is the same.
- Saying annihilation produces one photon.
- Forgetting to double the rest energy for pair production.
- Forgetting to convert MeV to J (× 1.60 × 10⁻¹³) before using E = hf.

### 3.2.1.4 Particle interactions

*Refs: AQA 3.2.1.4 · Eduqas C3.7(i), (k). Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The four fundamental interactions: gravity, electromagnetic, weak nuclear and strong nuclear.
- Forces between particles are explained by exchange particles.
- The electromagnetic force is carried by virtual photons.
- The weak interaction (in β⁻ decay, β⁺ decay, electron capture and electron–proton collisions) is carried by W⁺ and W⁻ bosons.
- Simple diagrams showing incoming and outgoing particles and the exchange particle.

#### Explained

| Interaction | Acts on | Range | Exchange particle |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Strong | Quarks, so all hadrons (not leptons) | About 10⁻¹⁵ m | Pions between nucleons (gluons between quarks, not tested) |
| Electromagnetic | Charged particles | Infinite | Virtual photon (γ) |
| Weak | All quarks and leptons | About 10⁻¹⁸ m | W⁺, W⁻ (and Z⁰, not tested) |
| Gravity | All particles with mass | Infinite | Graviton (hypothetical, not tested) |

Eduqas C3.7(i) adds: gravity is negligible except between very large objects; the weak interaction only shows up when the strong and electromagnetic interactions cannot act; neutral hadrons still feel the electromagnetic force because their quarks are charged.

**Exchange particles.** In particle physics, a force is the result of particles exchanging a virtual particle that carries momentum (and energy) between them. A model: two people on skateboards throwing a heavy ball to each other move apart (repulsion). Virtual particles exist only briefly; heavier exchange particles have shorter range, which is why the weak force (W mass about 80 GeV/c²) has such a short range and the electromagnetic force (massless photon) has infinite range.

**Weak interactions.** The weak interaction is the only one that can change one type (flavour) of quark into another, and it is the only one that involves neutrinos.

| Process | Equation | Exchange particle |
| --- | --- | --- |
| β⁻ decay | n → p + e⁻ + ν̄ₑ | W⁻ (from the neutron to the electron–antineutrino pair) |
| β⁺ decay | p → n + e⁺ + νₑ | W⁺ |
| Electron capture | p + e⁻ → n + νₑ | W⁺ (from proton to electron) or W⁻ (from electron to proton); both are accepted pictures |
| Electron–proton collision | e⁻ + p → n + νₑ | W⁻ (from electron to proton) |
| Neutrino–neutron | νₑ + n → p + e⁻ | W⁻ (from neutron to neutrino) or W⁺ the other way |

**Drawing interaction diagrams.** Time runs up the page. Draw each incoming particle as a line coming in from the bottom, each outgoing particle as a line leaving the top, and the exchange particle as a wavy line between the two vertices. Check charge is conserved at each vertex. For β⁻ decay: n comes in, p goes out at the same vertex, a W⁻ leaves that vertex (charge 0 → +1 + (−1)), and the W⁻ decays into e⁻ and ν̄ₑ at the second vertex. Electron–electron repulsion: two electrons in, two electrons out, a wavy line labelled γ between them.

#### Worked example

1. Show that charge is conserved at both vertices of a β⁺ decay diagram.

   Vertex 1: p (+1) → n (0) + W⁺ (+1). Vertex 2: W⁺ (+1) → e⁺ (+1) + νₑ (0). Both balance.

2. Which exchange particle is involved when an electron is captured by a proton, if the W comes from the proton?

   p (+1) → n (0) + W⁺; then W⁺ + e⁻ → νₑ. So W⁺.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying leptons feel the strong force.
- Saying the photon is the exchange particle of the weak interaction.
- Getting the W charge wrong: work out charge at the vertex rather than guessing.
- Drawing the W as a straight line, or showing particles appearing from nowhere.

### 3.2.1.5 Classification of particles

*Refs: AQA 3.2.1.5 · Edexcel 140 · OCR A 6.4.2(c), (d) · Eduqas C3.7(c), (f). Level: AS & A (AQA, Eduqas); A-level only (Edexcel, OCR A).*

#### What you need to know

- Hadrons feel the strong interaction. They are baryons (proton, neutron) and antibaryons, or mesons (pion, kaon).
- Baryon number is a quantum number that is always conserved.
- The proton is the only stable baryon; all others eventually decay into it.
- The pion is the exchange particle of the strong force between nucleons. The kaon can decay into pions.
- Leptons (electron, muon, electron neutrino, muon neutrino and their antiparticles) do not feel the strong force.
- Lepton number is conserved separately for electron leptons and muon leptons.
- The muon decays into an electron.
- Strange particles are produced by the strong interaction and decay by the weak interaction. Strangeness is conserved in strong interactions and can change by 0 or ±1 in weak interactions. Strange particles are produced in pairs.
- Particle physics depends on large, international teams to test and validate new results.

#### Explained

```
                    particles
          ┌────────────┴─────────────┐
       hadrons                    leptons
   (feel strong force)       (do not feel strong force)
     ┌──────┴──────┐          e⁻, μ⁻, νₑ, ν_μ
  baryons       mesons        and antiparticles
  (3 quarks)   (quark + antiquark)
  p, n, ...    π, K
```

**Baryons.** Proton (B = +1), neutron (B = +1). Antibaryons have B = −1. Mesons and leptons have B = 0. Baryon number is always conserved. The free neutron is unstable (half-life about 10 minutes) and decays to a proton; the proton has never been seen to decay.

**Mesons.**

| Meson | Charges | Rest energy | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Pion | π⁺, π⁰, π⁻ | About 140 MeV (π⁰ 135) | Exchange particle of the strong force between nucleons; found in cosmic ray showers |
| Kaon | K⁺, K⁰, K⁻ | About 494 MeV (K⁰ 498) | Strange; decays into pions (or muons) |

**Leptons.** Fundamental particles (not made of anything smaller).

| Lepton | L_e | L_μ | Charge |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| e⁻, νₑ | +1 | 0 | −1, 0 |
| e⁺, ν̄ₑ | −1 | 0 | +1, 0 |
| μ⁻, ν_μ | 0 | +1 | −1, 0 |
| μ⁺, ν̄_μ | 0 | −1 | +1, 0 |

The muon is a heavy version of the electron (rest energy 106 MeV) and decays: μ⁻ → e⁻ + ν̄ₑ + ν_μ. Check: L_μ: 1 = 0 + 0 + 1; L_e: 0 = 1 − 1 + 0.

**Strangeness.** Kaons were puzzling: they were produced quickly (by the strong interaction) but lived a long time (about 10⁻⁸ s, typical of a weak decay). Strangeness S explains this. It is conserved in strong interactions, so strange particles must be made in pairs with opposite strangeness (S = +1 and S = −1). In decay, they can only use the weak interaction, which allows S to change by 0 or ±1, and so they decay slowly.

| Particle | S |
| --- | --- |
| K⁺, K⁰ | +1 |
| K⁻, K̄⁰ | −1 |
| s quark | −1 |
| s̄ antiquark | +1 |
| Non-strange particles | 0 |

**Big science.** Particle accelerators like those at CERN cost billions and need thousands of physicists and engineers from many countries. Results are only accepted after independent teams and detectors confirm them and the work is peer reviewed (for example, the Higgs boson was confirmed by two separate detector teams, ATLAS and CMS).

#### Worked example

1. Classify each particle: K⁰, μ⁺, antineutron, π⁻.

   K⁰: meson (hadron). μ⁺: lepton. Antineutron: antibaryon (hadron). π⁻: meson.

2. Explain why the reaction p + p → p + p + K⁺ cannot happen, but p + p → p + p + K⁺ + K⁻ can.

   It would be a strong interaction (hadrons colliding), so strangeness must be conserved. First: S = 0 → 0 + 0 + 1, not conserved. Second: 0 → 0 + 0 + 1 − 1 = 0, conserved. (Charge: +2 → +2 + 1 − 1 = +2. Baryon number: 2 → 2.)

#### Common mistakes

- Calling a muon a meson (it is a lepton, despite the old name "mu meson").
- Mixing electron lepton number and muon lepton number; each is conserved separately.
- Saying strangeness is conserved in weak interactions.
- Saying the neutron is stable outside the nucleus.

### 3.2.1.6 Quarks and antiquarks

*Refs: AQA 3.2.1.6 · Edexcel 140, 141 · OCR A 6.4.2(e), (f), (g), (h) · OCR B 6.2.1(a)(v) · Eduqas C3.7(c), (e), (f), (g), (h). Level: AS & A (AQA, OCR B, Eduqas); A-level only (Edexcel, OCR A).*

#### What you need to know

- Charge, baryon number and strangeness of up, down and strange quarks and their antiquarks.
- Quark make-up of the proton, neutron, antiproton, antineutron, pions and kaons.
- The decay of the neutron.
- Quarks are never seen on their own (they are confined in hadrons).

#### Explained

| Quark | Charge (e) | Baryon number | Strangeness |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| up u | +2/3 | +1/3 | 0 |
| down d | −1/3 | +1/3 | 0 |
| strange s | −1/3 | +1/3 | −1 |
| anti-up ū | −2/3 | −1/3 | 0 |
| anti-down d̄ | +1/3 | −1/3 | 0 |
| anti-strange s̄ | +1/3 | −1/3 | +1 |

| Hadron | Quarks | Check of charge |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Proton | uud | 2/3 + 2/3 − 1/3 = +1 |
| Neutron | udd | 2/3 − 1/3 − 1/3 = 0 |
| Antiproton | ūūd̄ | −1 |
| Antineutron | ūd̄d̄ | 0 |
| π⁺ | ud̄ | 2/3 + 1/3 = +1 |
| π⁻ | dū | −1 |
| π⁰ | uū or dd̄ | 0 |
| K⁺ | us̄ | 2/3 + 1/3 = +1 (S = +1) |
| K⁻ | sū | −1 (S = −1) |
| K⁰ | ds̄ | 0 (S = +1) |
| K̄⁰ | sd̄ | 0 (S = −1) |

Baryons are three quarks; antibaryons are three antiquarks; mesons are one quark and one antiquark. Quarks are always bound in hadrons; trying to pull one out supplies enough energy to make a new quark–antiquark pair, so a meson forms instead (confinement).

**(Eduqas C3.7(h))** Other first-generation baryons can be worked out from charge: a baryon of charge +2 must be uuu; one of charge −1 must be ddd.

**(Edexcel 140)** The quark–lepton model is symmetric: quarks and leptons come in matching pairs (generations). After the bottom quark was found, the symmetry predicted a partner, the top quark, which was found in 1995.

**Neutron decay.** n → p + e⁻ + ν̄ₑ. In quark terms, one down quark becomes an up quark: udd → uud, so d → u + e⁻ + ν̄ₑ (via W⁻).

#### Worked example

1. A particle is made of a strange quark and an anti-down quark. Find its charge, baryon number and strangeness, and name it.

   Charge −1/3 + 1/3 = 0. B = 1/3 − 1/3 = 0 (a meson). S = −1. It is the K̄⁰ (anti-K-zero).

2. The Λ⁰ baryon has strangeness −1 and charge 0. Suggest its quark content.

   Three quarks, one of which is s (S = −1). Remaining charge must be +1/3 from two quarks: u + d (2/3 − 1/3). So uds.

#### Common mistakes

- Giving the antiquark of the down quark a charge of −1/3 (it is +1/3).
- Forgetting the s quark has S = −1, not +1.
- Writing mesons as two quarks (they are a quark and an antiquark).

### 3.2.1.7 Applications of conservation laws

*Refs: AQA 3.2.1.7 · Edexcel 135, 142, 143 · OCR A 6.4.2(i), (j), (k), (l) · OCR B 6.2.1(a)(vi) · Eduqas C3.7(j), (k). Level: AS & A (AQA, OCR B, Eduqas); A-level only (Edexcel, OCR A).*

#### What you need to know

- How quarks change in β⁻ and β⁺ decay.
- How to use conservation of charge, baryon number, lepton number and strangeness to decide whether an interaction can happen (data for unfamiliar particles will be given).
- Energy and momentum are conserved in all interactions.

#### Explained

**Quark changes in beta decay.**

- β⁻: d → u + e⁻ + ν̄ₑ (a neutron becomes a proton).
- β⁺: u → d + e⁺ + νₑ (a proton in a proton-rich nucleus becomes a neutron). This cannot happen to a free proton, because the neutron is heavier; the energy comes from the nucleus.

**Checklist for any reaction.**

| Quantity | Strong | Electromagnetic | Weak |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Charge | Conserved | Conserved | Conserved |
| Baryon number | Conserved | Conserved | Conserved |
| Lepton numbers L_e and L_μ (separately) | Conserved | Conserved | Conserved |
| Strangeness | Conserved | Conserved | Can change by 0, ±1 |
| Energy and momentum | Conserved | Conserved | Conserved |

If any always-conserved quantity fails, the reaction cannot happen. If only strangeness fails by one, the reaction can only happen by the weak interaction. In addition, the total rest energy of the products cannot exceed the total energy available.

**Which interaction?** If a neutrino is involved, or a quark changes flavour, it is weak. If only charged particles and photons are involved, it is electromagnetic. If only hadrons are involved and strangeness is conserved, it is probably strong.
