**(Edexcel 135)** Particle tracks in detectors: charged particles leave tracks; neutral ones do not, but their decay products do (a "V" appearing from nowhere shows a neutral particle decaying into two oppositely charged ones). Curvature in a magnetic field shows sign of charge and momentum (see file 31).

#### Worked example

1. Can the reaction p + ν̄ₑ → n + e⁺ happen? If so, by which interaction?

   Charge: +1 + 0 = 0 + 1 ✓. B: 1 = 1 ✓. L_e: 0 − 1 = 0 − 1 ✓. S: 0 = 0 ✓. Allowed. A neutrino is involved, so weak.

2. Can π⁻ + p → K⁰ + n happen?

   Charge: −1 + 1 = 0 + 0 ✓. B: 0 + 1 = 0 + 1 ✓. S: 0 → +1 + 0 ✗. A collision of hadrons that makes a strange particle is a strong interaction, and strangeness must be conserved, so this cannot happen. (π⁻ + p → K⁰ + Λ⁰ is allowed: S goes 0 → +1 − 1 = 0, so strange particles appear in pairs.)

3. Show that μ⁻ → e⁻ + γ is not observed.

   L_μ: +1 → 0 ✗; L_e: 0 → +1 ✗. Forbidden.

#### Common mistakes

- Checking only charge and stopping.
- Treating L_e and L_μ as one lepton number.
- Saying a reaction is "possible" when strangeness changes by 2.
- Forgetting that an antilepton has lepton number −1.

### Check questions

1. Calculate the specific charge of a ³⁵₁₇Cl⁻ ion. (3 marks)
2. Sketch a graph of how the strong nuclear force between two nucleons varies with separation, marking 0.5 fm and 3 fm. (3 marks)
3. Explain why the neutrino was proposed to explain beta decay. (3 marks)
4. A photon produces an electron–positron pair, each particle having kinetic energy 0.30 MeV. Calculate the energy of the photon in J. (3 marks)
5. Draw a diagram for β⁺ decay, labelling all particles and the exchange particle. (3 marks)
6. State the quark content of a K⁻ and explain why it is classed as a meson. (2 marks)
7. The Σ⁺ baryon is uus. Find its charge and strangeness. A Σ⁺ decays to p + π⁰. Explain which interaction is responsible. (4 marks)
8. Use conservation laws to decide whether each can occur: (a) n → p + e⁻ + νₑ; (b) K⁺ → π⁺ + π⁰. (4 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Charge = −1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ C (one extra electron) (1); mass ≈ 35 × 1.67 × 10⁻²⁷ = 5.85 × 10⁻²⁶ kg (1); specific charge = 2.7 × 10⁶ C kg⁻¹ (accept magnitude) (1).
2. Repulsive below about 0.5 fm (1); attractive with a maximum around 1 fm (1); falls to zero by about 3 fm (1).
3. Beta particles have a range / continuous spread of kinetic energies (1); if only the beta particle and daughter nucleus were produced, each would have a single fixed energy (1); a third particle carries away the missing energy (and momentum) so energy is conserved (1).
4. Total energy = 2 × 0.511 + 2 × 0.30 = 1.622 MeV (2); × 1.60 × 10⁻¹³ = 2.6 × 10⁻¹³ J (1).
5. p in, n out at one vertex (1); W⁺ exchange particle shown as wavy line from p/n vertex (1); W⁺ → e⁺ and νₑ at second vertex (1).
6. sū (1); a quark and an antiquark / baryon number 0 (1).
7. Charge 2/3 + 2/3 − 1/3 = +1; S = −1 (1 each). Strangeness changes from −1 to 0 (1); so it must be the weak interaction (1).
8. (a) Charge and B conserved (1); L_e: 0 → +1 + 1 = 2, not conserved, so cannot occur (1). (b) Charge +1 → +1 ✓, B 0 → 0 ✓ (1); S: +1 → 0, changes by 1, so allowed only as a weak decay; it occurs (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/19-mass-energy-fission-and-fusion.md -->

## Mass and energy: binding energy, fission, fusion, nuclear reactors and safety

A nucleus has less mass than the nucleons that make it. This file covers E = mc², the atomic mass unit, mass defect and binding energy, the binding energy per nucleon curve, energy released in fission and fusion, the conditions needed for fusion, chain reactions and thermal fission reactors (moderator, control rods, coolant), and the safety and waste issues of nuclear power.

### Overview

A-level only for every board. Particle–antiparticle creation and annihilation are in file 02. Decay modes and the N–Z graph are in file 18. Fusion in stars is in file 20.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.8.1.6 to 3.8.1.8 · Edexcel 164 to 167 · OCR A 6.4.4 · OCR B 6.2.2(a)(ii), (iii), (b), (c)(iv) · Eduqas C3.8.
- AQA 3.8.1.7 asks for the moderator, control rods and coolant, and a mechanical model of moderation by elastic collisions; OCR A 6.4.4(h) asks for the basic structure of a fission reactor too. Edexcel, OCR B and Eduqas need fission and fusion in terms of binding energy, not reactor design.
- Edexcel 167: the mechanism of fusion and the need for very high temperature and density.
- OCR A 6.4.4(i): environmental impact of nuclear waste. AQA 3.8.1.8: fuel handling, shielding, emergency shut-down, waste storage.
- AQA quotes 1 u = 931.5 MeV.

### 3.8.1.6 Mass and energy

*Refs: AQA 3.8.1.6 · Edexcel 164, 165, 166, 167 · OCR A 6.4.4(a), (b), (d), (e), (f), (j), (k) · OCR B 6.2.2(a)(ii), (a)(iii), (b)(i), (b)(ii), (c)(iv) · Eduqas C3.8(a) to (e). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- E = mc² applies to all energy changes.
- Mass difference (mass defect) and binding energy.
- The atomic mass unit u; 1 u = 931.5 MeV (or 1.661 × 10⁻²⁷ kg).
- Fission and fusion; calculating the energy released from nuclear masses.
- The graph of binding energy per nucleon against nucleon number, and the regions where fission and fusion release energy.
- (Edexcel 167) Why fusion needs very high temperatures and densities.
- Knowing the physics of nuclear energy lets society make informed decisions.

#### Explained

**Mass–energy.** Energy and mass are equivalent: ΔE = Δm c². Any change in energy of a system changes its mass, though for chemical reactions the change is far too small to measure. In nuclear reactions it is large enough (about 0.1% of the mass) to calculate.

**Atomic mass unit.** 1 u = 1/12 of the mass of a carbon-12 atom = 1.661 × 10⁻²⁷ kg. Its energy equivalent: 1.661 × 10⁻²⁷ × (3.00 × 10⁸)² = 1.49 × 10⁻¹⁰ J = 931.5 MeV.

**Mass defect.** The mass of a nucleus is less than the total mass of its separate protons and neutrons. Mass defect Δm = (Z m_p + N m_n) − m_nucleus.

**Binding energy.** The energy needed to separate a nucleus completely into its individual nucleons (equivalently, the energy released when the nucleus forms from them). Binding energy = Δm c². Binding energy per nucleon = binding energy ÷ A. The higher the binding energy per nucleon, the more stable the nucleus.

**The binding energy per nucleon curve.**

- Rises steeply from hydrogen (0) through deuterium (1.1 MeV), with a peak at helium-4 (7.1 MeV), to a broad maximum of about 8.8 MeV near iron-56 (A ≈ 56 to 62).
- Then falls slowly to about 7.6 MeV for uranium.

Energy is released whenever nucleons end up more tightly bound (higher on the curve):

- *Fusion* of light nuclei (left of iron) into heavier ones moves up the steep left side: large energy release per nucleon.
- *Fission* of heavy nuclei (right of iron) into two medium-sized ones moves up the gentle right side.
- Iron is at the top, so it can release energy by neither.

**Energy released** = (total mass before − total mass after) × c² = increase in total binding energy.

**Fission.** A heavy nucleus such as uranium-235 absorbs a slow neutron, becomes unstable (U-236), and splits into two smaller nuclei of roughly similar size, plus 2 or 3 neutrons and about 200 MeV of energy (mostly kinetic energy of the fragments). Example:

²³⁵₉₂U + ¹₀n → ¹⁴¹₅₆Ba + ⁹²₃₆Kr + 3¹₀n

**Fusion.** Two light nuclei join to make a heavier one, releasing energy. In the Sun, hydrogen fuses to helium (proton–proton chain). A reaction for fusion reactors: ²₁H + ³₁H → ⁴₂He + ¹₀n + 17.6 MeV.

**Why fusion is hard** (Edexcel 167, OCR A 6.4.4(j)). Nuclei are positive and repel. To fuse, they must get close enough (about 10⁻¹⁵ m) for the strong force to act. This needs very high kinetic energy, so temperatures of about 10⁸ K (10⁷ K in the Sun's core, helped by quantum tunnelling). At these temperatures matter is a plasma. A high density is also needed so collisions happen often enough to sustain the reaction. In stars, gravity provides the confinement; on Earth, strong magnetic fields (tokamaks) or lasers (inertial confinement).

**Society.** Understanding mass–energy allows informed decisions about nuclear power (low carbon emissions but waste and accident risks), fusion research funding and nuclear weapons.

#### Worked example

1. Find the binding energy per nucleon of iron-56 (nuclear mass 55.92068 u; m_p = 1.00728 u; m_n = 1.00867 u).

   Separate nucleons: 26 × 1.00728 + 30 × 1.00867 = 26.18928 + 30.26010 = 56.44938 u. Mass defect = 0.52870 u. Binding energy = 0.52870 × 931.5 = 492.5 MeV. Per nucleon = 8.79 MeV.

2. Find the energy released in the D–T fusion reaction. Masses: ²H 2.01355 u, ³H 3.01550 u, ⁴He 4.00151 u, n 1.00867 u.

   Before: 5.02905 u. After: 5.01018 u. Δm = 0.01887 u. E = 0.01887 × 931.5 = 17.6 MeV = 2.8 × 10⁻¹² J.

3. A fission of U-235 releases 200 MeV. How much energy is released by fission of 1.0 kg of U-235?

   Nuclei = (1.0/0.235) × 6.02 × 10²³ = 2.56 × 10²⁴. E = 2.56 × 10²⁴ × 200 × 1.60 × 10⁻¹³ = 8.2 × 10¹³ J (compare about 3 × 10⁷ J from 1 kg of coal).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying binding energy is energy stored in the nucleus that is released when it forms the nucleus (binding energy is the energy needed to pull it apart; a bigger binding energy means a more stable nucleus with less mass).
- Using atomic masses for one side and nuclear masses for the other.
- Saying fission of iron releases energy.
- Forgetting to multiply by 931.5 (MeV) or by c² with masses in kg (J).

### 3.8.1.7 Induced fission

*Refs: AQA 3.8.1.7 · OCR A 6.4.4(g), (h) · OCR B 6.2.2(a)(iii). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Fission induced by thermal (slow) neutrons; chain reaction; critical mass.
- The functions of the moderator, control rods and coolant in a thermal reactor.
- A simple mechanical model of moderation by elastic collisions.
- Factors affecting the choice of materials; examples of materials used.

#### Explained

**Thermal neutrons.** U-235 is far more likely to absorb slow neutrons (kinetic energy about 0.025 eV, similar to the thermal energy of particles at room temperature) than the fast neutrons (about 2 MeV) produced by fission.

**Chain reaction.** Each fission releases 2 or 3 neutrons. If, on average, at least one goes on to cause another fission, the reaction sustains itself. If exactly one does, the reactor is critical (steady power). More than one: supercritical (power rises; uncontrolled in a bomb).

**Critical mass.** The minimum mass of fissile material that can sustain a chain reaction. Below it, too many neutrons escape through the surface before causing fission (a small lump has a large surface-area-to-volume ratio).

**Thermal reactor.**

| Part | Function | Material and reason |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Fuel rods | Contain the fissile fuel | Uranium enriched to about 3% U-235 (the rest is U-238) |
| Moderator | Slows fast neutrons to thermal speeds by elastic collisions so they can cause more fission | Water, heavy water or graphite: light nuclei (close in mass to the neutron so it loses a large fraction of its energy per collision) that do not absorb neutrons much |
| Control rods | Absorb neutrons, to keep exactly one neutron per fission going on to cause another; lowered to reduce power or shut down | Boron or cadmium: they absorb neutrons readily without becoming unstable in a way that releases more |
| Coolant | Carries thermal energy from the core to a heat exchanger to make steam for turbines | Water (high specific heat capacity, cheap) or carbon dioxide gas; molten sodium in fast reactors; must not absorb neutrons strongly, must flow easily |

**Elastic collision model of moderation.** When a moving ball hits a stationary ball of the same mass head-on elastically, it stops and passes on all its kinetic energy. A neutron hitting a hydrogen nucleus (nearly equal mass) can lose most of its energy in one collision; hitting a heavy nucleus, it bounces off with nearly the same speed. For a head-on elastic collision with a nucleus of mass number A at rest, the fraction of kinetic energy kept is ((A − 1)/(A + 1))². So light nuclei are the best moderators. (Hydrogen in ordinary water absorbs some neutrons, which is why heavy water or graphite is sometimes used.)

#### Worked example

1. A neutron makes a head-on elastic collision with a stationary carbon-12 nucleus. What fraction of its kinetic energy does it keep?

   ((12 − 1)/(12 + 1))² = (11/13)² = 0.716. It loses 28%. About 50 such collisions take it from 2 MeV to thermal energies (0.716⁵⁰ × 2 MeV ≈ 1 × 10⁻⁷ MeV ≈ 0.1 eV; in practice about 100 collisions, since most are not head-on).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the moderator slows the reaction down (it slows neutrons, which keeps the reaction going).
- Confusing control rods (absorb neutrons) with the moderator (slows neutrons).
- Saying critical mass is the mass that explodes.

### 3.8.1.8 Safety aspects

*Refs: AQA 3.8.1.8 · OCR A 6.4.4(i) · OCR B 6.2.2(a)(iii). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Fuel used, remote handling of fuel, shielding, emergency shut-down.
- Producing, handling and storing radioactive waste.
- The balance between risks and benefits of nuclear power.

#### Explained

**In the reactor.**

- *Fuel*: enriched uranium oxide pellets in sealed metal cans; mildly radioactive before use, intensely radioactive after (fission products).
- *Remote handling*: used fuel is moved by robot arms or cranes, never by hand.
- *Shielding*: the core is inside a thick steel pressure vessel and several metres of concrete, which absorb neutrons and gamma rays.
- *Emergency shut-down*: control rods drop fully into the core (often by gravity, automatically on loss of power) to stop the chain reaction at once.

**Radioactive waste.**

- *Low-level* (gloves, clothing, tools): compacted, put in drums and buried in shallow sites.
- *Intermediate-level* (fuel cladding, reactor parts, resins): encased in concrete in steel drums; stored and later buried deeper.
- *High-level* (spent fuel and fission products): very hot and very radioactive. First cooled under water in ponds for years (still generating heat from decay), then may be reprocessed to recover uranium and plutonium; the rest is vitrified (made into glass), sealed in steel containers and stored, with plans for deep geological disposal. Some isotopes have half-lives of thousands of years, so storage must be secure for very long periods, safe from leaks into groundwater, earthquakes and theft.

**Risks and benefits.** Benefits: no carbon dioxide emitted in generation, reliable "base load" electricity, small fuel mass. Risks: accidents (Chernobyl 1986, Fukushima 2011), long-term waste storage, cost of building and decommissioning, risk of materials being used for weapons. Society weighs these against the risks of fossil fuels (climate change, air pollution).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying nuclear power stations can explode like a nuclear bomb (fuel is not enriched enough).
- Saying waste is safe after one half-life.

### Check questions

1. Define binding energy and mass defect. (2 marks)
2. Sketch the graph of binding energy per nucleon against nucleon number, marking the most stable region and showing where fission and fusion release energy. (4 marks)
3. Calculate the binding energy per nucleon of helium-4 (nuclear mass 4.00151 u). (3 marks)
4. In a fission reaction: ²³⁵U + n → ⁹⁰Sr + ¹⁴³Xe + 3n. Masses (nuclear, u): ²³⁵U 234.99346, n 1.00867, ⁹⁰Sr 89.88630, ¹⁴³Xe 142.89380. Calculate the energy released in MeV. (3 marks)
5. Explain why very high temperatures are needed for fusion. (3 marks)
6. Explain the functions of the moderator and the control rods in a thermal reactor and suggest a suitable material for each. (4 marks)
7. Explain why a moderator should be made of a material with light nuclei. (3 marks)
8. Describe how high-level radioactive waste is handled and stored. (4 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Binding energy: energy needed to separate a nucleus into its individual nucleons (1); mass defect: difference between total mass of separate nucleons and mass of nucleus (1).
2. Rapid rise to peak around A ≈ 56 (iron), about 8.8 MeV (1); gradual fall for larger A (1); fusion of light nuclei (left of peak) increases BE per nucleon, releasing energy (1); fission of heavy nuclei (right of peak) into medium nuclei releases energy (1).
3. 2 × 1.00728 + 2 × 1.00867 = 4.03190 u; Δm = 0.03039 u (1); BE = 0.03039 × 931.5 = 28.3 MeV (1); per nucleon = 7.08 MeV (1).
4. Before: 236.00213 u; after: 89.88630 + 142.89380 + 3.02601 = 235.80611 u (1); Δm = 0.19602 u (1); E = 183 MeV (1).
5. Nuclei are positively charged and repel (1); they need very high kinetic energy to come close enough for the strong force to act (1); high temperature means high mean kinetic energy (1).
6. Moderator slows fast neutrons to thermal speeds so they can be absorbed by U-235 / cause fission (1); e.g. water or graphite (1); control rods absorb neutrons to control the rate of fission / keep one neutron per fission (1); e.g. boron or cadmium (1).
7. Neutron transfers most kinetic energy in collision with a nucleus of similar mass (1); collisions are elastic (1); so fewer collisions are needed to slow neutrons down (1).
8. Removed by remote handling (1); cooled under water for years as it is still producing heat (1); vitrified / sealed in glass and steel containers (1); stored securely, deep underground, for thousands of years / long half-lives (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/17-magnetic-fields-induction-and-transformers.md -->

## Magnetic fields, forces on currents and charges, electromagnetic induction, alternating current and transformers

Moving charges make magnetic fields and feel forces in them; changing magnetic fields make e.m.f.s. This file covers magnetic field patterns, the force on a current (F = BIl) and on a moving charge (F = BQv), circular paths and the cyclotron, the Hall effect, magnetic flux and flux linkage, Faraday's and Lenz's laws, generators, alternating current and r.m.s. values, the oscilloscope, and transformers and power transmission, with AQA required practicals 10 and 11.

### Overview

A-level only for every board. Particle accelerators and detectors in more detail (Edexcel 132 to 134) are in file 31. The Hall effect sensor in electronics (AQA 3.13.1.4) is in file 28. A.c. circuits with capacitors and inductors (Eduqas option A) are in file 34. MRI is in file 23.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.7.5, RP10, RP11 · Edexcel 121 to 129 · OCR A 6.3 · OCR B 6.1.1, 6.1.2(a)(v) · Eduqas C3.9, C3.10.
- Fields of a long straight wire B = μ₀I/(2πa) and of a solenoid B = μ₀nI, the effect of an iron core, and the force between parallel wires: Eduqas C3.9(f) to (h). Field patterns only for OCR A 6.3.1(c).
- Hall effect: Eduqas C3.9(d) (with a specified practical using a Hall probe). AQA puts the Hall sensor in option 3.13.
- Velocity selector (crossed electric and magnetic fields): OCR A 6.3.2(c), Eduqas C3.9(j).
- R.m.s. values and the oscilloscope: AQA 3.7.5.5, Edexcel 128, 129. OCR A and OCR B do not require r.m.s. Eduqas puts a.c. in option A.
- Transformers: AQA, OCR A 6.3.3(f), OCR B. Edexcel 125 only asks about induction between linked coils. Eduqas C3.10 does not name transformers.
- OCR B 6.1.1(a)(iv): magnetic circuits (flux produced by current-turns, permeance, effect of iron and of air gaps).
- Measuring B with a current balance: AQA RP10, OCR A 6.3.1(e)(ii) (PAG11), OCR B 6.1.1(d)(ii), Eduqas specified practical.

### 3.7.5.1 Magnetic flux density

*Refs: AQA 3.7.5.1, RP10 · Edexcel 121, 123 · OCR A 6.3.1(a) to (f) · OCR B 6.1.1(a)(iii), (b)(i), (c)(ii), (d)(ii) · Eduqas C3.9(a), (b), (e), (f), (g), (h). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The force on a current-carrying wire in a magnetic field: F = BIl when the field is perpendicular to the current (F = BIl sin θ in general).
- Fleming's left-hand rule.
- Magnetic flux density B and the definition of the tesla.
- AQA required practical 10: how the force on a wire depends on B, I and l, using a top-pan balance.
- (OCR A, Eduqas) Field patterns for a long straight wire, a flat coil and a solenoid. (Eduqas) B = μ₀I/(2πa), B = μ₀nI, iron cores, force between parallel currents.

#### Explained

**Magnetic fields** arise from moving charges (currents) and from permanent magnets. Field lines go from north to south outside a magnet and never cross.

- Long straight wire: concentric circles around the wire; direction by the right-hand grip rule (thumb along the current, fingers curl in the field direction). B = μ₀I/(2πa) at distance a (Eduqas), where μ₀ = 4π × 10⁻⁷ T m A⁻¹.
- Flat circular coil: field through the middle is along the axis.
- Solenoid: uniform field inside, like a bar magnet outside. B = μ₀nI, where n = turns per metre (Eduqas). An iron core greatly increases B (Eduqas C3.9(g)).

**Force on a current.** A wire carrying current I across a magnetic field feels a force F = BIl sin θ, where θ is the angle between the wire and the field. Maximum when perpendicular (F = BIl); zero when parallel.

**Fleming's left-hand rule.** First finger: Field (N to S). Second finger: Current (conventional). Thumb: Motion (force). All at right angles.

**Magnetic flux density** B is defined by B = F/(Il) for a wire at right angles to the field. **1 tesla** is the flux density that produces a force of 1 N on 1 m of wire carrying 1 A at right angles to the field. Earth's field: about 5 × 10⁻⁵ T. Strong lab magnet: about 0.1 to 1 T. MRI: 1.5 to 3 T.

**Force between parallel wires** (Eduqas C3.9(h)). Each wire is in the other's field. Currents in the same direction attract; opposite directions repel. F/l = μ₀I₁I₂/(2πa).

**Why the force arises** (OCR B 6.1.1(a)(iii)). The field of the wire and the external field add on one side and cancel on the other; field lines "try to shorten", pushing the wire towards the weaker side.

#### Worked example

1. A 0.050 m length of wire carries 3.0 A at right angles to a field of 0.20 T. Find the force.

   F = 0.20 × 3.0 × 0.050 = 0.030 N.

2. The wire is turned so it makes 30° with the field. Find the new force.

   F = 0.030 × sin 30° = 0.015 N.

3. (Eduqas) Find B at 2.0 cm from a long wire carrying 10 A, and inside a solenoid with 2000 turns per metre carrying 1.5 A.

   Wire: 4π × 10⁻⁷ × 10/(2π × 0.020) = 1.0 × 10⁻⁴ T. Solenoid: 4π × 10⁻⁷ × 2000 × 1.5 = 3.8 × 10⁻³ T.

#### Common mistakes

- Using electron flow direction in Fleming's left-hand rule.
- Using the full length of wire when only part is in the field.
- Forgetting sin θ.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 10** (also OCR A PAG11, OCR B 6.1.1(d)(ii), Eduqas specified practical): investigate how the force on a wire varies with B, I and l using a top-pan balance.

1. Place a U-shaped magnet (or two magnets on a steel yoke) on a top-pan balance and zero it.
2. Clamp a stiff horizontal wire (or a rigid metal rod) so it passes between the poles, perpendicular to the field, without touching the magnet. Connect it in series with an ammeter, a variable resistor and a d.c. supply.
3. Switch on. The wire is pushed one way; by Newton's third law the magnet is pushed the other way, changing the balance reading by Δm. F = Δm g.
4. Vary I in steps and record F. Plot F against I: straight line through the origin; gradient = Bl, so B = gradient/l. Measure l (the length of wire in the field, roughly the width of the pole pieces) with a ruler.
5. To vary l, use magnets of different widths or add magnets side by side.

Tips: reverse the current and average to remove any effect from the wire's own heating or the Earth's field. Switch off between readings to stop the wire heating. If the reading goes down, reverse the current or magnet. Safety: the wire can get hot.

### 3.7.5.2 Moving charges in a magnetic field

*Refs: AQA 3.7.5.2 · Edexcel 122 · OCR A 6.3.2(a), (b), (c) · OCR B 6.1.2(a)(v), (c)(iv) · Eduqas C3.9(c), (d), (i), (j). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The force on a charged particle moving in a magnetic field: F = BQv when the field is perpendicular to the velocity (F = BQv sin θ in general).
- The direction of the force on positive and negative particles.
- The circular path of particles; application in the cyclotron.
- (OCR A, Eduqas) Velocity selector: crossed electric and magnetic fields.
- (Eduqas) The Hall effect and the Hall voltage.

#### Explained

**Force on a moving charge.** A current is moving charges, so each charge feels a force: F = BQv (perpendicular), from F = BIl with I = Q/t and l = vt. Use Fleming's left-hand rule with the current direction = direction of a positive charge's motion; for electrons, the current is opposite to their velocity.

**Circular motion.** The force is always perpendicular to velocity, so it does no work: the speed is constant and the particle moves in a circle (if v is perpendicular to B).

BQv = mv²/r → **r = mv/(BQ) = p/(BQ)**

- Faster or heavier particles: bigger circles.
- Stronger field or greater charge: smaller circles.
- Period T = 2πr/v = 2πm/(BQ), independent of speed. Frequency f = BQ/(2πm).

If v has a component along B, that part is unaffected and the path is a helix.

**Cyclotron.** Two hollow D-shaped electrodes ("dees") in a vacuum, with a uniform magnetic field perpendicular to them and an alternating p.d. across the gap between them.

1. Ions from a central source are accelerated across the gap by the electric field.
2. Inside a dee there is no electric field; the magnetic field bends the ion in a semicircle.
3. When it returns to the gap, the p.d. has reversed, so it is accelerated again.
4. Each time, its speed and radius increase, but the time for each semicircle (πm/BQ) stays the same, so a fixed-frequency a.c. supply works.
5. At the edge, the ions are deflected out as a high-energy beam.

Uses: producing radioisotopes for medicine (e.g. for PET), proton therapy for cancer. (At very high speeds relativistic mass increase makes the period change, which limits a simple cyclotron.)

**Velocity selector** (OCR A 6.3.2(c), Eduqas C3.9(j)). An electric field and a magnetic field at right angles to each other and to the beam. Electric force QE one way, magnetic force BQv the other. Particles go straight through only if QE = BQv, so v = E/B. Slower particles are bent by the electric force, faster by the magnetic force. Used before a mass spectrometer so all ions have the same speed.

**Hall effect** (Eduqas C3.9(c), (d)). A current I flows along a thin slice of semiconductor (or metal) of thickness t in a magnetic field B perpendicular to its face. The magnetic force pushes charge carriers sideways, so charge builds up on one side and the opposite charge on the other. This creates an electric field across the slice. Equilibrium is reached when the electric force balances the magnetic force: eV_H/d = Bev, where d is the width and v the drift velocity. Using I = nAev with A = dt:

**V_H = BI/(nte)**

V_H ∝ B, so a Hall probe (calibrated) measures magnetic flux density. Semiconductors have small n, so give a larger Hall voltage than metals. The sign of V_H shows whether carriers are positive or negative.

#### Worked example

1. A proton moving at 2.0 × 10⁶ m s⁻¹ enters a 0.50 T field at right angles. Find the radius of its path and its orbital frequency.

   r = mv/(BQ) = 1.67 × 10⁻²⁷ × 2.0 × 10⁶ / (0.50 × 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹) = 0.042 m. f = BQ/(2πm) = 7.6 × 10⁶ Hz.

2. Find the maximum kinetic energy of protons from a cyclotron of radius 0.50 m in a 1.2 T field.

   v = BQr/m = 1.2 × 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ × 0.50/1.67 × 10⁻²⁷ = 5.75 × 10⁷ m s⁻¹. Ek = ½mv² = 2.76 × 10⁻¹² J = 17 MeV.

3. A velocity selector has E = 3.0 × 10⁴ V m⁻¹ and B = 0.060 T. Which speed passes undeflected?

   v = E/B = 5.0 × 10⁵ m s⁻¹.

4. (Eduqas) A semiconductor slice 0.50 mm thick has n = 1.0 × 10²² m⁻³ and carries 10 mA in a field of 0.20 T. Find V_H.

   V_H = BI/(nte) = 0.20 × 0.010 / (1.0 × 10²² × 5.0 × 10⁻⁴ × 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹) = 2.5 × 10⁻³ V.

#### Common mistakes

- Getting the direction wrong for negative particles.
- Saying the magnetic force increases the speed of a charged particle.
- Saying the time per revolution in a cyclotron increases as the particle speeds up.

### 3.7.5.3 Magnetic flux and flux linkage

*Refs: AQA 3.7.5.3, RP11 · Edexcel 121 · OCR A 6.3.3(a), (b) · OCR B 6.1.1(a)(iv), (b)(i), (b)(iii), (c)(i) · Eduqas C3.10(a). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Magnetic flux Φ = BA, where B is normal to the area A. Unit the weber (Wb = T m²).
- Flux linkage NΦ, where N is the number of turns.
- For a coil at angle θ (between the field and the normal to the coil): NΦ = BAN cos θ.
- AQA required practical 11: flux linkage with angle, using a search coil and oscilloscope.
- (OCR B) Magnetic circuits.

#### Explained

**Flux** Φ = BA cos θ, where θ is the angle between the field and the normal to the area. Think of it as the number of field lines passing through the area. Maximum when the coil faces the field (θ = 0), zero when the coil is edge-on (θ = 90°).

**Flux linkage** NΦ = BAN cos θ, unit Wb (or Wb-turns). Each turn of a coil links the same flux.

**Magnetic circuits** (OCR B 6.1.1(a)(iv)). In a transformer or motor, flux follows a closed path (like current round an electric circuit). The "current-turns" NI drive the flux; the flux is larger when the path has high permeance (iron, short, wide paths) and smaller when it includes air gaps (low permeance). So: Φ = permeance × NI. Iron cores and small air gaps make efficient machines. Lines of flux form continuous loops. Making an electromagnetic machine bigger (scaling up all dimensions) increases the flux produced and the force more than proportionally, which is why large generators and motors are more efficient.

#### Worked example

1. A coil of 200 turns and area 4.0 × 10⁻³ m² is in a field of 0.050 T at 60° to the normal of the coil. Find the flux linkage.

   NΦ = 0.050 × 4.0 × 10⁻³ × 200 × cos 60° = 0.020 Wb-turns.

#### Common mistakes

- Using sin θ when θ is measured from the normal (or cos when θ is from the plane).
- Forgetting N.

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 11:** investigate how flux linkage varies with the angle between a search coil and the field.

1. Produce an alternating magnetic field with a large coil (or a pair of Helmholtz coils, or a solenoid) connected to a signal generator.
2. Place a small search coil (many turns of fine wire on a former, with a protractor) at the centre, connected to an oscilloscope.
3. The alternating field induces an alternating e.m.f. in the search coil; its peak value is proportional to the peak flux linkage.
4. Rotate the search coil in 10° steps from 0° to 90° (and beyond), measuring the peak-to-peak voltage on the oscilloscope each time.
5. Plot peak e.m.f. against cos θ: a straight line through the origin, confirming NΦ ∝ cos θ.

Keep the frequency and the current in the field coil constant throughout.

### 3.7.5.4 Electromagnetic induction

*Refs: AQA 3.7.5.4 · Edexcel 124, 125, 126, 127 · OCR A 6.3.3(c), (d), (e) · OCR B 6.1.1(a)(i), (ii), (b)(ii), (c)(i), (d)(i) · Eduqas C3.10(b) to (e). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Simple induction experiments.
- Faraday's law: induced e.m.f. = rate of change of flux linkage, ε = NΔΦ/Δt.
- Lenz's law: the induced e.m.f. opposes the change that produces it; link to conservation of energy.
- A straight conductor moving across a field: ε = Blv.
- A coil rotating uniformly in a field: ε = BANω sin ωt.
- (Edexcel 124, 125) Factors affecting the e.m.f. induced by a moving magnet or by a changing current in a linked coil.

#### Explained

**Induction.** An e.m.f. is induced in a conductor when the magnetic flux linking it changes. This happens when:

- a magnet moves into or out of a coil, or a coil moves near a magnet;
- a conductor cuts across field lines;
- the current in a nearby coil changes (so its field changes), as in a transformer;
- a coil rotates in a field (a generator).

If the circuit is complete, a current flows.

**Faraday's law.** The magnitude of the induced e.m.f. equals the rate of change of flux linkage: ε = Δ(NΦ)/Δt. A bigger e.m.f. comes from: a stronger magnet, faster movement, more turns, a larger coil area, or an iron core.

**Lenz's law.** The direction of the induced e.m.f. (and current) is such as to oppose the change causing it. Combined: ε = −d(NΦ)/dt (Edexcel 127).

Example: pushing the N pole of a magnet into a coil induces a current that makes the near end of the coil a N pole, repelling the magnet. You must do work to push the magnet in, and this work becomes electrical energy. If the induced current helped the magnet in, energy would be created from nothing, so Lenz's law is a consequence of conservation of energy.

**Moving straight conductor.** A rod of length l moving at speed v perpendicular to a field B sweeps out area lv each second, so the rate of change of flux is Blv: ε = Blv. Direction from Fleming's right-hand rule (generators), or by Lenz's law (the current in the rod produces a force opposing its motion).

**Rotating coil (a.c. generator).** A coil of N turns and area A rotates at angular speed ω in a uniform field B. Flux linkage NΦ = BAN cos ωt. Differentiating: ε = BANω sin ωt. Peak e.m.f. ε₀ = BANω.

- E.m.f. is maximum when the coil is parallel to the field (flux linkage zero but changing fastest).
- E.m.f. is zero when the coil is perpendicular to the field (flux linkage maximum, momentarily not changing).
- Doubling the rotation speed doubles both the peak e.m.f. and the frequency.

**Graphs** (OCR B 6.1.1(b)(ii)). The e.m.f.–time graph is minus the gradient of the flux linkage–time graph. A magnet dropped through a coil gives a positive pulse as it enters and a larger, shorter negative pulse as it leaves (it is moving faster); the areas of the two pulses (= change in flux linkage) are equal.

**Eddy currents.** Changing flux induces currents that swirl inside solid metal. By Lenz's law they oppose the change: they cause magnetic braking (a magnet falling through a copper tube falls slowly; braking in trains) and heating (induction hobs). In transformers they waste energy, so cores are laminated (thin insulated sheets).

#### Worked example

1. A 500-turn coil of area 2.0 × 10⁻³ m² is in a field that falls uniformly from 0.40 T to zero in 0.050 s. Find the induced e.m.f.

   ε = NAΔB/Δt = 500 × 2.0 × 10⁻³ × 0.40/0.050 = 8.0 V.

2. An aircraft with a 40 m wingspan flies horizontally at 250 m s⁻¹ where the vertical component of the Earth's field is 4.0 × 10⁻⁵ T. Find the e.m.f. between its wing tips.

   ε = Blv = 4.0 × 10⁻⁵ × 40 × 250 = 0.40 V.

3. A generator coil has 120 turns of area 0.010 m² in 0.15 T and spins at 50 revolutions per second. Find the peak e.m.f.

   ω = 2π × 50 = 314 rad s⁻¹. ε₀ = 0.15 × 0.010 × 120 × 314 = 57 V.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying an e.m.f. is induced when flux is large (it needs flux to be changing).
- Saying the generator e.m.f. is maximum when the coil faces the field.
- Getting Lenz's law direction wrong; ask "what change is happening, and how would the induced current oppose it?"

#### Practical

**Induced e.m.f. with a falling magnet** (OCR B 6.1.1(d)(i), OCR A 6.3.3(d)(ii)). Drop a bar magnet through a coil connected to a data logger or oscilloscope; observe the two opposite pulses. Drop from a greater height: larger, narrower peaks. Use more turns: larger peaks.

### 3.7.5.5 Alternating currents

*Refs: AQA 3.7.5.5 · Edexcel 128, 129. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Sinusoidal voltages and currents only: r.m.s., peak and peak-to-peak values.
- I_rms = I₀/√2, V_rms = V₀/√2.
- Calculating peak and peak-to-peak values of the mains.
- Using an oscilloscope as a d.c. and a.c. voltmeter, to measure time intervals and frequencies and to display waveforms (the controls, not the structure).

#### Explained

An alternating current or p.d. reverses direction regularly. For a sinusoidal supply: V = V₀ sin ωt.

- Peak value V₀: maximum from zero.
- Peak-to-peak: 2V₀.
- Period T and frequency f = 1/T (UK mains: 50 Hz).

**Root mean square.** The r.m.s. value of an alternating p.d. (or current) is the value of the steady direct p.d. (or current) that would dissipate energy at the same rate in a resistor. Square the values, take the mean over a cycle, then square root. For a sine wave the mean of sin² is ½, so V_rms = V₀/√2 = 0.707V₀.

Mean power in a resistor: P = I_rms V_rms = I_rms²R = V_rms²/R = ½I₀V₀.

UK mains is 230 V r.m.s.: peak 230√2 = 325 V; peak-to-peak 650 V.

**Oscilloscope.** The trace shows p.d. (vertical) against time (horizontal).

- Y-gain (volts per division): p.d. = number of vertical divisions × Y-gain. For d.c., the trace jumps up or down by V/Y-gain divisions.
- Time-base (seconds or ms per division): time = horizontal divisions × time-base. Find T across several cycles, then f = 1/T.
- Turning the time-base off shows a vertical line whose length gives the peak-to-peak value.

#### Worked example

1. An oscilloscope trace has a peak-to-peak height of 6.4 divisions at 2.0 V div⁻¹; one cycle spans 4.0 divisions at 5.0 ms div⁻¹. Find V₀, V_rms and f.

   V_pp = 12.8 V, V₀ = 6.4 V, V_rms = 4.5 V. T = 20 ms, f = 50 Hz.

2. A 2.3 kW heater runs on 230 V r.m.s. mains. Find the r.m.s. and peak currents.

   I_rms = 10 A. I₀ = 14 A.

#### Common mistakes

- Using peak values in P = IV for mean power.
- Measuring peak-to-peak and calling it the peak.

### 3.7.5.6 The operation of a transformer

*Refs: AQA 3.7.5.6 · Edexcel 125 · OCR A 6.3.3(f) · OCR B 6.1.1(a)(i), (c)(iii), (iv), (d)(iii). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The transformer equation Ns/Np = Vs/Vp.
- Transformer efficiency = IsVs/(IpVp).
- Eddy currents and other causes of inefficiency.
- Transmission of electrical power at high voltage, including calculating power losses in transmission lines.

#### Explained

**How it works.** An alternating current in the primary coil produces an alternating magnetic flux in the iron core. The core carries this changing flux through the secondary coil, inducing an alternating e.m.f. there (Faraday's law). It only works with a.c. (d.c. gives no changing flux, except at switch-on).

**Equations.** For an ideal transformer (100% efficient, all flux linked):

- Vs/Vp = Ns/Np. Step-up: Ns > Np. Step-down: Ns < Np.
- Power in = power out: VpIp = VsIs, so Is/Ip = Np/Ns. A step-up transformer steps current down.
- Efficiency = IsVs/(IpVp).

**Losses and how to reduce them.**

| Loss | Cause | Reduce by |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Eddy currents | The changing flux induces currents in the core, which heat it | Laminated core: thin insulated sheets |
| Resistance of coils | I²R heating in the copper | Thick, low-resistance copper wire (especially for the high-current coil) |
| Hysteresis | Energy needed to magnetise and demagnetise the core each cycle | Soft iron core, which is easily magnetised and demagnetised |
| Flux leakage | Not all flux from the primary passes through the secondary | Wind coils on top of each other; well-designed core |

**Power transmission.** Power P = VI is sent along cables of resistance R. The power wasted as heat is P_loss = I²R. For a given power, increasing V by a factor of 10 reduces I by 10 and the loss by 100. So power stations step up to hundreds of kV (the National Grid uses 275 kV and 400 kV) and step down near consumers. Thinner cables can be used, saving cost. A.c. is used because transformers make changing voltage easy.

#### Worked example

1. A transformer steps 230 V down to 12 V. The primary has 1150 turns. How many turns on the secondary? If the secondary delivers 3.0 A and the primary current is 0.18 A, find the efficiency.

   Ns = 1150 × 12/230 = 60 turns. Efficiency = (12 × 3.0)/(230 × 0.18) = 36/41.4 = 0.87.

2. A power station sends 50 MW through cables of total resistance 4.0 Ω. Compare the loss at 25 kV and 400 kV.

   At 25 kV: I = 2000 A, loss = 2000² × 4.0 = 1.6 × 10⁷ W (32%). At 400 kV: I = 125 A, loss = 6.25 × 10⁴ W (0.13%).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying transformers work with d.c.
- Saying a step-up transformer increases power.
- Using V²/R with the transmission voltage for the cable loss (the full voltage is not across the cable).

#### Practical

**Investigating transformers** (OCR B 6.1.1(d)(iii), suggested by AQA). Wind primary and secondary coils with different numbers of turns on a C-core. Connect the primary to a low-voltage a.c. supply. Measure Vp and Vs with a.c. voltmeters (or an oscilloscope) for different turns ratios; plot Vs against Ns/Np. Add a load resistor and measure currents to find efficiency. Compare with and without a laminated core, or with a gap in the core.

### Check questions

1. Define the tesla. (2 marks)
2. Describe how to measure the magnetic flux density between the poles of a magnet using a top-pan balance. (5 marks)
3. An electron moves at 4.0 × 10⁷ m s⁻¹ perpendicular to a field of 2.5 mT. Calculate the radius of its path. (2 marks)
4. Explain why the frequency of the a.c. supply in a cyclotron can be constant. (3 marks)
5. State Faraday's and Lenz's laws. (2 marks)
6. Explain, using Lenz's law, why a magnet dropped down a vertical copper pipe falls slowly. (3 marks)
7. A coil of 80 turns and area 25 cm² rotates at 3000 rpm in a 0.20 T field. Calculate the peak e.m.f. and sketch its variation with time. (4 marks)
8. Explain why electricity is transmitted at high voltage. (3 marks)
9. Give two causes of energy loss in a transformer and how each is reduced. (4 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Flux density that produces a force of 1 N (1); on 1 m of wire carrying 1 A perpendicular to the field (1).
2. Magnet on balance, zeroed; wire clamped between poles perpendicular to field (1); measure current with ammeter, vary with rheostat (1); force = change in reading × g (1); plot F against I, gradient = Bl (1); measure length of wire in field; B = gradient/l (1).
3. r = mv/(Be) (1) = 9.11 × 10⁻³¹ × 4.0 × 10⁷/(2.5 × 10⁻³ × 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹) = 0.091 m (1).
4. r = mv/BQ, so radius increases in proportion to speed (1); time for a semicircle = πr/v = πm/BQ (1); independent of speed, so particles always reach the gap after the same time (1).
5. Induced e.m.f. equals rate of change of flux linkage (1); direction opposes the change that causes it (1).
6. Falling magnet changes flux through the pipe, inducing eddy currents (1); by Lenz's law these produce a field opposing the motion of the magnet (1); so an upward force slows it; kinetic energy becomes heat in the pipe (1).
7. ω = 2π × 50 = 314 rad s⁻¹ (1); ε₀ = BANω = 0.20 × 2.5 × 10⁻³ × 80 × 314 = 12.6 V (2); sine curve, period 0.020 s (1).
8. For a given power, higher voltage means lower current (1); power loss = I²R (1); so much smaller losses / thinner cables (1).
9. Any two pairs: eddy currents, laminated core; resistance of coils, thick copper wire; hysteresis, soft iron core; flux leakage, coils wound together / good core design (2 + 2).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/31-particle-accelerators-and-detectors.md -->

## Particle accelerators and detectors: linacs, cyclotrons, particle tracks and probing the nucleon

To study the smallest structures in matter, physicists accelerate particles to very high energies and smash them together, then track what comes out. This file covers producing charged particles by thermionic emission, the linear accelerator and the cyclotron, the radius of a charged particle's path in a magnetic field (r = p/BQ), how detectors work (ionisation and deflection), interpreting particle tracks with conservation laws, and why high energies are needed to probe nucleons.

### Overview

A-level only. Edexcel 132 to 136; OCR B 6.2.1(a)(i), (c)(i); Eduqas C3.9(i), (j) (ion beams in fields). The force on a moving charge and the cyclotron (AQA) are in file 17; electric fields in file 15; quarks, conservation laws and creation of particle–antiparticle pairs in file 02; relativistic effects in file 27; thermionic emission in file 26; electron diffraction from nuclei in file 18.

#### Board differences

- Edexcel 132 to 136 · OCR B 6.2.1(a)(i), (c)(i) · Eduqas C3.9(i), (j) · AQA 3.7.5.2.
- Edexcel 133 names the linac and cyclotron, and detectors in terms of ionisation and deflection only. Edexcel 134: derive r = p/(BQ). Edexcel 136: why high energies are needed to investigate the structure of nucleons.
- OCR B 6.2.1(a)(i): accelerators generate high-energy beams for scattering experiments.
- Eduqas C3.9(i), (j): how ion beams are deflected in electric and magnetic fields (including mass spectrometry and velocity selection, file 17).

### Accelerating charged particles

*Refs: Edexcel 132, 133 · OCR B 6.2.1(a)(i) · AQA 3.7.5.2. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Electrons are released by thermionic emission and accelerated by electric fields; magnetic fields bend them.
- The role of electric and magnetic fields in the linear accelerator (linac) and cyclotron.

#### Explained

**Sources.** Electrons come from a heated filament (thermionic emission). Protons and other ions come from ionising a gas.

**Electric fields accelerate.** A particle of charge Q crossing a p.d. V gains kinetic energy QV. A magnetic force is always perpendicular to the velocity, so magnetic fields change direction but never speed: they steer and focus beams.

**Linear accelerator (linac).** A long evacuated tube containing a line of cylindrical electrodes ("drift tubes"), connected alternately to the two terminals of a high-frequency alternating supply.

1. A particle is accelerated across the gap between two tubes, because the next tube has the opposite charge at that moment.
2. Inside a tube there is no electric field (it is a hollow conductor), so the particle drifts at constant speed.
3. While it is inside, the supply reverses polarity, so when it reaches the next gap it is accelerated again.
4. Because the particle speeds up, each tube must be longer than the one before, so that the time spent in each is the same (half a period of the a.c.).

At relativistic speeds (close to c) the speed hardly changes, so tubes become nearly equal in length. Linacs produce beams for X-ray therapy and inject particles into larger accelerators. Advantages: no energy loss from bending the beam (no synchrotron radiation). Disadvantages: very long for high energies (the Stanford linac is 3 km).

**Cyclotron.** See file 17 for the full description: two dees with an alternating p.d. across the gap and a uniform magnetic field perpendicular to them. The particle spirals outwards, accelerated at each gap crossing; the time for each semicircle (πm/BQ) is independent of speed, so a constant-frequency supply works. At relativistic speeds, the mass increase makes the period grow, so simple cyclotrons are limited to protons of tens of MeV. (A synchrotron varies the magnetic field and the frequency to keep particles in a fixed circular ring, reaching TeV energies, as at the LHC.)

#### Worked example

1. A proton passes through 20 gaps of a linac, each with a p.d. of 200 kV. Find its final kinetic energy.

   20 × 200 keV = 4.0 MeV (6.4 × 10⁻¹³ J).

2. A linac uses a 10 MHz supply. A proton has speed 3.0 × 10⁶ m s⁻¹ inside one drift tube. How long must the tube be?

   Time in tube = half period = 5.0 × 10⁻⁸ s. Length = 3.0 × 10⁶ × 5.0 × 10⁻⁸ = 0.15 m.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the particle is accelerated inside the drift tubes.
- Saying magnetic fields increase particle speed in a cyclotron.

### Charged particles in magnetic fields: r = p/BQ

*Refs: Edexcel 134 · OCR B 6.2.1(c)(i) · Eduqas C3.9(i), (j). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Derive and use r = p/(BQ) for a charged particle in a magnetic field.

#### Explained

A particle of charge Q and momentum p = mv moving perpendicular to a uniform field B feels a force BQv towards the centre of a circle:

BQv = mv²/r → **r = mv/(BQ) = p/(BQ)**

So the radius of a track measures momentum. In a detector:

- Opposite charges curve opposite ways (Fleming's left-hand rule).
- Higher-momentum particles leave straighter tracks.
- A particle losing energy as it ionises the material slows down, so its track spirals inwards.

This relation also holds relativistically (with p the relativistic momentum), which is why it is used in high-energy physics.

**Mass spectrometer** (Eduqas C3.9(i), (j)). Ions are accelerated, pass through a velocity selector (v = E/B), then enter a region of field B₂ where r = mv/(QB₂). Ions of different mass (for the same charge and speed) land at different places, separating isotopes.

#### Worked example

1. A track of a particle of charge +e in a 1.5 T field has radius 0.40 m. Find its momentum.

   p = BQr = 1.5 × 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ × 0.40 = 9.6 × 10⁻²⁰ kg m s⁻¹ (≈ 180 MeV/c).

#### Common mistakes

- Using the mass in place of the momentum when comparing track radii.
- Getting the curvature direction wrong for negative particles.

### Detectors and particle tracks

*Refs: Edexcel 133, 135. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- General principles of detectors: ionisation and deflection.
- Apply conservation of charge, energy and momentum to interactions; interpret particle tracks.

#### Explained

**Ionisation.** Charged particles passing through matter ionise atoms along their path. Detectors make this ionisation visible or measurable:

- Cloud chamber: ions in a supersaturated vapour act as centres where droplets condense, leaving a visible trail.
- Bubble chamber: ions in a superheated liquid start bubbles.
- Wire chambers and silicon trackers: the freed electrons are collected on wires or electrodes, giving electronic signals that a computer uses to reconstruct the path.
- Neutral particles (neutrons, photons, neutrinos) leave no track; their presence is inferred from conservation laws or from the charged particles they produce.

**Deflection.** A magnetic field across the detector curves the tracks, so charge sign and momentum can be measured (r = p/BQ).

**Reading tracks.**

- A track that begins from nowhere, or a "V" of two oppositely curving tracks appearing from a point, shows a neutral particle decaying or a photon making a pair.
- Track thickness: more heavily ionising (slower or more highly charged) particles leave thicker tracks.
- At a vertex, apply conservation of charge, momentum (vector sum of momenta before and after), energy, baryon number and lepton number to identify unseen particles.
- Spiral tracks: particles losing energy; electrons spiral much more tightly than protons of similar energy (smaller momentum).

**Calorimeters** measure energy by absorbing the particles completely; muon detectors (on the outside) catch muons, which pass through everything else.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying neutral particles leave tracks.
- Forgetting that momentum is a vector when balancing at a vertex.

### Why high energies are needed

*Refs: Edexcel 136. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Why high energies are needed to investigate the structure of nucleons.

#### Explained

1. **Resolution.** To probe a structure, the probing particle's de Broglie wavelength λ = h/p must be about the size of the structure or smaller. A nucleon is about 10⁻¹⁵ m across, so particles need momenta of about h/10⁻¹⁶ m, i.e. energies of several GeV (deep inelastic scattering of electrons from protons at about 20 GeV revealed quarks).
2. **Creating new particles.** To make new, heavy particles, enough energy must be available to supply their rest energy (ΔE = c²Δm): e.g. at least 2 × 938 MeV to make a proton–antiproton pair; the W and Z bosons (80 to 91 GeV) and the Higgs boson (125 GeV) needed collider energies of hundreds of GeV to TeV.
3. **Overcoming forces.** Particles must get close enough to overcome strong electrostatic repulsion and interact via the strong force.

Colliding beams (two beams in opposite directions) make all the energy available for creating particles, since the total momentum is zero; firing at a fixed target wastes much of the energy as kinetic energy of the products.

#### Worked example

1. Estimate the energy of electrons needed to have a de Broglie wavelength of 1.0 × 10⁻¹⁶ m.

   p = h/λ = 6.6 × 10⁻¹⁸ kg m s⁻¹. At these energies E ≈ pc = 2.0 × 10⁻⁹ J ≈ 12 GeV.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying higher energy gives a longer de Broglie wavelength.

### Check questions

1. Explain why the drift tubes in a linear accelerator get longer along the accelerator. (3 marks)
2. Derive r = p/(BQ) for a charged particle in a uniform magnetic field. (2 marks)
3. An electron and a proton have the same momentum and enter the same magnetic field. Compare their tracks. (2 marks)
4. Explain how a particle detector shows the paths of charged particles and how their charge and momentum can be found. (4 marks)
5. A neutral particle decays into a π⁺ and a π⁻, seen as a "V" in a bubble chamber. Explain how conservation laws apply. (3 marks)
6. Explain why very high energies are needed to investigate the internal structure of protons. (3 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Particles accelerate at each gap, so speed increases (1); time in each tube must be the same (half the a.c. period) so they reach the next gap when polarity is right (1); so faster particles need longer tubes (1).
2. Magnetic force provides centripetal force: BQv = mv²/r (1); r = mv/(BQ) = p/(BQ) (1).
3. Same radius of curvature (same p, B, magnitude of Q) (1); curve in opposite directions (opposite charges) (1).
4. Charged particles ionise the medium along their path (1); ions trigger droplets / bubbles / electronic signals, showing the track (1); magnetic field across the detector curves the tracks (1); direction of curvature gives sign of charge; radius gives momentum (r = p/BQ) (1).
5. Charge: 0 = +1 + (−1) (1); momentum: the two tracks' momenta add to the momentum of the unseen neutral particle, along the line where it travelled (1); energy: rest energy + kinetic energy of the parent = total energy of the pions (1).
6. De Broglie wavelength must be comparable to or smaller than the size of the nucleon (about 10⁻¹⁵ m) to resolve structure (1); λ = h/p, so a large momentum is needed (1); also high energy to create new particles (ΔE = c²Δm) / overcome repulsion (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/34-alternating-current-circuits.md -->

## Alternating currents: generators, r.m.s. values, reactance, phasors, impedance and LCR resonance

This file covers the Eduqas option on alternating currents: the a.c. generator and its e.m.f., peak and r.m.s. values, mean power, using an oscilloscope, the phase relationships and reactance of inductors and capacitors, adding p.d.s with phasors in RC, RL and RCL circuits, impedance and phase angle, series resonance and the Q factor.

### Overview

Eduqas option A (A-level only). Faraday's law, the rotating coil and r.m.s. values for AQA and Edexcel are in file 17. Capacitors are in file 16. Parallel LC filters and Q in the AQA electronics option are in file 28.

#### Board differences

- Eduqas OA(a) to (p) only. AQA 3.7.5.4, 3.7.5.5 and Edexcel 128, 129 cover the generator and r.m.s. values (file 17), but not reactance, phasors or LCR circuits.

### OA(a) to (g) Generating a.c., r.m.s. values and the oscilloscope

*Refs: Eduqas OA(a) to (g). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Faraday's law applied to a coil rotating in a magnetic field; flux linkage BAN cos ωt; e.m.f. V = ωBAN sin ωt.
- Frequency, period, peak and r.m.s. values; I_rms = I₀/√2, V_rms = V₀/√2 (including V_rms = ωBAN/√2).
- The r.m.s. value relates to the energy dissipated per cycle; mean power in a resistor P = I_rmsV_rms = I_rms²R = V_rms²/R.
- Using an oscilloscope (CRO, or PC-based) to measure a.c. and d.c. voltages and currents and frequencies.

#### Explained

A flat coil of N turns and area A rotates at angular speed ω in a uniform field B. The angle between the field and the normal to the coil is θ = ωt, so the flux linkage is NΦ = BAN cos ωt. By Faraday's law, e.m.f. = −d(NΦ)/dt = BANω sin ωt. Peak e.m.f. V₀ = ωBAN, so V_rms = ωBAN/√2.

The r.m.s. value is the d.c. value that would dissipate the same mean power in a resistor. For a sinusoid, the mean of sin² over a cycle is ½, so mean power = ½I₀²R = (I₀/√2)²R. Hence I_rms = I₀/√2 and P = I_rmsV_rms.

**Oscilloscope.** The Y-gain (V per division) gives voltage; the time-base (s per division) gives time. To measure a current, measure the p.d. across a known resistor and use I = V/R. A d.c. voltage shifts the trace up or down; an a.c. voltage gives a waveform whose peak-to-peak height gives 2V₀. (Full details in file 17.)

#### Worked example

1. A 200-turn coil of area 0.012 m² rotates at 50 Hz in a 0.080 T field. Find V₀ and V_rms.

   ω = 314 rad s⁻¹. V₀ = 314 × 0.080 × 0.012 × 200 = 60 V. V_rms = 43 V.

#### Common mistakes

- Using peak values in P = IV for mean power.

### OA(h) to (k) Inductors and capacitors in a.c. circuits

*Refs: Eduqas OA(h), (i), (j), (k). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- In an inductor, current lags the p.d. by 90°; reactance X_L = V_rms/I_rms = ωL.
- In a capacitor, current leads the p.d. by 90°; X_C = V_rms/I_rms = 1/(ωC).
- The mean power dissipated in a pure inductor or capacitor is zero.

#### Explained

**Inductor.** An inductor (a coil, inductance L in henry) produces a back e.m.f. L dI/dt opposing changes in current. For an a.c. current I = I₀ sin ωt, the p.d. across the inductor is V = L dI/dt = ωLI₀ cos ωt. The p.d. is a quarter cycle ahead of the current: **current lags p.d. by 90°** (π/2). The ratio V₀/I₀ = ωL is the inductive reactance X_L (ohms). X_L increases with frequency: an inductor blocks high frequencies and passes low frequencies (and d.c.).

**Capacitor.** The charge on a capacitor Q = CV. Current I = dQ/dt = C dV/dt. For V = V₀ sin ωt, I = ωCV₀ cos ωt: **current leads p.d. by 90°**. The capacitive reactance X_C = V₀/I₀ = 1/(ωC). X_C decreases with frequency: a capacitor blocks d.c. and low frequencies and passes high frequencies.

**Zero mean power.** In a pure inductor or capacitor, current and p.d. are 90° out of phase. For a quarter of each cycle energy flows in (stored in the magnetic or electric field), and for the next quarter it flows back out. The mean power over a cycle is zero. Only resistance dissipates energy.

#### Worked example

1. Find the reactance of a 0.20 H inductor and of a 10 μF capacitor at 50 Hz.

   X_L = 2π × 50 × 0.20 = 63 Ω. X_C = 1/(2π × 50 × 10 × 10⁻⁶) = 318 Ω.

2. A 230 V r.m.s., 50 Hz supply is connected across the capacitor alone. Find the r.m.s. current.

   I = 230/318 = 0.72 A.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the current lags the p.d. in a capacitor (it leads).
- Saying reactance is the same at all frequencies.

### OA(l), (m) Phasors and impedance in RC, RL and RCL circuits

*Refs: Eduqas OA(l), (m). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Adding p.d.s across series RC, RL and RCL combinations using phasors.
- Calculating the phase angle and the impedance Z = V_rms/I_rms.

#### Explained

In a series circuit, the same current flows through every component. The p.d.s across them are not in phase, so they cannot simply be added; they are added as **phasors** (rotating vectors whose length is the r.m.s. or peak value and whose angle shows the phase).

Draw the current phasor horizontally. Then:

- V_R = IR is along the current (in phase).
- V_L = IX_L points up (leads the current by 90°).
- V_C = IX_C points down (lags the current by 90°).

**RCL series.** Supply p.d. V = √(V_R² + (V_L − V_C)²). Dividing by I:

**Z = √(R² + (X_L − X_C)²)**

Phase angle between supply p.d. and current: tan φ = (X_L − X_C)/R. If X_L > X_C, the p.d. leads the current (inductive); if X_C > X_L, the current leads (capacitive).

- RC only: Z = √(R² + X_C²), tan φ = X_C/R (current leads).
- RL only: Z = √(R² + X_L²), tan φ = X_L/R (current lags).

#### Worked example

1. A 100 Ω resistor and a 10 μF capacitor are in series across 12 V r.m.s. at 200 Hz. Find Z, the current and the phase angle.

   X_C = 1/(2π × 200 × 10⁻⁵) = 79.6 Ω. Z = √(100² + 79.6²) = 128 Ω. I = 12/128 = 0.094 A. tan φ = 79.6/100, φ = 38.5° (current leads the supply p.d.).

#### Common mistakes

- Adding V_R, V_L and V_C arithmetically.
- Taking the phase angle from Z instead of from tan φ = (X_L − X_C)/R.

### OA(n) to (p) Series resonance and Q factor

*Refs: Eduqas OA(n), (o), (p). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Derive the resonance frequency of a series RCL circuit.
- Q factor = V_L/V_R = V_C/V_R at resonance.
- The sharpness of the resonance curve depends on Q.

#### Explained

**Resonance.** As frequency rises, X_L rises and X_C falls. At one frequency they are equal: X_L = X_C, so ωL = 1/(ωC):

**ω₀ = 1/√(LC), f₀ = 1/(2π√(LC))**

At resonance:

- Z = R (minimum impedance), so the current is a maximum: I = V/R.
- The p.d. across L and the p.d. across C are equal and opposite (they cancel), so the supply p.d. equals V_R and the current is in phase with the supply.
- V_L and V_C can each be much larger than the supply p.d.

**Q factor.** Q = V_L/V_R = V_C/V_R at resonance = ω₀L/R. A small resistance gives a large Q: a tall, narrow peak of current against frequency (very selective, as in tuning a radio). A large R gives a low Q and a broad, flat peak.

#### Worked example

1. A series circuit has L = 50 mH, C = 2.0 μF and R = 20 Ω, with a 5.0 V r.m.s. supply. Find f₀, the current at resonance, Q and V_L at resonance.

   f₀ = 1/(2π√(1.0 × 10⁻⁷)) = 503 Hz. I = 5.0/20 = 0.25 A. X_L = 2π × 503 × 0.050 = 158 Ω. Q = 158/20 = 7.9. V_L = 0.25 × 158 = 40 V (eight times the supply p.d.).

#### Common mistakes

- Adding V_R, V_L and V_C as numbers instead of as phasors.
- Saying the current lags the p.d. in a capacitor.
- Saying a capacitor or inductor dissipates power.
- Saying the impedance is zero at resonance (it equals R).

### Check questions

1. Show that the r.m.s. value of a sinusoidal current is I₀/√2. (3 marks)
2. Explain why the mean power in a pure capacitor is zero. (2 marks)
3. Calculate the frequency at which a 0.10 H inductor has a reactance of 500 Ω. (2 marks)
4. A 60 Ω resistor and a 0.15 H inductor are in series across a 24 V r.m.s., 50 Hz supply. Find the impedance, the current and the phase angle. (4 marks)
5. Derive the resonant frequency of a series RCL circuit. (2 marks)
6. Sketch the current against frequency for two series RCL circuits with the same L and C but different R. Explain the difference. (3 marks)
7. A series RCL circuit at resonance has V_R = 2.0 V and V_C = 30 V. Find Q and the supply p.d. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Power at any instant = I₀²R sin²ωt (1); mean of sin² over a cycle = ½ (1); mean power = ½I₀²R = (I₀/√2)²R, so I_rms = I₀/√2 (1).
2. Current and p.d. are 90° out of phase (1); energy stored in the field for a quarter cycle is returned in the next, so net energy transfer per cycle is zero (1).
3. f = X_L/(2πL) (1) = 500/(2π × 0.10) = 800 Hz (1).
4. X_L = 47.1 Ω (1); Z = √(60² + 47.1²) = 76.3 Ω (1); I = 0.31 A (1); tan φ = 47.1/60, φ = 38° (current lags) (1).
5. At resonance X_L = X_C so ωL = 1/(ωC) (1); ω₀ = 1/√(LC), f₀ = 1/(2π√(LC)) (1).
6. Both peak at the same f₀ (1); smaller R gives a higher, sharper peak (1); larger R gives a lower, broader peak, since Q = ω₀L/R (1).
7. Q = 30/2.0 = 15 (1); supply p.d. = V_R = 2.0 V (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/23-medical-imaging-and-radionuclides.md -->

## Medical imaging and therapy: ultrasound, endoscopy, MRI, X-rays, CT, radionuclide imaging, PET and radiotherapy

Doctors use physics to see inside the body and to treat disease. This file covers ultrasound (acoustic impedance, A- and B-scans, Doppler), fibre-optic endoscopes, magnetic resonance imaging, the production, absorption and detection of X-rays, CT scanning, radioactive tracers, the gamma camera, PET, effective half-life, radiation dose, and radiotherapy with X-rays and implants, with comparisons between techniques.

### Overview

AQA option 3.10.4 to 3.10.6 (A-level only); OCR A 6.5 (A-level only); Eduqas option B (A-level only); Edexcel 88, 89 (pulse-echo, AS). Total internal reflection and fibres are in file 05; the photoelectric effect in file 03; exponential decay, technetium-99m and dose units in file 18; annihilation in file 02; magnetic fields and superconductors in files 10 and 17.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.10.4 to 3.10.6 · OCR A 6.5 · Eduqas OB(a) to (t) · Edexcel 88, 89.
- OCR A 6.5.1(c) asks for the four X-ray attenuation mechanisms (simple scatter, photoelectric effect, Compton scattering, pair production). AQA excludes the details of absorption processes.
- Doppler ultrasound for blood flow: OCR A 6.5.3(g) and Eduqas OB(k) (Δf/f = 2v cos θ/c). Not AQA.
- MRI: AQA 3.10.4.3 (no relaxation times, no magnet production), Eduqas OB(l), (m) (with relaxation time and Larmor frequency f = 42.6 × 10⁶ B for hydrogen). Not OCR A.
- Endoscopes: AQA only. Flat-panel detectors, intensifying screens and image intensifiers: AQA 3.10.5.2. Digital image receptors: Eduqas OB(f).
- Physical, biological and effective half-life: AQA 3.10.6.2. Dose (Gy, Sv), radiation weighting and tissue weighting factors: Eduqas OB(o) to (q) (also OCR B in file 18).
- Tracers: AQA names Tc-99m, I-131, In-111 and the Mo–Tc generator; OCR A names Tc-99m and fluorine-18; Eduqas names Tc-99m.
- Edexcel 89: pulse-echo and how wavelength and pulse duration limit the information.

### 3.10.4.1 Ultrasound imaging

*Refs: AQA 3.10.4.1 · Edexcel 88, 89 · OCR A 6.5.3(a) to (g) · Eduqas OB(h), (i), (j), (k), (n). Level: A-level only (Edexcel AS & A).*

#### What you need to know

- Reflection and transmission of sound at tissue boundaries; acoustic impedance Z = ρc; attenuation.
- Fraction reflected: I_r/I_i = ((Z₂ − Z₁)/(Z₂ + Z₁))².
- Piezoelectric transducers: generating and detecting ultrasound pulses.
- A-scans and B-scans; examples of applications.
- Advantages and disadvantages of ultrasound compared with other methods, including safety and resolution.
- (Edexcel 89) Pulse-echo: how wavelength and pulse duration limit the detail.
- (OCR A, Eduqas) Coupling gel (impedance matching); Doppler measurement of blood flow.

#### Explained

**Ultrasound** is sound (a longitudinal wave) with frequency above 20 kHz; medical scanners use 1 to 15 MHz. In soft tissue c ≈ 1500 m s⁻¹, so λ ≈ 0.1 to 1.5 mm.

**Piezoelectric transducer.** Certain crystals (e.g. quartz, lead zirconate titanate) change shape when a p.d. is applied, and produce a p.d. when squeezed. An alternating p.d. at the crystal's resonant frequency makes it vibrate strongly, producing ultrasound. The same crystal detects the returning echoes: the pressure wave makes it vibrate and generate a small alternating p.d. A backing material damps the crystal so it produces short pulses (a few cycles), which give better depth resolution, and so that it is ready to receive echoes.

**Acoustic impedance** Z = ρc (kg m⁻² s⁻¹), where ρ is density and c the speed of sound. At a boundary between materials with impedances Z₁ and Z₂, the fraction of intensity reflected is:

I_r/I_i = ((Z₂ − Z₁)/(Z₂ + Z₁))²

A big difference in Z gives strong reflection. Air (Z ≈ 430) and skin (Z ≈ 1.7 × 10⁶): nearly 100% is reflected, so without help no ultrasound would enter. A **coupling gel** with impedance close to skin's is spread between transducer and skin to remove the air and let most ultrasound enter (impedance matching). Bone–soft tissue boundaries reflect about 40%, so it is hard to image behind bone; gas in the gut also blocks imaging.

**Attenuation.** Intensity falls exponentially with depth as energy is absorbed and scattered. Higher frequencies are attenuated more, so lower frequencies are used for deep organs (at the cost of poorer resolution).

**Pulse-echo** (Edexcel 89). A short pulse is sent; echoes return from each boundary. Depth d = ct/2 (t = time to the echo). Limits:

- Two boundaries closer than about half the pulse length cannot be distinguished (echoes overlap). Shorter pulses give better depth resolution.
- Details smaller than about the wavelength cannot be seen (they diffract the wave rather than reflect it). Higher frequency (shorter λ) gives finer detail.
- The next pulse cannot be sent until echoes from the deepest structure return, limiting the pulse rate.

**A-scan** (amplitude scan). One transducer, fixed direction. The display shows echo amplitude (vertical) against time (horizontal). Used to measure distances, e.g. the depth of the eye (to fit lens implants) or the midline of the brain.

**B-scan** (brightness scan). The transducer (or an array of them) sweeps across the body. Each echo appears as a dot whose brightness depends on its amplitude, positioned by its depth and direction. A computer builds a 2D image. Used for fetal scans, organs, heart (moving images).

**Doppler ultrasound** (OCR A 6.5.3(g), Eduqas OB(k)). Ultrasound reflected from moving red blood cells changes frequency. If the beam makes angle θ with the blood flow: Δf = 2fv cos θ/c (the factor 2 because the cells both receive and re-emit a shifted wave). Measuring Δf gives the blood speed, to detect blockages or check the fetal heart.

**Advantages and disadvantages.**

| Advantages | Disadvantages |
| --- | --- |
| Non-ionising, so no known risk; safe for fetuses | Cannot see through bone or air (lungs, gut) |
| Cheap, portable, quick | Poorer resolution than CT or MRI (about 1 mm) |
| Real-time moving images (heart, blood flow) | Image quality depends on the operator |
| No side effects | Limited depth for high-resolution scans |

#### Worked example

1. Find the fraction reflected at a fat–muscle boundary. Z_fat = 1.38 × 10⁶, Z_muscle = 1.70 × 10⁶ kg m⁻² s⁻¹.

   ((1.70 − 1.38)/(1.70 + 1.38))² = (0.32/3.08)² = 0.011 (1.1%).

2. An echo returns 65 μs after the pulse. How deep is the boundary? (c = 1540 m s⁻¹)

   d = ct/2 = 1540 × 65 × 10⁻⁶/2 = 0.050 m.

3. (OCR A, Eduqas) 4.0 MHz ultrasound at 30° to blood flow gives a shift of 2.5 kHz. Find the blood speed.

   v = Δf c/(2f cos θ) = 2500 × 1540/(2 × 4.0 × 10⁶ × 0.866) = 0.56 m s⁻¹.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting the factor 2 in d = ct/2 or in the Doppler shift.
- Saying gel improves resolution (it reduces reflection at the skin).
- Forgetting to square the impedance fraction.

### 3.10.4.2 Fibre optics and endoscopy

*Refs: AQA 3.10.4.2. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Properties of fibre optics, total internal reflection at the core–cladding boundary, and uses in medicine.
- The optical system of a flexible endoscope; coherent and non-coherent fibre bundles; uses for internal imaging, and advantages.

#### Explained

A flexible endoscope is a tube that a doctor passes into the body (through the mouth or a small cut) to look inside.

- **Non-coherent bundle**: fibres arranged randomly. It carries light from an external source into the body to illuminate the area. The arrangement does not matter, and it is cheaper.
- **Coherent bundle**: fibres in exactly the same arrangement at both ends. Each fibre carries light from one tiny part of the image, so the image is reproduced point by point. An objective lens at the far end forms an image on the end of the bundle; an eyepiece (or camera) at the near end views it. Thinner fibres give better resolution.
- Each fibre has a core and cladding of lower refractive index, so light is guided by total internal reflection; cladding stops light leaking between touching fibres.
- Extra channels let the surgeon pump in water or air, take tissue samples (biopsies), or do keyhole surgery with tools or a laser.

**Advantages**: minimally invasive (small or no incisions), quicker recovery, less risk of infection, real-time colour images, no ionising radiation, can treat at the same time.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the illuminating bundle must be coherent.

### 3.10.4.3 Magnetic resonance (MR) scanner

*Refs: AQA 3.10.4.3 · Eduqas OB(l), (m), (n). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The basic principles of the MR scanner: protons (hydrogen nuclei) aligned in a strong magnetic field; precession; gradient coils select the slice; RF pulses excite protons; protons emit RF signals as they de-excite; signals processed by computer into an image.
- (Eduqas) Precession, resonance, relaxation time, and the Larmor frequency f = 42.6 × 10⁶ B (Hz, for hydrogen).

#### Explained

1. The patient lies inside a very strong uniform magnetic field (0.5 to 3 T) from a superconducting magnet.
2. Hydrogen nuclei (protons, mostly in water and fat) behave like tiny magnets because they spin. In the field, they line up with spins parallel (low energy) or antiparallel (higher energy) to it, with a slight excess parallel. They precess (wobble like a spinning top) about the field direction at the Larmor frequency, proportional to B: f = 42.6 MHz per tesla for hydrogen.
3. Gradient coils add a small field that varies across the body, so the Larmor frequency is different at each position. Only protons in a chosen slice (where f matches the radio pulse) can resonate.
4. A short pulse of radio waves at the Larmor frequency is applied. Protons absorb energy (resonance) and flip into the higher-energy (antiparallel) state.
5. When the pulse stops, the protons relax back, emitting radio signals at the Larmor frequency, picked up by receiver coils.
6. The time the protons take to relax (relaxation time, Eduqas) depends on the type of tissue (water-rich tissue differs from fat), giving contrast. By changing the gradients, the computer locates where each signal came from and builds a detailed 2D or 3D image.

**Advantages**: no ionising radiation; excellent soft-tissue contrast (brain, spinal cord, joints, tumours); any plane can be imaged; very good resolution (about 1 mm). **Disadvantages**: very expensive; slow (patient must keep still for a long time); noisy; claustrophobic; cannot be used with some metal implants or pacemakers; poorer images of bone.

#### Worked example

1. (Eduqas) Find the Larmor frequency for hydrogen in a 1.5 T scanner.

   f = 42.6 × 10⁶ × 1.5 = 6.4 × 10⁷ Hz (64 MHz).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying MRI uses ionising radiation.
- Saying MRI detects electrons.

### 3.10.5.1 The physics of diagnostic X-rays

*Refs: AQA 3.10.5.1 · OCR A 6.5.1(a), (b) · Eduqas OB(a), (b), (c). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- How X-rays are produced; the maximum photon energy; the energy spectrum (continuous and characteristic).
- The rotating-anode X-ray tube; controlling intensity, photon energy, image sharpness and contrast, and patient dose.

#### Explained

**X-ray tube.** In an evacuated glass tube, a heated filament (cathode) emits electrons by thermionic emission. A high p.d. (30 to 150 kV) accelerates them to a metal target (anode), often tungsten (high melting point, high atomic number). When the electrons hit the target, they decelerate and produce X-rays. About 99% of their energy becomes heat.

**Spectrum.** A graph of intensity against photon energy (or wavelength) has two parts:

- *Continuous (bremsstrahlung, "braking radiation")*: electrons decelerate in the electric fields of target nuclei, losing any amount of energy, giving a smooth range of photon energies. Maximum photon energy = eV (an electron loses all its kinetic energy in one go): hf_max = eV, so λ_min = hc/(eV).
- *Characteristic lines*: sharp peaks. An incoming electron knocks out an inner (K-shell) electron of a target atom; an outer electron drops into the vacancy, emitting an X-ray photon of a definite energy characteristic of the target element.

Low-energy X-rays are filtered out (by aluminium in the beam), since they would be absorbed by the skin and add to the dose without contributing to the image.

**Rotating anode.** The anode is a disc spinning at about 3000 rpm, so the electron beam strikes a different spot all the time, spreading the heat and stopping the target melting. The anode is cooled with oil. This lets a small, intense focal spot be used.

**Controls.**

| To change | Adjust |
| --- | --- |
| Intensity (number of photons) | Tube current (filament heating) |
| Photon energy (penetration, "hardness") | Accelerating p.d. (also increases intensity) |
| Image sharpness | Small focal spot; collimating the beam; patient close to detector; short exposure so movement does not blur |
| Contrast | Lower p.d. for soft tissue (more difference in absorption); contrast media (barium, iodine); anti-scatter grid |
| Patient dose | Filter low-energy X-rays; collimate to the area needed; short exposure; sensitive detectors |

**Diagnosis and therapy** (Eduqas OB(c)). Low-energy X-rays (tens of keV) are used for diagnosis (they are absorbed differently by bone and soft tissue). High-energy X-rays (MeV) are used for therapy (file section 3.10.6.4).

#### Worked example

1. An X-ray tube operates at 80 kV. Find the maximum photon energy and minimum wavelength.

   E = 80 keV = 1.28 × 10⁻¹⁴ J. λ_min = hc/E = 1.989 × 10⁻²⁵/1.28 × 10⁻¹⁴ = 1.55 × 10⁻¹¹ m.

2. The tube current is 50 mA. Find the power delivered to the anode, and the heat produced if 1% becomes X-rays.

   P = 80 000 × 0.050 = 4000 W; heat = 3960 W.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying increasing the tube current increases the maximum photon energy (it increases intensity only).
- Saying characteristic lines depend on the accelerating p.d. (their positions depend only on the target element).

### 3.10.5.2 Image detection and enhancement

*Refs: AQA 3.10.5.2 · OCR A 6.5.1(e) · Eduqas OB(e), (f). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The flat panel (FTP) detector: X-ray scintillator, photodiode pixels, electronic scanning; its advantages over photographic detection.
- Contrast enhancement using X-ray opaque material (barium meal; iodine).
- Photographic detection with intensifying screens and fluoroscopic image intensification, and why they are used.

#### Explained

**Photographic film with intensifying screen.** X-rays are poorly absorbed by film. Fluorescent screens on each side of the film absorb X-rays and emit many visible-light photons, which expose the film. This reduces the exposure (and dose) needed by a large factor, at the cost of slightly blurrier images (light spreads in the screen).

**Fluoroscopy and the image intensifier.** For moving, real-time images (e.g. guiding a catheter, following a barium meal through the gut). X-rays hit a fluorescent screen; the light releases electrons from a photocathode; the electrons are accelerated by a high p.d. and focused onto a small output phosphor screen, producing a much brighter image, viewed by a camera.

**Flat panel detector.** A layer of scintillator (e.g. caesium iodide) converts X-rays to visible light, which falls on a grid of photodiode pixels (amorphous silicon) that convert light to charge. The charges are read electronically, pixel by pixel, to form a digital image. Advantages over film: instant digital image (no developing), can be stored and sent electronically, can be processed (adjust contrast, zoom), smaller dose (more sensitive), wider range of exposures recorded, real-time images possible.

**Contrast media.** Soft tissues (e.g. stomach, intestines, blood vessels) absorb X-rays similarly, so they are hard to distinguish. A substance with high atomic number (strongly absorbing) is introduced: barium sulfate swallowed (barium meal) to show the digestive tract, or iodine compounds injected to show blood vessels (angiography) or kidneys.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying barium makes the patient radioactive (it is not radioactive; it just absorbs X-rays strongly).

### 3.10.5.3 Absorption of X-rays

*Refs: AQA 3.10.5.3 · OCR A 6.5.1(c), (d) · Eduqas OB(d), (e). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know
