- Exponential attenuation: I = I₀e^(−μx); linear attenuation coefficient μ; mass attenuation coefficient μ_m = μ/ρ; half-value thickness.
- Different tissues absorb X-rays differently (AQA: no details of the processes).
- (OCR A) Attenuation mechanisms: simple scatter, photoelectric effect, Compton scattering, pair production.

#### Explained

**Attenuation.** A beam of intensity I₀ passing through thickness x of material is reduced to I = I₀e^(−μx). μ (m⁻¹) depends on the material and the photon energy.

**Half-value thickness** x½: the thickness that halves the intensity. x½ = ln 2/μ.

**Mass attenuation coefficient** μ_m = μ/ρ (m² kg⁻¹). Useful because it depends on the element and photon energy but not on density: I = I₀e^(−μ_m ρx).

**Differential absorption.** Bone (contains calcium, Z = 20) absorbs X-rays much more strongly than soft tissue (mostly H, C, O). At diagnostic energies the photoelectric effect dominates and absorption rises steeply with atomic number (roughly Z³), so bone appears white (little X-ray reaches the detector) and soft tissue dark.

**Mechanisms** (OCR A 6.5.1(c)).

| Mechanism | Photon energy | What happens |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Simple (Rayleigh) scatter | 1 to 20 keV | Photon bounces off an atom with no energy loss (just changes direction) |
| Photoelectric effect | below about 100 keV | Photon is absorbed completely, ejecting an inner electron |
| Compton scattering | 0.5 to 5 MeV | Photon gives part of its energy to an outer electron (which is ejected) and is scattered with lower energy |
| Pair production | above 1.02 MeV | Photon near a nucleus turns into an electron–positron pair |

#### Worked example

1. Soft tissue has μ = 20 m⁻¹ for a certain X-ray energy. What fraction passes through 5.0 cm?

   I/I₀ = e^(−20 × 0.050) = e⁻¹ = 0.37.

2. The half-value thickness of bone is 1.2 cm. Find μ and the fraction transmitted through 3.0 cm.

   μ = 0.693/0.012 = 58 m⁻¹. Fraction = e^(−58 × 0.030) = 0.18 (or (½)^2.5 = 0.18).

#### Common mistakes

- Using x in cm with μ in m⁻¹.
- Confusing μ with μ_m.

### 3.10.5.4 CT scanner

*Refs: AQA 3.10.5.4 · OCR A 6.5.1(f), (g) · Eduqas OB(g). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Basic principles: the X-ray tube moves around the patient; a narrow, monochromatic beam; an array of detectors; a computer processes the signals into an image.
- Advantages and disadvantages: resolution, cost and safety.

#### Explained

In a computed tomography (CT, or CAT) scanner, the patient lies on a bed inside a ring. An X-ray tube rotates around them, producing a thin fan-shaped beam (nearly monochromatic, to make attenuation easier to interpret). An arc or ring of detectors opposite records the intensity transmitted along many paths through the body from many angles. A computer combines all these measurements to calculate how strongly each small volume (voxel) of the slice absorbs, producing a cross-sectional image. The bed moves through the ring (often in a spiral) to build up many slices, which can be combined into a 3D image.

**Advantages over a simple X-ray image**: shows cross-sections and 3D structure, not overlapping shadows; distinguishes soft tissues with small differences in density; images can be manipulated. **Disadvantages**: much higher radiation dose (equivalent to hundreds of chest X-rays); expensive; slower; patient must keep still. Compared with MRI: faster, cheaper, better for bone and bleeding, but uses ionising radiation and has poorer soft-tissue contrast.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying CT uses a gamma source.

### 3.10.6.1 Imaging techniques

*Refs: AQA 3.10.6.1 · OCR A 6.5.2(a), (c), (d), (e) · Eduqas OB(r), (t). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Gamma-emitting radioisotopes as tracers: technetium-99m, iodine-131 and indium-111 and their properties (radiation emitted, half-life, gamma energy, attachment to compounds that target organs).
- The molybdenum–technetium generator and why it matters.
- PET scans (OCR A: fluorine-18 in fluorodeoxyglucose).

#### Explained

A **tracer** is a radioactive isotope attached to a chemical that the body takes to a particular organ. It is injected or swallowed; a gamma camera outside the body detects where it goes, showing how organs are working (function), not just their shape.

**Good tracer properties**: emits gamma only (penetrates the body to reach the detector; little ionisation inside); gamma energy about 100 to 200 keV (easy to detect, collimate and shield); half-life of hours (long enough to prepare and do the scan, short enough to keep the dose low); can be attached to compounds targeting particular organs; quickly removed by the body.

| Tracer | Emits | Half-life | Uses |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Technetium-99m | γ, 140 keV | 6 h | Most common: bones, heart, brain, lungs, kidneys (attached to different compounds) |
| Iodine-131 | β⁻ and γ (364 keV) | 8 days | Thyroid (which absorbs iodine); also treatment of thyroid cancer because of the β |
| Indium-111 | γ (171 and 245 keV), by electron capture | 2.8 days | Labelling white blood cells to find infection or abscesses; slower processes |

**Molybdenum–technetium generator.** Molybdenum-99 (T½ = 66 h) is held on an alumina column. It decays by β⁻ to Tc-99m. Saline washed through the column dissolves the Tc-99m (but not the molybdenum) and is collected for use ("milking"). Because the parent has a longer half-life, the generator can be delivered to a hospital and supply Tc-99m for about a week, even though Tc-99m itself only lasts hours.

**PET (positron emission tomography).** A positron-emitting tracer, usually fluorine-18 (T½ = 110 min) in fluorodeoxyglucose (FDG), a glucose analogue, is injected. Cells using a lot of glucose (active brain regions, many tumours) take it up. Each positron annihilates with an electron within a millimetre or so, producing two 511 keV gamma photons travelling in opposite directions. A ring of detectors around the patient records pairs arriving at the same time (coincidence detection); the annihilation lies on the line joining the two detectors, and the small difference in arrival times can locate it along the line. A computer builds a 3D image of tracer concentration. PET shows function (metabolism), good for cancer detection and brain studies. F-18 must be made in a cyclotron close to the hospital because of its short half-life.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying alpha emitters make good tracers.
- Saying PET detects positrons directly.

### 3.10.6.2 Half-life

*Refs: AQA 3.10.6.2. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Physical, biological and effective half-lives: 1/T_E = 1/T_B + 1/T_P, and definitions.

#### Explained

- **Physical half-life** T_P: the time for the activity of the isotope to halve by radioactive decay.
- **Biological half-life** T_B: the time for half the substance to be removed from the body by biological processes (excretion).
- **Effective half-life** T_E: the time for the activity in the body to halve by both together. Since the two removal rates add (λ_E = λ_P + λ_B):

1/T_E = 1/T_B + 1/T_P

T_E is always shorter than both.

#### Worked example

1. A tracer has T_P = 6.0 h and T_B = 24 h. Find T_E.

   1/T_E = 1/6 + 1/24 = 5/24; T_E = 4.8 h.

#### Common mistakes

- Adding the half-lives instead of their reciprocals.

### 3.10.6.3 Gamma camera

*Refs: AQA 3.10.6.3 · OCR A 6.5.2(b), (c) · Eduqas OB(s). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The basic structure and workings of a photomultiplier tube and a gamma camera.

#### Explained

**Gamma camera** (from the patient outwards):

1. **Collimator**: a thick lead plate with many long, narrow parallel holes. Only gamma photons travelling straight along the holes (perpendicular to the camera) pass; others are absorbed. So each point on the crystal receives gamma rays only from the part of the body directly beneath it, giving a sharp image.
2. **Scintillator crystal**: a large sodium iodide crystal (doped with thallium). Each gamma photon absorbed produces a flash of thousands of visible-light photons.
3. **Photomultiplier tubes**: an array behind the crystal. Each tube converts light to an electrical pulse: light releases electrons from a photocathode (photoelectric effect); these are accelerated to a series of electrodes (dynodes) at increasing potential; each electron knocks out several more at each dynode, so after about 10 dynodes one electron becomes about a million, giving a measurable pulse at the anode.
4. **Position logic and computer**: the relative sizes of the pulses from neighbouring tubes locate each flash; the computer builds up an image of where the tracer is concentrated over time.

Resolution is about 5 to 10 mm, limited by the collimator hole size and the spread of light in the crystal. Longer collimator holes give better resolution but fewer counts (longer scan or higher dose).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the collimator focuses gamma rays (it only selects them by absorbing others).

### 3.10.6.4 Use of high-energy X-rays

*Refs: AQA 3.10.6.4 · Eduqas OB(c). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- External treatment of tumours with high-energy X-rays; methods used to limit exposure of healthy cells.

#### Explained

X-rays of several MeV (from a linear accelerator) penetrate deep into the body and destroy cancer cells by ionising their DNA. Healthy tissue must be protected:

- The beam is rotated around the patient (or several beams from different directions are used), all crossing at the tumour, so the tumour gets a large dose but each region of healthy tissue gets only a fraction.
- The beam is shaped to the tumour outline with collimators (multileaf collimators of moving tungsten leaves).
- The dose is split into many small doses over weeks (fractionation), so healthy cells, which repair better, can recover between treatments.
- Imaging (CT) is used to locate the tumour precisely; the patient is held still with moulds.

High-energy X-rays deposit their maximum dose below the skin surface, sparing the skin.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying radiotherapy uses gamma cameras.
- Saying one large dose is safer than many small (fractionated) doses.

### 3.10.6.5 Use of radioactive implants

*Refs: AQA 3.10.6.5. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Internal treatment using beta-emitting implants.

#### Explained

In brachytherapy, small sealed sources (seeds or wires) emitting beta particles (or low-energy gamma) are placed inside or next to a tumour, e.g. iodine-125 seeds in the prostate, or a source in the cervix. Beta particles have a short range (a few mm in tissue), so they deliver a high dose to the tumour while healthy tissue further away receives very little. The implant is either removed after a set time or left in place if its half-life is short. Iodine-131 taken by mouth is concentrated in the thyroid, where its beta radiation destroys thyroid cancer cells.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying gamma emitters are used as implants because they have a short range (beta has the short range).

### 3.10.6.6 Imaging comparisons

*Refs: AQA 3.10.6.6 · OCR A 6.5.1(g) · Eduqas OB(n). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Compare imaging techniques in terms of image resolution, convenience and safety.

#### Explained

| Technique | Resolution | Convenience | Safety |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Ultrasound | About 1 mm; poor through bone and gas | Cheap, portable, quick, real time | Non-ionising; very safe |
| Endoscopy | Very good, colour | Invasive, uncomfortable; allows treatment | Non-ionising; small risk of damage or infection |
| X-ray (planar) | Good (about 0.5 mm) for bone | Quick, cheap, widely available | Ionising; low dose |
| CT | Good (about 0.5 to 1 mm), 3D | Fairly quick; expensive | High ionising dose |
| MRI | Very good (about 1 mm) in soft tissue | Slow, noisy, expensive, claustrophobic; not with metal implants | Non-ionising; strong field risk with metal |
| Gamma camera / PET | Poor (5 to 10 mm; PET about 4 mm) | Slow, expensive (PET needs a cyclotron) | Ionising; dose from tracer |

The choice depends on what needs to be seen: anatomy (structure) or function (metabolism), bone or soft tissue, and the risks to the patient (e.g. ultrasound for a fetus).

#### Common mistakes

- Choosing CT for a fetus (ultrasound is used, as it is non-ionising).
- Saying gamma camera images have better resolution than CT.

### Dose and effects of radiation (Eduqas)

*Refs: Eduqas OB(o), (p), (q). Level: A-level only. (OCR B 6.2.2 dose is in file 18.)*

#### What you need to know

- The effects of α, β and γ radiation on living tissue.
- Absorbed dose (gray), equivalent dose and effective dose (sievert).
- H = D × W_R; E = Σ(H × W_T).

#### Explained

**Effects.** Ionising radiation ionises atoms in cells, breaking chemical bonds and damaging DNA. Low doses may cause mutations leading to cancer years later (a stochastic effect, probability ∝ dose). High doses kill cells, causing radiation sickness, burns and death (deterministic effects, with a threshold). Rapidly dividing cells (bone marrow, gut, fetus, tumours) are most sensitive.

- **Absorbed dose** D = energy absorbed per unit mass, in gray (Gy = J kg⁻¹).
- **Equivalent dose** H = D × W_R, in sievert (Sv). Radiation weighting factor W_R: 1 for X-rays, γ and β; 20 for α.
- **Effective dose** E = Σ(H × W_T), in sievert, summed over the organs exposed. Tissue weighting factor W_T reflects each organ's sensitivity (e.g. 0.12 for lungs and bone marrow, 0.01 for skin; the factors for the whole body add to 1).

#### Worked example

1. A 2.0 kg organ (W_T = 0.12) absorbs 3.0 × 10⁻³ J of γ radiation. Find the absorbed, equivalent and effective doses.

   D = 1.5 × 10⁻³ Gy; H = 1.5 mSv; E = 0.18 mSv.

#### Common mistakes

- Confusing absorbed dose (Gy) with equivalent or effective dose (Sv).
- Forgetting the radiation weighting factor of 20 for alpha.

### Check questions

1. Explain why a coupling gel is used in ultrasound scanning. Calculate the fraction reflected at a gel–skin boundary (Z_gel = 1.60 × 10⁶, Z_skin = 1.70 × 10⁶ kg m⁻² s⁻¹). (4 marks)
2. Describe the difference between an A-scan and a B-scan. (3 marks)
3. Explain the difference between coherent and non-coherent fibre bundles in an endoscope. (3 marks)
4. Describe the principles of MRI. (6 marks)
5. Sketch an X-ray spectrum from a tungsten target and explain its features. How does it change if the tube voltage is increased? (5 marks)
6. Lead has a half-value thickness of 0.30 mm for some X-rays. What thickness reduces the intensity to 1%? (3 marks)
7. Describe how a gamma camera forms an image. (5 marks)
8. A tracer has physical half-life 8.0 days and effective half-life 5.0 days. Find its biological half-life. (2 marks)
9. Compare CT and MRI for imaging the brain. (4 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Without gel, air between probe and skin; large impedance difference reflects nearly all ultrasound (1); gel has impedance close to skin, so most ultrasound transmitted (1); ((1.70 − 1.60)/(3.30))² (1) = 9.2 × 10⁻⁴ (0.09%) (1).
2. A-scan: single direction, amplitude of echoes against time (1); used for distances/depths (1); B-scan: probe moved/array, echo brightness at positions builds a 2D image (1).
3. Non-coherent: fibres arranged randomly, used to carry light in to illuminate (1); coherent: same arrangement at both ends (1); carries the image back point by point (1).
4. Strong magnetic field aligns hydrogen nuclei (1); they precess at the Larmor frequency (1); gradient coils select a slice (1); RF pulse at Larmor frequency excites / flips protons (1); protons relax, emitting RF signals detected by coils (1); relaxation time depends on tissue; computer builds image (1).
5. Continuous curve from minimum wavelength (maximum energy eV) (1): electrons decelerating (bremsstrahlung) (1); sharp characteristic lines (1): inner-shell electron knocked out, outer electron drops in (1); higher voltage: greater maximum energy, higher intensity, lines unchanged in position (1).
6. μ = 0.693/0.30 = 2.31 mm⁻¹ (1); 0.01 = e^(−2.31x) (1); x = 2.0 mm (1).
7. Collimator of lead with parallel holes selects gamma rays travelling perpendicular (1); scintillator crystal converts gamma to light flashes (1); photomultiplier tubes convert light to electrical pulses and amplify them (1); position of flash found from relative pulse sizes (1); computer builds up image of tracer distribution (1).
8. 1/5 = 1/T_B + 1/8 (1); T_B = 13 days (1).
9. CT: uses ionising X-rays, higher dose (1); quicker, cheaper, better for bone/bleeding (1); MRI: no ionising radiation (1); better soft-tissue contrast but slower, expensive, not with metal implants (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/24-rotational-dynamics-and-physics-of-sports.md -->

## Rotational dynamics and the physics of sports

Everything in linear mechanics has a rotational twin: mass becomes moment of inertia, force becomes torque, momentum becomes angular momentum. This file covers moment of inertia, rotational kinetic energy and flywheels, angular kinematics, torque and angular acceleration, angular momentum and its conservation, and work and power in rotation. It then covers the Eduqas option on the physics of sports: stability, muscles as levers, impulse, the coefficient of restitution, Bernoulli's equation and drag.

### Overview

AQA option 3.11.1 (A-level only) and Eduqas option C (A-level only). Angular speed and the radian are in file 12; moments, centre of gravity and equilibrium in file 06; impulse and collisions in file 08; projectiles in file 07.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.11.1 · Eduqas OC(a) to (o).
- AQA gives expressions for moments of inertia when needed. Eduqas OC(f) asks you to use I = (2/5)mr² for a solid sphere and I = (2/3)mr² for a thin spherical shell.
- AQA 3.11.1.2: flywheels (energy storage, smoothing torque). AQA 3.11.1.6: frictional torque in machines. Not Eduqas.
- Eduqas only: coefficient of restitution, Bernoulli's equation and the drag equation F_D = ½ρv²AC_D, muscles and moments.

### 3.11.1.1 Concept of moment of inertia

*Refs: AQA 3.11.1.1 · Eduqas OC(e), (f). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- I = mr² for a point mass; I = Σmr² for an extended object.
- Qualitative factors that affect the moment of inertia.
- (Eduqas) I = (2/5)mr² (solid sphere), I = (2/3)mr² (thin spherical shell).

#### Explained

The moment of inertia I of an object about an axis measures how hard it is to change its rotation (the rotational equivalent of mass). For a point mass m at distance r from the axis: I = mr². For an extended object, add up every particle: I = Σmr². Unit kg m².

**Factors.** I is larger when:

- the mass is larger;
- the mass is further from the axis (r² makes this very important);
- so it depends on the axis chosen and the shape.

| Object | Axis | I |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Thin ring or hoop, radius r | Through centre, perpendicular to plane | mr² |
| Uniform disc or solid cylinder | Through centre, along axis | ½mr² |
| Solid sphere | Through centre | (2/5)mr² |
| Thin spherical shell | Through centre | (2/3)mr² |
| Uniform rod, length L | Through centre, perpendicular | (1/12)mL² |
| Uniform rod, length L | Through one end, perpendicular | (1/3)mL² |

A hoop has a bigger I than a disc of the same mass and radius because all its mass is at the rim.

#### Worked example

1. Find I of two 2.0 kg masses on a light rod, each 0.30 m from the axis.

   I = 2 × 2.0 × 0.30² = 0.36 kg m².

2. (Eduqas) Compare I for a solid ball and a hollow ball of the same mass (0.45 kg) and radius (0.11 m).

   Solid: 0.4 × 0.45 × 0.0121 = 2.2 × 10⁻³ kg m². Hollow: 0.667 × 0.45 × 0.0121 = 3.6 × 10⁻³ kg m².

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting to square r.
- Saying I is a fixed property of an object regardless of the axis.

### 3.11.1.2 Rotational kinetic energy

*Refs: AQA 3.11.1.2 · Eduqas OC(k), (l). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- E_k = ½Iω².
- Factors affecting the energy storage capacity of a flywheel; uses of flywheels (smoothing torque and speed; storing energy in vehicles and production machines).
- (Eduqas) Energy conservation with linear and rotational kinetic energy, gravitational and elastic potential energy.

#### Explained

A rotating object has kinetic energy E_k = ½Iω² (each particle has ½mv² = ½m(rω)²). A rolling object has both translational and rotational kinetic energy: E = ½mv² + ½Iω², with v = rω (no slipping).

**Flywheels.** A heavy wheel that stores energy as rotational kinetic energy.

- Storage capacity increases with I (so with mass concentrated at the rim, e.g. spokes and a heavy rim) and especially with ω (energy ∝ ω²). The limit is the strength of the material: at high ω, the stress at the rim (needed to provide the centripetal force) can make it burst. Composite materials (carbon fibre) are strong and light, allowing very high speeds. Energy losses are reduced by low-friction bearings (or magnetic bearings) and running in a vacuum.
- *Smoothing*: in an engine, each cylinder gives a burst of torque; a flywheel absorbs energy during the bursts and releases it between them, keeping the speed steady. Used in punch presses and similar machines: a motor slowly spins up the flywheel, which releases a large energy quickly for each stamping operation.
- *Energy storage in vehicles*: regenerative braking (KERS) transfers kinetic energy of the vehicle to a flywheel when braking, and returns it for acceleration.

**Rolling down a slope** (Eduqas OC(l)). mgh = ½mv² + ½Iω². A hoop (large I) rolls down more slowly than a solid sphere, because more of the energy goes into rotation.

#### Worked example

1. A flywheel (I = 12 kg m²) spins at 3000 rpm. Find its energy.

   ω = 3000 × 2π/60 = 314 rad s⁻¹. E = ½ × 12 × 314² = 5.9 × 10⁵ J.

2. (Eduqas) A solid ball rolls from rest down a slope, falling 1.2 m. Find its speed.

   mgh = ½mv² + ½(2/5)mr²(v/r)² = (7/10)mv². v = √(10gh/7) = √(10 × 9.81 × 1.2/7) = 4.1 m s⁻¹.

#### Common mistakes

- Using rpm for ω.
- Forgetting the rotational kinetic energy of a rolling object.

### 3.11.1.3 Rotational motion

*Refs: AQA 3.11.1.3 · Eduqas OC(g). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Angular displacement, angular speed, angular velocity, angular acceleration: ω = Δθ/Δt, α = Δω/Δt.
- Graphs of uniform and non-uniform angular acceleration.
- Equations for uniform angular acceleration and the analogy with linear motion.

#### Explained

| Linear | Rotational | Link |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Displacement s (m) | Angular displacement θ (rad) | s = rθ |
| Velocity v (m s⁻¹) | Angular velocity ω (rad s⁻¹) | v = rω |
| Acceleration a (m s⁻²) | Angular acceleration α (rad s⁻²) | a = rα (tangential) |
| Mass m | Moment of inertia I | I = Σmr² |
| Force F | Torque T | T = Fr |
| F = ma | T = Iα | |
| Momentum p = mv | Angular momentum L = Iω | |
| E_k = ½mv² | E_k = ½Iω² | |
| Work W = Fs | W = Tθ | |
| Power P = Fv | P = Tω | |

**Equations of uniform angular acceleration:**

- ω₂ = ω₁ + αt
- θ = ½(ω₁ + ω₂)t
- θ = ω₁t + ½αt²
- ω₂² = ω₁² + 2αθ

**Graphs.** On an ω–t graph, the gradient is α and the area is θ. Curved lines show non-uniform angular acceleration.

#### Worked example

1. A wheel speeds up uniformly from 20 rad s⁻¹ to 50 rad s⁻¹ in 6.0 s. Find α and the number of revolutions.

   α = 30/6.0 = 5.0 rad s⁻². θ = ½(20 + 50) × 6.0 = 210 rad = 33 revolutions.

#### Common mistakes

- Mixing revolutions and radians.

### 3.11.1.4 Torque and angular acceleration

*Refs: AQA 3.11.1.4 · Eduqas OC(h). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Torque T = Fr.
- T = Iα.

#### Explained

Torque (moment) is the turning effect of a force: T = Fr, where r is the perpendicular distance from the axis to the line of action (N m). A resultant torque produces angular acceleration: T = Iα (Newton's second law for rotation). Eduqas uses τ for torque.

In a real machine, frictional torque in bearings opposes rotation, so the resultant torque = applied torque − frictional torque.

#### Worked example

1. A 30 N force acts tangentially at the rim of a disc of radius 0.20 m and I = 0.40 kg m². A frictional torque of 1.0 N m acts. Find α.

   Resultant torque = 6.0 − 1.0 = 5.0 N m. α = 5.0/0.40 = 12.5 rad s⁻².

#### Common mistakes

- Ignoring frictional torque.

### 3.11.1.5 Angular momentum

*Refs: AQA 3.11.1.5 · Eduqas OC(i), (j). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Angular momentum = Iω.
- Conservation of angular momentum.
- Angular impulse = change in angular momentum: TΔt = Δ(Iω) for constant T.
- Applications, including sport.

#### Explained

Angular momentum L = Iω (kg m² s⁻¹ or N m s).

**Conservation.** If no external resultant torque acts, the total angular momentum of a system stays constant: I₁ω₁ = I₂ω₂.

- An ice skater spinning with arms out pulls them in: I falls, so ω rises. Rotational kinetic energy ½Iω² = L²/2I increases; the extra energy comes from the work done by the skater's muscles pulling the arms in.
- A diver tucks to spin faster, then straightens to slow down before entering the water.
- A dropped disc landing on a spinning turntable: the combined I is larger, so ω falls (kinetic energy is lost, like an inelastic collision).

**Angular impulse.** TΔt = ΔL. A small torque for a long time gives the same change in angular momentum as a large torque for a short time.

#### Worked example

1. A skater (I = 3.5 kg m²) spins at 2.0 rev s⁻¹ and pulls in her arms so I = 1.4 kg m². Find the new rate and the change in kinetic energy.

   ω₂ = 3.5 × 2.0/1.4 = 5.0 rev s⁻¹. E₁ = ½ × 3.5 × (4π)² = 276 J; E₂ = ½ × 1.4 × (10π)² = 691 J. Gain = 415 J (from the work done by her muscles).

2. A turntable (I = 0.050 kg m²) spins at 4.0 rad s⁻¹. A ring (I = 0.030 kg m²) is dropped on it. Find the final ω.

   0.050 × 4.0 = 0.080ω, ω = 2.5 rad s⁻¹.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying rotational kinetic energy is conserved when the skater pulls in her arms.

### 3.11.1.6 Work and power

*Refs: AQA 3.11.1.6. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- W = Tθ; P = Tω.
- Frictional torque must be taken into account in rotating machinery.

#### Explained

A torque T turning through angle θ does work W = Tθ (from W = Fs with s = rθ). Power P = Tω. An engine's output power is its torque × angular speed (this is the "brake power" in file 25). At a steady speed, the engine's torque equals the total frictional and load torque.

#### Worked example

1. A motor provides 2.5 N m at 1500 rpm. Find its power.

   ω = 157 rad s⁻¹. P = 2.5 × 157 = 390 W.

2. A flywheel at 120 rad s⁻¹ (I = 5.0 kg m²) is slowed to rest by a constant frictional torque in 80 s. Find the torque and the angle turned.

   α = 1.5 rad s⁻²; T = 7.5 N m. θ = ½ × 120 × 80 = 4800 rad. Check: W = Tθ = 36 000 J = ½ × 5.0 × 120².

#### Common mistakes

- Using ω in rpm in P = Tω.
- Forgetting frictional torque when finding the output of a machine.

### The physics of sports (Eduqas option C)

*Refs: Eduqas OC(a), (b), (c), (d), (m), (n), (o). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Using the centre of gravity to explain stability and toppling in sport.
- Using the principle of moments to find forces in muscle systems and in other contexts (e.g. sailing).
- Ft = mv − mu in sporting contexts.
- The coefficient of restitution e = relative speed after collision ÷ relative speed before; for a dropped ball e = √(h/H).
- Projectile motion in sport.
- Bernoulli's equation p = p₀ − ½ρv² in sporting contexts.
- The drag force F_D = ½ρv²AC_D.

#### Explained

**Stability** (OC(a)). An object is stable when its centre of gravity is above its base. It topples when the centre of gravity moves beyond the edge of the base, because the weight then gives a toppling moment about the edge. Athletes widen their stance and lower their centre of gravity (crouching wrestlers, rugby scrums) to be more stable; a sprinter in the "set" position leans forward so the centre of gravity is ahead of the hands and feet, ready to fall into the run.

**Muscles as levers** (OC(b)). The forearm is a lever pivoted at the elbow. The biceps attaches close to the elbow (about 4 cm), while a weight in the hand is about 35 cm away. Taking moments about the elbow: F_biceps × 0.04 = W × 0.35 (+ forearm weight × its distance), so the muscle force is about 9 times the weight held. Muscles give large forces but the hand moves a long way for a small muscle contraction, giving speed.

**Sailing.** The forces of the wind on the sail and the water on the keel produce moments about the centre of the boat; the crew lean out (hiking) to give a counter-moment so the boat does not capsize.

**Impulse** (OC(c)). Ft = mv − mu: follow-through in a kick or racket stroke increases contact time and so the change in momentum; landing with bent knees or on a soft mat increases stopping time and reduces force.

**Coefficient of restitution** (OC(d)). e = (relative speed of separation)/(relative speed of approach). e = 1 for a perfectly elastic collision; e = 0 if the bodies stick together. For a ball dropped from height H onto a fixed surface, bouncing to height h: speed before = √(2gH), after = √(2gh), so **e = √(h/H)**. (The spec writes e = h/H in one form; the square-root form follows from speeds. Use whichever the question defines.)

**Projectiles in sport** (OC(m)). As in file 07. A long jumper or shot putter: range depends on launch speed, angle and height. For a launch above the ground, the best angle is a little less than 45°.

**Bernoulli's equation** (OC(n)). For a fluid flowing smoothly, where its speed is higher, its pressure is lower: p = p₀ − ½ρv², where p₀ is the pressure where the fluid is at rest relative to the object. Spinning balls: a ball with topspin drags air so that air moves faster over the bottom surface (relative to the ball), lowering the pressure there; the ball dips sharply (Magnus effect). Sidespin makes a football swerve. The same idea explains lift on wings and spoilers on racing cars (shaped to push the car down for grip).

**Drag** (OC(o)). For objects moving fast through air (turbulent flow): F_D = ½ρv²AC_D, where A is the frontal area and C_D the drag coefficient (depends on shape: about 0.5 for a sphere, about 1 for an upright cyclist, 0.3 for a streamlined car). Cyclists crouch (smaller A, lower C_D) and wear smooth clothing and pointed helmets. Since drag ∝ v², the power needed to overcome drag ∝ v³.

#### Worked example

1. A ball dropped from 2.0 m bounces to 1.3 m. Find e.

   e = √(1.3/2.0) = 0.81.

2. A cyclist has AC_D = 0.30 m² and rides at 12 m s⁻¹ (ρ_air = 1.2 kg m⁻³). Find the drag and the power needed against it.

   F_D = ½ × 1.2 × 144 × 0.30 = 26 N. P = F_D v = 310 W.

3. Air passes over a spinning ball at 32 m s⁻¹ on one side and 28 m s⁻¹ on the other. Find the pressure difference.

   Δp = ½ρ(v₁² − v₂²) = 0.6 × (1024 − 784) = 144 Pa, lower on the faster side.

4. A forearm holds a 50 N weight 0.34 m from the elbow; the forearm (weight 15 N) acts 0.15 m from the elbow; the biceps is 0.040 m from the elbow, vertical. Find the biceps force.

   F × 0.040 = 50 × 0.34 + 15 × 0.15 = 17 + 2.25 = 19.25; F = 480 N.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying Bernoulli's effect makes a ball curve towards the higher-pressure side.
- Using e = h/H when e is defined by speeds (e² = h/H).

### Check questions

1. Explain why a hoop and a disc of the same mass and radius have different moments of inertia. (2 marks)
2. A flywheel of I = 0.80 kg m² is accelerated from rest by a constant torque of 4.0 N m for 10 s. Find its final angular speed, kinetic energy and number of revolutions. (4 marks)
3. Explain how a flywheel smooths the output of an engine. (2 marks)
4. State the principle of conservation of angular momentum and use it to explain how a diver controls her rate of rotation. (4 marks)
5. A motor delivers 80 W at 1200 rpm. Calculate the torque. (2 marks)
6. (Eduqas) A solid sphere and a thin spherical shell of the same mass and radius roll down the same slope from rest. Which reaches the bottom first? Explain. (3 marks)
7. (Eduqas) A tennis ball is dropped from 1.5 m and bounces to 0.80 m. Find the coefficient of restitution and the speed just after the bounce. (3 marks)
8. (Eduqas) Explain why a racing cyclist crouches. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. All of the hoop's mass is at radius r (1); disc's mass is spread from 0 to r, so mean r² is smaller; I(hoop) = mr² > I(disc) = ½mr² (1).
2. α = 5.0 rad s⁻²; ω = 50 rad s⁻¹ (1); E = ½ × 0.80 × 2500 = 1000 J (1); θ = ½ × 5.0 × 100 = 250 rad (1) = 40 revolutions (1).
3. Flywheel stores energy as rotational kinetic energy during power strokes and releases it between them (1); large I means small change in ω, so speed stays steady (1).
4. Total angular momentum is constant if no external resultant torque acts (1); in a tuck, the diver reduces I (1); so ω increases (1); straightening increases I so ω decreases for a clean entry (1).
5. ω = 125.7 rad s⁻¹ (1); T = P/ω = 0.64 N m (1).
6. Solid sphere (1); it has the smaller I (2/5 vs 2/3 mr²) (1); so a smaller share of the energy goes into rotation and more into translational KE, giving a larger v at each point (1).
7. e = √(0.80/1.5) = 0.73 (1); speed before = √(2 × 9.81 × 1.5) = 5.42 m s⁻¹ (1); after = 0.73 × 5.42 = 4.0 m s⁻¹ (1).
8. Reduces frontal area (and drag coefficient) (1); drag F = ½ρv²AC_D is reduced, so less power needed at the same speed / higher speed for same power (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/35-energy-and-the-environment.md -->

## Energy and the environment: the Earth's energy balance, energy resources, fuel cells and thermal conduction

This file covers the Eduqas option on energy and the environment: the balance between energy from the Sun and energy radiated by the Earth, the greenhouse effect and carbon dioxide, the solar spectrum, rising sea levels, solar, wind, tidal, hydroelectric and nuclear power, fuel cells, and thermal conduction and insulation (U-values).

### Overview

Eduqas option D (A-level only). Black-body radiation, Wien's and Stefan's laws are in file 20; density and Archimedes' principle in file 30; fission, fusion and binding energy in file 19; the photoelectric effect in file 03; specific heat capacity in file 13.

#### Board differences

- Eduqas OD only. No other England board has an energy and environment option, though Edexcel 6 to 8 and OCR A's nuclear power points (file 19) touch on similar decisions.

### OD(a) The Earth's temperature and energy balance

*Refs: Eduqas OD(a)(i) to (iv). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Thermal equilibrium: the balance between energy arriving from the Sun and energy re-radiated by the Earth, in the context of global energy demand and CO₂ levels.
- The origin and transmission of solar energy; the shape of the Sun's power spectrum; energy converted to near infrared in the atmosphere.
- Wien's law and the Stefan–Boltzmann law in the context of solar power.
- Density and Archimedes' principle to explain sea-level rise: melting ice on land raises sea level; melting icebergs do not.

#### Explained

**Energy balance.** The Earth's temperature is steady when the power it absorbs from the Sun equals the power it radiates into space. Absorbed power = (1 − albedo) × I × πR² (the Earth presents a disc of area πR² to the Sun; albedo ≈ 0.3 is the fraction reflected). Radiated power = σT⁴ × 4πR² (the whole surface radiates). Setting these equal with I = 1360 W m⁻²:

T⁴ = (1 − 0.3) × 1360/(4σ) → T ≈ 255 K (−18 °C)

The actual average surface temperature is about 288 K (15 °C). The difference is the greenhouse effect.

**Solar spectrum.** The Sun's energy comes from nuclear fusion in its core and reaches its surface (about 5800 K) by radiation and convection. It radiates like a black body peaking near 500 nm (Wien's law), mostly visible and near infrared, with some UV. This crosses space as electromagnetic radiation. Much of the short-wavelength radiation passes through the atmosphere and is absorbed by the ground, which warms up and re-emits energy at much longer wavelengths (infrared, peaking near 10 μm since T ≈ 288 K).

**Greenhouse effect.** Gases such as CO₂, water vapour and methane are transparent to most incoming visible light but absorb outgoing infrared and re-emit it in all directions, including back down. This keeps the surface warmer than it would otherwise be. Increasing CO₂ (from burning fossil fuels to meet growing global energy demand) absorbs more infrared, so the Earth must warm to a higher temperature before it radiates enough to restore balance.

**Sea levels** (OD(a)(iv)).

- A floating iceberg displaces its own weight of sea water (Archimedes). When it melts, the water it becomes has the same weight, so it fills the same volume it displaced: sea level does not change (ignoring the small difference between fresh and salt water).
- Ice on land (glaciers, Greenland and Antarctic ice sheets) is not displacing any sea water; when it melts and flows into the sea, it adds volume, raising sea level.
- Warming also expands sea water (lower density), raising levels further.

#### Worked example

1. If the Earth's albedo fell from 0.30 to 0.28, estimate the new equilibrium temperature (no greenhouse effect).

   T ∝ (1 − albedo)^(1/4): 255 × (0.72/0.70)^(1/4) = 255 × 1.0071 = 256.8 K (about 2 K warmer).

#### Common mistakes

- Using the Earth's surface area 4πR² for the absorbed power (the Earth intercepts sunlight over πR²).

### OD(b) Energy resources

*Refs: Eduqas OD(b)(i) to (iv). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Common renewable and non-renewable energy sources, and their development and use in the UK and worldwide.
- Solar: the proton–proton chain; I = P/A and the inverse-square law; photovoltaic cells and efficiency.
- Wind: power available from a flowing fluid = ½Aρv³; factors affecting turbine efficiency.
- Tidal barrages, hydroelectric power and pumped storage: energy conversion (Ep to Ek) and power calculations; comparison with wind.
- Nuclear: breeding and enrichment in fission; difficulties of fusion power and the fusion triple product.

#### Explained

**Renewable** (replenished naturally): solar, wind, hydroelectric, tidal, wave, geothermal, biomass. **Non-renewable**: coal, oil, gas, nuclear fission fuels. In the UK, coal has almost disappeared; gas, wind (especially offshore) and nuclear supply most electricity, with solar growing; worldwide, coal remains large (especially in Asia) but solar and wind are growing fastest. Considerations: cost, reliability (intermittency of wind and solar needs storage or back-up), carbon emissions, land use and environmental impact.

**Solar power.**

- The Sun's main energy source is the proton–proton chain: four protons fuse (in steps) to form a helium-4 nucleus, two positrons and two neutrinos, releasing about 26.7 MeV. Main branch: p + p → ²H + e⁺ + νₑ; ²H + p → ³He + γ; ³He + ³He → ⁴He + 2p.
- Intensity at distance d from a source of power P: I = P/(4πd²) (inverse square). At the Earth, I ≈ 1360 W m⁻² above the atmosphere; about 1000 W m⁻² reaches the ground on a clear day with the Sun overhead.
- Photovoltaic (PV) cells: photons release charge carriers in a semiconductor p–n junction, producing a p.d. and current. Efficiency = electrical power output ÷ solar power falling on the cell (typically 15 to 22%).

**Wind power.** In time t, a column of air of length vt and cross-sectional area A (the swept area of the blades) passes the turbine. Its mass is ρAvt and its kinetic energy ½ρAvt × v². So power available = **½Aρv³**. Doubling the wind speed gives 8 times the power. A turbine cannot extract all of it (the air must keep moving away; the theoretical maximum is 59%, the Betz limit). Efficiency depends on blade design and angle, wind speed (turbines cut out in very strong winds), and losses in gearbox and generator. Large blades sweep a bigger area (A ∝ r²).

**Hydroelectric, tidal and pumped storage.** Water stored at a height h has gravitational potential energy mgh; it flows down through turbines, becoming kinetic energy and then electrical energy. Power = (mass per second) × g × h × efficiency = ρ × (volume flow rate) × g × h × efficiency.

- *Hydroelectric*: water from a high reservoir behind a dam.
- *Tidal barrage*: a dam across an estuary traps water at high tide; it is released through turbines as the tide falls (and sometimes on the way in). Predictable, but only at certain times and harms estuary habitats.
- *Pumped storage* (e.g. Dinorwig in Wales): spare electricity at times of low demand pumps water uphill; at peak demand it flows back down through turbines within seconds. It stores energy (about 75% efficient overall) rather than generating it.

**Nuclear.**

- *Enrichment*: natural uranium is 0.7% U-235 (fissile) and 99.3% U-238. Reactor fuel is enriched to about 3 to 5% U-235 (by separating isotopes, e.g. with centrifuges, which exploit the tiny mass difference).
- *Breeding*: U-238 absorbs a fast neutron to become U-239, which decays (by two β⁻ decays) to plutonium-239, which is fissile. A fast breeder reactor makes more fuel than it uses, extending uranium supplies many times.
- *Fusion* (file 19): needs temperatures above about 10⁸ K so nuclei overcome repulsion, and the plasma must be confined (by magnetic fields in a tokamak) without touching the walls. To produce more energy than is put in, the **triple product** n T τ_E (plasma density × temperature × energy confinement time) must exceed about 3 × 10²¹ keV s m⁻³ (Lawson criterion). Achieving high density, temperature and confinement time together is very hard; materials must survive intense neutron bombardment.

#### Worked example

1. A wind turbine with 40 m blades faces a 10 m s⁻¹ wind (ρ = 1.2 kg m⁻³). Find the power in the wind and the output at 40% efficiency.

   A = π × 40² = 5030 m². P = ½ × 5030 × 1.2 × 1000 = 3.0 × 10⁶ W. Output = 1.2 MW.

2. A hydroelectric station has a head of 150 m and a flow of 40 m³ s⁻¹, with 85% efficiency. Find its output.

   P = 1000 × 40 × 9.81 × 150 × 0.85 = 5.0 × 10⁷ W (50 MW).

3. A 1.6 m² solar panel receives 800 W m⁻² and gives 220 W. Find its efficiency.

   Input = 1280 W; efficiency = 0.17.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying pumped storage generates energy (it stores it).

### OD(c) Fuel cells

*Refs: Eduqas OD(c). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The principles of fuel cell operation and their benefits, particularly for greenhouse gas emissions.

#### Explained

A hydrogen fuel cell converts chemical energy directly into electrical energy, without burning. Hydrogen is fed to the anode, where a catalyst (platinum) splits it into protons and electrons: H₂ → 2H⁺ + 2e⁻. The protons pass through an electrolyte membrane; the electrons cannot, so they flow through the external circuit as a current. At the cathode, oxygen combines with the protons and electrons to make water: O₂ + 4H⁺ + 4e⁻ → 2H₂O.

Benefits: the only product is water, so there are no CO₂ or other pollutant emissions at the point of use; more efficient (about 40 to 60%) than combustion engines, since there is no heat-engine limit; quiet; can run continuously while fuel is supplied. Drawbacks: hydrogen must be produced (often from natural gas, which does release CO₂, unless made by electrolysis using renewable electricity), stored and transported safely; catalysts are expensive.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying fuel cells burn hydrogen.

### OD(d), (e) Thermal conduction and insulation

*Refs: Eduqas OD(d), (e). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The thermal conduction equation ΔQ/Δt = −AK Δθ/Δx.
- The effect of insulation on energy loss; heat loss through parallel surfaces using rate of transfer = UAΔθ, including different materials in contact.

#### Explained

**Conduction.** The rate of energy flow through a slab of material of area A, thickness Δx, with a temperature difference Δθ across it:

ΔQ/Δt = −AK Δθ/Δx

where K is the thermal conductivity of the material (W m⁻¹ K⁻¹) and Δθ/Δx the temperature gradient. The minus sign shows that heat flows from hot to cold (down the temperature gradient). Good conductors (copper, K ≈ 400) have high K; insulators (air, K ≈ 0.025; polystyrene, 0.03; brick, 0.6; glass, 1.0) have low K. Trapped air (in foam, fibre or double glazing) is a good insulator because air conducts poorly and cannot convect when trapped.

**U-values.** For building elements (walls, windows, roofs), rate of energy loss = UAΔθ, where U (W m⁻² K⁻¹) is the U-value: the rate of energy transfer per square metre per kelvin difference. Lower U means better insulation. For a single layer, U = K/thickness.

**Layers in contact.** The same power flows through each layer in series (steady state), and the temperature differences across the layers add. So the "thermal resistances" (thickness/K, or 1/U) add: 1/U_total = 1/U₁ + 1/U₂ + …. For walls and windows side by side (parallel surfaces), the energy losses add: total = (U₁A₁ + U₂A₂)Δθ.

#### Worked example

1. A glass window 4.0 mm thick and 1.5 m² in area has 20 °C inside and 5 °C outside surfaces. Find the rate of heat loss (K = 1.0 W m⁻¹ K⁻¹).

   P = 1.5 × 1.0 × 15/0.0040 = 5600 W. (Real windows lose far less because thin air layers at each surface insulate; the glass surfaces are not at the room and outside air temperatures.)

2. A wall of area 12 m² has U = 0.35 W m⁻² K⁻¹; a window of 2.0 m² in it has U = 2.8 W m⁻² K⁻¹. Inside 20 °C, outside 0 °C. Find the total heat loss.

   Wall: 0.35 × 10 × 20 = 70 W (wall area excluding window = 10 m²). Window: 2.8 × 2.0 × 20 = 112 W. Total 182 W.

3. A wall has 0.10 m of brick (K = 0.60) and 0.050 m of insulation (K = 0.030). Find the U-value.

   1/U = 0.10/0.60 + 0.050/0.030 = 0.167 + 1.667 = 1.83; U = 0.55 W m⁻² K⁻¹.

#### Common mistakes

- Adding U-values for layers in series (add 1/U).
- Forgetting to subtract the window area from the wall area.
- Saying melting icebergs raise sea levels significantly.
- Using wind power ∝ v² instead of v³.

### Check questions

1. Explain how the Earth's surface temperature depends on a balance of energy, and how increasing CO₂ affects it. (4 marks)
2. Explain why melting land ice raises sea levels but melting floating ice does not. (3 marks)
3. Derive the expression for the power available from the wind. (3 marks)
4. The wind speed increases from 6 m s⁻¹ to 9 m s⁻¹. By what factor does the available power increase? (1 mark)
5. Explain the purpose of pumped storage schemes and calculate the energy stored when 2.0 × 10⁶ m³ of water is pumped up 500 m. (3 marks)
6. Explain what is meant by breeding in nuclear reactors. (2 marks)
7. Describe how a hydrogen fuel cell produces electricity and give one environmental benefit. (4 marks)
8. A loft has area 50 m² and is insulated to U = 0.16 W m⁻² K⁻¹. Find the heat loss for a 20 K temperature difference, and the energy lost per day. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Surface temperature is steady when absorbed solar power equals power radiated (1); Earth radiates infrared (1); CO₂ absorbs outgoing infrared and re-emits some back (1); more CO₂ means equilibrium is reached at a higher temperature (1).
2. Floating ice displaces its own weight of water (Archimedes) (1); when it melts it occupies the volume it displaced, so no change (1); land ice is not displacing sea water, so adds extra water when it melts (1).
3. Volume of air passing per second = Av; mass per second = ρAv (1); KE per second = ½(ρAv)v² (1); P = ½ρAv³ (1).
4. (9/6)³ = 3.4 (1).
5. Store surplus energy at low demand and release it quickly at peak demand (1); E = ρVgh = 1000 × 2.0 × 10⁶ × 9.81 × 500 (1) = 9.8 × 10¹² J (1).
6. U-238 absorbs neutrons and becomes Pu-239 (after beta decays) (1); Pu-239 is fissile, so the reactor produces more fuel (1).
7. Hydrogen split at anode into protons and electrons using catalyst (1); protons pass through electrolyte, electrons flow through external circuit as current (1); combine with oxygen at cathode to form water (1); only product is water / no CO₂ at point of use (1).
8. P = 0.16 × 50 × 20 = 160 W; E = 160 × 86 400 = 1.4 × 10⁷ J (2).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/32-practical-skills-and-required-practicals.md -->

## Practical skills, working scientifically, and every board's required practicals

All five qualifications test practical skills in the written papers (planning, measuring, analysing and evaluating) and through the separate Practical Endorsement. This file sets out the skills, the standard techniques and instruments, how to analyse data with graphs (including logarithmic graphs), and lists each board's required, core or specified practicals with the file where each is described in full.

### Overview

AS & A for every board. Errors and uncertainties are in file 01; each practical's method is in its topic file (see the tables below).

#### Board differences

- AQA: 12 required practicals (RP1 to RP6 in AS); apparatus and techniques ATa to ATk; practical skills PS1 to PS4 (independent thinking, use and application of scientific methods, numeracy and data handling, instruments and equipment).
- Edexcel: 16 core practicals (CP1 to CP8 in AS); Topic 1 statements 2 (practical skills), 5 (communication), 6 (applications, benefits and risks), 7 (the scientific community), 8 (science and decision making).
- OCR A: Module 1: 1.1 (planning, implementing, analysis, evaluation, in the written exam) and 1.2 (the Practical Endorsement skills and apparatus); 12 Practical Activity Groups PAG1 to PAG12.
- OCR B: Module 1 with the same structure (1.1.1 to 1.1.4, 1.2.1, 1.2.2) and its own PAG list; practical activities are listed in each topic's (d) points.
- Eduqas: specified practical work listed in each topic; the Practical Endorsement.
- The Practical Endorsement (CPAC: common practical assessment criteria) is the same for all boards: follow procedures, use apparatus safely, make and record observations, research and reference, and present data.

### Working scientifically and planning

*Refs: Edexcel 2, 5 to 8 · OCR A 1.1.1, 1.2.1(a), (h), (i) · OCR B 1.1.1, 1.2.1 · AQA PS1, PS2. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Designing an experiment: identifying the independent, dependent and control variables; choosing apparatus and ranges; risk assessment.
- Evaluating whether a method is valid (actually tests the hypothesis) and will give precise, accurate results.
- Communicating with correct terminology; citing sources.
- How the scientific community validates new knowledge (peer review, repeat experiments), and how society uses science to make decisions, weighing benefits and risks.

#### Explained

**Variables.** Independent: the one you change. Dependent: the one you measure. Control: everything else that could affect the result, kept constant. In the stationary waves experiment (file 04), for example, length is independent, frequency dependent, and tension and mass per unit length controlled.

**A good plan states:**

1. The aim or hypothesis.
2. The apparatus (with a labelled diagram) and why each instrument was chosen (resolution, range).
3. The variables and how controls are kept constant.
4. The range and number of values of the independent variable (at least 6 to 8, evenly spread), and repeats.
5. How each measurement is made, with techniques to reduce uncertainty.
6. How the data will be analysed (which graph, what the gradient or intercept means).
7. Hazards and how risks are reduced.

**Validity.** A valid experiment measures what it claims to and controls other variables. A conclusion is valid only if supported by data with acceptable uncertainty.

**Science and society** (Edexcel 6 to 8). New results are published in journals after peer review (checking by independent experts), then accepted only when others reproduce them. Examples: the discovery of the Higgs boson (two independent detectors), the faster-than-light neutrino claim (2011), withdrawn when a faulty cable was found. Societies use scientific evidence (and its uncertainty) to decide on nuclear power, medical imaging doses, climate policy and mobile phone masts, weighing benefits against risks.

#### Common mistakes

- Listing variables without saying how the control variables are kept constant.
- Writing 'repeat to make it more accurate' (repeating improves precision; reduces random error).

### Implementing: apparatus and techniques

*Refs: AQA ATa to ATk · OCR A 1.1.2, 1.2.1(b) to (g), (j), 1.2.2(a) to (l) · OCR B 1.1.2, 1.2.2 · Edexcel 2. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

The standard techniques that every board expects you to have used.

#### Explained

| Technique | Key points |
| --- | --- |
| Metre rule, ruler | Resolution 1 mm; read at eye level to avoid parallax; use a set square or fiducial marker |
| Vernier calipers | Resolution 0.1 mm (or 0.02 mm); internal and external jaws; check zero error |
| Micrometer | Resolution 0.01 mm; use the ratchet so the same pressure is applied; check zero error; measure at several places and orientations |
| Top-pan balance | Resolution 0.01 g or 0.1 g; tare (zero) before use |
| Stopwatch | Resolution 0.01 s, but human reaction time (about 0.1 to 0.2 s) dominates; time many oscillations; use a fiducial marker |
| Light gates and data logger | Measure short times and speeds precisely (no reaction time); speed = length of card ÷ time it blocks the beam |
| Video analysis | Film with a scale in view; frame-by-frame position and time |
| Thermometer / temperature probe | Stir; wait for equilibrium; read at eye level |
| Ammeter, voltmeter, multimeter | Ammeter in series, voltmeter in parallel; choose a suitable range; digital meters are nearly ideal |
| Signal generator and oscilloscope | Set frequency; use time-base and Y-gain to measure period and p.d. (file 17) |
| Lasers | Low power; do not look into the beam; beware reflections (file 05) |
| Radioactive sources and GM tube | Measure background; tongs; minimum time; shielding (file 18) |
| Circuits | Build from a diagram; check connections; switch off between readings to avoid heating |
| ICT | Data loggers, spreadsheets for calculations, graphs and modelling (iterative methods) |

**Reducing uncertainty.**

- Measure over larger quantities: time 20 swings, measure across 10 fringes, use a long wire.
- Repeat and average; discard anomalies before averaging, with a reason.
- Choose instruments with finer resolution when the quantity is small.
- Use a graph rather than a single calculation.

**Recording data.** Tables with headings showing quantity and unit (e.g. "t / s"), consistent decimal places matching the instrument's resolution, raw data and processed data in separate columns.

#### Common mistakes

- Recording readings to more decimal places than the instrument's resolution.
- Timing a single oscillation.

### Analysis

*Refs: OCR A 1.1.3(a) to (d) · OCR B 1.1.3 · AQA PS3 · Edexcel 2. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Processing data with appropriate mathematics and significant figures.
- Plotting graphs: choice and labelling of axes, scales, best-fit lines; finding gradients and intercepts.
- Linearising relationships, including logarithmic plots for exponential and power laws.

#### Explained

**Graph skills.**

- Put the independent variable on the x-axis (unless the analysis needs otherwise).
- Choose scales so the data cover at least half the grid in each direction, with easy divisions (1, 2, 5, 10); avoid awkward multiples of 3.
- Plot points as small crosses accurately to half a small square.
- Draw a smooth best-fit line or curve with points scattered evenly either side; do not join dot to dot.
- For a gradient, use a large triangle (at least half the line), and read points on the line, not data points.

**Linearising.** Rearrange the equation into y = mx + c.

| Relationship | Plot | Gradient | Intercept |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| T = 2π√(l/g) | T² against l | 4π²/g | 0 |
| V = ε − Ir | V against I | −r | ε |
| f = (1/2l)√(T/μ) | f against 1/l | √(T/μ)/2 | 0 |
| V = V₀e^(−t/RC) | ln V against t | −1/RC | ln V₀ |
| y = kxⁿ (power law) | ln y against ln x (or log y against log x) | n | ln k |
| I = k/x² | 1/√I against x | gradient 1/√k | gives offset |

**Logarithmic graphs.** For an exponential y = Ae^(−kx), ln y = ln A − kx (straight line). For a power law y = kxⁿ, log y = log k + n log x; the gradient gives the power n. Log scales also show data that range over many orders of magnitude (OCR B 5.1.3(b)(i)).

**Significant figures.** Give results to the same number of significant figures as the least precise data (file 01).

#### Common mistakes

- Using data points that are not on the line to find a gradient.
- Using a small triangle for the gradient.

### Evaluation

*Refs: OCR A 1.1.4(a) to (e) · OCR B 1.1.4 · AQA PS2, PS4. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Drawing conclusions from results; identifying anomalies; limitations of the procedure; precision and accuracy; percentage error and uncertainty; suggesting improvements.

#### Explained

- **Conclusion**: state what the graph shows (e.g. "T² is directly proportional to l, since the line is straight and passes through the origin within the uncertainties"), and give the derived value with its uncertainty.
- **Compare with an accepted value**: percentage difference = |experimental − accepted| ÷ accepted × 100%. If this is smaller than the percentage uncertainty, the result agrees with the accepted value.
- **Anomalies**: points far from the line; suggest a cause and repeat them.
- **Limitations and improvements**: identify the largest source of uncertainty and suggest a specific, practical improvement (e.g. "use a light gate instead of a stopwatch to remove reaction-time errors"), not "be more careful".
- **Systematic errors**: a non-zero intercept where theory predicts zero suggests one (zero error, end correction).

#### Common mistakes

- Suggesting vague improvements such as 'be more careful'.
- Calling a result accurate because it is precise.

### Check questions

1. A student times 20 swings of a pendulum with a stopwatch. Explain why 20 swings are timed and where the fiducial marker should be placed. (3 marks)
2. Explain the difference between a valid experiment and a reliable (repeatable) one. (2 marks)
3. The relationship between two quantities is believed to be y = kxⁿ. Describe how a graph can be used to find n and k. (3 marks)
4. A student's value for g is 9.62 m s⁻² with a percentage uncertainty of 3%. Does it agree with 9.81 m s⁻²? Explain. (2 marks)
5. Identify the independent, dependent and control variables in AQA required practical 5 (resistivity of a wire). (3 marks)
6. Suggest why a micrometer, not a ruler, is used to measure the diameter of a wire. (2 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Reduces the percentage uncertainty in the time, since reaction-time error is the same for 1 or 20 swings (1); fiducial marker at the centre / equilibrium position (1); where the bob moves fastest, so the moment of passing is clearest (1).
2. Valid: the experiment measures what it is meant to / controls other variables (1); repeatable: similar results obtained when repeated by the same method (1).
3. Plot log y against log x (1); gradient = n (1); intercept = log k, so k = 10^intercept (1).
4. Percentage difference = (9.81 − 9.62)/9.81 = 1.9% (1); less than the 3% uncertainty, so it agrees (1).
5. Independent: length of wire between contacts (1); dependent: resistance (from V and I) (1); control: same wire / diameter / material / temperature (low current) (1).
6. Diameter is very small (under 1 mm) (1); micrometer resolution 0.01 mm gives much smaller percentage uncertainty than a ruler's 1 mm (1).
